Orientation · Scope and Compilation Model

0.1 Scope and audience0.2 The compilation model0.3 Why the model matters0.4 Version pinning0.5 File resolution and naming0.6 Tooling reference

This guide documents Blade as it behaves in Laravel 13 on PHP 8.3. It opens with the scope of the templating layer, the compile-to-PHP model that underpins every other behaviour, and the version assumptions the rest of the document relies on.

0.1 Scope and audience

Defines what Blade is responsible for inside a Laravel application and what it deliberately delegates elsewhere. Reading this section tells you which concerns belong in a template and which belong in a view model, an action, or a component class.

Blade is the default view layer of Laravel 13. It owns one responsibility: converting a declarative template file into a renderable PHP file and executing it against data supplied by the application layer. It does not query the database, validate input, hold application state, or decide authorisation policy. Those concerns live in controllers, actions, Eloquent, Form Requests and policies; a template that performs them is a template that has become a liability.

The audience for this guide is engineers who already ship Laravel applications and need to reason about Blade precisely — its compilation, its escaping guarantees, its component system and its performance characteristics — rather than merely imitate syntax.

Convention. "View", "template" and "Blade file" are used interchangeably throughout this guide. All three refer to a file resolved by the view factory and rendered through the Blade compiler.

<p>Before the syntax: what this guide considers a template's job, and — more usefully — what it does not. The line is simple, and it is the same line that decides whether a template can be cached, tested in isolation, and reused by a different caller.</p> <h4>What a template is responsible for</h4> <p>Exactly two things: turning data into markup, and nothing else. A template decides what appears and in what order. It does not decide what the data is, who may see it, or whether the request is allowed to exist at all. That boundary is not a style preference — it is the reason a template can be rendered by a different caller, cached by a key that means something, and read by someone who was not in the room when it was written.</p> <p>The test for whether a template has stayed inside the boundary is simple: could this file be reused for a different route, or for a different user, without changing a line? If answering yes requires editing the template, then logic has leaked into it. A template that decides whether the current user is an administrator has taken on a job that belongs to a policy, and the consequence is that the same partial cannot be included from a console command, a queued job or an API response without the administrator check failing in a context where there is no administrator.</p> <h4>The three jobs that look like a template's job</h4> <p>Fetching data, enforcing access, and choosing what to display are all decisions that change independently of how the page looks. Fetching belongs in a controller or a query service, because the shape of the query is a data-layer concern. Access belongs in a policy, because it needs to be testable in isolation and auditable without rendering HTML. Choosing what to display is a genuine template concern, and it is the one worth guarding carefully — because a template that computes a display decision correctly has a name for that logic, and a template that computes it as a side effect of a loop does not.</p>

Before the syntax: what this guide considers a template's job, and — more usefully — what it does not. The line is simple, and it is the same line that decides whether a template can be cached, tested in isolation, and reused by a different caller.

What a template is responsible for

Exactly two things: turning data into markup, and nothing else. A template decides what appears and in what order. It does not decide what the data is, who may see it, or whether the request is allowed to exist at all. That boundary is not a style preference — it is the reason a template can be rendered by a different caller, cached by a key that means something, and read by someone who was not in the room when it was written.

The test for whether a template has stayed inside the boundary is simple: could this file be reused for a different route, or for a different user, without changing a line? If answering yes requires editing the template, then logic has leaked into it. A template that decides whether the current user is an administrator has taken on a job that belongs to a policy, and the consequence is that the same partial cannot be included from a console command, a queued job or an API response without the administrator check failing in a context where there is no administrator.

The three jobs that look like a template's job

Fetching data, enforcing access, and choosing what to display are all decisions that change independently of how the page looks. Fetching belongs in a controller or a query service, because the shape of the query is a data-layer concern. Access belongs in a policy, because it needs to be testable in isolation and auditable without rendering HTML. Choosing what to display is a genuine template concern, and it is the one worth guarding carefully — because a template that computes a display decision correctly has a name for that logic, and a template that computes it as a side effect of a loop does not.


{{-- THE SCOPE IN ONE VIEW. Everything the template needs arrives
     as props; nothing it does decides anything about the request. --}}

{{-- resources/views/components/invoice.blade.php --}}
@props([
    'invoice',
    'currency' => config('app.currency', 'GBP'),
])

<article class="invoice">
    <header>
        <h1>{{ __('invoices.title') }}</h1>
        <p class="number">{{ $invoice->number }}</p>
    </header>

    <table>
        <thead>
            <tr>
                <th>{{ __('invoices.description') }}</th>
                <th>{{ __('invoices.amount') }}</th>
            </tr>
        </thead>
        <tbody>
            @forelse ($invoice->lines as $line)
                {{-- The empty case is RENDERED, not skipped. A
                     component that assumes a non-empty collection
                     crashes on the first invoice that has none. --}}
                <tr>
                    <td>{{ $line->description }}</td>
                    <td>{{ $line->amount->format($currency) }}</td>
                </tr>
            @empty
                <tr><td colspan="2">{{ __('invoices.no_lines') }}</td></tr>
            @endforelse
        </tbody>
    </table>
</article>

{{-- AND THE CONTROLLER, showing what the template does NOT do: it
     does not query, it does not authorise, and it does not format a
     value it was not given. --}}
// app/Http/Controllers/InvoiceController.php
public function show(Invoice $invoice): Response
{
    $this->authorize('view', $invoice);            // an access
                                                       // decision, in
                                                       // the
                                                       // controller

    $invoice->loadMissing(['lines.amount', 'customer']);

    return response()->view('invoices.show', [
        'invoice'  => $invoice,
        'currency' => $invoice->currency->code,
    ]);
}


{{-- THE RECURSION THAT PROVES THE BOUNDARY. A component that
     renders itself with different data cannot loop, because the
     parent supplies a finite tree and the component never widens
     it. --}}

{{-- resources/views/components/tree-node.blade.php --}}
@props(['node'])

<li>
    <a href="{{ $node->url }}">{{ $node->title }}</a>

    @if ($node->children->isNotEmpty())
        {{-- Recursion through the COMPONENT SYSTEM, not through a
             PHP function returning HTML. Each level is a
             component instance with its own typed props, so a deep
             tree cannot leak a variable from three levels up. --}}
        <ul>
            @foreach ($node->children as $child)
                <x-tree-node :node="$child" />
            @endforeach
        </ul>
    @endif
</li>

{{-- THE TEST, applied to each of the three jobs: --}}

{{-- 1. Fetching. "Could this template be rendered without a
        database?" For a pure display component: yes, and it should
        be — which is why the parent passes a fully-loaded model and
        the component never calls load() or a relation accessor that
        lazy-loads. --}}

{{-- 2. Access. "Could this template be rendered for any user?" For
        a component that takes the rows it needs: yes. For one that
        calls auth()->user()->isAdmin() to decide whether to render a
        button: no — and that template is now uncacheable, cannot be
        snapshot-tested, and its behaviour differs between a web
        request and a queued job that renders an email. --}}

{{-- 3. Presentation. "Could this template choose differently for
        the same data?" Yes, and it should: a component that
        receives a status and renders a badge for it is making a
        DISPLAY decision, which is exactly what belongs in a
        template. --}}

     as props; nothing it does decides anything about the request. --}}

    </header>

    <table>

                <th>{{ __('invoices.description') }}</th>
                <th>{{ __('invoices.amount') }}</th>
            </tr>
        </thead>

                {{-- The empty case is RENDERED, not skipped. A
                     component that assumes a non-empty collection
                     crashes on the first invoice that has none. --}}

                    <td>{{ $line->description }}</td>
                    <td>{{ $line->amount->format($currency) }}</td>
                </tr>
            @empty

            @endforelse
        </tbody>

     does not query, it does not authorise, and it does not format a
     value it was not given. --}}

{

                                                       // decision, in
                                                       // the
                                                       // controller

        'invoice'  => $invoice,
        'currency' => $invoice->currency->code,

0.2 The compilation model

Shows the compiled PHP that Blade produces for a small template. Understanding the exact output makes every directive in the following chapters predictable rather than magical.

Blade performs no interpretation at request time. A .blade.php file is compiled once into plain PHP and stored on disk; the runtime path only includes that PHP file. Given the template below:

<h4>What the compiler actually emits</h4> <p>Every directive becomes a PHP function call in the compiled file, and the transformation is uniform: a directive is replaced by a function invocation with the raw source of its expression as the first argument. This is worth knowing precisely because it explains three behaviours that otherwise look arbitrary — why a directive's expression can contain parentheses and commas without them confusing the parser, why the argument is a string rather than parsed PHP, and why an undefined variable inside a directive behaves differently from one outside it.</p> <p>The compiled output is plain PHP that could have been written by hand. Nothing about it is faster than equivalent hand-written PHP, because it <em>is</em> hand-written PHP, just not by you. The performance characteristics of Blade are therefore the performance characteristics of the function calls it emits plus the cost of resolving variables from the view data — which is why a template with a thousand <code>@if</code> directives and a template with none take roughly the same time, and why the real cost is almost always the number of data lookups rather than the number of directives.</p> <h4>Why the expression is a string</h4> <p>The compiler stores the directive's argument as a string and evaluates it at run time. The alternative — parsing the expression at compile time and emitting the parse tree — would give better diagnostics and a faster run time, and it would break every use of a directive with an expression the parser cannot handle: a variable reference that only exists at run time, a function call the compiler cannot resolve, or a construct whose meaning depends on a conditional definition earlier in the request.</p> <p>This is why a custom directive compiles to a body that calls <code>call_user_func</code> with your compiled expression string, and why the expression is evaluated in the view's scope — that is, with the view data available as local variables. It also explains why a directive cannot see a variable that was defined after it in the template, and why passing the same name into a component does not automatically create a local: the data is in the view's shared scope, not in a component's isolated one.</p>

What the compiler actually emits

Every directive becomes a PHP function call in the compiled file, and the transformation is uniform: a directive is replaced by a function invocation with the raw source of its expression as the first argument. This is worth knowing precisely because it explains three behaviours that otherwise look arbitrary — why a directive's expression can contain parentheses and commas without them confusing the parser, why the argument is a string rather than parsed PHP, and why an undefined variable inside a directive behaves differently from one outside it.

The compiled output is plain PHP that could have been written by hand. Nothing about it is faster than equivalent hand-written PHP, because it is hand-written PHP, just not by you. The performance characteristics of Blade are therefore the performance characteristics of the function calls it emits plus the cost of resolving variables from the view data — which is why a template with a thousand @if directives and a template with none take roughly the same time, and why the real cost is almost always the number of data lookups rather than the number of directives.

Why the expression is a string

The compiler stores the directive's argument as a string and evaluates it at run time. The alternative — parsing the expression at compile time and emitting the parse tree — would give better diagnostics and a faster run time, and it would break every use of a directive with an expression the parser cannot handle: a variable reference that only exists at run time, a function call the compiler cannot resolve, or a construct whose meaning depends on a conditional definition earlier in the request.

This is why a custom directive compiles to a body that calls call_user_func with your compiled expression string, and why the expression is evaluated in the view's scope — that is, with the view data available as local variables. It also explains why a directive cannot see a variable that was defined after it in the template, and why passing the same name into a component does not automatically create a local: the data is in the view's shared scope, not in a component's isolated one.

{{-- resources/views/welcome.blade.php --}}
<ul>
    @foreach ($users as $user)
        <li>{{ $user->name }}</li>
    @endforeach
</ul>

Blade compiles it into the following PHP, which is what the application actually executes:

// storage/framework/views/8f2c....php
<ul>
    @foreach ($users as $user):
        <li><?= e($user->name) ?></li>
    @endforeach;
</ul>

Two consequences follow immediately. First, {{ $user->name }} becomes <?= e($user->name) ?>, so escaped output is a property of the compiler rather than a habit you must remember. Second, because directives expand to PHP control structures, the compiled file is cacheable and executable by opcache with no Blade runtime in the hot path.


{{-- THE TEMPLATE --}}
{{-- resources/views/report.blade.php --}}
<ul>
    @foreach ($users as $user)
        @if ($user->isActive())
            <li>{{ $user->name }}</li>
        @endif
    @endforeach
</ul>

{{-- THE COMPILED PHP, and this is what is actually executed. The
     stored file is byte-for-byte equivalent to the below; the only
     thing the runtime path adds is the include. --}}

<ul>
    
        isActive())): ?>
            <li>name); ?></li>
        
    
</ul>

{{-- THREE THINGS THAT OUTPUT MAKES PLAIN --}}

{{-- 1. e() IS CALLED. {{ }} compiles to e(), so escaping is not a
        property of the syntax but of the function it calls. {{ }}
        with three braces compiles to a raw echo, which is why it
        exists and why it is the thing to look for in a review. --}}

{{-- 2. LOOP AND CONDITION COMPILE TO PHP'S ALTERNATE SYNTAX, so
        the body is emitted once and the loop runs it N times. There
        is no re-parsing, no closure, and no per-iteration call. A
        loop over a thousand rows emits the body once. --}}

{{-- 3. ($users ?? []) IS NOT WHAT YOU WROTE. The compiler adds
        null coalescing around a loop variable, so an undefined
        $users renders an empty list rather than a fatal. That is
        convenient and it is also a trap: a typo in a variable name
        is now a SILENT empty render, not an error. Type the loop
        variable and check for it in a typed component instead. --}}


{{-- THE TEMPLATE --}}
{{-- resources/views/report.blade.php --}}
<ul>
    @foreach ($users as $user)
        @if ($user->isActive())
            <li>{{ $user->name }}</li>
        @endif
    @endforeach
</ul>

{{-- THE COMPILED PHP, and this is what is actually executed. The
     stored file is byte-for-byte equivalent to the below; the only
     thing the runtime path adds is the include. --}}

<ul>
    
        isActive())): ?>
            <li>name); ?></li>
        
    
</ul>

{{-- THREE THINGS THAT OUTPUT MAKES PLAIN --}}

{{-- 1. e() IS CALLED. {{ }} compiles to e(), so escaping is not a
        property of the syntax but of the function it calls. {{ }}
        with three braces compiles to a raw echo, which is why it
        exists and why it is the thing to look for in a review. --}}

{{-- 2. LOOP AND CONDITION COMPILE TO PHP'S ALTERNATE SYNTAX, so
        the body is emitted once and the loop runs it N times. There
        is no re-parsing, no closure, and no per-iteration call. A
        loop over a thousand rows emits the body once. --}}

{{-- 3. ($users ?? []) IS NOT WHAT YOU WROTE. The compiler adds
        null coalescing around a loop variable, so an undefined
        $users renders an empty list rather than a fatal. That is
        convenient and it is also a trap: a typo in a variable name
        is now a SILENT empty render, not an error. Type the loop
        variable and check for it in a typed component instead. --}}

0.3 Why the model matters

Connects the compilation model to practical decisions about caching, debugging, editor tooling and refactoring. Each is a direct consequence of Blade being a compiler rather than an interpreter.

  • Caching is per file. Invalidation is keyed on the source file’s modification time, so a cache flush is not required after every code change.
  • Errors are misreported. A stack trace points at a compiled file in storage/framework/views, not at your template, until you read the compiled output.
  • Tooling is limited by syntax. Editors cannot evaluate Blade expressions, so type inference inside templates is best-effort by nature.
  • Directives are macros, not functions. A custom directive is textual substitution performed at compile time; it has no runtime behaviour of its own.

Rule of thumb. Because Blade is a compiler, anything that must be inspectable, diffable or statically analysed must live outside the template. Push logic into PHP, not into directives.

<h4>The specific problems the model solves</h4> <p>Blade is not a general-purpose template engine and it is not trying to be. Its design follows from four problems, each of which it solves by removing a capability rather than by adding a feature — and each removal is a decision that shows up as a limitation elsewhere.</p> <p><strong>Safe by default.</strong> Interpolation compiles to an escaping call, so a value carrying markup is escaped unless the author asked otherwise. An engine that emitted raw text would push the decision onto every template author, and the decision would be made correctly approximately as often as any other decision made a hundred thousand times.</p> <p><strong>Compile once.</strong> A <code>.blade.php</code> file becomes PHP on disk. The cost of parsing a template is paid when the file changes, not on every request, which is what makes a template with a large static structure affordable.</p> <p><strong>Composition with inheritance.</strong> A layout is a template that yields, and a page is a template that extends. Because both are compiled to ordinary PHP with an output buffer, a child can inject into a parent's named sections without either knowing whether the other exists at compile time.</p> <p><strong>A component model.</strong> Reusable fragments with typed props, a default value for each prop, and an attribute bag are the mechanism that keeps a template from growing by copy-and-paste.</p> <h4>What was left out, and where it went</h4> <p>No inheritance chain more than one level deep, because a chain makes the rendered output depend on the whole chain and nobody can reason about it. No arbitrary expressions in the template language, because an expression evaluator in a view is a second language with no type checking and no way to find the callers. No implicit scope sharing, because a template that sees every variable in the request cannot be reasoned about locally. Each of these was moved out of the view: inheritance into components with props, expressions into view models and presenters, scope into explicit props.</p>

The specific problems the model solves

Blade is not a general-purpose template engine and it is not trying to be. Its design follows from four problems, each of which it solves by removing a capability rather than by adding a feature — and each removal is a decision that shows up as a limitation elsewhere.

Safe by default. Interpolation compiles to an escaping call, so a value carrying markup is escaped unless the author asked otherwise. An engine that emitted raw text would push the decision onto every template author, and the decision would be made correctly approximately as often as any other decision made a hundred thousand times.

Compile once. A .blade.php file becomes PHP on disk. The cost of parsing a template is paid when the file changes, not on every request, which is what makes a template with a large static structure affordable.

Composition with inheritance. A layout is a template that yields, and a page is a template that extends. Because both are compiled to ordinary PHP with an output buffer, a child can inject into a parent's named sections without either knowing whether the other exists at compile time.

A component model. Reusable fragments with typed props, a default value for each prop, and an attribute bag are the mechanism that keeps a template from growing by copy-and-paste.

What was left out, and where it went

No inheritance chain more than one level deep, because a chain makes the rendered output depend on the whole chain and nobody can reason about it. No arbitrary expressions in the template language, because an expression evaluator in a view is a second language with no type checking and no way to find the callers. No implicit scope sharing, because a template that sees every variable in the request cannot be reasoned about locally. Each of these was moved out of the view: inheritance into components with props, expressions into view models and presenters, scope into explicit props.


{{-- 1. SAFE BY DEFAULT: the cost of the escape-by-default rule. --}}

{{-- {{ }} ALWAYS ESCAPES. A value carrying markup is rendered as
     text unless the author asked otherwise, which means the
     dangerous case is the opt-in one and the safe case needs no
     review. --}}
{{ $user->bio }}
{{-- compiles to e($user->bio), so <b> renders as <b> --}}

{{-- THE OPT-IN IS A DIFFERENT SYNTAX, and that is the whole point:
     the escape and the raw are not a flag and a value, they are
     two different characters, so a reviewer sees them separately
     and a global search for the raw form finds every instance. --}}
{!! $user->bioHtml !!}

{{-- THE COST: there is no way to interpolate markup you generated
     yourself without a helper, and that helper needs to exist and
     be reviewed rather than being improvised at the call site. --}}
@php($safeHtml = new \Illuminate\Support\HtmlString($generatedHtml))
{{ $safeHtml }}

{{-- 2. COMPILE ONCE: the cost is a cache entry. --}}
{{-- The compiled file is a cache with mtime invalidation, so the
     costs are a writable storage directory, a stale-template class
     of deployment bug, and a build step in a read-only container. --}}
php artisan view:cache     // the build step that removes both
php artisan view:clear     // the recovery when a stale template is
                           // suspected

{{-- 3. INHERITANCE: the cost is coupling by name, in both
     directions, with nothing checking it. --}}

{{-- Parent: the name is a string. Child: the name is a string.
     Renaming the section in the parent SILENTLY empties the yield
     in the child — the page renders, with a hole in it. --}}
{{-- resources/views/layouts/app.blade.php --}}
<main>@yield('content')</main>

{{-- resources/views/orders/show.blade.php --}}
@section('content')
    <h1>{{ $order->reference }}</h1>
@endsection

{{-- 4. COMPONENTS: the cost is that a component is a class. --}}
{{-- It needs a namespace registration, it cannot be rendered
     before the provider boots, and it is resolved through the
     container so it can be tested with a constructor dependency
     rather than by passing data down through the view. --}}
<x-order-summary :order="$order" />

{{-- WHICH OF THE FOUR COSTS ARE WORTH PAYING, and the honest
     answer is that all four are cheap and the alternative is not.
     An escaping-by-request template has an XSS audit cost that
     scales with the number of interpolations; an interpreted
     template has a per-request parse cost that scales with the
     number of requests; a template language without composition
     has a copy-paste cost that scales with the number of layouts.
     The limitations are where the scalability went. --}}


{{-- THE LIMITATION THAT BITES MOST OFTEN: extends is ONE LEVEL. --}}

{{-- A chain of two or more layouts is legal, and it is where
     "why is this section empty" becomes hard to answer — because a
     section defined twice is rendered by the closest ancestor, and
     the closest ancestor is not the one you are reading. --}}

{{-- WRONG: three layers, and @yield('content') in the base is
     never reached from orders/show because admin.blade.php also
     defines a content section and it wins. --}}
{{-- base.blade.php --}}
<html><body>@yield('content')</body></html>

{{-- admin.blade.php --}}
@extends('base')
@section('content')
    <nav>Admin</nav>
    @yield('content')      {{-- the nested yield: works, but now
                                   TWO places define 'content' and
                                   the reader has to know which
                                   wins --}}
@endsection

{{-- RIGHT: one layout, with a slot for the variation. A component
     composes; a layout substitutes. --}}
{{-- resources/views/layouts/app.blade.php --}}
<x-app-shell :title="$title">
    {{ $slot }}

    <x-slot:sidebar>
        @isset($sidebar)
            {{ $sidebar }}
        @else
            <x-default-sidebar />
        @endisset
    </x-slot:sidebar>
</x-app-shell>

{{-- AND THE COMPOSITION THAT REPLACES THE CHAIN ENTIRELY: --}}
{{-- resources/views/admin/reports/index.blade.php --}}
<x-app-shell :title="__('reports.title')">
    <x-reports-filters :filters="$filters" />

    <x-reports-table :rows="$rows" />

    <x-slot:sidebar>
        <x-reports-nav :active="$activeFilter" />
    </x-slot:sidebar>
</x-app-shell>

{{-- FOUR FILES WHERE THE CHAIN HAD THREE, and every one of them is
     independently testable and independently readable. The chain
     had a hidden dependency between two files that share a section
     name; this has none. --}}


{{-- 1. SAFE BY DEFAULT: the cost of the escape-by-default rule. --}}

{{-- {{ }} ALWAYS ESCAPES. A value carrying markup is rendered as

0.4 Version pinning

States the exact platform this document describes and calls out the differences from earlier major versions that affect template code. Version-specific syntax is the most common source of advice that does not apply to your application.

ComponentPinned versionNote
Laravel13.xBlade directives and component syntax as shipped in this line
PHP8.3 or newerReadonly classes and typed class constants are available
Blade compilerIlluminate\ViewCache path storage/framework/views
Livewire3.xReferenced only where Blade and Livewire meet
Alpine.js3.xUsed as the client-side reactivity layer

Nothing in this guide relies on a package version that is not listed here. Where a directive behaves differently across major versions — for example the component attribute bag merging rules — the difference is called out explicitly at the point of use.

<h4>Why a caret is a policy decision</h4> <p><code>^12.0</code> permits any 12.x release, which means an installation that worked in January can install different code in March without a single file in your repository changing. That is the point of the constraint — it lets security and bug-fix releases arrive without a commit from you — and it is also the risk: the upgrade is invisible to your version control, to your lockfile review, and to whoever reads the log of a deployment six weeks later.</p> <p>Inside an application the trade is nearly always worth taking, because staying behind on a patch release is a security liability and the maintenance window belongs to the framework team rather than to you. The risk is bounded by the lockfile, which records the exact resolution — so a second developer running <code>composer install</code> gets the same versions, and the real exposure is the next person to run <code>composer update</code>.</p> <h4>Where pinning tightens</h4> <p>A package that changes behaviour within a major version is a package that cannot be updated safely by a stranger. Two categories deserve an exact constraint: a package whose major version is not actually breaking when it breaks, and a package you depend on a specific internal detail of rather than its interface. Both are more common than they should be, and both are discovered during an upgrade rather than before it.</p> <h4>The commands that differ, and which one CI should run</h4> <p>The distinction that matters operationally is between the command that resolves and the command that installs. <code>update</code> re-resolves the dependency graph and rewrites the lockfile; <code>install</code> installs exactly what the lockfile records and fails if it is inconsistent with the manifest. CI should run <code>install</code>, always — a build that re-resolves is a build whose result depends on when it ran, and a lockfile that CI never checks is a lockfile that drifts the first time a developer's local database has an extension nobody else does.</p>

Why a caret is a policy decision

^12.0 permits any 12.x release, which means an installation that worked in January can install different code in March without a single file in your repository changing. That is the point of the constraint — it lets security and bug-fix releases arrive without a commit from you — and it is also the risk: the upgrade is invisible to your version control, to your lockfile review, and to whoever reads the log of a deployment six weeks later.

Inside an application the trade is nearly always worth taking, because staying behind on a patch release is a security liability and the maintenance window belongs to the framework team rather than to you. The risk is bounded by the lockfile, which records the exact resolution — so a second developer running composer install gets the same versions, and the real exposure is the next person to run composer update.

Where pinning tightens

A package that changes behaviour within a major version is a package that cannot be updated safely by a stranger. Two categories deserve an exact constraint: a package whose major version is not actually breaking when it breaks, and a package you depend on a specific internal detail of rather than its interface. Both are more common than they should be, and both are discovered during an upgrade rather than before it.

The commands that differ, and which one CI should run

The distinction that matters operationally is between the command that resolves and the command that installs. update re-resolves the dependency graph and rewrites the lockfile; install installs exactly what the lockfile records and fails if it is inconsistent with the manifest. CI should run install, always — a build that re-resolves is a build whose result depends on when it ran, and a lockfile that CI never checks is a lockfile that drifts the first time a developer's local database has an extension nobody else does.


{{-- WHY A FLOATING CONSTRAINT IS A POLICY, IN PRACTICE. --}}

{{-- composer.json says ^11.0. Two developers, two months apart: --}}
{{-- January, on the developer's machine --}}
composer update laravel/framework
# laravel/framework 11.30.1  ->  11.44.2   (14 minor releases)

{{-- March, on the developer's machine, same composer.json --}}
composer update
# laravel/framework 11.44.2  ->  11.46.1

{{-- Neither developer changed a file. The application's behaviour
     changed between them, and composer.lock is the only record of
     what actually happened — which is why the lockfile is a
     reviewed artefact and not a convenience. --}}

{{-- WHAT A MINOR RELEASE CONTAINS, and why the risk is not zero: --}}
{{--   - bug fixes and security patches        (the good 95%)
     - new features, gated by the version       (safe)
     - deprecation notices                      (safe, and useful)
     - behaviour changes not covered by a       (the 1-2% that
       major-version contract, including:          produces a bug
         - a default value changing
         - a return type widening
         - a service provider boot order change
         - a config key's meaning changing while
           the key itself is unchanged           }}
{{-- The last three are why "we do patch releases, so it is safe"
   is not a complete argument, and why the lockfile is what makes
   the risk manageable rather than absent. --}}

{{-- THE ANSWER THAT ACTUALLY WORKS: update on a schedule, not on
     demand. --}}
{{-- composer.json, for an application --}}
"require": {
    "laravel/framework": "^11.0"
}

{{-- .github/workflows/dependencies.yml — weekly, opens a pull
     request, and the diff IS the review. --}}
name: dependencies
on:
  schedule:
    - cron: '0 6 * * 1'      # Monday 06:00
  workflow_dispatch:

jobs:
  update:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: shivammathur/setup-php@v2
        with: { php-version: '8.3', coverage: none }
      - run: composer update --prefer-dist --no-interaction
      - name: The suite is the review
        run: php artisan test --parallel
      - name: Nothing outside the lockfile changed
        run: |
          # A dependency update that also changes composer.json or a
          # source file is not a dependency update.
          git diff --exit-code -- composer.json src/             || { echo "::error::source changed during a dependency bump"; exit 1; }


{{-- WHERE THE CONSTRAINT TIGHTENS, and the diagnostic that finds
     it. --}}

{{-- composer.json — the two categories that deserve an exact
     pin, and the reason each is a different problem. --}}
{
    "require": {
        {{-- (a) YOU DEPEND ON AN INTERNAL DETAIL. The public API
             is stable; the detail you read is not, and it changed
             in 2.5 without a major bump because it was not part of
             the contract. A floating constraint means composer
             update can break your code without changing composer
             require at all. --}}
        "vendor/report-builder": "2.4.1",

        {{-- (b) A PACKAGE WHOSE MAJOR VERSION IS NOT BREAKING. It
             changed behaviour in 3.0 for two of its users and a
             feature flag for everyone else, so ^3.0 gives you a
             semver signal that does not exist. --}}
        "vendor/legacy-mailer": "3.2.0",

        {{-- (c) A NATIVE EXTENSION. Its constraint must intersect
             PHP's, and the intersection is where a floating
             constraint produces a machine that installs and another
             that does not. --}}
        "ext-redis": "*",

        {{-- (d) A PACKAGE YOU FORKED. The fork is a different
             version with the same name, and its constraint is
             whatever you published. Pin it. --}}
        "acme/report-builder": "dev-main"
    },

    "conflict": {
        {{-- THE PIN AS A CONFLICT RATHER THAN A CONSTRAINT. This
             form lets you KEEP the caret for update purposes while
             forbidding the version you know is broken, which is
             the shape a real project wants: float, except here. --}}
        "laravel/framework": "11.44.0",
        "vendor/report-builder": "<2.4.1"
    }
}

{{-- THE DIAGNOSTIC, which finds the problem before the upgrade
     does: list every package whose installed version came from a
     range wider than one major. --}}
// a script worth keeping
$installed = json_decode(shell_exec('composer show --format=json'), true);
foreach ($installed['installed'] as $pkg) {
    $constraint = $installed['dev'] ? null : null;
    if (isset($composerJson['require'][$pkg['name']])
        && str_starts_with($composerJson['require'][$pkg['name']], '^')) {
        // Report it: a caret constraint means an untested version
        // can arrive without a commit from this repository.
        echo "{$pkg['name']} {$pkg['version']} (floating)
";
    }
}

0.5 File resolution and naming

Documents how a view name becomes a file path, and how namespaced view and component names follow dot notation. Resolution is unambiguous once you know which namespaces are registered by default.

<h4>The lookup is a search, and the search order is the design</h4> <p>A view name is resolved by taking each configured directory in turn and testing whether the file exists. The first match wins. That is the entire mechanism, and every convention about where views live follows from it: namespaced views become subdirectories because a namespace maps to a directory, and a name maps to a path under it. The resolution is filesystem-based rather than class-based, which is what lets a view be added without touching a registry.</p> <p>Because the search is ordered and the first match wins, an override is just a file placed earlier in the list. This is the mechanism behind published views, behind a package overriding a vendor's view, and behind the "why does my edit have no effect" problem — a package's view directory is registered after the application's, so a file with the same relative name in <code>resources/views</code> wins, and editing the vendor file has no effect because the vendor copy is never reached.</p> <h4>Namespacing, and the dot that is a directory separator</h4> <p>A namespace is a directory with a prefix. <code>mail::message</code> resolves to <code>mail/message.blade.php</code> under that namespace's path, and the double colon is a separator rather than part of a name. Package view namespaces are registered from the package's service provider, and the registration is what makes <code>vendor-package::</code> resolvable at all.</p> <h4>Why a missing view throws rather than rendering nothing</h4> <p>Resolution failure is an exception, and that is the right behaviour for a typo but the wrong one for an optional view. A template that includes something that may or may not exist needs to test for it explicitly, which is why the framework provides a "does this view exist" check and why <code>@include</code> has an ignore-missing variant. The asymmetry is deliberate: a missing layout is a bug, and a missing optional partial is a normal condition, so the template language provides two ways to say each.</p>

The lookup is a search, and the search order is the design

A view name is resolved by taking each configured directory in turn and testing whether the file exists. The first match wins. That is the entire mechanism, and every convention about where views live follows from it: namespaced views become subdirectories because a namespace maps to a directory, and a name maps to a path under it. The resolution is filesystem-based rather than class-based, which is what lets a view be added without touching a registry.

Because the search is ordered and the first match wins, an override is just a file placed earlier in the list. This is the mechanism behind published views, behind a package overriding a vendor's view, and behind the "why does my edit have no effect" problem — a package's view directory is registered after the application's, so a file with the same relative name in resources/views wins, and editing the vendor file has no effect because the vendor copy is never reached.

Namespacing, and the dot that is a directory separator

A namespace is a directory with a prefix. mail::message resolves to mail/message.blade.php under that namespace's path, and the double colon is a separator rather than part of a name. Package view namespaces are registered from the package's service provider, and the registration is what makes vendor-package:: resolvable at all.

Why a missing view throws rather than rendering nothing

Resolution failure is an exception, and that is the right behaviour for a typo but the wrong one for an optional view. A template that includes something that may or may not exist needs to test for it explicitly, which is why the framework provides a "does this view exist" check and why @include has an ignore-missing variant. The asymmetry is deliberate: a missing layout is a bug, and a missing optional partial is a normal condition, so the template language provides two ways to say each.

// view("orders.index")
resources/views/orders/index.blade.php

// view("admin::dashboard")  — namespaced
resources/views/vendor/admin/dashboard.blade.php

// <x-alerts::notice />
resources/views/components/alerts/notice.blade.php

The compiler resolves view() names against registered namespaces before falling back to the resources/views hint, and resolves component tags against every registered component namespace, then against anonymous components in resources/views/components. Namespaced views under resources/views/vendor additionally support package publishing through php artisan vendor:publish.


{{-- THE RESOLUTION, spelled out. Given two registered paths, in
     this order:

         resources/views/
         vendor/acme/ui/resources/views/

     then mail::message resolves by testing, in order:

         1. resources/views/mail/message.blade.php
         2. vendor/acme/ui/resources/views/mail/message.blade.php

     and stops at the first that exists. --}}

{{-- NAMESPACES ARE PREFIXES ON A DIRECTORY, and the :: is a
     separator rather than a character. --}}
{{-- service provider of a package --}}
use IlluminateSupportServiceProvider;

class UiServiceProvider extends ServiceProvider
{
    public function boot(): void
    {
        // A namespace maps to a directory. There is no registry of
        // view NAMES: adding a file makes it exist, which is what
        // lets a package ship views without a manifest.
        $this->loadViewsFrom(__DIR__ . '/../resources/views', 'ui');

        // THE OVERRIDE. The application's path goes FIRST, so a
        // file of the same name in resources/views wins and the
        // package's copy is never reached. This is how a user
        // customises a package view without forking it - and how
        // an edit to the vendor file has no effect whatsoever.
        $this->publishes([
            __DIR__ . '/../resources/views' => resource_path('views/vendor/ui'),
        ], 'ui-views');
    }
}

{{-- TWO WAYS TO SAY "MAYBE IT IS NOT THERE", and the asymmetry is
     deliberate. --}}

{{-- A MISSING LAYOUT IS A BUG: let it throw, loudly, with the name
     in the message. An include does this by default. --}}
@extends('layouts.app')

{{-- A MISSING OPTIONAL PARTIAL IS A NORMAL CONDITION: test for it.
     includeIf and the -once form both return nothing rather than
     throwing. --}}
@includeIf('partials.' . $section)
@includeWhen($condition, 'partials.promo')
@includeFirst(['partials.promo', 'partials.default'])

{{-- THE FAILURE MESSAGE, which is why the exception is right. --}}
// InvalidArgumentException: View [mail.mesage] not found.
//      ^^^^^^ a typo, and it says exactly which name failed


{{-- THE RESOLUTION, spelled out. Given two registered paths, in
     this order:

         resources/views/
         vendor/acme/ui/resources/views/

     then mail::message resolves by testing, in order:

         1. resources/views/mail/message.blade.php
         2. vendor/acme/ui/resources/views/mail/message.blade.php

     and stops at the first that exists. --}}

{{-- NAMESPACES ARE PREFIXES ON A DIRECTORY, and the :: is a
     separator rather than a character. --}}
{{-- service provider of a package --}}
use IlluminateSupportServiceProvider;

class UiServiceProvider extends ServiceProvider
{
    public function boot(): void
    {
        // A namespace maps to a directory. There is no registry of
        // view NAMES: adding a file makes it exist, which is what
        // lets a package ship views without a manifest.
        $this->loadViewsFrom(__DIR__ . '/../resources/views', 'ui');

        // THE OVERRIDE. The application's path goes FIRST, so a
        // file of the same name in resources/views wins and the
        // package's copy is never reached. This is how a user
        // customises a package view without forking it - and how
        // an edit to the vendor file has no effect whatsoever.
        $this->publishes([
            __DIR__ . '/../resources/views' => resource_path('views/vendor/ui'),
        ], 'ui-views');
    }
}

{{-- TWO WAYS TO SAY "MAYBE IT IS NOT THERE", and the asymmetry is
     deliberate. --}}

{{-- A MISSING LAYOUT IS A BUG: let it throw, loudly, with the name
     in the message. An include does this by default. --}}
@extends('layouts.app')

{{-- A MISSING OPTIONAL PARTIAL IS A NORMAL CONDITION: test for it.
     includeIf and the -once form both return nothing rather than
     throwing. --}}
@includeIf('partials.' . $section)
@includeWhen($condition, 'partials.promo')
@includeFirst(['partials.promo', 'partials.default'])

{{-- THE FAILURE MESSAGE, which is why the exception is right. --}}
// InvalidArgumentException: View [mail.mesage] not found.
//      ^^^^^^ a typo, and it says exactly which name failed

0.6 Tooling reference

Lists the Artisan commands and configuration keys that the rest of this guide refers to. Keeping them in one place avoids repeating the invocation of each command in every chapter.

<h4>Format on save, not on review</h4> <p>Blade's formatter is not a matter of taste, and the reason is worth stating: a template file is not read only by people. It is read by the compiler, by a diff, and by the person reviewing the diff. Formatting that is unstable produces diffs that touch every line, which makes a review impossible — and a review that has become impossible is a review nobody does. So the formatter's job is to make the formatting decision once and then never make it again.</p> <p>That argues for formatting on save rather than in a separate step, because a separate step produces a commit whose only content is whitespace, and a whitespace commit is a review nobody reads. It also argues for running the formatter in continuous integration as a check rather than as a fixer: a fixer in CI makes the build pass while producing a change nobody reviews, which is the same problem with an extra step.</p> <h4>The formatter changes behaviour, and that is the point</h4> <p>The Blade formatter is not purely cosmetic. Normalising an attribute quoting style, putting a directive's argument on one line, and collapsing whitespace inside a directive are all cosmetic — but sorting nothing, moving nothing, and never touching the contents of a code block is what makes it safe to run. The practical consequence: a formatter that reformats inside a code sample or inside a verbatim block will change what the page renders, and a formatter run over a library with verbatim blocks is a rendering change disguised as a formatting change.</p> <h4>What belongs in a review command</h4> <p>Three checks, in increasing order of how much they find. A formatter check finds nothing interesting and catches everything else cheaply. A syntax check catches a template that does not compile, which is a class of error that would otherwise surface as a blank page in production. A static analysis pass over the templates catches the things a formatter cannot: an undefined variable, a component used with a prop it does not declare, a directive closed but never opened.</p>

Format on save, not on review

Blade's formatter is not a matter of taste, and the reason is worth stating: a template file is not read only by people. It is read by the compiler, by a diff, and by the person reviewing the diff. Formatting that is unstable produces diffs that touch every line, which makes a review impossible — and a review that has become impossible is a review nobody does. So the formatter's job is to make the formatting decision once and then never make it again.

That argues for formatting on save rather than in a separate step, because a separate step produces a commit whose only content is whitespace, and a whitespace commit is a review nobody reads. It also argues for running the formatter in continuous integration as a check rather than as a fixer: a fixer in CI makes the build pass while producing a change nobody reviews, which is the same problem with an extra step.

The formatter changes behaviour, and that is the point

The Blade formatter is not purely cosmetic. Normalising an attribute quoting style, putting a directive's argument on one line, and collapsing whitespace inside a directive are all cosmetic — but sorting nothing, moving nothing, and never touching the contents of a code block is what makes it safe to run. The practical consequence: a formatter that reformats inside a code sample or inside a verbatim block will change what the page renders, and a formatter run over a library with verbatim blocks is a rendering change disguised as a formatting change.

What belongs in a review command

Three checks, in increasing order of how much they find. A formatter check finds nothing interesting and catches everything else cheaply. A syntax check catches a template that does not compile, which is a class of error that would otherwise surface as a blank page in production. A static analysis pass over the templates catches the things a formatter cannot: an undefined variable, a component used with a prop it does not declare, a directive closed but never opened.

php artisan view:clear          // delete compiled views
php artisan view:cache           // pre-compile every Blade file
php artisan optimize             // config, route, event, view caches
php artisan component:cache       // speed up component discovery
php artisan about                // environment summary

The relevant configuration lives in config/view.php, chiefly the paths array of view directories, the registered compiled path, and any additional namespaces published by packages. The cache expiry behaviour is not configurable in Laravel 13: a compiled view is recompiled whenever the source file is newer than the compiled artifact, which is the freshness model described in Chapter 1.


{{-- .editorconfig — the formatting decision made once, so nobody
     makes it again in review. --}}
root = true

[*.{php,blade.php}]
charset = utf-8
indent_style = space
indent_size = 4
end_of_line = lf
insert_final_newline = true
trim_trailing_whitespace = true

{{-- THE FORMATTER, run on save rather than as a separate commit. --}}
// .vscode/settings.json
{
    "[php]": {
        "editor.defaultFormatter": "onecentlin.laravel-blade",
        "editor.formatOnSave": true,
        "editor.formatOnModeChange": false
    },
    "files.associations": {
        "*.blade.php": "php"
    }
}

{{-- THE THREE CHECKS, in the order they belong in CI. --}}
# .github/workflows/lint.yml
- name: Formatting is stable
  run: blade-formatter --config .blade-formatterrc --check resources/views

- name: Every template compiles
  run: |
    find resources/views -name '*.blade.php' -print0 |
      xargs -0 -n1 -P4 php artisan view:compile-file

- name: No undeclared component props and no undefined variables
  run: ./vendor/bin/phpstan analyse resources/views --level=5

{{-- WHAT THE FORMATTER CONFIG LOOKS LIKE, and the one option that
     matters for safety: it never reformats inside a code block, a
     verbatim block, or a comment holding a template. --}}
// .blade-formatterrc
{
    "indent_size": 4,
    "use_tabs": false,
    "sort_class_attributes": false,
    "wrap_attributes": false,
    "line_ending": "lf"
}

{{-- WHY WRAP_ATTRIBUTES IS OFF. Wrapping a long attribute list
     changes which line a directive occupies, which changes what a
     diff shows, which means a real change to one attribute produces
     a diff touching five lines. Stable beats pretty. --}}

1 · Compilation: The View Cache, The Compile Path, and Clearing It

1.1 The compile pipeline1.2 The compiled view cache1.3 Freshness and automatic recompilation1.4 Storage layout and permissions1.5 Clearing and pre-compiling1.6 Deployment order and pre-warming

Blade compiles a template once and executes plain PHP thereafter. This chapter documents that pipeline end to end: how a view name reaches a file, how the compiler decides whether to recompile, where artifacts are stored, and the commands used to clear or pre-warm the cache.

1.1 The compile pipeline

Traces a single view() call from the view factory through the finder, the compiler and the evaluator. Each stage is a separate, replaceable service.

A view render proceeds through four collaborators held by the application container:

  • ViewFinder maps a logical name such as orders.show to an absolute path using the registered hints and namespaces.
  • EngineResolver selects the engine by file extension, which for .blade.php is always CompilerEngine.
  • BladeCompiler returns a compiled path, recompiling only when required.
  • CompilerEngine evaluates the compiled file and captures its output as a string.

Because the resolver keys on extension rather than on name, a .php view in the same directory executes directly with no compilation step, and a .blade.php file always takes the compiled path. Laravel 13 offers no configuration that makes Blade interpret templates at request time.

<h4>The four stages, and which of them you can influence</h4> <p>A <code>.blade.php</code> file passes through four transformations before a byte reaches the client: the Blade compiler replaces directives with PHP calls, the PHP compiler produces opcodes, the autoloader resolves names, and the view factory resolves the path and provides the data. Only the first is Blade's. The second is the PHP engine's, the third is Composer's, and the fourth is the framework's — and knowing which stage a problem belongs to is most of what makes a Blade performance question answerable.</p> <p>A template that takes a long time to render is almost always in stage four — the data access behind the view — or in stage one with an unexpectedly large compiled output. It is essentially never stage two, because the PHP compiler's output is not what the timer is measuring. A common misdiagnosis is to blame the template because the render is slow, when the template is simply where the waiting happens.</p> <h4>Compilation is per file and conditional</h4> <p>The compiler runs once per file, and only when the stored output is older than the source. The freshness comparison is against modification times, which has two consequences that matter in practice. A deployment that sets every file's mtime to the time of extraction will recompile everything once, which is a warm-up cost rather than a permanent one. And a build that sets source mtimes to a value <em>older</em> than the compiled files' mtimes will serve a stale compiled view indefinitely, which is the failure mode that a container image with baked compiled views can produce.</p> <h4>Pre-compiling, and when it is the right answer</h4> <p>Pre-compiling removes the freshness check and the compile from the request path entirely. It is correct when the deployment produces the same views every time — which is the normal case — and it is required when the compiled directory is not writable at run time, which is the case in a read-only container. The cost is one more build step that can produce a stale image, and the discipline that cost is a build that fails if compilation produces an error.</p>

The four stages, and which of them you can influence

A .blade.php file passes through four transformations before a byte reaches the client: the Blade compiler replaces directives with PHP calls, the PHP compiler produces opcodes, the autoloader resolves names, and the view factory resolves the path and provides the data. Only the first is Blade's. The second is the PHP engine's, the third is Composer's, and the fourth is the framework's — and knowing which stage a problem belongs to is most of what makes a Blade performance question answerable.

A template that takes a long time to render is almost always in stage four — the data access behind the view — or in stage one with an unexpectedly large compiled output. It is essentially never stage two, because the PHP compiler's output is not what the timer is measuring. A common misdiagnosis is to blame the template because the render is slow, when the template is simply where the waiting happens.

Compilation is per file and conditional

The compiler runs once per file, and only when the stored output is older than the source. The freshness comparison is against modification times, which has two consequences that matter in practice. A deployment that sets every file's mtime to the time of extraction will recompile everything once, which is a warm-up cost rather than a permanent one. And a build that sets source mtimes to a value older than the compiled files' mtimes will serve a stale compiled view indefinitely, which is the failure mode that a container image with baked compiled views can produce.

Pre-compiling, and when it is the right answer

Pre-compiling removes the freshness check and the compile from the request path entirely. It is correct when the deployment produces the same views every time — which is the normal case — and it is required when the compiled directory is not writable at run time, which is the case in a read-only container. The cost is one more build step that can produce a stale image, and the discipline that cost is a build that fails if compilation produces an error.


{{-- THE FOUR STAGES, and which one a problem belongs to. --}}

{{-- STAGE 1: BLADE COMPILES. Once per file, when the source mtime
     is newer than the compiled file. Produces plain PHP. --}}
php artisan view:clear && php artisan view:cache
cat storage/framework/views/<hash>.php

{{-- STAGE 2: PHP COMPILES TO OPCODES. Opcache holds them. Not
     Blade's, and not what a render timer measures. --}}
php -i | grep opcache.enable
php -r 'echo (opcache_get_status(false)["opcache_enabled"] ? "on" : "off"), PHP_EOL;'

{{-- STAGE 3: THE AUTOLOADER RESOLVES NAMES. A missing class is a
     500 that names the class, and it is never a Blade problem. --}}
composer dump-autoload --optimize --classmap-authoritative

{{-- STAGE 4: THE VIEW FACTORY RESOLVES AND SUPPLIES DATA. This is
     where a slow render almost always is. --}}

{{-- SO THE DIAGNOSIS ORDER MATTERS: instrument stage 4 first,
     because that is where time goes. A render timer around the
     whole call cannot distinguish a slow template from a slow query
     behind it. --}}
// app/Support/RenderTimer.php  — registered as a view composer on
// the * wildcard, so it fires for every view.
View::composer('*', function ($view) {
    $view->with(['__render_start' => hrtime(true)]);
});

// app/Providers/AppServiceProvider.php
View::composer('*', function ($view) {
    $ms = (hrtime(true) - $view->getData()['__render_start']) / 1e6;
    if ($ms > 50) {                       // a THRESHOLD, so this is
        Log::warning('slow view', [      // data rather than noise
            'view'  => $view->name(),
            'ms'    => round($ms, 1),
        ]);
    }
});

{{-- AND THE ONE THING THAT MAKES THE NUMBER ACTIONABLE: the query
     count beside it. A 40 ms render with 1 query is fine; a 12 ms
     render with 300 queries is a page that will be slow on a
     different database. --}}
DB::listen(function ($q) use (&$queries) {
    $queries[] = ['sql' => $q->sql, 'ms' => $q->time, 'bindings' => $q->bindings];
});


{{-- THE CACHE COMPARISON, made concrete: what pre-compiling
     actually buys and what it does not. --}}

{{-- Measure the compile cost by rendering twice. The first render
     compiles; the second includes the compiled file. The difference
     IS the saving, and it is the honest number to quote. --}}
// A throwaway Artisan command: bench:compile
Artisan::command('bench:compile {view}', function () {
    $name = $this->argument('view');
    $data = sample_data_for($name);

    // COLD: no compiled file, so this call compiles AND runs.
    View::clearResolvedViews();
    @unlink(View::getFinder()->getCompiledPath($name));
    $t0 = hrtime(true);
    view($name, $data)->render();
    $cold = (hrtime(true) - $t0) / 1e6;

    // WARM: compiled file exists, so this call only includes it.
    $t1 = hrtime(true);
    view($name, $data)->render();
    $warm = (hrtime(true) - $t1) / 1e6;

    $this->table(['run', 'ms'], [
        ['cold (compiles)', number_format($cold, 2)],
        ['warm (includes)', number_format($warm, 2)],
        ['saving',          number_format($cold - $warm, 2)],
    ]);
});

{{-- WHAT THE RESULT USUALLY SHOWS, and why it is not the answer to
     "the page is slow": --}}
{{--   cold  38 ms   warm  34 ms      saving 4 ms
       ^^^^^^^^^^^ the compile is 10% of the render

     The compile is real and it is worth removing from the request
     path. It is also four milliseconds, and a page at 34 ms with
     an N+1 in the loop is at 340 ms. Fix the query. --}}

{{-- AND WHERE THE COMPILE COST ACTUALLY BITES: the FIRST REQUEST
     after a deploy, for EVERY template. A cold page can be a 200 ms
     page on a 15 ms page, and it is the request that a smoke test
     makes and that a monitor records as p99. --}}

{{-- THE FIX IS ONE BUILD COMMAND, and it is not optional in a
     container: --}}
php artisan view:cache

{{-- THEN MEASURE THE DEPLOY, not the average. The number that
     matters is the first request, and the only way to have a good
     one is for it to not exist. --}}
curl -s -o /dev/null -w "%{time_starttransfer}
" https://app.example/health


{{-- THE FOUR STAGES, and which one a problem belongs to. --}}

{{-- STAGE 1: BLADE COMPILES. Once per file, when the source mtime
     is newer than the compiled file. Produces plain PHP. --}}

     Blade's, and not what a render timer measures. --}}

1.2 The compiled view cache

Describes the artifact naming scheme and its storage directory. The hashed filename is deterministic, which is what makes the cache portable and safe to pre-warm.

Each compiled template is written to storage/framework/views under a filename derived from a hash of the absolute source path, for example 8f2c1a0e9b3d4f7a6c5b2e1d0a9f8c7b.php:

<h4>What is stored, and what it is not</h4> <p>The cache holds one compiled PHP file per template, keyed by a hash of the template's path. The stored file is the compiled output and nothing else — no data, no partial results, no state. A request that hits the cache includes a PHP file and continues; the saving is the compilation, not the rendering. That distinction matters because it bounds what caching the views can possibly buy: it removes a parse from the request path and nothing else.</p> <p>The consequence is that on a warm cache a template's rendering cost is essentially unchanged. If a page is slow, the view cache is not the answer, and treating it as one is the most common wasted afternoon in this area. The saving is real but small — a few hundred microseconds per template — and it is dwarfed by a single N+1 query in the loop.</p> <h4>The freshness rule, and the deployments it breaks</h4> <p>The stored file is recompiled when the source is newer. That is the whole invalidation mechanism, and it is a modification-time comparison, which produces two distinct failure modes worth knowing. A build that preserves source modification times from a previous release can serve a stale compiled view, because the comparison is between the source file and a compiled file produced later. And a deployment that touches every file to the same timestamp recompiles the entire tree once, which is a warm-up cost — noticeable, bounded, and usually mistaken for a regression on the first request after deploy.</p> <h4>Pre-compiling versus warming</h4> <p>Pre-compiling at build time is strictly better than warming at deploy time, because it makes compilation a build failure rather than a first-request cost, and it works in an environment where the storage directory is not writable. Warming by making one request per view at deploy time has the advantage of not needing a build step and the disadvantage of needing a writable storage directory and a process that can make HTTP requests. Most deployments should use the build step.</p>

What is stored, and what it is not

The cache holds one compiled PHP file per template, keyed by a hash of the template's path. The stored file is the compiled output and nothing else — no data, no partial results, no state. A request that hits the cache includes a PHP file and continues; the saving is the compilation, not the rendering. That distinction matters because it bounds what caching the views can possibly buy: it removes a parse from the request path and nothing else.

The consequence is that on a warm cache a template's rendering cost is essentially unchanged. If a page is slow, the view cache is not the answer, and treating it as one is the most common wasted afternoon in this area. The saving is real but small — a few hundred microseconds per template — and it is dwarfed by a single N+1 query in the loop.

The freshness rule, and the deployments it breaks

The stored file is recompiled when the source is newer. That is the whole invalidation mechanism, and it is a modification-time comparison, which produces two distinct failure modes worth knowing. A build that preserves source modification times from a previous release can serve a stale compiled view, because the comparison is between the source file and a compiled file produced later. And a deployment that touches every file to the same timestamp recompiles the entire tree once, which is a warm-up cost — noticeable, bounded, and usually mistaken for a regression on the first request after deploy.

Pre-compiling versus warming

Pre-compiling at build time is strictly better than warming at deploy time, because it makes compilation a build failure rather than a first-request cost, and it works in an environment where the storage directory is not writable. Warming by making one request per view at deploy time has the advantage of not needing a build step and the disadvantage of needing a writable storage directory and a process that can make HTTP requests. Most deployments should use the build step.

<?php $__env = $__env ?? null; ?>
<h1><?= e($order->reference) ?></h1>
<?= $__env->startSection('body') ?>
<p>Placed on <?= e($order->placed_at->format('j M Y')) ?></p>
<?= $__env->endSection() ?>

The mapping is deterministic for a given path, which has three practical consequences:

  • The same template produces the same artifact path on every machine, so a build job and a web process agree on filenames.
  • Two templates never collide, because the path rather than the name is hashed.
  • The cache directory may be generated at build time and shipped with the release.

{{-- WHAT IS ACTUALLY STORED: one compiled PHP file per template,
     keyed by a hash of its path. No data, no rendered HTML, no
     state. The saving is the COMPILE, not the render. --}}

// Force a rebuild of everything
php artisan view:clear      // delete the compiled views
php artisan view:cache      // compile every template NOW

{{-- SO A SLOW RENDER IS NOT A VIEW-CACHE PROBLEM. This will show
     the same time before and after warming: --}}
$start = hrtime(true);
$html  = view('dashboard', $data)->render();
Log::debug('render', ['ms' => (hrtime(true) - $start) / 1e6]);

{{-- THE FRESHNESS COMPARISON IS ON MODIFICATION TIMES, which is
     where the deployment traps live. --}}

{{-- TRAP 1: a build step that sets every source file's mtime to
     the extraction time. The first request after deploy recompiles
     the entire tree — 200-800 ms for a large application, and it
     looks exactly like a performance regression on the first
     request and nothing on the second. --}}
// .github/workflows/deploy.yml
- run: php artisan view:cache          # compile at BUILD time
- run: |
    # Then the shipped compiled files are NEWER than every source,
    # so the run-time check finds nothing stale.
    find storage/framework/views -type f | wc -l   # assert non-zero

{{-- TRAP 2: a container image that COPIES compiled views and then
     restores an older mtime on the sources. The comparison now
     finds the source older than the compiled file, and serves a
     stale template until something touches it. --}}
// Dockerfile — copy compiled views AFTER the sources, and never
// restore mtimes on the sources afterwards
COPY . .
RUN php artisan view:cache
COPY --from=builder /app/storage/framework/views ./storage/framework/views

{{-- THE READ-ONLY CONTAINER CASE, where caching at run time is not
     possible at all and pre-compiling is the only answer. --}}
{{-- config/view.php --}}
{{-- The compiled path lives on the writable volume. If it does not
     exist, every request compiles, and the first request after a
     deploy pays for all of them. --}}
'compiled' => env('VIEW_COMPILED_PATH',
                   realpath(storage_path('framework/views'))),


{{-- WHAT IS ACTUALLY STORED: one compiled PHP file per template,
     keyed by a hash of its path. No data, no rendered HTML, no
     state. The saving is the COMPILE, not the render. --}}

     restores an older mtime on the sources. The comparison now

     possible at all and pre-compiling is the only answer. --}}

     exist, every request compiles, and the first request after a

                   realpath(storage_path('framework/views'))),

1.3 Freshness and automatic recompilation

Explains the timestamp comparison that decides whether a template is recompiled. This is the mechanism that makes local development work without a manual cache flush.

On every render the compiler checks whether the source file is newer than the compiled artifact. If it is, the template is recompiled and rewritten. Laravel 13 performs no content hashing: the test is a modification-time comparison, and an expired artifact is never evaluated.

<h4>The rule, stated once</h4> <p>A compiled view is regenerated when its source file's modification time is newer than the compiled file's. That is the entire invalidation mechanism — there is no content hash, no dependency graph, and no build step in the default configuration. It is simple, it is correct for a single developer's machine, and it has three consequences in deployment that are worth enumerating precisely.</p> <p>First, a build step that copies source files with fresh modification times invalidates every compiled view, so the first request after a deploy recompiles the whole tree. Second, a build step that <em>preserves</em> older modification times — which some archive and layer-caching steps do — can leave a compiled view newer than its source and therefore stale. Third, two machines sharing a compiled directory but with different source timestamps will disagree about what is current, so the compiled views are an artefact of one machine rather than of the code.</p> <h4>What the mechanism cannot express</h4> <p>A view has no declared dependencies. When a component is included in a template, nothing records that relationship, so changing the component does not invalidate the templates that use it — only the component's own compiled file is rebuilt. In development this is invisible because both are recompiled on request. In a pre-compiled deployment it means a change to a partial does not appear unless the whole view cache is rebuilt, which is why a deployment that only runs <code>view:cache</code> for changed files will serve a mixture of new and old.</p> <p>The practical rule follows from that: the compiled directory is a build artefact of a specific source tree, and it should be built from that tree in one step and shipped with it. Rebuilding it incrementally on the target machine reintroduces exactly the timestamp ambiguity the mechanism is built on.</p> <h4>Watching the mechanism, rather than guessing</h4> <p>When a template is not updating, the useful facts are the two timestamps and the resolved path. The framework will tell you where it looked and what it found, and comparing the two modification times is a five-second diagnosis that replaces an hour of reasoning about permissions, containers and opcache.</p>

The rule, stated once

A compiled view is regenerated when its source file's modification time is newer than the compiled file's. That is the entire invalidation mechanism — there is no content hash, no dependency graph, and no build step in the default configuration. It is simple, it is correct for a single developer's machine, and it has three consequences in deployment that are worth enumerating precisely.

First, a build step that copies source files with fresh modification times invalidates every compiled view, so the first request after a deploy recompiles the whole tree. Second, a build step that preserves older modification times — which some archive and layer-caching steps do — can leave a compiled view newer than its source and therefore stale. Third, two machines sharing a compiled directory but with different source timestamps will disagree about what is current, so the compiled views are an artefact of one machine rather than of the code.

What the mechanism cannot express

A view has no declared dependencies. When a component is included in a template, nothing records that relationship, so changing the component does not invalidate the templates that use it — only the component's own compiled file is rebuilt. In development this is invisible because both are recompiled on request. In a pre-compiled deployment it means a change to a partial does not appear unless the whole view cache is rebuilt, which is why a deployment that only runs view:cache for changed files will serve a mixture of new and old.

The practical rule follows from that: the compiled directory is a build artefact of a specific source tree, and it should be built from that tree in one step and shipped with it. Rebuilding it incrementally on the target machine reintroduces exactly the timestamp ambiguity the mechanism is built on.

Watching the mechanism, rather than guessing

When a template is not updating, the useful facts are the two timestamps and the resolved path. The framework will tell you where it looked and what it found, and comparing the two modification times is a five-second diagnosis that replaces an hour of reasoning about permissions, containers and opcache.

if ($this->compiler->isExpired($path)) {
    $this->compiler->compile($path);
}

// Illuminate\View\Engines\CompilerEngine::get()
// Illuminate\View\Compilers\Compiler::isExpired()

Because the check runs on every render, deploying by copying files preserves correctness: copied templates are newer than shipped artifacts, so each recompiles once.

Timestamp-preserving deploys. If your release process restores original modification times, a stale compiled view can survive a deploy. Compile during the build rather than relying on mtime comparison.


{{-- THE RULE: recompile when the SOURCE mtime is newer than the
     COMPILED mtime. Nothing else participates. --}}

{{-- Diagnose it in five seconds rather than an hour: --}}
php artisan view:clear
php artisan view:cache

{{-- WHERE IT LOOKED, and whether the file is there. --}}
// A small Artisan command, worth having on any project that has
// ever shipped a stale template.
Artisan::command('view:inspect {view}', function () {
    $name = $this->argument('view');
    $path = View::getFinder()->find($name);   // throws if not found

    // The COMPILED path is derived from the SOURCE path by a hash,
    // so two different templates never collide.
    $compiled = View::getFinder()->getCompiledPath($name);

    $this->table(['which', 'path', 'mtime'], [
        ['source',   $path,     filemtime($path)],
        ['compiled', $compiled,
         file_exists($compiled) ? filemtime($compiled) : 'MISSING'],
    ]);

    // The verdict, stated rather than inferred:
    if (! file_exists($compiled)) {
        $this->error('never compiled — permissions, or the path is not writable');
    } elseif (filemtime($compiled) >= filemtime($path)) {
        $this->info('up to date');
    } else {
        $this->warn('source is NEWER than compiled — it will recompile on next request');
    }
});

{{-- THE DEPENDENCY THAT DOES NOT EXIST. A partial used by fifty
     templates records nothing about them, so changing the partial
     invalidates only its own compiled file. --}}

{{-- resources/views/components/price.blade.php — changed --}}
@props(['amount'])
<span>{{ $amount->format() }}</span>

{{-- resources/views/orders/show.blade.php — compiled output is
     UNCHANGED and still contains the OLD inlined component markup,
     because this template's source did not change. In development
     you never see it; in a pre-compiled deployment you ship a
     mixture of new and old. --}}
<div>{{ $order->total->format() }}</div>

{{-- THE FIX, and it is the only one: the compiled views are a
     build artefact of a whole source tree. --}}
// .github/workflows/deploy.yml
- run: rm -rf storage/framework/views        # never incremental
- run: php artisan view:cache                # one step, one tree
- run: php artisan config:cache && php artisan route:cache


{{-- THE RULE: recompile when the SOURCE mtime is newer than the
     COMPILED mtime. Nothing else participates. --}}

{{-- Diagnose it in five seconds rather than an hour: --}}
php artisan view:clear
php artisan view:cache

{{-- WHERE IT LOOKED, and whether the file is there. --}}
