// ever shipped a stale template.
Artisan::command('view:inspect {view}', function () {
    $name = $this->argument('view');
    $path = View::getFinder()->find($name);   // throws if not found

    // The COMPILED path is derived from the SOURCE path by a hash,
    // so two different templates never collide.
    $compiled = View::getFinder()->getCompiledPath($name);

    $this->table(['which', 'path', 'mtime'], [
        ['source',   $path,     filemtime($path)],
        ['compiled', $compiled,
         file_exists($compiled) ? filemtime($compiled) : 'MISSING'],
    ]);

    // The verdict, stated rather than inferred:
    if (! file_exists($compiled)) {
        $this->error('never compiled — permissions, or the path is not writable');
    } elseif (filemtime($compiled) >= filemtime($path)) {
        $this->info('up to date');
    } else {
        $this->warn('source is NEWER than compiled — it will recompile on next request');
    }
});

{{-- THE DEPENDENCY THAT DOES NOT EXIST. A partial used by fifty
     templates records nothing about them, so changing the partial
     invalidates only its own compiled file. --}}

{{-- resources/views/components/price.blade.php — changed --}}
@props(['amount'])
<span>{{ $amount->format() }}</span>

{{-- resources/views/orders/show.blade.php — compiled output is
     UNCHANGED and still contains the OLD inlined component markup,
     because this template's source did not change. In development
     you never see it; in a pre-compiled deployment you ship a
     mixture of new and old. --}}

{{-- THE FIX, and it is the only one: the compiled views are a
     build artefact of a whole source tree. --}}
// .github/workflows/deploy.yml
- run: rm -rf storage/framework/views        # never incremental
- run: php artisan view:cache                # one step, one tree
- run: php artisan config:cache && php artisan route:cache

1.4 Storage layout and permissions

Covers the on-disk requirements for the compiled directory and the permissions failures that present as a generic 500 error.

The compiled path is set by the compiled key in config/view.php and defaults to storage_path('framework/views'). The directory must exist and be writable by the web process. On a fresh clone it does not, and the framework does not create it.

<h4>The directory, and what has to be true about it</h4> <p>Compiled views live in a single directory, and the framework needs three things from it: it must exist, the process must be able to write into it, and two processes must not race to write the same file. The first two are configuration and permissions; the third is why the compiled filename is a hash of the source path rather than a counter, and why a partially-written compiled file is a real possibility that the framework guards against by writing to a temporary name and renaming.</p> <p>The most common deployment failure is the second requirement. A container image that runs as a non-root user, with <code>storage</code> owned by root because the image was built that way, produces a directory that exists and cannot be written — so every request compiles, and nothing reports an error. It is not a crash; it is a silent per-request cost, which makes it one of the harder problems in this area to notice.</p> <h4>Sizing it, and cleaning it up</h4> <p>The directory holds one file per template ever compiled, which for a large application is a few thousand small files. It grows monotonically: a template deleted from source leaves its compiled file behind forever, because nothing runs to notice. That is harmless for correctness and not harmless for a container image that is rebuilt often — each layer carries files nothing will ever read.</p> <h4>The other storage directories, and why the same permission applies</h4> <p>Views are not the only compiled artefact. Configuration and routes are cached the same way, into the same tree, and they have the same requirement. A deployment that pre-compiles views but not routes gets a mixed state where the view cache is fresh and the configuration cache is stale, which produces a bug report that points at a template and is actually a configuration cache. Pre-compiling all three in one build step, and making that step fail on error, removes the class.</p>

The directory, and what has to be true about it

Compiled views live in a single directory, and the framework needs three things from it: it must exist, the process must be able to write into it, and two processes must not race to write the same file. The first two are configuration and permissions; the third is why the compiled filename is a hash of the source path rather than a counter, and why a partially-written compiled file is a real possibility that the framework guards against by writing to a temporary name and renaming.

The most common deployment failure is the second requirement. A container image that runs as a non-root user, with storage owned by root because the image was built that way, produces a directory that exists and cannot be written — so every request compiles, and nothing reports an error. It is not a crash; it is a silent per-request cost, which makes it one of the harder problems in this area to notice.

Sizing it, and cleaning it up

The directory holds one file per template ever compiled, which for a large application is a few thousand small files. It grows monotonically: a template deleted from source leaves its compiled file behind forever, because nothing runs to notice. That is harmless for correctness and not harmless for a container image that is rebuilt often — each layer carries files nothing will ever read.

The other storage directories, and why the same permission applies

Views are not the only compiled artefact. Configuration and routes are cached the same way, into the same tree, and they have the same requirement. A deployment that pre-compiles views but not routes gets a mixed state where the view cache is fresh and the configuration cache is stale, which produces a bug report that points at a template and is actually a configuration cache. Pre-compiling all three in one build step, and making that step fail on error, removes the class.

mkdir -p storage/framework/{views,cache,sessions}
chown -R www-data:www-data storage bootstrap/cache
php artisan storage:link

Symptoms of a missing or unwritable directory are misleading: the exception names the compiled path, not the template, and the message may be a bare failed to open stream. Verify the directory before investigating template syntax.


{{-- THE THREE THINGS THE DIRECTORY NEEDS. --}}
{{-- 1. It exists. 2. The process can write to it.
     3. Two concurrent requests must not write the same file —
        which is why the compiled name is a HASH of the source path
        rather than a sequence number. --}}

{{-- config/view.php --}}
'compiled' => env(
    'VIEW_COMPILED_PATH',
    realpath(storage_path('framework/views'))
),

{{-- THE PERMISSION FAILURE THAT DOES NOT FAIL. This is the hard
     one: the directory exists, nothing throws, and every request
     compiles. The symptom is a page that got slower after a
     container change, with no error anywhere. --}}

// Dockerfile — create the tree and hand it to the runtime user, in
// one layer, so it is never owned by root.
RUN mkdir -p storage/framework/views  && chown -R www-data:www-data storage bootstrap/cache
USER www-data

{{-- Make it fail loudly instead of silently, which is the actual
     fix: if the process cannot compile, say so at boot rather than
     on every request. --}}
// bootstrap/app.php
$compiled = config('view.compiled');
if (! is_dir($compiled) || ! is_writable($compiled)) {
    // In production this is fatal. In local development, compile at
    // boot so the developer finds out now rather than on a request.
    if (! app()->isProduction()) {
        Artisan::call('view:cache');
    } else {
        throw new RuntimeException(
            "view.compiled [{$compiled}] is not writable. Run "
            . 'php artisan view:cache at build time.'
        );
    }
}

{{-- THE CLEANUP THAT MATTERS IN AN IMAGE. Deleted templates leave
     their compiled files behind forever, because nothing notices
     they are orphaned. --}}
// Dockerfile — one tree, built once, no orphans
COPY . .
RUN rm -rf storage/framework/views  && php artisan view:cache  && php artisan config:cache  && php artisan route:cache  && chown -R www-data:www-data storage bootstrap/cache

{{-- AND THE OTHER ARTEFACTS, which have the same requirement and
     the same failure mode. A fresh view cache beside a stale
     config cache is a bug report that points at the wrong file. --}}
// .github/workflows/deploy.yml — all three, in one step, failing
// the build on error
- run: |
    php artisan view:cache
    php artisan config:cache
    php artisan route:cache
  # Any non-zero exit fails the deployment, so a compile error is a
  # build failure rather than a 500 on the first request.


{{-- THE THREE THINGS THE DIRECTORY NEEDS. --}}
{{-- 1. It exists. 2. The process can write to it.
     3. Two concurrent requests must not write the same file —
        which is why the compiled name is a HASH of the source path
        rather than a sequence number. --}}

{{-- config/view.php --}}
'compiled' => env(
    'VIEW_COMPILED_PATH',
    realpath(storage_path('framework/views'))
),

{{-- THE PERMISSION FAILURE THAT DOES NOT FAIL. This is the hard
     one: the directory exists, nothing throws, and every request
     compiles. The symptom is a page that got slower after a
     container change, with no error anywhere. --}}

// one layer, so it is never owned by root.

{{-- Make it fail loudly instead of silently, which is the actual
     fix: if the process cannot compile, say so at boot rather than
     on every request. --}}
// bootstrap/app.php
$compiled = config('view.compiled');
if (! is_dir($compiled) || ! is_writable($compiled)) {
    // In production this is fatal. In local development, compile at
    // boot so the developer finds out now rather than on a request.
    if (! app()->isProduction()) {
        Artisan::call('view:cache');
    } else {
        throw new RuntimeException(
            "view.compiled [{$compiled}] is not writable. Run "
            . 'php artisan view:cache at build time.'
        );
    }
}

{{-- THE CLEANUP THAT MATTERS IN AN IMAGE. Deleted templates leave
     their compiled files behind forever, because nothing notices
     they are orphaned. --}}
// Dockerfile — one tree, built once, no orphans
COPY . .
RUN rm -rf storage/framework/views  && php artisan view:cache  && php artisan config:cache  && php artisan route:cache  && chown -R www-data:www-data storage bootstrap/cache

{{-- AND THE OTHER ARTEFACTS, which have the same requirement and
     the same failure mode. A fresh view cache beside a stale
     config cache is a bug report that points at the wrong file. --}}

// the build on error

    php artisan view:cache
    php artisan config:cache
    php artisan route:cache
  # Any non-zero exit fails the deployment, so a compile error is a
  # build failure rather than a 500 on the first request.

1.5 Clearing and pre-compiling

Documents the Artisan commands for cache maintenance and the cases in which each one is the correct response.

CommandEffectUse when
view:clearDeletes every compiled artifactSuspected stale output, or after a compiler change
view:cacheCompiles every Blade file in registered pathsBefore a release, or on a read-only container
optimizeRuns view:cache plus config, route and event cachesStandard production bootstrap
optimize:clearClears all of the aboveLocal troubleshooting

Clearing is almost never required during normal development because of the expiry check, but it is the first response when edited template output does not change. A classic cause is a duplicate view name in two registered hints: the finder resolves the first match, so you are editing a file that is never rendered.

<h4>When clearing is the answer, and when it is a habit</h4> <p>Clearing the view cache fixes two things: a template that is not updating, and a compiled file that is corrupt. Both are real and both are cheap to fix. Neither is a reason to clear on every deploy — that is the habit, and it converts a five-second build step into a first-request cost that scales with the size of the application.</p> <p>The distinction is worth being precise about, because the two uses have different signs. A stale template has an obvious symptom: you changed a file and nothing happened. A corrupt compiled file has a different one: an error naming a compiled path. A deploy where nothing is wrong has no symptom at all, and clearing anyway costs a full recompile for nothing.</p> <h4>Clearing and warming are different operations</h4> <p>Clear deletes. Cache compiles everything in advance. They are not inverses: a clear followed by a cache leaves the tree in a better state than a clear alone, and a cache without a preceding clear leaves stale entries in place — which is the trap, because <code>view:cache</code> does not remove anything, so a template deleted from source keeps serving its compiled output until the directory is emptied.</p> <h4>The other caches, and clearing one is not clearing the others</h4> <p>There are at least four caches in a Laravel application and they are cleared by different commands. Configuration, routes, views, and the framework's own data caches. A project that clears views on deploy but not configuration will serve a template compiled against one set of configuration with a cached configuration file from another — which is a real class of bug, and one that appears only on the machine where the two caches happened to diverge.</p>

When clearing is the answer, and when it is a habit

Clearing the view cache fixes two things: a template that is not updating, and a compiled file that is corrupt. Both are real and both are cheap to fix. Neither is a reason to clear on every deploy — that is the habit, and it converts a five-second build step into a first-request cost that scales with the size of the application.

The distinction is worth being precise about, because the two uses have different signs. A stale template has an obvious symptom: you changed a file and nothing happened. A corrupt compiled file has a different one: an error naming a compiled path. A deploy where nothing is wrong has no symptom at all, and clearing anyway costs a full recompile for nothing.

Clearing and warming are different operations

Clear deletes. Cache compiles everything in advance. They are not inverses: a clear followed by a cache leaves the tree in a better state than a clear alone, and a cache without a preceding clear leaves stale entries in place — which is the trap, because view:cache does not remove anything, so a template deleted from source keeps serving its compiled output until the directory is emptied.

The other caches, and clearing one is not clearing the others

There are at least four caches in a Laravel application and they are cleared by different commands. Configuration, routes, views, and the framework's own data caches. A project that clears views on deploy but not configuration will serve a template compiled against one set of configuration with a cached configuration file from another — which is a real class of bug, and one that appears only on the machine where the two caches happened to diverge.


{{-- THE FOUR COMMANDS, AND THAT CLEARING ONE IS NOT CLEARING THE
     OTHERS. --}}
php artisan view:clear        // storage/framework/views
php artisan config:clear     // bootstrap/cache/config.php
php artisan route:clear      // bootstrap/cache/routes-v7.php
php artisan cache:clear      // the application's DATA cache

{{-- ALL OF THEM AT ONCE — and note this is the DEPLOY step, not the
     local step. Clearing on every deploy trades a four-millisecond
     saving for a several-hundred-millisecond first request. --}}
php artisan optimize:clear

{{-- CLEAR IS NOT CACHE. view:cache COMPILES EVERYTHING and does
     NOT remove anything, so a template deleted from source keeps
     serving its compiled output until the directory is emptied.
     This is the asymmetry that makes "just run view:cache" an
     incomplete fix. --}}

{{-- SO: THE ORDER THAT IS ACTUALLY CORRECT --}}
php artisan view:clear       // 1. remove, including orphans
php artisan view:cache       // 2. compile the current tree

{{-- AND THE SAME FOR THE OTHER CACHES: --}}
php artisan config:clear && php artisan config:cache
php artisan route:clear  && php artisan route:cache

{{-- THE PROPERTY THAT MAKES THESE USABLE IN A PIPELINE: every one
     exits non-zero on error, so a compile failure is a build
     failure rather than a 500. --}}
php artisan view:cache || { echo "view:cache failed"; exit 1; }

{{-- WHICH IS TRUE BUT NOT SUFFICIENT, and is the trap: --}}
{{-- view:cache exits 0 having compiled NOTHING if the compiled
     directory does not exist or is not writable. The command
     succeeds, the build passes, and every request compiles. So the
     build has to ASSERT the artefact, not just run the command. --}}
php artisan view:clear && php artisan view:cache
test -s storage/framework/views || {
  echo "view:cache produced nothing — check VIEW_COMPILED_PATH"
  exit 1
}
ls storage/framework/views/*.php | wc -l   # a real count, for the log


{{-- WHEN CLEARING IS THE ANSWER: the two cases, with their signs. --}}

{{-- CASE 1. A STALE TEMPLATE. Obvious symptom: you changed a file
     and nothing happened. The cause is always one of: the compiled
     file is newer than the source, or you are editing a copy that
     is not the one being resolved. --}}

{{-- The second cause is more common than the first, and no amount
     of clearing will help with it. Check WHICH file resolved: --}}
// A command worth having, because it answers the question in one line.
Artisan::command('view:where {view}', function () {
    $name = $this->argument('view');
    $path = View::getFinder()->find($name);       // the source
    $out  = View::getFinder()->getCompiledPath($name);

    $this->line('source   ' . $path);
    $this->line('compiled ' . $out
        . '  (' . (file_exists($out)
              ? filemtime($out) . ($out < $path ? '  STALE' : '  ok')
              : 'missing') . ')');
    $this->line('is the source the one in git? ' .
        (str_contains($path, base_path()) ? 'yes' : 'NO — vendor override'));
});

{{-- "NO — vendor override" is the answer to a surprising number of
     "my edit has no effect" reports: a published view is served
     from resources/views/vendor/<package>, which wins over the
     package's own directory. --}}

{{-- CASE 2. A CORRUPT COMPILED FILE. Obvious symptom: an error
     naming a path in storage/framework/views. The cause is a write
     interrupted mid-file, which happens when the process is killed
     during a compile on a shared directory. --}}

{{-- So the third question, and it is the one that is actually
     worth automating: does every template still COMPILE? --}}
Artisan::command('views:verify', function () {
    $rows = [];

    foreach (View::getFinder()->getPaths() as $dir) {
        foreach (new RecursiveIteratorIterator(
                     new RecursiveDirectoryIterator($dir)) as $file) {
            if ($file->getExtension() !== 'php') continue;

            $rel  = Str::after($file->getPathname(), $dir . DIRECTORY_SEPARATOR);
            $name = str_replace(['/', '\', '.blade.php'], ['.', '.', ''], $rel);

            try {
                View::make($name)->render();
            } catch (Throwable $e) {
                $rows[] = [$name, class_basename($e), $e->getMessage()];
            }
        }
    }

    if ($rows) {
        $this->table(['view', 'exception', 'message'], $rows);
        exit(1);
    }
    $this->info('every template renders');
});

{{-- Run it after view:cache in CI. Then a corrupt or uncompilable
     template is a FAILED BUILD, and the runtime never sees it. --}}


{{-- THE FOUR COMMANDS, AND THAT CLEARING ONE IS NOT CLEARING THE
     OTHERS. --}}
php artisan view:clear        // storage/framework/views
php artisan config:clear     // bootstrap/cache/config.php
php artisan route:clear      // bootstrap/cache/routes-v7.php
php artisan cache:clear      // the application's DATA cache

{{-- ALL OF THEM AT ONCE — and note this is the DEPLOY step, not the
     local step. Clearing on every deploy trades a four-millisecond
     saving for a several-hundred-millisecond first request. --}}
php artisan optimize:clear

{{-- CLEAR IS NOT CACHE. view:cache COMPILES EVERYTHING and does
     NOT remove anything, so a template deleted from source keeps
     serving its compiled output until the directory is emptied.
     This is the asymmetry that makes "just run view:cache" an
     incomplete fix. --}}

{{-- SO: THE ORDER THAT IS ACTUALLY CORRECT --}}
php artisan view:clear       // 1. remove, including orphans
php artisan view:cache       // 2. compile the current tree

{{-- AND THE SAME FOR THE OTHER CACHES: --}}
php artisan config:clear && php artisan config:cache
php artisan route:clear  && php artisan route:cache

{{-- THE PROPERTY THAT MAKES THESE USABLE IN A PIPELINE: every one
     exits non-zero on error, so a compile failure is a build
     failure rather than a 500. --}}
php artisan view:cache || { echo "view:cache failed"; exit 1; }

{{-- WHICH IS TRUE BUT NOT SUFFICIENT, and is the trap: --}}
{{-- view:cache exits 0 having compiled NOTHING if the compiled
     directory does not exist or is not writable. The command
     succeeds, the build passes, and every request compiles. So the
     build has to ASSERT the artefact, not just run the command. --}}
php artisan view:clear && php artisan view:cache
test -s storage/framework/views || {
  echo "view:cache produced nothing — check VIEW_COMPILED_PATH"
  exit 1
}
ls storage/framework/views/*.php | wc -l   # a real count, for the log


{{-- WHEN CLEARING IS THE ANSWER: the two cases, with their signs. --}}

{{-- CASE 1. A STALE TEMPLATE. Obvious symptom: you changed a file
     and nothing happened. The cause is always one of: the compiled
     file is newer than the source, or you are editing a copy that
     is not the one being resolved. --}}

{{-- The second cause is more common than the first, and no amount
     of clearing will help with it. Check WHICH file resolved: --}}

     template is a FAILED BUILD, and the runtime never sees it. --}}

1.6 Deployment order and pre-warming

Specifies the correct order of build and cache commands in a release pipeline, and the failure mode produced when the order is reversed.

A release must install dependencies, then build assets, then compile views, then warm caches, then switch traffic. Compiling views before composer install fails because package views are not yet present, and compiling before a database-backed custom component is registered produces artifacts that call a class which does not exist.

<h4>The order, and why it is this order</h4> <p>Views, then configuration, then routes, then the warmed application cache. The order is not arbitrary even though the three are independent: configuration and route caching both read configuration, so they must run after the configuration cache exists or they will compile against the uncached values and then disagree with the cached file. Views are independent of both, so they can run at any point — and running them first means a template compile error fails the build before the other two have produced artefacts that would then be shipped alongside a broken view.</p> <p>The step that is usually missing is the last one: warming the framework's own data caches. A cached configuration file, a cached route table and a warm application cache are three different things, and a deployment that does all but the third still recompiles the service providers and the view finder on the first request — which is the first request after every deploy, and therefore the request that is most likely to be the one being watched by whoever deployed it.</p> <h4>Running the steps in the build, not in the container start</h4> <p>Every one of these commands should run at build time with the production environment already selected. Running them at container start means the first request pays for them, and it means a compile error is a runtime error rather than a failed deployment. The complication is that building requires the environment variables to be present at build time, which conflicts with the practice of baking one image and configuring it per environment — and the resolution is a two-stage image where the caching step runs in the final stage with the real environment loaded.</p> <h4>What to assert, so a failure is a build failure</h4> <p>The commands exit non-zero on error, which is the property that makes them usable in a pipeline. What is worth adding is an assertion that the artefacts exist and are non-trivial, because a cache command that silently produced nothing — because of a permissions problem, or because the directory was empty — will otherwise be discovered by the first request.</p>

The order, and why it is this order

Views, then configuration, then routes, then the warmed application cache. The order is not arbitrary even though the three are independent: configuration and route caching both read configuration, so they must run after the configuration cache exists or they will compile against the uncached values and then disagree with the cached file. Views are independent of both, so they can run at any point — and running them first means a template compile error fails the build before the other two have produced artefacts that would then be shipped alongside a broken view.

The step that is usually missing is the last one: warming the framework's own data caches. A cached configuration file, a cached route table and a warm application cache are three different things, and a deployment that does all but the third still recompiles the service providers and the view finder on the first request — which is the first request after every deploy, and therefore the request that is most likely to be the one being watched by whoever deployed it.

Running the steps in the build, not in the container start

Every one of these commands should run at build time with the production environment already selected. Running them at container start means the first request pays for them, and it means a compile error is a runtime error rather than a failed deployment. The complication is that building requires the environment variables to be present at build time, which conflicts with the practice of baking one image and configuring it per environment — and the resolution is a two-stage image where the caching step runs in the final stage with the real environment loaded.

What to assert, so a failure is a build failure

The commands exit non-zero on error, which is the property that makes them usable in a pipeline. What is worth adding is an assertion that the artefacts exist and are non-trivial, because a cache command that silently produced nothing — because of a permissions problem, or because the directory was empty — will otherwise be discovered by the first request.

composer install --no-dev --optimize-autoloader
npm ci && npm run build
php artisan view:cache
php artisan config:cache && php artisan route:cache
php artisan queue:restart

Pre-warming moves compilation out of the first request after each deploy, where a small number of concurrent workers would otherwise all recompile the same templates simultaneously. For a large template set this is a measurable reduction in p95 latency on first hit.

Practice. Run php artisan view:cache in CI. If it fails, the release fails — which is preferable to discovering a missing component class when a customer loads the page.

Exercises 1

  1. Create a view that renders a nested loop, then run view:clear and inspect the compiled artifact in storage/framework/views to confirm the escaped echo.
  2. Register a second view directory in config/view.php containing a file with the same name as an existing view, and demonstrate which one the finder resolves.
  3. Write a deployment script with the correct command order, then run it against a deployment with view:cache removed and measure first-request latency.
  4. Reproduce a stale-template scenario by restoring original modification times after editing a template, and confirm the output is stale.

{{-- THE ORDER, and each step's dependency on the one before. --}}

// Dockerfile — the FINAL stage, with the environment already set
// so a compile error fails the BUILD rather than the first request.
FROM php:8.3-fpm AS final

RUN apt-get update && apt-get install -y libzip-dev git  && docker-php-ext-install zip pcntl

COPY --from=builder /app /var/www/html
WORKDIR /var/www/html

RUN composer install --no-dev --optimize-autoloader --no-interaction

# One stage, one source tree, all artefacts built together. Any
# non-zero exit fails the image build.
RUN php artisan view:clear  && php artisan view:cache  && php artisan config:cache  && php artisan route:cache  && php artisan optimize

# The assertion that turns a silently-empty cache into a build
# failure. view:cache can exit 0 having compiled nothing if the
# directory is unwritable, and the first request is where that
# becomes a 200 ms response instead of a 15 ms one.
RUN test -s storage/framework/views  && test -s bootstrap/cache/config.php  && test -s bootstrap/cache/routes-v7.php  && test -f bootstrap/cache/packages.php

RUN chown -R www-data:www-data storage bootstrap/cache
USER www-data

{{-- THE PRODUCTION ONLY COMMANDS, in one place, so a deployment
     cannot pick three of the five. --}}
// composer.json
"scripts": {
    "deploy:prepare": [
        "@php artisan view:clear",
        "@php artisan view:cache",
        "@php artisan config:cache",
        "@php artisan route:cache",
        "@php artisan optimize"
    ],
    "post-autoload-dump": [
        "@php artisan package:discover --ansi"
    ]
}

{{-- AND THE ONE STEP THAT IS NOT A COMMAND. The container must not
     recompile on start, because a first request that compiles is a
     first request that is ten times slower than every other one —
     and it is the request a smoke test usually makes. --}}
// docker-compose.yml
services:
  app:
    image: myapp:latest
    environment:
      APP_ENV: production
      APP_DEBUG: "false"
      VIEW_COMPILED_PATH: /var/www/html/storage/framework/views
    # No command that runs artisan at start. The artefacts are in
    # the image.

2 · Escaping: Echo Syntax and the XSS Boundary

2.1 The escaped echo2.2 The raw echo2.3 Blade comments2.4 The XSS boundary2.5 Escaping in attributes and JavaScript2.6 Serialising data safely

Blade’s escaping behaviour is a compiler guarantee rather than a convention. This chapter documents the three echo forms, what each one emits, and how to keep untrusted data on the correct side of the escaping boundary.

2.1 The escaped echo

Documents {{ }}, the default output form, and the helper the compiler emits for it.

The double-curly echo compiles to <?= e($value) ?>, where e() is Illuminate\Support\htmlspecialchars with double encoding enabled:

<h4>Two syntaxes, one difference</h4> <p>Interpolation comes in two forms and they differ in exactly one thing: whether the value passes through the escaping function on its way out. The doubled braces call it; the tripled braces do not. Everything else — where they can appear, what expressions they accept, how they behave inside attributes and script blocks — is the same.</p> <p>The reason for two syntaxes rather than a flag is that a review can see them. A global search for the raw form finds every instance in the codebase, which means the dangerous construct is enumerable. Had the choice been an attribute or a helper call, the safe case would need auditing instead of the unsafe one, and the count of interpolations across a real application is thousands.</p> <h4>What escaping actually does, and what it does not</h4> <p>The escaping function replaces the five characters that are structurally significant in HTML text: the angle brackets and the ampersand. It is a text-context function. It does not touch a quote character, and it does not know about JavaScript, a URL, or a CSS context — which means it is the correct function for text between tags and the wrong function everywhere else. Section 2.4 and 2.5 are entirely about that distinction, and the general rule is worth stating here so it is not lost: the escaping must match the context the value lands in, and there are more contexts than there are braces.</p> <p>There is a second consequence, which is subtler. Escaping makes the value inert as markup, which means a value that legitimately contains markup — a description with formatting, a message with a link — is rendered as visible angle brackets rather than as a tag. That is correct behaviour, and it means the fix is not a different escaping function but a different value: a sanitised HTML string, produced deliberately by a function whose name says what it does, rather than a raw one.</p>

Two syntaxes, one difference

Interpolation comes in two forms and they differ in exactly one thing: whether the value passes through the escaping function on its way out. The doubled braces call it; the tripled braces do not. Everything else — where they can appear, what expressions they accept, how they behave inside attributes and script blocks — is the same.

The reason for two syntaxes rather than a flag is that a review can see them. A global search for the raw form finds every instance in the codebase, which means the dangerous construct is enumerable. Had the choice been an attribute or a helper call, the safe case would need auditing instead of the unsafe one, and the count of interpolations across a real application is thousands.

What escaping actually does, and what it does not

The escaping function replaces the five characters that are structurally significant in HTML text: the angle brackets and the ampersand. It is a text-context function. It does not touch a quote character, and it does not know about JavaScript, a URL, or a CSS context — which means it is the correct function for text between tags and the wrong function everywhere else. Section 2.4 and 2.5 are entirely about that distinction, and the general rule is worth stating here so it is not lost: the escaping must match the context the value lands in, and there are more contexts than there are braces.

There is a second consequence, which is subtler. Escaping makes the value inert as markup, which means a value that legitimately contains markup — a description with formatting, a message with a link — is rendered as visible angle brackets rather than as a tag. That is correct behaviour, and it means the fix is not a different escaping function but a different value: a sanitised HTML string, produced deliberately by a function whose name says what it does, rather than a raw one.

// Template
<p>{{ $user->name }}</p>

// Compiled
<p><?= e($user->name) ?></p>

// Input:  <b>Ada</b>
// Output: &lt;b&gt;Ada&lt;/b&gt;

Escaping applies to the rendered string, so DataTransfer objects that implement Htmlable are passed through untouched. An object whose toHtml() returns unescaped markup therefore bypasses the guarantee; only trust Htmlable implementations you control.

Null handling. {{ $missing ?? null }} renders an empty string, not the literal text "null". Blade compiles the echo through e(), which returns an empty string for null.


{{-- THE TWO, and the whole difference. --}}
{{ $user->name }}          {{-- e($user->name): escaped --}}
{!! $user->bioHtml !!}     {{-- raw: NOT escaped --}}

{{-- WHICH COMPILED PHP EACH PRODUCES, which is the form to read
     when you want to know exactly what happens to a value. --}}
{{ $user->name }}
{{-- compiles to: name); ?> --}}

{!! $user->bioHtml !!}
{{-- compiles to: bioHtml; ?> --}}

{{-- SO THE COMPILED FILE IS THE GROUND TRUTH. Run this and read
     it whenever the behaviour of a template surprises you; it
     answers the question without guesswork. --}}
php artisan view:clear && php artisan view:cache
grep -rn 'echo e(' storage/framework/views | head

{{-- WHAT e() ACTUALLY REPLACES: the five characters that are
     structurally significant in HTML TEXT. It is a text-context
     function and nothing more — which is why it is the wrong one
     in an attribute or inside a script block. --}}
// Illuminate/Support/helpers.php
function e($value, $doubleEncode = true): string
{
    if ($value instanceof DeferringDisplayableValue) {
        $value = $value->resolveDisplayableValue();
    }

    if ($value instanceof Htmlable) {
        return $value->toHtml();
    }

    if ($value instanceof BackedEnum) {
        $value = $value->value;
    }

    return htmlspecialchars($value ?? '', ENT_QUOTES | ENT_SUBSTITUTE, 'UTF-8', $doubleEncode);
}

{{-- THE ENUM AND HTMLABLE BRANCHES, which are the two that change
     behaviour rather than merely escaping. A BackedEnum is escaped
     by its VALUE, so an enum case cannot accidentally leak an
     object; and an Htmlable is passed through, which is how a
     deliberately-sanitised string reaches the output without
     reaching for {!! !!}. --}}

{{-- THE SECOND CONSEQUENCE, and it is not a bug. Escaping makes a
     value INERT as markup, so a value that legitimately contains
     markup renders as visible angle brackets. --}}
{{-- A description stored as plain text containing HTML --}}
{{ $product->description }}
{{-- renders: "Made from <b>steel</b> and <i>glass</i>"
     ^ visible brackets, which is CORRECT for a text field

     The fix is not a different escape function. It is a different
     VALUE: sanitise deliberately, name the function for what it
     does, and let the type say the value is already safe. --}}

@php($safe = AppSupportHtml::sanitise($product->description))
{{ $safe }}

{{-- WHICH COMPILES TO e() AGAIN — because a value wrapped in
     HtmlString is returned by e() unchanged, so the raw form is
     not needed. This is the mechanism that makes a sanitiser
     reviewable: ONE call site produces it, and every output of it
     is safe by construction. --}}


{{-- THE TWO, and the whole difference. --}}
{{ $user->name }}          {{-- e($user->name): escaped --}}
{!! $user->bioHtml !!}     {{-- raw: NOT escaped --}}

{{-- WHICH COMPILED PHP EACH PRODUCES, which is the form to read
     when you want to know exactly what happens to a value. --}}
{{ $user->name }}
{{-- compiles to: name); ?> --}}

{!! $user->bioHtml !!}
{{-- compiles to: bioHtml; ?> --}}

{{-- SO THE COMPILED FILE IS THE GROUND TRUTH. Run this and read
     it whenever the behaviour of a template surprises you; it
     answers the question without guesswork. --}}
php artisan view:clear && php artisan view:cache
grep -rn 'echo e(' storage/framework/views | head

{{-- WHAT e() ACTUALLY REPLACES: the five characters that are
     structurally significant in HTML TEXT. It is a text-context
     function and nothing more — which is why it is the wrong one
     in an attribute or inside a script block. --}}
// Illuminate/Support/helpers.php
function e($value, $doubleEncode = true): string
{
    if ($value instanceof DeferringDisplayableValue) {
        $value = $value->resolveDisplayableValue();
    }

    if ($value instanceof Htmlable) {
        return $value->toHtml();
    }

    if ($value instanceof BackedEnum) {
        $value = $value->value;
    }

    return htmlspecialchars($value ?? '', ENT_QUOTES | ENT_SUBSTITUTE, 'UTF-8', $doubleEncode);
}

{{-- THE ENUM AND HTMLABLE BRANCHES, which are the two that change
     behaviour rather than merely escaping. A BackedEnum is escaped
     by its VALUE, so an enum case cannot accidentally leak an
     object; and an Htmlable is passed through, which is how a
     deliberately-sanitised string reaches the output without
     reaching for {!! !!}. --}}

{{-- THE SECOND CONSEQUENCE, and it is not a bug. Escaping makes a
     value INERT as markup, so a value that legitimately contains
     markup renders as visible angle brackets. --}}
{{-- A description stored as plain text containing HTML --}}
{{ $product->description }}
{{-- renders: "Made from <b>steel</b> and <i>glass</i>"
     ^ visible brackets, which is CORRECT for a text field

     The fix is not a different escape function. It is a different
     VALUE: sanitise deliberately, name the function for what it
     does, and let the type say the value is already safe. --}}

@php($safe = AppSupportHtml::sanitise($product->description))
{{ $safe }}

{{-- WHICH COMPILES TO e() AGAIN — because a value wrapped in
     HtmlString is returned by e() unchanged, so the raw form is
     not needed. This is the mechanism that makes a sanitiser
     reviewable: ONE call site produces it, and every output of it
     is safe by construction. --}}

2.2 The raw echo

Documents the unescaped echo and defines the narrow set of situations in which it is legitimate.

The curly-brace-tilde echo emits the value verbatim:

<h4>What the raw form is for, precisely</h4> <p>It is for exactly three things: markup you generated yourself and know to be correct, markup that has been sanitised by a function whose name says so, and content that is already a trusted fragment such as a translated string from your own language files. It is not for content that merely happens to be safe today, and it is not a way to avoid an escaping bug.</p> <p>The distinction that matters is between <em>trusted</em> and <em>currently harmless</em>. Content is trusted when the program produced it, or when a sanitiser validated it against a policy. Content is merely harmless when nobody has checked it this time. The difference becomes a security defect the first time a user, an administrator, a translation contributor or an upstream package can put a value in that field — and each of those is a thing that changes without a code change.</p> <h4>The pattern that makes it reviewable</h4> <p>The risk with a raw interpolation is not the individual instance; a reviewer looking at one line can tell whether it is safe. The risk is that the number of them is unbounded and there is no way to tell a safe one from an unsafe one without following the value to its source. The fix is structural rather than editorial: make the trusted value a <em>type</em>. A value that has been through the sanitiser carries a wrapper the escaping function recognises and passes through, so a raw interpolation is not required and a value that forgot to be sanitised renders as visible markup instead of executing.</p>

What the raw form is for, precisely

It is for exactly three things: markup you generated yourself and know to be correct, markup that has been sanitised by a function whose name says so, and content that is already a trusted fragment such as a translated string from your own language files. It is not for content that merely happens to be safe today, and it is not a way to avoid an escaping bug.

The distinction that matters is between trusted and currently harmless. Content is trusted when the program produced it, or when a sanitiser validated it against a policy. Content is merely harmless when nobody has checked it this time. The difference becomes a security defect the first time a user, an administrator, a translation contributor or an upstream package can put a value in that field — and each of those is a thing that changes without a code change.

The pattern that makes it reviewable

The risk with a raw interpolation is not the individual instance; a reviewer looking at one line can tell whether it is safe. The risk is that the number of them is unbounded and there is no way to tell a safe one from an unsafe one without following the value to its source. The fix is structural rather than editorial: make the trusted value a type. A value that has been through the sanitiser carries a wrapper the escaping function recognises and passes through, so a raw interpolation is not required and a value that forgot to be sanitised renders as visible markup instead of executing.

// Template
<div>{!! $post->body_html !!}</div>

// Compiled
<div><?php echo $post->body_html; ?></div>

It exists for three legitimate uses: rendering trusted, framework-produced HTML such as a paginator or an error summary; rendering content that has already been sanitised on write by a dedicated library; and rendering a rich-text body authored inside a constrained administrative interface. It is not legitimate for user-submitted text, even when that text is behind authentication.

Authentication is not sanitisation. Any user who can submit HTML — including an authenticated administrator, and including users you intend to trust — can submit markup containing script. If the value must be rendered raw, sanitise it on write, store the sanitised result, and re-sanitise on output for existing rows.


{{-- THE THREE LEGITIMATE USES, and what makes each one safe. --}}

{{-- (1) MARKUP YOU GENERATED. The program built it, so there is
     no path by which a user put anything in it. --}}
{!! $chart->toSvg() !!}

{{-- (2) SANITISED CONTENT, through a function whose name says
     what it does. The important part is that the name is
     SPECIFIC: "sanitiseRichText", not "clean" or "process". --}}
@php($body = AppSupportHtml::sanitiseRichText($comment->bodyHtml))
{!! $body !!}

{{-- (3) A TRANSLATION FROM YOUR OWN LANGUAGE FILES. A translator
     controls these, and they are reviewed on the way in. This is
     the case people most often forget is legitimate. --}}
{!! __('emails.welcome_body', ['name' => $user->name]) !!}

{{-- AND THE FOURTH CASE, which is the dangerous one and the reason
     the review needs a rule: a value that is merely CURRENTLY
     harmless. Every one of these is safe today and becomes an XSS
     the day a second party can reach the field. --}}
{!! $user->bio !!}                  {{-- a bio is user input --}}
{!! $product->description !!}       {{-- imported from a partner feed --}}
{!! $setting->footer_html !!}       {{-- set by an administrator --}}
{!! $widget->markup !!}             {{-- from a plugin you do not own --}}

{{-- THE STRUCTURAL FIX, which is the point of this section: make
     the trusted value a TYPE, so the raw form is not needed. --}}
// app/Support/Html.php
final class Html
{
    /**
     * @param  string  $input  UNTRUSTED
     * @return SanitisedHtml  a type the view layer recognises
     */
    public static function sanitiseRichText(string $input): SanitisedHtml
    {
        return new SanitisedHtml(
            // An ALLOWLIST, not a blocklist. A blocklist of tags and
            // attributes has been bypassed repeatedly over the life
            // of this problem; an allowlist of what is permitted
            // fails safe when the standard gains a new tag.
            (new HtmlSanitizer(config('security.sanitiser.allow')))
                ->sanitize($input)
        );
    }
}

{{-- AND THEN THE OUTPUT IS THE ORDINARY INTERPOLATION, because the
     escaping function passes a trusted value through unchanged. A
     value that forgot to be sanitised renders as VISIBLE MARKUP
     instead of executing, so the failure mode is cosmetic rather
     than a compromise. --}}
{{ $comment->bodyHtml }}     {{-- safe whether or not it was
                                    sanitised: the TYPE decides --}}

{{-- WHICH COMPILED TO e($comment->bodyHtml), and e() returned it
     unchanged because it implements Htmlable. So the escaped form
     is correct in both cases, and there is no reason in this design
     for a raw interpolation to exist anywhere in the codebase. --}}


{{-- THE THREE LEGITIMATE USES, and what makes each one safe. --}}

{{-- (1) MARKUP YOU GENERATED. The program built it, so there is
     no path by which a user put anything in it. --}}
{!! $chart->toSvg() !!}

{{-- (2) SANITISED CONTENT, through a function whose name says
     what it does. The important part is that the name is
     SPECIFIC: "sanitiseRichText", not "clean" or "process". --}}

{!! $body !!}

            // attributes has been bypassed repeatedly over the life
            // of this problem; an allowlist of what is permitted

     escaping function passes a trusted value through unchanged. A
     value that forgot to be sanitised renders as VISIBLE MARKUP
     instead of executing, so the failure mode is cosmetic rather
     than a compromise. --}}

                                    sanitised: the TYPE decides --}}

{{-- WHICH COMPILED TO e($comment->bodyHtml), and e() returned it
     unchanged because it implements Htmlable. So the escaped form
     is correct in both cases, and there is no reason in this design
     for a raw interpolation to exist anywhere in the codebase. --}}

2.3 Blade comments

Describes Blade comments, their removal at compile time, and the reason they are preferable to HTML comments for conditional markup.

Blade comments are stripped by the compiler and never reach the browser:

<h4>Which comment syntax, and why there are three</h4> <p>Three syntaxes exist because three different requirements exist, and picking the wrong one has a real cost. The doubled-brace form is removed by the compiler and never reaches the output, which is what you want for explaining the template. The doubled-brace form on its own line removes a line from the output, which is what you want for commenting out a section without leaving a blank line behind. The percent form is passed through to the output and removed by the browser, which is what you want for a marker that has to survive into the rendered HTML so a JavaScript tool or a screen reader can find it.</p> <p>The distinction that catches people is between a comment that is removed at compile time and one that is removed at parse time. A compile-time comment cannot be read in the browser, so it cannot explain the rendered output; a parse-time comment ships to every visitor, which means it is not private. The rule is that an explanation of the template is a compile-time comment and nothing else is.</p> <h4>What comments cannot do</h4> <p>A comment cannot disable a line of markup without leaving a stray closing tag behind, and it cannot comment out part of a line. Both are consequences of the comment being removed as a unit at compile time rather than being parsed as markup. This is why the practical technique for temporarily removing a single element is a directive with a condition rather than a comment, and why a template full of commented-out markup is a template whose rendered structure nobody can determine by reading it.</p> <h4>The cost that is easy to forget</h4> <p>Comments are in the source, so they are in the compiled view file, so they are in the cache. A comment explaining an internal decision is therefore readable by anyone who can read the compiled directory — which on a shared host or a misconfigured container is not necessarily the same set of people as those who can read the repository. That is a mild concern for most comments and a real one for any comment that names an unfixed vulnerability.</p>

Which comment syntax, and why there are three

Three syntaxes exist because three different requirements exist, and picking the wrong one has a real cost. The doubled-brace form is removed by the compiler and never reaches the output, which is what you want for explaining the template. The doubled-brace form on its own line removes a line from the output, which is what you want for commenting out a section without leaving a blank line behind. The percent form is passed through to the output and removed by the browser, which is what you want for a marker that has to survive into the rendered HTML so a JavaScript tool or a screen reader can find it.

The distinction that catches people is between a comment that is removed at compile time and one that is removed at parse time. A compile-time comment cannot be read in the browser, so it cannot explain the rendered output; a parse-time comment ships to every visitor, which means it is not private. The rule is that an explanation of the template is a compile-time comment and nothing else is.

What comments cannot do

A comment cannot disable a line of markup without leaving a stray closing tag behind, and it cannot comment out part of a line. Both are consequences of the comment being removed as a unit at compile time rather than being parsed as markup. This is why the practical technique for temporarily removing a single element is a directive with a condition rather than a comment, and why a template full of commented-out markup is a template whose rendered structure nobody can determine by reading it.

The cost that is easy to forget

Comments are in the source, so they are in the compiled view file, so they are in the cache. A comment explaining an internal decision is therefore readable by anyone who can read the compiled directory — which on a shared host or a misconfigured container is not necessarily the same set of people as those who can read the repository. That is a mild concern for most comments and a real one for any comment that names an unfixed vulnerability.

{{-- Single line: removed entirely --}}

{{--
    Multi line.
    Also removed entirely.
--}}

{{-- @if ($debug) Never compiled, never parsed --}} --}}

Because Blade removes these at compile time, they cost nothing at runtime and cannot be read in the page source. An HTML comment, by contrast, is transmitted, is visible in view-source, and may accidentally disclose internal conditionals or version information. Prefer {{-- --}} for all template-level commenting.

Nesting. Blade comments do not nest. The first --}} encountered terminates the comment, so any {{-- }} appearing earlier in the comment body ends it prematurely. Split such content into two comments.


{{-- (1) THE COMPILE-TIME COMMENT. Removed by the compiler, never
     reaches the output. This is what almost every comment should
     be: an explanation for whoever reads the template next. --}}
{{-- Prices are in minor units (cents) throughout. A Money value
     is used rather than a float so that a sum of ten thousand
     lines is exact. --}}
{{ $line->amount->format() }}

{{-- (2) THE LINE REMOVAL. Leaves nothing behind, including the
     newline, which is why it exists separately from (1). --}}
{{-- <p class="legacy-note">Orders placed before 2024.</p> --}}

{{-- (3) THE PASSTHROUGH. NOT removed by the compiler — it reaches
     the rendered HTML and is removed by the browser. Use it only
     when a tool on the rendered page needs to find the marker. --}}
<!-- navigation:begin -->
<nav>...</nav>
<!-- navigation:end -->

{{-- (4) AND THE PHP COMMENT, which the compiler passes through
     verbatim. It reaches the output as a PHP comment, which the
     PHP engine discards — so it costs a little compile time and
     nothing else. Reach for (1) instead. --}}
{{-- /* why this loop is not a collection helper */ --}}
{{-- @php /* discouraged */ --}}

{{-- WHAT A COMMENT CANNOT DO, and both of these are why a template
     full of commented-out markup is unreadable. --}}

{{-- A comment cannot comment out PART of a line: --}}
{{-- <span class="{{ $class }}">{{ $text }}</span> --}}
{{-- ^ removing this removes the whole element. To remove only the
     class you need the condition, not the comment. --}}

{{-- A comment cannot remove a CLOSING TAG without removing the
     element, because the compiler does not parse markup: --}}
<div>
    {{-- </div> --}}
</div>
{{-- ^ this still produces <div></div>, and the comment is the only
     thing you removed --}}

{{-- THE TECHNIQUE FOR ONE ELEMENT: a directive with a condition
     that is obviously temporary, so a search finds it. --}}
@if (false) {{-- TEMPORARY: the promo banner is disabled --}}
    <x-promo-banner />
@endif

{{-- AND THE COMPILE-TIME COST, which is small but real and worth
     knowing before commenting out a large block: the comment is
     in the source, so it is in the compiled file, so it is parsed
     on every request the view is included. --}}
php artisan view:clear && php artisan view:cache
grep -c 'this is why the buffer is copied' storage/framework/views/*.php


{{-- (1) THE COMPILE-TIME COMMENT. Removed by the compiler, never
     reaches the output. This is what almost every comment should
     be: an explanation for whoever reads the template next. --}}
{{-- Prices are in minor units (cents) throughout. A Money value
     is used rather than a float so that a sum of ten thousand
     lines is exact. --}}
{{ $line->amount->format() }}

{{-- (2) THE LINE REMOVAL. Leaves nothing behind, including the
     newline, which is why it exists separately from (1). --}}

     the rendered HTML and is removed by the browser. Use it only
     when a tool on the rendered page needs to find the marker. --}}

<nav>...</nav>
<!-- navigation:end -->

{{-- (4) AND THE PHP COMMENT, which the compiler passes through
     verbatim. It reaches the output as a PHP comment, which the
     PHP engine discards — so it costs a little compile time and
     nothing else. Reach for (1) instead. --}}

{{-- @php /* discouraged */ --}}

{{-- WHAT A COMMENT CANNOT DO, and both of these are why a template
     full of commented-out markup is unreadable. --}}

{{-- A comment cannot comment out PART of a line: --}}
{{-- <span class="{{ $class }}">{{ $text }}</span> --}}
{{-- ^ removing this removes the whole element. To remove only the
     class you need the condition, not the comment. --}}

{{-- A comment cannot remove a CLOSING TAG without removing the
     element, because the compiler does not parse markup: --}}

    {{-- </div> --}}
</div>
{{-- ^ this still produces <div></div>, and the comment is the only
     thing you removed --}}

     that is obviously temporary, so a search finds it. --}}

@endif

     knowing before commenting out a large block: the comment is

     on every request the view is included. --}}
