2.4 The XSS boundary

States the invariant that keeps a template safe, and names the three ways it is routinely violated.

The invariant is simple: every value that originates outside the application’s trusted code path is rendered with {{ }}. Anything rendered with {!! !!} must be provably free of attacker-controlled markup at the point of output.

In practice the boundary fails in three ways. Storing raw HTML in the database and echoing it with {!! !!} is the first. Reflection is the second: a value taken from the query string, a flash message or a URL segment and echoed raw is exploitable even when the value is usually harmless. The third is partial sanitisation — stripping <script> with a regular expression is not sanitisation, because event handler attributes, javascript: URLs and obfuscated payloads all bypass it.

<h4>Where the boundary is, and why it is a context rather than a character</h4> <p>The escaping function is chosen by the syntax you write, and it escapes for exactly one context: HTML text. That is sufficient in a template body and wrong in three other places that occur constantly — inside an attribute value, inside a script block, and inside a style attribute or a URL. In each of those, the significant character is the quote, not the angle bracket, so escaping angle brackets protects nothing.</p> <p>This is the single most common real vulnerability in template layers and it survives review because every individual interpolation looks escaped. The value goes through the escaping function, so it is escaped — for the wrong context. A payload that closes the attribute and adds an event handler contains no angle brackets at all in the position that matters, so an escaper that only handles angle brackets passes it through untouched.</p> <h4>The contexts, and the right answer for each</h4> <p>Text between tags uses the ordinary escaping. An attribute value uses the attribute-escaping function, or better, is avoided entirely by passing data through a property or a data attribute and reading it in the script. A URL uses a scheme allowlist, because the dangerous part of a URL is its scheme rather than its characters. A script block needs the value to be a JSON literal or a properly-escaped JavaScript string, and neither of those is achieved by HTML-escaping.</p> <p>The general principle underneath all four: escaping is a function of the destination, so the template language needs to know the destination and therefore offers a different construct for each. Where it offers one construct for all of them — as a template language with only one escape function does — the correct answer is a convention plus a linter, because the language cannot help.</p>

Where the boundary is, and why it is a context rather than a character

The escaping function is chosen by the syntax you write, and it escapes for exactly one context: HTML text. That is sufficient in a template body and wrong in three other places that occur constantly — inside an attribute value, inside a script block, and inside a style attribute or a URL. In each of those, the significant character is the quote, not the angle bracket, so escaping angle brackets protects nothing.

This is the single most common real vulnerability in template layers and it survives review because every individual interpolation looks escaped. The value goes through the escaping function, so it is escaped — for the wrong context. A payload that closes the attribute and adds an event handler contains no angle brackets at all in the position that matters, so an escaper that only handles angle brackets passes it through untouched.

The contexts, and the right answer for each

Text between tags uses the ordinary escaping. An attribute value uses the attribute-escaping function, or better, is avoided entirely by passing data through a property or a data attribute and reading it in the script. A URL uses a scheme allowlist, because the dangerous part of a URL is its scheme rather than its characters. A script block needs the value to be a JSON literal or a properly-escaped JavaScript string, and neither of those is achieved by HTML-escaping.

The general principle underneath all four: escaping is a function of the destination, so the template language needs to know the destination and therefore offers a different construct for each. Where it offers one construct for all of them — as a template language with only one escape function does — the correct answer is a convention plus a linter, because the language cannot help.

// Vulnerable: search term reflected into results page
<h2>Results for {!! $request->q() !!}</h2>

// Correct: escaped at the boundary
<h2>Results for {{ $request->q() }}</h2>

{{-- THE FOUR CONTEXTS, and the wrong answer in three of them. --}}

{{-- (1) TEXT BETWEEN TAGS. The ordinary escape is correct. --}}
<p>{{ $user->name }}</p>

{{-- (2) ATTRIBUTE VALUE. WRONG, and this is a real vulnerability:
     the significant character in an attribute is the QUOTE, and
     htmlspecialchars with ENT_QUOTES does escape it — but only if
     you did not disable quoting. The version that IS broken is
     common in hand-rolled helpers: --}}
<div data-id="{{ $id }}"></div>
{{-- Looks escaped. Is it? e() uses ENT_QUOTES, so yes, this one
     happens to be safe. The broken version is below. --}}

<div id='{{ $id }}'>
{{-- STILL safe for the same reason: the escaper does not care
     which quote the attribute uses, only that it escapes both. --}}

{{-- THE ACTUALLY BROKEN VERSION is a raw interpolation, or a
     helper that concatenates without escaping: --}}
{!! '<div data-id="' . $id . '">' !!}
{{-- the payload </div><img src=x onerror=alert(1)> contains no
     quote, so even a quote-escaping escaper would have passed it,
     and here nothing escapes at all --}}

{{-- (3) SCRIPT BLOCK. The most dangerous context, because the
     escaper does not know JavaScript. A value containing a
     closing script tag terminates the block, and everything after
     it is parsed as markup. --}}
<script>
    var name = '{{ $user->name }}';
    {{-- a name of  </script><img src=x onerror=alert(1)>  ends the
         block right here, and the rest is HTML now. The escaper
         handled the angle brackets; it did not handle the fact
         that the destination was JavaScript. --}}
</script>

{{-- (4) STYLE AND URL. The dangerous part is not a character. --}}
<div style="background: {{ $user->colour }}">
{{-- $colour = "red; background-image: url(javascript:...)" --}}

<a href="{{ $user->website }}">
{{-- a scheme of javascript:alert(1) has no angle brackets and no
     quotes, so no amount of character escaping stops it --}}


{{-- THE FOUR CONTEXTS, and the wrong answer in three of them. --}}

{{-- (1) TEXT BETWEEN TAGS. The ordinary escape is correct. --}}
<p>{{ $user->name }}</p>

{{-- (2) ATTRIBUTE VALUE. WRONG, and this is a real vulnerability:
     the significant character in an attribute is the QUOTE, and
     htmlspecialchars with ENT_QUOTES does escape it — but only if
     you did not disable quoting. The version that IS broken is
     common in hand-rolled helpers: --}}
<div data-id="{{ $id }}"></div>
{{-- Looks escaped. Is it? e() uses ENT_QUOTES, so yes, this one
     happens to be safe. The broken version is below. --}}

<div id='{{ $id }}'>
{{-- STILL safe for the same reason: the escaper does not care
     which quote the attribute uses, only that it escapes both. --}}

{{-- THE ACTUALLY BROKEN VERSION is a raw interpolation, or a
     helper that concatenates without escaping: --}}
{!! '<div data-id="' . $id . '">' !!}
{{-- the payload </div><img src=x onerror=alert(1)> contains no
     quote, so even a quote-escaping escaper would have passed it,
     and here nothing escapes at all --}}

{{-- (3) SCRIPT BLOCK. The most dangerous context, because the
     escaper does not know JavaScript. A value containing a
     closing script tag terminates the block, and everything after
     it is parsed as markup. --}}
<script>
    var name = '{{ $user->name }}';
    {{-- a name of  </script><img src=x onerror=alert(1)>  ends the
         block right here, and the rest is HTML now. The escaper
         handled the angle brackets; it did not handle the fact
         that the destination was JavaScript. --}}
</script>

{{-- (4) STYLE AND URL. The dangerous part is not a character. --}}
<div style="background: {{ $user->colour }}">
{{-- $colour = "red; background-image: url(javascript:...)" --}}

<a href="{{ $user->website }}">
{{-- a scheme of javascript:alert(1) has no angle brackets and no
     quotes, so no amount of character escaping stops it --}}

2.5 Escaping in attributes and JavaScript

Covers the contexts where HTML escaping alone is insufficient: attribute values, URLs and inline scripts. Each requires a different treatment.

Escaping &, <, >, " and ' is sufficient for element text and quoted attribute values. It is not sufficient for URL attributes, where a javascript: scheme survives HTML escaping intact:

<h4>Attributes, and the quote is the significant character</h4> <p>Inside an attribute value the characters that can break out are the quote character delimiting the attribute and, depending on the attribute, the whitespace that separates attributes. HTML escaping covers the angle brackets and the ampersand, and the framework's escape function additionally escapes both quote characters — which means an ordinary interpolation inside an attribute is safe. The failures in this area come from three other places, and it is worth being precise about which is which.</p> <p>A raw interpolation inside an attribute is the first and it is the simplest: nothing is escaped, so a value containing a quote ends the attribute and everything after it is markup. A hand-rolled attribute helper that concatenates without escaping is the second, and it looks like the first with extra steps. The third is subtler and is the one that survives review: an unquoted attribute, where a value containing a space becomes two attributes and a value containing <code>=</code> supplies its own. Every one of these is fixed by quoting the attribute and using the ordinary interpolation, which is the reason that habit matters more than any helper you write.</p> <h4>Script blocks, which need a different answer entirely</h4> <p>A value inside a script block cannot be made safe with HTML escaping, because the destination is JavaScript and the character that terminates the block is the opening tag of a closing script element. The three workable answers are: pass data as a JSON literal, which has its own escaping rules; pass it as an attribute value and read it from the DOM, so the parser handles it; or generate the whole block server-side. Of the three, the attribute route is the one to prefer, because it hands the problem to a parser that is not a templating language and has been attacking this problem for thirty years.</p>

Attributes, and the quote is the significant character

Inside an attribute value the characters that can break out are the quote character delimiting the attribute and, depending on the attribute, the whitespace that separates attributes. HTML escaping covers the angle brackets and the ampersand, and the framework's escape function additionally escapes both quote characters — which means an ordinary interpolation inside an attribute is safe. The failures in this area come from three other places, and it is worth being precise about which is which.

A raw interpolation inside an attribute is the first and it is the simplest: nothing is escaped, so a value containing a quote ends the attribute and everything after it is markup. A hand-rolled attribute helper that concatenates without escaping is the second, and it looks like the first with extra steps. The third is subtler and is the one that survives review: an unquoted attribute, where a value containing a space becomes two attributes and a value containing = supplies its own. Every one of these is fixed by quoting the attribute and using the ordinary interpolation, which is the reason that habit matters more than any helper you write.

Script blocks, which need a different answer entirely

A value inside a script block cannot be made safe with HTML escaping, because the destination is JavaScript and the character that terminates the block is the opening tag of a closing script element. The three workable answers are: pass data as a JSON literal, which has its own escaping rules; pass it as an attribute value and read it from the DOM, so the parser handles it; or generate the whole block server-side. Of the three, the attribute route is the one to prefer, because it hands the problem to a parser that is not a templating language and has been attacking this problem for thirty years.

// Unsafe: escaped, but the scheme is still executable
<a href="{{ $user->website }}">Profile</a>

// Correct: validate the scheme when it is application-supplied
@if (str_starts_with($user->website, 'https://'))
    <a href="{{ $user->website }}" rel="noopener">Profile</a>
@endif

Inside an inline script, the correct boundary is JSON encoding followed by a script-safe escape, never string concatenation. Blade offers no dedicated directive for this; use the framework helpers described next.

Links from user data. For any URL that comes from a profile, a comment or an imported feed, parse it with parse_url() and allow only http and https schemes before rendering it.


{{-- THE RULE: ALWAYS QUOTE, ALWAYS USE THE ORDINARY
     INTERPOLATION. e() uses ENT_QUOTES, so it escapes BOTH quote
     characters regardless of which one delimits the attribute. --}}
<div id="{{ $id }}" data-role="user"></div>

{{-- THE UNQUOTED ATTRIBUTE, which is broken in three separate ways
     and looks fine: --}}
<div class={{ $classes }}>
{{-- A value of "btn btn-primary" produces TWO attributes. A value
     of "a=b" supplies its own. A value containing " ends it. --}}

{{-- THE RAW INTERPOLATION INSIDE AN ATTRIBUTE: --}}
<div data-id="{!! $id !!}">
{{-- a payload of 1 onmouseover=alert(1)  needs no quote at all,
     so this is exploitable with a two-character payload --}}

{{-- A HAND-ROLLED HELPER THAT CONCATENATES: --}}
{!! '<div class="' . $classes . '">' !!}
{{-- same result, and now the escaping is in a helper nobody will
     remember to audit when they add the next attribute --}}

{{-- THE SCRIPT BLOCK, where HTML escaping is the wrong function
     because the destination is JavaScript. --}}
<script>
    var name = '{{ $user->name }}';
    {{-- a name of  </script><img src=x onerror=alert(1)>  closes
         the block. The escaper handled the angle brackets; it had
         no way to know the destination was JavaScript. --}}
</script>

{{-- ANSWER 1, THE ONE TO PREFER: data as an attribute, read from
     the DOM. The HTML parser does the escaping, and it has been
     attacking this problem for thirty years. --}}
<div id="user-panel" data-name="{{ $user->name }}">
    <span x-text="$el.dataset.name"></span>
</div>

{{-- ANSWER 2: a JSON literal. Note json_encode is called with
     flags that matter — JSON_HEX_TAG stops a </script> inside a
     string, which the default does NOT do. --}}
<script type="application/json" id="user-data">
    {!! json_encode($user, JSON_HEX_TAG | JSON_HEX_AMP | JSON_HEX_APOS | JSON_HEX_QUOT) !!}
</script>
<script>
    // Read it as JSON, so the value never enters the JS source.
    const user = JSON.parse(document.getElementById('user-data').textContent);
</script>

{{-- ANSWER 3: generate the whole block server-side, so there is no
     value in a script context at all. --}}
<script src="{{ route('app.user', $user) }}"></script>

{{-- WHAT JSON_HEX_TAG PREVENTS, which is the non-obvious one: --}}
{{ json_encode('</script>', JSON_HEX_TAG) }}
{{-- "</script>"  ->  "<></script>" ... precisely: < is emitted,
     so the browser never sees the closing tag and the string is
     still the value you meant. Without the flag, JSON encodes it
     verbatim and the block ends early. --}}


{{-- THE RULE: ALWAYS QUOTE, ALWAYS USE THE ORDINARY
     INTERPOLATION. e() uses ENT_QUOTES, so it escapes BOTH quote
     characters regardless of which one delimits the attribute. --}}
<div id="{{ $id }}" data-role="user"></div>

{{-- THE UNQUOTED ATTRIBUTE, which is broken in three separate ways
     and looks fine: --}}
<div class={{ $classes }}>
{{-- A value of "btn btn-primary" produces TWO attributes. A value
     of "a=b" supplies its own. A value containing " ends it. --}}

{{-- THE RAW INTERPOLATION INSIDE AN ATTRIBUTE: --}}
<div data-id="{!! $id !!}">
{{-- a payload of 1 onmouseover=alert(1)  needs no quote at all,
     so this is exploitable with a two-character payload --}}

{{-- A HAND-ROLLED HELPER THAT CONCATENATES: --}}
{!! '<div class="' . $classes . '">' !!}
{{-- same result, and now the escaping is in a helper nobody will
     remember to audit when they add the next attribute --}}

{{-- THE SCRIPT BLOCK, where HTML escaping is the wrong function
     because the destination is JavaScript. --}}
<script>
    var name = '{{ $user->name }}';
    {{-- a name of  </script><img src=x onerror=alert(1)>  closes
         the block. The escaper handled the angle brackets; it had
         no way to know the destination was JavaScript. --}}
</script>

{{-- ANSWER 1, THE ONE TO PREFER: data as an attribute, read from
     the DOM. The HTML parser does the escaping, and it has been
     attacking this problem for thirty years. --}}
<div id="user-panel" data-name="{{ $user->name }}">
    <span x-text="$el.dataset.name"></span>
</div>

{{-- ANSWER 2: a JSON literal. Note json_encode is called with
     flags that matter — JSON_HEX_TAG stops a </script> inside a
     string, which the default does NOT do. --}}
<script type="application/json" id="user-data">
    {!! json_encode($user, JSON_HEX_TAG | JSON_HEX_AMP | JSON_HEX_APOS | JSON_HEX_QUOT) !!}
</script>
<script>
    // Read it as JSON, so the value never enters the JS source.
    const user = JSON.parse(document.getElementById('user-data').textContent);
</script>

{{-- ANSWER 3: generate the whole block server-side, so there is no
     value in a script context at all. --}}
<script src="{{ route('app.user', $user) }}"></script>

{{-- WHAT JSON_HEX_TAG PREVENTS, which is the non-obvious one: --}}
{{ json_encode('</script>', JSON_HEX_TAG) }}
{{-- "</script>"  ->  "<></script>" ... precisely: < is emitted,
     so the browser never sees the closing tag and the string is
     still the value you meant. Without the flag, JSON encodes it
     verbatim and the block ends early. --}}

2.6 Serialising data safely

Introduces @js and Js::from(), the correct way to pass server data into inline JavaScript or Alpine expressions.

@js($value) compiles to a Js::from() call, which JSON-encodes the value and escapes the result so it is safe inside a script element:

<h4>Serialising into a script block, and the one flag that matters</h4> <p>Putting a PHP value into a page so that JavaScript can read it is a routine requirement and a routine vulnerability. The escaping problem is that the value has to survive two parsers — PHP's serialiser and the browser's HTML parser — and the second one is operating before the script is ever handed to the JavaScript engine. A string containing a closing script tag ends the block at the HTML level regardless of what the JavaScript engine would have done with it.</p> <p>The fix is a flag, and it is not the default. The hexadecimal flags make the serialiser emit <code><</code>, <code>></code>, <code>&</code> and the quotes as <code>u</code>-escapes, which are ordinary characters as far as the HTML parser is concerned and which the JavaScript engine decodes back to the original characters. The result is a script block in which no byte of the data can terminate it, and which still carries the exact value.</p> <h4>Why the script tag with a type is better than a bare assignment</h4> <p>The common form assigns to a variable, and it works until a value contains a newline or a sequence that the JavaScript grammar reads differently. The <code>type="application/json"</code> form is better for three reasons: the browser does not execute it, so there is no grammar to satisfy; the content is read as text and parsed with <code>JSON.parse</code>, which is a single well-defined operation; and the data cannot accidentally be a function call. It also means the data is not a global, which removes an entire class of collision between two templates on the same page.</p> <p>What to avoid is a round trip through <code>json_decode</code> on the client, and a hand-built string with escaped quotes. Both are ways of doing this that re-implement the serialiser badly.</p>

Serialising into a script block, and the one flag that matters

Putting a PHP value into a page so that JavaScript can read it is a routine requirement and a routine vulnerability. The escaping problem is that the value has to survive two parsers — PHP's serialiser and the browser's HTML parser — and the second one is operating before the script is ever handed to the JavaScript engine. A string containing a closing script tag ends the block at the HTML level regardless of what the JavaScript engine would have done with it.

The fix is a flag, and it is not the default. The hexadecimal flags make the serialiser emit <, >, & and the quotes as u-escapes, which are ordinary characters as far as the HTML parser is concerned and which the JavaScript engine decodes back to the original characters. The result is a script block in which no byte of the data can terminate it, and which still carries the exact value.

Why the script tag with a type is better than a bare assignment

The common form assigns to a variable, and it works until a value contains a newline or a sequence that the JavaScript grammar reads differently. The type="application/json" form is better for three reasons: the browser does not execute it, so there is no grammar to satisfy; the content is read as text and parsed with JSON.parse, which is a single well-defined operation; and the data cannot accidentally be a function call. It also means the data is not a global, which removes an entire class of collision between two templates on the same page.

What to avoid is a round trip through json_decode on the client, and a hand-built string with escaped quotes. Both are ways of doing this that re-implement the serialiser badly.

<div x-data="{ open: @js($open) }">
    <span x-text="label"></span>
</div>

<script>
    const config = @js($config);
</script>

For a plain PHP context, Illuminate\Support\Js is available directly. Prefer both over hand-written json_encode calls, because Js::from() handles the quote, angle-bracket and line-separator escaping that causes a script element to terminate early.

Exercises 2

  1. Render a value containing <script> and " with both echo forms, and inspect the raw response in the browser network panel.
  2. Prove that Blade comments are absent from the response while HTML comments are present.
  3. Write a controller action that reflects a query parameter safely, then break it deliberately and record the exact injection payload.
  4. Replace an inline json_encode call with @js() and confirm identical behaviour for a value containing a closing script tag.

{{-- THE FLAG SET, and JSON_HEX_TAG is the one that is not the
     default and the one that matters most. --}}
<script type="application/json" id="user-data">
    {!! json_encode($user, JSON_HEX_TAG | JSON_HEX_AMP | JSON_HEX_APOS | JSON_HEX_QUOT) !!}
</script>

{{-- WHAT JSON_HEX_TAG PREVENTS. Without it, this value: --}}
{{--   json_encode('</script><img src=x onerror=alert(1)>') --}}
{{-- produces the string verbatim, the HTML parser ends the block
     at </script>, and everything after it is markup. With it: --}}
{!! json_encode('</script><img src=x onerror=alert(1)>', JSON_HEX_TAG) !!}
{{-- produces  </script><img src=x onerror=alert(1)>
     ^ every angle bracket became u003C / u003E, so the browser
       never sees a closing tag, and JSON.parse returns the
       ORIGINAL string --}}

{{-- THE READ SIDE, which is where the choice shows: the block is
     NOT executed, so there is no JavaScript grammar to satisfy and
     no variable is created as a global. --}}
<script>
    const user = JSON.parse(document.getElementById('user-data').textContent);
</script>

{{-- AND IN A COMPONENT, which is where this pattern actually
     appears — inside a loop, with an id per instance. --}}
<div x-data="userPanel(@js($user))">
    ...
</div>

@php
    // @js() is the framework helper. It json_encodes AND applies the
    // escaping flags, and it returns a Js object whose __toString
    // does it — so it is safe in an attribute, in a script, and in
    // a single-quoted context, which raw json_encode is not.
@endphp

{{-- WHAT NOT TO DO, all three of which re-implement the
     serialiser badly: --}}

{{-- (a) A BARE ASSIGNMENT. It works until a value contains a
        newline, a </script>, or a sequence the JS grammar reads
        differently — and the failure is a syntax error in a file
        that is otherwise correct. --}}
<script>var user = {!! json_encode($user) !!};</script>

{{-- (b) HAND-ESCAPED QUOTES. --}}
<script>var name = '{!! addslashes($user->name) !!}';</script>
{{-- addslashes is not a JavaScript escaper: it does not handle
     </script>, it does not handle a newline, and it does not
     handle U+2028/U+2029, which are line terminators in JavaScript
     but not in JSON. --}}

{{-- (c) A ROUND TRIP THROUGH JSON.parse ON THE CLIENT for a value
        the server already has as a PHP structure. --}}
<script>var rows = JSON.parse('{!! json_encode($rows) !!}');</script>
{{-- double-encoding: the quotes in the JSON string are escaped,
     then the whole thing is embedded in another string literal,
     and any content that breaks the OUTER literal breaks it. --}}

{{-- THE LARGE-VALUE CASE, which is a different problem: a payload
     over a megabyte in a script block is a megabyte the browser
     parses before the page can render. Move it to an endpoint. --}}
@if (strlen(json_encode($rows)) < 100_000)
    <script type="application/json" id="rows">{!! json_encode($rows, JSON_HEX_TAG) !!}</script>
@else
    <script type="application/json" id="rows-src">{"url": "/api/rows"}</script>
@endif


{{-- THE FLAG SET, and JSON_HEX_TAG is the one that is not the
     default and the one that matters most. --}}
<script type="application/json" id="user-data">
    {!! json_encode($user, JSON_HEX_TAG | JSON_HEX_AMP | JSON_HEX_APOS | JSON_HEX_QUOT) !!}
</script>

{{-- WHAT JSON_HEX_TAG PREVENTS. Without it, this value: --}}
{{--   json_encode('</script><img src=x onerror=alert(1)>') --}}
{{-- produces the string verbatim, the HTML parser ends the block
     at </script>, and everything after it is markup. With it: --}}
{!! json_encode('</script><img src=x onerror=alert(1)>', JSON_HEX_TAG) !!}
{{-- produces  </script><img src=x onerror=alert(1)>
     ^ every angle bracket became u003C / u003E, so the browser
       never sees a closing tag, and JSON.parse returns the
       ORIGINAL string --}}

     NOT executed, so there is no JavaScript grammar to satisfy and
     no variable is created as a global. --}}
<script>

</script>

     appears — inside a loop, with an id per instance. --}}

    ...
</div>

     serialiser badly: --}}

        newline, a </script>, or a sequence the JS grammar reads

        that is otherwise correct. --}}

     </script>, it does not handle a newline, and it does not
     handle U+2028/U+2029, which are line terminators in JavaScript
     but not in JSON. --}}

        the server already has as a PHP structure. --}}

     then the whole thing is embedded in another string literal,
     and any content that breaks the OUTER literal breaks it. --}}

     over a megabyte in a script block is a megabyte the browser

    <script type="application/json" id="rows">{!! json_encode($rows, JSON_HEX_TAG) !!}</script>
@else
    <script type="application/json" id="rows-src">{"url": "/api/rows"}</script>
@endif

3 · Control Flow: Directives That Emit PHP Structures

3.1 Conditional directives3.2 Loop directives3.3 Switch, break and continue3.4 The @php directive3.5 Loop metadata3.6 Escaping Blade with @verbatim

Control-flow directives are not functions; each is a textual expansion into a PHP structure with a matching end directive. Knowing what they expand to explains their placement rules and their interaction with the loop variable.

3.1 Conditional directives

Lists the conditional directives and their compiled form, including the shorthand forms that omit the closing endif.

<h4>The three forms, and when each is right</h4> <p>There are three conditional constructs and they differ in what happens when the condition is false. The first emits nothing at all. The second emits nothing but an alternative. The third emits nothing but records the branch as taken and undoes everything emitted inside it, which is a completely different mechanism and is the one people are surprised by.</p> <p>The first is the one to reach for by default. A condition that renders one thing or nothing is a condition whose false branch is the empty string, and writing it that way means there is no alternative to read and no alternative to keep in sync. The second form earns its place when both branches are substantial, and the common mistake is using it for a two-line body where the reader has to compare two texts to see how different they are.</p> <h4>Strictness, and the bug it catches</h4> <p>By default a condition on an undefined variable is false, silently. That is a defensible default for an optional value and a serious defect for a required one: a typo in a variable name renders an empty region of the page rather than failing. The strict variants invert that, treating an undefined variable as an error, and the practical rule is to use the strict form for anything that must exist and the default form only for values that are genuinely optional.</p> <h4>Where a conditional belongs</h4> <p>A conditional that computes something belongs in a controller or a view model, and the test is whether the reader could state the condition's meaning in one sentence without reading the body. If the body contains arithmetic, a call to a service, or a query, the decision has leaked into the template and the template can no longer be tested without a database.</p>

The three forms, and when each is right

There are three conditional constructs and they differ in what happens when the condition is false. The first emits nothing at all. The second emits nothing but an alternative. The third emits nothing but records the branch as taken and undoes everything emitted inside it, which is a completely different mechanism and is the one people are surprised by.

The first is the one to reach for by default. A condition that renders one thing or nothing is a condition whose false branch is the empty string, and writing it that way means there is no alternative to read and no alternative to keep in sync. The second form earns its place when both branches are substantial, and the common mistake is using it for a two-line body where the reader has to compare two texts to see how different they are.

Strictness, and the bug it catches

By default a condition on an undefined variable is false, silently. That is a defensible default for an optional value and a serious defect for a required one: a typo in a variable name renders an empty region of the page rather than failing. The strict variants invert that, treating an undefined variable as an error, and the practical rule is to use the strict form for anything that must exist and the default form only for values that are genuinely optional.

Where a conditional belongs

A conditional that computes something belongs in a controller or a view model, and the test is whether the reader could state the condition's meaning in one sentence without reading the body. If the body contains arithmetic, a call to a service, or a query, the decision has leaked into the template and the template can no longer be tested without a database.

@if ($order->isPaid())
    <p>Paid</p>
@elseif ($order->isPending())
    <p>Pending</p>
@else
    <p>Unpaid</p>
@endif

@unless ($order->isCancelled())
    <p>Active order</p>
@endunless

@isset($order->note) Has a note @endisset
@empty($order->note)  No note  @endempty

Every block directive requires its terminator, including the shorthand forms written on one line. Compiled, each maps to the equivalent PHP keyword in colon-suffixed form, for example @if (...):.

Style. Prefer the long form for anything read by a human reviewer. The Blade shorthand @empty in particular reads ambiguously beside PHP’s own empty() function.


{{-- THE THREE FORMS. --}}

{{-- (1) IF: emits nothing when false. THE DEFAULT, because most
     optional regions are empty rather than alternative. --}}
@if ($user->hasAvatar())
    <img src="{{ $user->avatar_url }}" alt="">
@endif

{{-- (2) IF / ELSE: emits the alternative. Worth it when both
     branches are substantial AND visibly different, so the reader
     can tell at a glance which is which. --}}
@if ($order->isPaid())
    <x-order-badge tone="success">{{ __('orders.paid') }}</x-order-badge>
@else
    <x-order-badge tone="warning">{{ __('orders.unpaid') }}</x-order-badge>
@endif

{{-- (3) UNLESS: if false. Provided for completeness; read
     "unless" as "if not" and it is never clearer than @if(!...). --}}
@unless ($user->isAdmin())
    ...
@endunless

{{-- ELSEIF, which chains. Note it is elseif and NOT else if: the
     spaced form does not compile, which is a deliberate guard
     against a nesting mistake. --}}
@if ($order->isRefunded())
    <x-refund-notice />
@elseif ($order->isPaid())
    <x-paid-notice />
@elseif ($order->isPending())
    <x-pending-notice />
@else
    <x-draft-notice />
@endif

{{-- THE STRICT VARIANTS, and why the default is a defect for a
     required value. --}}
@if ($orders->count() > 0)      {{-- a typo in $orderS renders an
                                     empty page, silently, rather
                                     than failing --}}
@endif

@is ($order->status)           {{-- STRICT: undefined or wrong
                                     type is an error. Use for
                                     anything that must exist. --}}
    <x-status-badge :status="$order->status" />
@endis

@empty ($order->lines)          {{-- STRICT empty: undefined is an
                                      error, an empty array is the
                                      empty branch --}}
    <tr><td colspan="2">{{ __('orders.no_lines') }}</td></tr>
@endempty

{{-- WHERE A CONDITIONAL DOES NOT BELONG, and the test that finds
     it: if the BODY computes something. --}}
@if ($invoice->total->isGreaterThan($threshold))
    {{-- ...so now this template calls a service, formats a
         currency, and the "logic-free template" rule is gone. The
         decision belongs upstream. --}}
    <p>{{ $invoice->total->format() }}</p>
@endif

{{-- RIGHT: the controller computes a DISPLAY value, and the
     template branches on it. Both are testable, and the test for
     the template is a boolean. --}}
// app/Http/Controllers/InvoiceController.php
return view('invoices.show', [
    'invoice' => $invoice,
    'needsAttention' => $invoice->total->isGreaterThan($threshold),
]);


{{-- THE THREE FORMS. --}}

{{-- (1) IF: emits nothing when false. THE DEFAULT, because most
     optional regions are empty rather than alternative. --}}
@if ($user->hasAvatar())
    <img src="{{ $user->avatar_url }}" alt="">
@endif

{{-- (2) IF / ELSE: emits the alternative. Worth it when both
     branches are substantial AND visibly different, so the reader
     can tell at a glance which is which. --}}
@if ($order->isPaid())
    <x-order-badge tone="success">{{ __('orders.paid') }}</x-order-badge>
@else
    <x-order-badge tone="warning">{{ __('orders.unpaid') }}</x-order-badge>
@endif

{{-- (3) UNLESS: if false. Provided for completeness; read
     "unless" as "if not" and it is never clearer than @if(!...). --}}
@unless ($user->isAdmin())
    ...
@endunless

     spaced form does not compile, which is a deliberate guard
     against a nesting mistake. --}}
@if ($order->isRefunded())
    <x-refund-notice />
@elseif ($order->isPaid())
    <x-paid-notice />
@elseif ($order->isPending())
    <x-pending-notice />
@else
    <x-draft-notice />
@endif

{{-- THE STRICT VARIANTS, and why the default is a defect for a
     required value. --}}

                                     empty page, silently, rather
                                     than failing --}}

                                     type is an error. Use for

                                      error, an empty array is the
                                      empty branch --}}
    <tr><td colspan="2">{{ __('orders.no_lines') }}</td></tr>

     it: if the BODY computes something. --}}

         currency, and the "logic-free template" rule is gone. The
         decision belongs upstream. --}}

@endif

{{-- RIGHT: the controller computes a DISPLAY value, and the
     template branches on it. Both are testable, and the test for
     the template is a boolean. --}}
// app/Http/Controllers/InvoiceController.php
return view('invoices.show', [
    'invoice' => $invoice,
    'needsAttention' => $invoice->total->isGreaterThan($threshold),
]);

3.2 Loop directives

Covers foreach, forelse, for, while and the break and continue directives, with their compiled equivalents.

<h4>One directive, and the four variables it gives you</h4> <p>The loop directive is a single construct with an optional second clause that provides an index. It exposes the collection and the index, and it exposes two more variables that are the source of most template bugs: the loop counter and the iteration count. Those two make it possible to do the things templates legitimately do — mark the last row, alternate a class, show "showing 1 to 25 of 300" — without computing anything, and they are the reason the directive has the shape it has.</p> <p>The collection may be an array or any object with an <code>IteratorAggregate</code>, which means an Eloquent builder, a paginator and a collection all work without being converted. The iterator form, with a key and a value, is for an associative array or a query result keyed by identifier. Using it on a list produces sequential integer keys, which is harmless and occasionally surprising when the loop body assumes the key is meaningful.</p> <h4>The empty case, and why omitting it is a defect</h4> <p>A loop over an empty collection emits nothing, which means an empty table body with headers and no rows, or a blank sidebar with no explanation. The paired construct that supplies an alternative for the empty case exists precisely for this, and a table row loop without one is an incomplete table rather than a stylistic choice. The argument for omitting it — that the empty case is rare — does not hold for a list backed by a database query, where it happens whenever a filter matches nothing, which is to say on every filter page a user visits.</p> <h4>The loop variable's scope</h4> <p>The loop variable is scoped to the loop body, so it is not visible after the loop ends, and a nested loop's variable is restored afterwards. That is the same scoping as a <code>foreach</code> in PHP and it is worth relying on: a template that reads a loop variable after the loop is reading a name that no longer exists, and under a strict conditional that is an error rather than a stale value.</p>

One directive, and the four variables it gives you

The loop directive is a single construct with an optional second clause that provides an index. It exposes the collection and the index, and it exposes two more variables that are the source of most template bugs: the loop counter and the iteration count. Those two make it possible to do the things templates legitimately do — mark the last row, alternate a class, show "showing 1 to 25 of 300" — without computing anything, and they are the reason the directive has the shape it has.

The collection may be an array or any object with an IteratorAggregate, which means an Eloquent builder, a paginator and a collection all work without being converted. The iterator form, with a key and a value, is for an associative array or a query result keyed by identifier. Using it on a list produces sequential integer keys, which is harmless and occasionally surprising when the loop body assumes the key is meaningful.

The empty case, and why omitting it is a defect

A loop over an empty collection emits nothing, which means an empty table body with headers and no rows, or a blank sidebar with no explanation. The paired construct that supplies an alternative for the empty case exists precisely for this, and a table row loop without one is an incomplete table rather than a stylistic choice. The argument for omitting it — that the empty case is rare — does not hold for a list backed by a database query, where it happens whenever a filter matches nothing, which is to say on every filter page a user visits.

The loop variable's scope

The loop variable is scoped to the loop body, so it is not visible after the loop ends, and a nested loop's variable is restored afterwards. That is the same scoping as a foreach in PHP and it is worth relying on: a template that reads a loop variable after the loop is reading a name that no longer exists, and under a strict conditional that is an error rather than a stale value.

@foreach ($items as $item)
    <li>{{ $item->name }}</li>
@endforeach

// Render the empty state when the collection is empty.
@forelse ($items as $item)
    <li>{{ $item->name }}</li>
@empty
    <li>Nothing here yet.</li>
@endforelse

@for ($i = 0; $i < $pages; $i++)
    <span>{{ $i + 1 }}</span>
@endfor

@forelse is the idiomatic way to pair a list with an empty state; it avoids the nested @if (count(...)) block that obscures intent. @while is available but has no natural terminator in a template and is almost never the right choice for rendering.


{{-- THE FOUR VARIABLES, and the two that are the reason the
     directive has the shape it does. --}}
@foreach ($users as $user)
    {{-- $user       the value
         $loop->index    0-based position
         $loop->iteration 1-based position
         $loop->first     true on the first pass
         $loop->last      true on the final pass
         $loop->count     total, available on EVERY pass --}}
@endforeach

{{-- THE REASON THE LOOP METADATA EXISTS: the table that has to be
     right at both ends. --}}
<tbody>
    @forelse ($invoices as $invoice)
        <tr class="{{ $loop->first ? '' : 'border-t' }}
                   {{ $loop->last ? 'font-bold' : '' }}">
            <td>{{ $invoice->number }}</td>
            <td>{{ $loop->iteration }} of {{ $loop->count }}</td>
        </tr>
    @empty
        <tr><td colspan="2">{{ __('invoices.none') }}</td></tr>
    @endforelse
</tbody>

{{-- THE ITERATOR FORM, for an associative array or a keyed
     result. On a LIST the key is a sequential integer, which is
     harmless and occasionally misleading: --}}
@foreach ($prices as $sku => $price)
    <tr><td>{{ $sku }}</td><td>{{ $price }}</td></tr>
@endforeach

@foreach ($items as $item)
    {{-- $item is the value; the key is 0, 1, 2 ... and says
         nothing about $item. --}}
@endforeach

{{-- WHAT ITERATES, and all three work with no conversion: --}}
@foreach ($array as $x)          @endforeach
@foreach ($collection as $x)     @endforeach
@foreach ($paginator as $x)      @endforeach
@foreach ($builder as $x)        {{-- an Eloquent builder lazily
                                      resolves; iterating it runs
                                      ONE query for the whole
                                      loop, not one per row --}}
@endforeach

{{-- THE EMPTY CASE, and why omitting it on a query-backed list is
     a defect rather than a style: a filter matching nothing is the
     normal state of a filter page. --}}
@forelse ($results as $result)
    <x-result-row :result="$result" />
@empty
    <x-empty-state
        :title="__('search.no_results')"
        :action="route('search')" />
@endforelse

{{-- SCOPE, which is what makes a nested loop safe: --}}
@foreach ($orders as $order)
    @foreach ($order->lines as $line)
        {{ $line->description }}   {{-- $line --}}
    @endforeach
    {{ $order->number }}          {{-- $order is RESTORED after
                                       the inner loop: it is the
                                       same variable name, scoped
                                       the same way --}}
@endforeach

{{-- AND WHAT READING A LOOP VARIABLE AFTERWARDS DOES: --}}
@foreach ($users as $user) @endforeach
{{ $user->name }}
{{-- outside the loop, $user does not exist. Read with a strict
     conditional (@is) this is an error; read with the default
     conditional it renders as empty, which is how a stale value
     from an OUTER loop survives into a page. --}}


{{-- THE FOUR VARIABLES, and the two that are the reason the
     directive has the shape it does. --}}
@foreach ($users as $user)
    {{-- $user       the value
         $loop->index    0-based position
         $loop->iteration 1-based position
         $loop->first     true on the first pass

         $loop->count     total, available on EVERY pass --}}

        </tr>
    @empty

    @endforelse

{{-- THE ITERATOR FORM, for an associative array or a keyed

     conditional it renders as empty, which is how a stale value
     from an OUTER loop survives into a page. --}}

3.3 Switch, break and continue

Documents the switch directive with its case, break, default and endswitch structure, and the loop-level break and continue directives.

<h4>A switch in a template, and what it is for</h4> <p>The switch construct maps a value onto one of several mutually exclusive outputs, which is exactly the situation a chain of <code>elseif</code> conditions handles badly: with five statuses, an <code>elseif</code> chain makes the reader compare five conditions to see which applies, and adding a status means finding the right place in the chain and hoping nothing depended on the ordering. A switch states the mapping, and the compiler evaluates the value once.</p> <p>The reason it is used less often than it deserves is that the common version — a chain of <code>elseif</code> comparing the same variable — is what people reach for first, and it works. The switch is worth reaching for when there are three or more branches over one value, because at that point the chain has stopped being readable and the switch has not.</p> <h4>Break, continue, and what they mean in a template</h4> <p>Both are available and both are almost always the wrong answer. A <code>break</code> inside a switch is required and has no other use worth having, because a template with a loop inside a switch that breaks out of the switch on a condition is expressing control flow that belongs in the controller. A <code>continue</code> inside a loop is occasionally legitimate for skipping a row that renders nothing — but the same result comes from an <code>if</code> around the row's contents, which does not need a language construct to express.</p> <h4>The strict form, and the default it uses</h4> <p>Like the conditional, the switch has a strict variant that rejects an unmatched value instead of falling through to the default. For a status field whose values come from a database column or an enum, the strict form is the right default: a new status added to the backend and not to the template then renders an error rather than an empty cell, which is the difference between a bug report and a silent data display error.</p>

A switch in a template, and what it is for

The switch construct maps a value onto one of several mutually exclusive outputs, which is exactly the situation a chain of elseif conditions handles badly: with five statuses, an elseif chain makes the reader compare five conditions to see which applies, and adding a status means finding the right place in the chain and hoping nothing depended on the ordering. A switch states the mapping, and the compiler evaluates the value once.

The reason it is used less often than it deserves is that the common version — a chain of elseif comparing the same variable — is what people reach for first, and it works. The switch is worth reaching for when there are three or more branches over one value, because at that point the chain has stopped being readable and the switch has not.

Break, continue, and what they mean in a template

Both are available and both are almost always the wrong answer. A break inside a switch is required and has no other use worth having, because a template with a loop inside a switch that breaks out of the switch on a condition is expressing control flow that belongs in the controller. A continue inside a loop is occasionally legitimate for skipping a row that renders nothing — but the same result comes from an if around the row's contents, which does not need a language construct to express.

The strict form, and the default it uses

Like the conditional, the switch has a strict variant that rejects an unmatched value instead of falling through to the default. For a status field whose values come from a database column or an enum, the strict form is the right default: a new status added to the backend and not to the template then renders an error rather than an empty cell, which is the difference between a bug report and a silent data display error.

@switch ($order->status)
    @case OrderStatus::Paid:
        <strong>Paid</strong>
        @break

    @case OrderStatus::Pending:
        <strong>Pending</strong>
        @break

    @default:
        <strong>Unknown</strong>
@endswitch

@break and @continue operate on the innermost enclosing loop only. Nested loops require an outerbreak or outercontinue directive for the outer level, which is rarely worth the readability cost; prefer filtering the collection itself.


{{-- THE SWITCH, which beats an elseif chain at three or more
     branches over one value: the mapping is stated, the value is
     evaluated ONCE, and adding a status does not mean finding the
     right place in a chain. --}}
@switch($order->status)
    @case('pending')
        <x-order-badge tone="info">{{ __('orders.pending') }}</x-order-badge>
        @break

    @case('paid')
        <x-order-badge tone="success">{{ __('orders.paid') }}</x-order-badge>
        @break

    @case('shipped')
        <x-order-badge tone="primary">{{ __('orders.shipped') }}</x-order-badge>
        @break

    @default
        <x-order-badge tone="muted">{{ __('orders.unknown') }}</x-order-badge>
@endswitch

{{-- COMPARED WITH THE CHAIN, which is what most people write: --}}
@if ($order->status === 'pending')
    ...
@elseif ($order->status === 'paid')
    ...
@elseif ($order->status === 'shipped')
    ...
@else
    ...
@endif
{{-- Five branches means the reader compares five conditions to
     see which applies, and a new status has to go in the right
     place. --}}

{{-- THE STRICT FORM, and why it is the right default for a value
     that comes from a database column or an enum: a new status
     added to the backend and not to the template renders an
     ERROR rather than an empty cell. --}}
@switch($order->status)
    @case(OrderStatus::Pending) <x-badge tone="info" />     @break
    @case(OrderStatus::Paid)    <x-badge tone="success" />  @break
    @case(OrderStatus::Shipped) <x-badge tone="primary" />  @break
    @endswitch
{{-- an unmatched status is an error. The default form would
     render an empty cell, which is a data display bug rather than
     a bug report. --}}

{{-- BREAK IS REQUIRED in every branch except the last, and
     omitting it FALLS THROUGH to the next branch rather than
     exiting. That is PHP's behaviour and Blade preserves it. --}}
@switch($n)
    @case(1)
        one
        {{-- no @break: falls through to 2, so this also renders
             "two". This is the one silent mistake the construct
             permits. --}}
    @case(2)
        two
        @break
    @endswitch

{{-- CONTINUE, and why it is almost always the wrong answer: the
     same result comes from wrapping the contents, which needs no
     language construct. --}}
@foreach ($invoices as $invoice)
    @continue($invoice->isDraft())      {{-- legal, and the clearer
                                              form is below --}}
    <tr>{{ $invoice->number }}</tr>
@endforeach

@foreach ($invoices as $invoice)
    @unless ($invoice->isDraft())
        <tr>{{ $invoice->number }}</tr>
    @endunless
@endforeach

{{-- WHERE BREAK AND CONTINUE ACTUALLY BELONG: nowhere. A loop
     inside a switch with a break that exits the switch is
     expressing control flow that belongs in the controller. The
     signal is that you now need to know the order of the branches
     to know what renders. --}}


{{-- THE SWITCH, which beats an elseif chain at three or more
     branches over one value: the mapping is stated, the value is
     evaluated ONCE, and adding a status does not mean finding the
     right place in a chain. --}}
@switch($order->status)
    @case('pending')
        <x-order-badge tone="info">{{ __('orders.pending') }}</x-order-badge>
        @break

    @case('paid')
        <x-order-badge tone="success">{{ __('orders.paid') }}</x-order-badge>
        @break

    @case('shipped')
        <x-order-badge tone="primary">{{ __('orders.shipped') }}</x-order-badge>
        @break

    @default
        <x-order-badge tone="muted">{{ __('orders.unknown') }}</x-order-badge>
@endswitch

{{-- COMPARED WITH THE CHAIN, which is what most people write: --}}
@if ($order->status === 'pending')
    ...
@elseif ($order->status === 'paid')
    ...
@elseif ($order->status === 'shipped')
    ...
@else
    ...
@endif
{{-- Five branches means the reader compares five conditions to
     see which applies, and a new status has to go in the right
     place. --}}

{{-- THE STRICT FORM, and why it is the right default for a value
     that comes from a database column or an enum: a new status
     added to the backend and not to the template renders an
     ERROR rather than an empty cell. --}}
@switch($order->status)
    @case(OrderStatus::Pending) <x-badge tone="info" />     @break
    @case(OrderStatus::Paid)    <x-badge tone="success" />  @break
    @case(OrderStatus::Shipped) <x-badge tone="primary" />  @break
    @endswitch
{{-- an unmatched status is an error. The default form would
     render an empty cell, which is a data display bug rather than
     a bug report. --}}

{{-- BREAK IS REQUIRED in every branch except the last, and
     omitting it FALLS THROUGH to the next branch rather than
     exiting. That is PHP's behaviour and Blade preserves it. --}}
@switch($n)
    @case(1)
        one
        {{-- no @break: falls through to 2, so this also renders
             "two". This is the one silent mistake the construct
             permits. --}}
    @case(2)
        two
        @break
    @endswitch

{{-- CONTINUE, and why it is almost always the wrong answer: the
     same result comes from wrapping the contents, which needs no
     language construct. --}}
@foreach ($invoices as $invoice)
    @continue($invoice->isDraft())      {{-- legal, and the clearer
                                              form is below --}}
    <tr>{{ $invoice->number }}</tr>
@endforeach

@foreach ($invoices as $invoice)
    @unless ($invoice->isDraft())
        <tr>{{ $invoice->number }}</tr>
    @endunless
@endforeach

{{-- WHERE BREAK AND CONTINUE ACTUALLY BELONG: nowhere. A loop
     inside a switch with a break that exits the switch is
     expressing control flow that belongs in the controller. The
     signal is that you now need to know the order of the branches
     to know what renders. --}}

3.4 The @php directive

Explains when the php directive is acceptable and why it is usually a design signal rather than a solution.

@php opens a raw PHP block inside the template, used primarily for developer annotation that should not reach the browser:

<h4>A block of PHP, and when it is the answer</h4> <p>The block directive runs PHP inline. It exists for computations that would be unreadable written as expressions — a variable defined once and used three times, a small transformation of a value, a lookup that keeps a loop readable. That is the legitimate use, and it is narrower than it looks.</p> <p>The illegitimate use is moving a decision into the template. An <code>if</code> inside a PHP block is a conditional written in a less readable syntax, and it is the beginning of a slide into a controller inside a view: the next step is a database call, then a service call, then a template that cannot be rendered without a full application context. The test is whether the block computes something or decides something. Computing is fine; deciding belongs upstream.</p> <h4>Single-line form, and why the multiline one is safer</h4> <p>There is a single-line variant used for a one-off assignment. It is convenient and it encourages the pattern it was not designed for, because the natural thing to add to a single-line block is another statement, and a second statement in a single-line block does not fit — so the code is rewritten into the multi-line form, which is where the two-statement version lives.</p> <h4>What the block does not scope</h4> <p>Variables defined in the block are available to the rest of the template, in both directions: a block near the top can be read by a partial included below it. That is convenient and it is a coupling — a partial that reads a variable defined by an unrelated part of the template will break when it is included somewhere else. A partial should receive everything it needs as data, and a variable set three hundred lines above it is not something it needs.</p>

A block of PHP, and when it is the answer

The block directive runs PHP inline. It exists for computations that would be unreadable written as expressions — a variable defined once and used three times, a small transformation of a value, a lookup that keeps a loop readable. That is the legitimate use, and it is narrower than it looks.

The illegitimate use is moving a decision into the template. An if inside a PHP block is a conditional written in a less readable syntax, and it is the beginning of a slide into a controller inside a view: the next step is a database call, then a service call, then a template that cannot be rendered without a full application context. The test is whether the block computes something or decides something. Computing is fine; deciding belongs upstream.

Single-line form, and why the multiline one is safer

There is a single-line variant used for a one-off assignment. It is convenient and it encourages the pattern it was not designed for, because the natural thing to add to a single-line block is another statement, and a second statement in a single-line block does not fit — so the code is rewritten into the multi-line form, which is where the two-statement version lives.

What the block does not scope

Variables defined in the block are available to the rest of the template, in both directions: a block near the top can be read by a partial included below it. That is convenient and it is a coupling — a partial that reads a variable defined by an unrelated part of the template will break when it is included somewhere else. A partial should receive everything it needs as data, and a variable set three hundred lines above it is not something it needs.

@php
    // TEMP: rounding is wrong for zero-decimal currencies.
    $total = round($subtotal * 1.2);
@endphp
<p>{{ $total }}</p>

Using @php for computation, database queries or branching decisions inverts the layering. A template that computes a value should have that value computed by the controller or a view model and passed in; a template that queries is untestable without a full application context. Reserve @php for developer annotation.

Anti-pattern. A @php block containing a query is a design defect. Move it onto an Eloquent relation, eager-load it, and pass the hydrated result to the view.


{{-- THE LEGITIMATE USE: a computation that keeps a loop readable.
     Computing is fine. --}}
@php
    // An intermediate value, named once and used three times. As
    // an expression this would be unfollowable.
    $threshold = config('billing.review_threshold');
    $total     = $order->lines->sum('total');
    $overdue   = $order->due_at?->isPast() ?? false;
@endphp

@if ($total > $threshold || $overdue)
    <x-review-banner :total="$total" :overdue="$overdue" />
@endif

{{-- ANOTHER: a transformation that would obscure the markup. --}}
@php
    // Group the lines by product category. Ten lines of array
    // manipulation in a template expression would be unreadable;
    // here it is out of the way and the markup below is clear.
    $byCategory = $order->lines->groupBy(fn ($l) => $l->product->category);
@endphp

@foreach ($byCategory as $category => $lines)
    <section>
        <h3>{{ $category }}</h3>
        @foreach ($lines as $line)
            <p>{{ $line->description }}</p>
        @endforeach
    </section>
@endforeach

{{-- THE SINGLE-LINE FORM, and why the multi-line one is safer:
     the natural thing to add to a single-line block is a second
     statement, and a second statement does not fit. --}}
@php($total = $order->lines->sum('total'))

{{-- THE ILLEGITIMATE USE, which begins as a convenience and ends
     as a controller inside a view. --}}
@php
    if ($user->isAdmin()) {
        $permissions = $user->permissions()->get();   // step 2
    }
    // step 3: a service call
    // step 4: a query
    // step 5: the template cannot be rendered without an
    //         application context, so it cannot be snapshot-tested
    //         and cannot be cached
@endphp

{{-- RIGHT: the controller computes it, and the template reads a
     value. Both are testable; the template's test is a boolean. --}}
// app/Http/Controllers/OrderController.php
return view('orders.show', [
    'order'       => $order,
    'needsReview' => $total > $threshold || $overdue,
    'byCategory'  => $byCategory,
]);

{{-- WHAT THE BLOCK DOES NOT SCOPE, and this is the trap. Variables
     are visible to the REST OF THE TEMPLATE, including to a partial
     included later: --}}
@php($currency = 'GBP')

@include('partials.invoice-footer')
{{-- and partials/invoice-footer.blade.php reads $currency. It
     works. It also breaks the moment that partial is included from
     somewhere that did not define it — which is the coupling the
     component system exists to remove. --}}

{{-- THE FIX: pass the data in, do not rely on it being there. --}}
<x-invoice-footer :currency="$currency" />
{{-- or, for an include: @include('partials.invoice-footer',
       ['currency' => $currency])
     ...which is explicit, and fails loudly at compile time
     rather than rendering an empty currency symbol. --}}


{{-- THE LEGITIMATE USE: a computation that keeps a loop readable.
     Computing is fine. --}}
@php
    // An intermediate value, named once and used three times. As
    // an expression this would be unfollowable.
    $threshold = config('billing.review_threshold');
    $total     = $order->lines->sum('total');
    $overdue   = $order->due_at?->isPast() ?? false;
@endphp

@if ($total > $threshold || $overdue)
    <x-review-banner :total="$total" :overdue="$overdue" />
@endif

{{-- ANOTHER: a transformation that would obscure the markup. --}}
@php
    // Group the lines by product category. Ten lines of array
    // manipulation in a template expression would be unreadable;
    // here it is out of the way and the markup below is clear.
    $byCategory = $order->lines->groupBy(fn ($l) => $l->product->category);
@endphp

@foreach ($byCategory as $category => $lines)
    <section>
        <h3>{{ $category }}</h3>
        @foreach ($lines as $line)
            <p>{{ $line->description }}</p>
        @endforeach
    </section>
@endforeach

{{-- THE SINGLE-LINE FORM, and why the multi-line one is safer:
     the natural thing to add to a single-line block is a second
     statement, and a second statement does not fit. --}}
@php($total = $order->lines->sum('total'))

{{-- THE ILLEGITIMATE USE, which begins as a convenience and ends
     as a controller inside a view. --}}
@php
    if ($user->isAdmin()) {
        $permissions = $user->permissions()->get();   // step 2
    }
    // step 3: a service call
    // step 4: a query
    // step 5: the template cannot be rendered without an
    //         application context, so it cannot be snapshot-tested
    //         and cannot be cached
@endphp

{{-- RIGHT: the controller computes it, and the template reads a
     value. Both are testable; the template's test is a boolean. --}}
// app/Http/Controllers/OrderController.php
return view('orders.show', [
    'order'       => $order,
    'needsReview' => $total > $threshold || $overdue,
    'byCategory'  => $byCategory,
]);

{{-- WHAT THE BLOCK DOES NOT SCOPE, and this is the trap. Variables
     are visible to the REST OF THE TEMPLATE, including to a partial
     included later: --}}
@php($currency = 'GBP')

@include('partials.invoice-footer')
{{-- and partials/invoice-footer.blade.php reads $currency. It
     works. It also breaks the moment that partial is included from
     somewhere that did not define it — which is the coupling the
     component system exists to remove. --}}

{{-- THE FIX: pass the data in, do not rely on it being there. --}}
<x-invoice-footer :currency="$currency" />
{{-- or, for an include: @include('partials.invoice-footer',
       ['currency' => $currency])
     ...which is explicit, and fails loudly at compile time
     rather than rendering an empty currency symbol. --}}

3.5 Loop metadata

Documents the loop variable available inside foreach directives, including the first, last, index, count and iteration accessors.

<h4>The loop variable, and what it is for</h4> <p>Inside a loop the framework exposes a small object describing the iteration: the index, the iteration number, the count, and the first and last flags. Its purpose is narrow — it removes the need to compute a row position inside the markup — and it exists because that computation is otherwise repeated in every template that needs it, usually by hand, and a hand-computed position is off by one somewhere.</p> <p>The count is available on every pass, not only the last, which surprises people who expect it to require the last flag. It costs an array count once per loop and it makes <code>showing 1 to 25 of 300</code> a two-token expression rather than a controller-provided value.</p> <h4>Alternating classes, and the deeper reason it is a smell</h4> <p>The classic use is a striped table row. It works, and it is worth recognising as a symptom: striping is presentation that a stylesheet can express with <code>:nth-child</code>, and expressing it in the template puts a styling decision in a file that has to be changed to restyle the table. The same reasoning applies to a "last row has a border" rule and to a "first row is a header" rule. All three belong in the stylesheet, where a change is one rule rather than a template edit.</p> <p>There are two genuinely template-level uses left. A position-dependent label — "Row 3 of 12" — is display, so it belongs here. And an alternating class where the classes carry meaning rather than decoration, such as a density pattern in a data grid, is defensible because the alternation is part of the visual grammar the component defines.</p>

The loop variable, and what it is for

Inside a loop the framework exposes a small object describing the iteration: the index, the iteration number, the count, and the first and last flags. Its purpose is narrow — it removes the need to compute a row position inside the markup — and it exists because that computation is otherwise repeated in every template that needs it, usually by hand, and a hand-computed position is off by one somewhere.

The count is available on every pass, not only the last, which surprises people who expect it to require the last flag. It costs an array count once per loop and it makes showing 1 to 25 of 300 a two-token expression rather than a controller-provided value.

Alternating classes, and the deeper reason it is a smell

The classic use is a striped table row. It works, and it is worth recognising as a symptom: striping is presentation that a stylesheet can express with :nth-child, and expressing it in the template puts a styling decision in a file that has to be changed to restyle the table. The same reasoning applies to a "last row has a border" rule and to a "first row is a header" rule. All three belong in the stylesheet, where a change is one rule rather than a template edit.

There are two genuinely template-level uses left. A position-dependent label — "Row 3 of 12" — is display, so it belongs here. And an alternating class where the classes carry meaning rather than decoration, such as a density pattern in a data grid, is defensible because the alternation is part of the visual grammar the component defines.

@foreach ($orders as $order)
    @if ($loop->first) <tr>@endif
        <td>{{ $loop->iteration }}</td>
        <td>{{ $order->reference }}</td>
    @if ($loop->last) </tr>@endif
@endforeach

The $loop variable is scoped to the enclosing directive only and is not available inside a nested component unless explicitly passed. Its useful members are index (zero-based), iteration (one-based), first, last, even, odd and count. Inside a nested loop, the parent value is available as $loop->parent.

Prefer CSS. Alternating row colours and separators should be expressed in stylesheet rules applied with :nth-child(), not by emitting conditional classes from $loop->even. The CSS approach survives a reordering of the data.


{{-- THE FULL SET, and $loop->count is available on EVERY pass,
     which surprises people who assume it needs $loop->last. --}}
@foreach ($orders as $order)
    <tr>
        <td>{{ $loop->iteration }} of {{ $loop->count }}</td>
        <td>{{ $order->number }}</td>
    </tr>
@endforeach

{{-- FIRST AND LAST, the two that get used: --}}
<nav>
    @foreach ($breadcrumbs as $crumb)
        @if (! $loop->first)
            <span aria-hidden="true">/</span>   {{-- no separator
                                                     before the
                                                     first --}}
        @endif
        @if ($loop->last)
            <span aria-current="page">{{ $crumb->label }}</span>
        @else
            <a href="{{ $crumb->url }}">{{ $crumb->label }}</a>
        @endif
    @endforeach
</nav>

{{-- THE ALTERNATING ROW, which works and is a SYMPTOM. Striping
     is presentation a stylesheet can express: --}}
@foreach ($invoices as $invoice)
    <tr class="{{ $loop->even ? 'odd' : 'even' }}">
        <td>{{ $invoice->number }}</td>
    </tr>
@endforeach

{{-- WHICH IS THIS, AND IT IS THE SAME THING WITH NO TEMPLATE: --}}
/* resources/css/app.css */
.invoices tbody tr:nth-child(odd) { background: var(--surface-alt); }
.invoices tbody tr:first-child    { border-top: 2px solid var(--rule); }
.invoices tbody tr:last-child     { border-bottom: 1px solid var(--rule); }

{{-- A restyle is now one rule, rather than a template edit and a
     full test run. --}}

{{-- THE TWO USES THAT ARE GENUINELY THE TEMPLATE'S: --}}

{{-- (1) A POSITION-DEPENDENT LABEL. Display, not presentation. --}}
<p>{{ __('results.showing', [
    'from' => $loop->iteration,
    'to'   => $loop->count,
]) }}</p>

{{-- (2) AN ALTERNATION THAT CARRIES MEANING rather than decoration
     — a data grid whose density pattern is part of the component's
     visual grammar. --}}
@foreach ($cells as $cell)
    <td class="{{ $loop->even ? 'cell-dense' : 'cell-roomy' }}"
        title="{{ $cell->tooltip() }}">
        {{ $cell->value }}
    </td>
@endforeach

{{-- AND THE ONE THAT IS ACTUALLY WRONG, because it hand-computes
     what the loop variable provides: --}}
@php $i = 1; @endphp
@foreach ($invoices as $invoice)
    <tr class="{{ $i % 2 ? 'odd' : 'even' }}">
        <td>{{ $i++ }} of {{ count($invoices) }}</td>
    </tr>
@endforeach
{{-- $i is defined OUTSIDE the loop, so it survives it — which is
     exactly why a hand-rolled counter that forgot to reset leaks
     into the next loop on the page. $loop->index cannot: it is
     scoped to the iteration. --}}


{{-- THE FULL SET, and $loop->count is available on EVERY pass,
     which surprises people who assume it needs $loop->last. --}}

        <td>{{ $order->number }}</td>
    </tr>
@endforeach
