3.6 Escaping Blade with @verbatim

Introduces the verbatim directive for emitting literal Blade syntax, and the json directive for raw JSON expressions.

<h4>What the verbatim directive does, and why it exists</h4> <p>Blade compiles directives by pattern, so a template that documents the template language — a page that lists the directives, an editor component with syntax highlighting, a documentation page rendered through the same view layer as the application — will have its own examples compiled. The verbatim directive makes the compiler treat its contents as literal text, which is the only mechanism that achieves this.</p> <p>The obvious alternative is to not put template syntax in a template, and that is usually right. But it is not always possible: a documentation site renders its own examples, and an admin screen that previews a customer-supplied template has to render template syntax as text. In both cases the content arrives at render time and cannot be pre-escaped in the source.</p> <h4>Where it must not be used</h4> <p>The verbatim directive is not an escaping mechanism for content, and treating it as one is a vulnerability. It suppresses compilation, so <code>{{ $name }}</code> inside it renders as those literal characters — but it does not escape anything, so <code><script></code> inside it is still a script tag. The two concerns are independent, and a template that uses a verbatim block around untrusted content is executing it.</p> <p>The correct mechanism for rendering template syntax as text from untrusted input is to escape it and not use a verbatim directive at all: the raw form with a sanitiser, or a dedicated preview component that writes to a temporary file and renders that. Both produce escaped output; the verbatim directive produces unescaped output that happens not to compile.</p> <h4>The nested case</h4> <p>A verbatim block cannot contain another verbatim block, because the closing marker is matched by a literal string and the inner one ends the outer. Showing a template that demonstrates the verbatim directive therefore needs the closing marker itself to be escaped or built, and this is worth knowing before designing a page whose subject is the template language.</p>

What the verbatim directive does, and why it exists

Blade compiles directives by pattern, so a template that documents the template language — a page that lists the directives, an editor component with syntax highlighting, a documentation page rendered through the same view layer as the application — will have its own examples compiled. The verbatim directive makes the compiler treat its contents as literal text, which is the only mechanism that achieves this.

The obvious alternative is to not put template syntax in a template, and that is usually right. But it is not always possible: a documentation site renders its own examples, and an admin screen that previews a customer-supplied template has to render template syntax as text. In both cases the content arrives at render time and cannot be pre-escaped in the source.

Where it must not be used

The verbatim directive is not an escaping mechanism for content, and treating it as one is a vulnerability. It suppresses compilation, so {{ $name }} inside it renders as those literal characters — but it does not escape anything, so <script> inside it is still a script tag. The two concerns are independent, and a template that uses a verbatim block around untrusted content is executing it.

The correct mechanism for rendering template syntax as text from untrusted input is to escape it and not use a verbatim directive at all: the raw form with a sanitiser, or a dedicated preview component that writes to a temporary file and renders that. Both produce escaped output; the verbatim directive produces unescaped output that happens not to compile.

The nested case

A verbatim block cannot contain another verbatim block, because the closing marker is matched by a literal string and the inner one ends the outer. Showing a template that demonstrates the verbatim directive therefore needs the closing marker itself to be escaped or built, and this is worth knowing before designing a page whose subject is the template language.

@verbatim
    <div x-data="{ open: false }">
        {{ this is NOT interpolated }}
    </div>
@endverbatim

<script>
    const routes = @json($routes);
</script>

@verbatim suppresses compilation between its markers, which is required when documenting Blade itself or when shipping template source to a browser-based editor. @json emits a JSON-encoded expression with script-safe escaping; it remains appropriate for static data, though @js is preferred for values embedded in JavaScript because it applies the same escaping guarantees to PHP types the JSON encoder cannot represent.

Exercises 3

  1. Rewrite a nested @if (count(...)) block as @forelse and confirm identical output.
  2. Render a table using $loop->first and $loop->last, then replace the conditional row markup with a pure CSS equivalent.
  3. Use @verbatim to document the directive you are currently implementing inside a live preview page.
  4. Move a query out of a @php block into an eager-loaded relation and measure the query count before and after.

{{-- WHAT IT DOES: suppresses COMPILATION for its contents, so
     template syntax inside renders as literal characters. --}}
@verbatim
    {{-- this comment is NOT removed --}}
    {{ $variable }} is not interpolated
    @if ($x) is not a directive
@endverbatim

{{-- THE LEGITIMATE USE: a page that documents the template
     language, whose examples must survive compilation. --}}
<div class="docs-example">
    <pre><code>@verbatim
@foreach ($users as $user)
    <li>{{ $user->name }}</li>
@endforeach
    @endverbatim</code></pre>
</div>

{{-- THE OTHER LEGITIMATE USE: an admin screen previewing a
     customer-supplied template. Note it is escaped BEFORE rendering,
     because a verbatim block suppresses compilation, NOT escaping.
     @verbatim around untrusted content EXECUTES it. --}}
<x-template-preview :source="$customerTemplate" />

{{-- where the preview component writes to a temporary file and
     renders that, so the content is parsed as a template in a
     location that cannot touch the application's data --}}
// app/View/Components/TemplatePreview.php
public function render(): View
{
    $path = tempnam(sys_get_temp_dir(), 'tpl') . '.blade.php';

    // ESCAPED FIRST. The directive below is what makes the example
    // visible; this is what makes it safe. They are unrelated.
    file_put_contents($path, e($this->source));

    return view('components.template-preview-frame', [
        'rendered' => View::file($path)->render(),
    ]);
}

{{-- THE MISUSE, and it is a vulnerability rather than a style
     issue: --}}
@verbatim
    {!! $userSuppliedContent !!}
@endverbatim
{{-- the directive suppressed compilation, so the raw expression
     is not a raw expression: it is a LITERAL "{!! ... !!}" printed
     as text. Nothing is executed here - which means this form is
     also not what anyone wanted, and the confusion is how the
     genuinely dangerous version gets written: --}}

@verbatim
    {!! $userSuppliedContent !!}   {{-- SAFE but useless: printed
                                         as literal characters --}}
@endverbatim

{!! $userSuppliedContent !!}      {{-- DANGEROUS: executed --}}

{{-- THE NESTED CASE. A verbatim block cannot contain another one,
     because the closing marker is matched literally and the inner
     marker ends the outer block. Showing a template that DEMONSTRATES
     the directive therefore needs the marker built rather than
     written. --}}
@php
    // The marker is assembled so this template's own compiler does
    // not see it, which is the only way to display the directive.
    $end = '@' . 'endverbatim';
@endphp

<pre><code>@verbatim
{{-- a template that itself uses @verbatim --}}
    @{{ 'verbatim' }}
        nested content
    {{ $end }}
</code></pre>

     template syntax inside renders as literal characters. --}}

    {{ $variable }} is not interpolated
    @if ($x) is not a directive
@endverbatim

    <li>{{ $user->name }}</li>
@endforeach

4 · Layouts: Inheritance, Sections and Yields

4.1 The extends directive4.2 Sections and yields4.3 Appending with the parent directive4.4 Passing data to layouts4.5 Inline sections4.6 Layouts versus layout components

Blade layouts are PHP class inheritance expressed in templates. This chapter documents the inheritance directives, the section and yield contract, and how to choose between classic layouts and layout components.

4.1 The extends directive

Explains how a child template declares its parent, and the one rule that makes the mechanism work: sections must be defined after the extends call.

<h4>What extends compiles to, and why it runs last</h4> <p>A template that extends is compiled into two pieces: a registration that names the parent, and the section content. At render time the framework resolves the parent chain, renders the parent's structure, and fills its named sections from the children. The child contributes nothing to the output except where a section says it does, which is the whole point and also the property that makes the mechanism hard to reason about when a section name is wrong.</p> <p>The consequence to internalise is that a child template's output is not its own markup. Any text outside a section in a child is discarded, because the parent's structure is what gets emitted. So a child that puts content at the top of the file and then opens a section produces a page missing that content, and a child that opens no section at all produces an empty page — with no error anywhere, because every construct it used was valid.</p> <h4>One level, and why the framework refuses to nest them</h4> <p>It is legal for a layout to extend another layout, and it is where this mechanism stops being comprehensible. With two levels there are two files defining a section with the same name, the closest one wins, and "closest" means a property of the chain that a reader of either file cannot see. With three the rendering depends on which intermediate sections happen to be defined, and a template that looks complete can render a hole.</p> <p>The framework's answer is that you do not need it: a composition primitive exists, and a layout with a slot for the variation handles the case a chain would. Two files, one name each, and no hidden dependency.</p>

What extends compiles to, and why it runs last

A template that extends is compiled into two pieces: a registration that names the parent, and the section content. At render time the framework resolves the parent chain, renders the parent's structure, and fills its named sections from the children. The child contributes nothing to the output except where a section says it does, which is the whole point and also the property that makes the mechanism hard to reason about when a section name is wrong.

The consequence to internalise is that a child template's output is not its own markup. Any text outside a section in a child is discarded, because the parent's structure is what gets emitted. So a child that puts content at the top of the file and then opens a section produces a page missing that content, and a child that opens no section at all produces an empty page — with no error anywhere, because every construct it used was valid.

One level, and why the framework refuses to nest them

It is legal for a layout to extend another layout, and it is where this mechanism stops being comprehensible. With two levels there are two files defining a section with the same name, the closest one wins, and "closest" means a property of the chain that a reader of either file cannot see. With three the rendering depends on which intermediate sections happen to be defined, and a template that looks complete can render a hole.

The framework's answer is that you do not need it: a composition primitive exists, and a layout with a slot for the variation handles the case a chain would. Two files, one name each, and no hidden dependency.

@extends('layouts.app')

@section('title', 'Order detail')

@section('content')
    <h1>{{ $order->reference }}</h1>
@endsection

@extends registers the parent view with the environment before anything else executes, which is why it must be the first meaningful statement in the child template. Sections may be defined in any order after that call, but keeping @extends at the top of the file keeps the template readable.

One parent only. A template may extend exactly one layout. Deep hierarchies work because an intermediate template is itself a child, but each level must forward every section it does not redefine.


{{-- WHAT IT COMPILES TO, and the part that surprises people: any
     content OUTSIDE a section in the child is discarded. --}}
{{-- resources/views/orders/show.blade.php --}}
@extends('layouts.app')

@section('title', 'Order ' . $order->number)

@section('content')
    <h1>{{ $order->number }}</h1>
@endsection

{{-- storage/framework/views/<hash>.php, in essence: --}}
startSection('content', ...);
// ... sections are captured into the view's section store ...

// 2. The PARENT is rendered, and the sections are emitted at their
//    @yield points.
echo $__env->make('layouts.app', ...)->render();
?>

{{-- SO THIS PRODUCES A PAGE WITH A HOLE AND NO ERROR, because every
     construct used is valid: --}}
{{-- resources/views/orders/show.blade.php --}}
@extends('layouts.app')

<p>This text is discarded: it is outside any section.</p>

@section('content')
    <h1>Order</h1>
@endsection

{{-- AND THIS PRODUCES AN EMPTY PAGE. --}}
@extends('layouts.app')

@section('sidebar')
    <x-filter-panel />
@endsection
{{-- no @section('content'), so the parent's @yield('content')
     emits nothing at all --}}

{{-- THE TEMPLATE THAT MAKES THE RULE VISIBLE, which is what the
     parent should look like: every region it offers is an explicit
     named section, so a reader can see what a child may fill. --}}
{{-- resources/views/layouts/app.blade.php --}}
<!doctype html>
<html lang="{{ str_replace('_', '-', app()->getLocale()) }}">
<head>
    <title>@yield('title', __('app.default_title'))</title>
    @stack('head')
</head>
<body class="antialiased">
    <x-app-header :user="auth()->user()" />

    <main>@yield('content')</main>

    @hasSection('sidebar')
        <aside>@yield('sidebar')</aside>
    @endif

    @stack('scripts')
</body>
</html>

{{-- NOTE @yield('title', $default). The second argument is the
     fallback for a child that does not define the section, which is
     what stops an undefined yield from being a fatal. --}}


{{-- WHAT IT COMPILES TO, and the part that surprises people: any
     content OUTSIDE a section in the child is discarded. --}}
{{-- resources/views/orders/show.blade.php --}}
@extends('layouts.app')

@section('title', 'Order ' . $order->number)

@section('content')
    <h1>{{ $order->number }}</h1>
@endsection

{{-- storage/framework/views/<hash>.php, in essence: --}}
startSection('content', ...);
// ... sections are captured into the view's section store ...

// 2. The PARENT is rendered, and the sections are emitted at their
//    @yield points.
echo $__env->make('layouts.app', ...)->render();
?>

{{-- SO THIS PRODUCES A PAGE WITH A HOLE AND NO ERROR, because every
     construct used is valid: --}}
{{-- resources/views/orders/show.blade.php --}}
@extends('layouts.app')

<p>This text is discarded: it is outside any section.</p>

@section('content')
    <h1>Order</h1>
@endsection

{{-- AND THIS PRODUCES AN EMPTY PAGE. --}}
@extends('layouts.app')

@section('sidebar')
    <x-filter-panel />
@endsection
{{-- no @section('content'), so the parent's @yield('content')
     emits nothing at all --}}

{{-- THE TEMPLATE THAT MAKES THE RULE VISIBLE, which is what the
     parent should look like: every region it offers is an explicit
     named section, so a reader can see what a child may fill. --}}
{{-- resources/views/layouts/app.blade.php --}}
<!doctype html>
<html lang="{{ str_replace('_', '-', app()->getLocale()) }}">
<head>
    <title>@yield('title', __('app.default_title'))</title>
    @stack('head')
</head>
<body class="antialiased">
    <x-app-header :user="auth()->user()" />

    <main>@yield('content')</main>

    @hasSection('sidebar')
        <aside>@yield('sidebar')</aside>
    @endif

    @stack('scripts')
</body>
</html>

{{-- NOTE @yield('title', $default). The second argument is the
     fallback for a child that does not define the section, which is
     what stops an undefined yield from being a fatal. --}}

4.2 Sections and yields

Documents section definition in both block and inline form, and how yields resolve when a section is absent.

<h4>The three operations, and they are not symmetric</h4> <p>A section is declared, filled at a point of use, and optionally filled twice — once before the parent's yield and once after it. That third operation is the one that has no equivalent in most layout systems and the one worth understanding first, because it is what makes a child able to prepend something to a region rather than replace it.</p> <p>Filling a section outside a yield produces a warning rather than an error, which is a deliberate choice: it means a child that defines a section the parent does not yield is not fatal, but it also means a typo in a section name fails silently with a message in the log. The message names both the section and the view, which is enough to diagnose — but only if somebody reads the log.</p> <h4>What the render order actually is</h4> <p>The child registers its sections first, then the parent is rendered, and each yield emits whatever the child provided at that point. When a child both appends and prepends to the same section, the prepended content comes first, then the parent, then the appended. This is worth writing down because it is the answer to "why is my pushed content in the wrong place", and it is not what people expect: they expect the child's content to appear where they wrote it in the child file, and it appears where the parent yields.</p> <h4>Sections versus stacks</h4> <p>A section replaces; a stack accumulates. That is the entire distinction and it decides the mechanism: use a section when there is exactly one contributor to a region, and a stack when there are many — a stylesheet from a component plus a stylesheet from a page, scripts from a layout plus a script from an inline widget. Reaching for a stack where a section belongs produces duplicate output, and reaching for a section where a stack belongs silently discards the earlier contributors.</p>

The three operations, and they are not symmetric

A section is declared, filled at a point of use, and optionally filled twice — once before the parent's yield and once after it. That third operation is the one that has no equivalent in most layout systems and the one worth understanding first, because it is what makes a child able to prepend something to a region rather than replace it.

Filling a section outside a yield produces a warning rather than an error, which is a deliberate choice: it means a child that defines a section the parent does not yield is not fatal, but it also means a typo in a section name fails silently with a message in the log. The message names both the section and the view, which is enough to diagnose — but only if somebody reads the log.

What the render order actually is

The child registers its sections first, then the parent is rendered, and each yield emits whatever the child provided at that point. When a child both appends and prepends to the same section, the prepended content comes first, then the parent, then the appended. This is worth writing down because it is the answer to "why is my pushed content in the wrong place", and it is not what people expect: they expect the child's content to appear where they wrote it in the child file, and it appears where the parent yields.

Sections versus stacks

A section replaces; a stack accumulates. That is the entire distinction and it decides the mechanism: use a section when there is exactly one contributor to a region, and a stack when there are many — a stylesheet from a component plus a stylesheet from a page, scripts from a layout plus a script from an inline widget. Reaching for a stack where a section belongs produces duplicate output, and reaching for a section where a stack belongs silently discards the earlier contributors.

<html lang="{{ str_replace('_', '-', app()->getLocale()) }}">
<head>
    <title>@yield('title', 'Dashboard')</title>
    @stack('head')
</head>
<body>
    <main>@yield('content')</main>
</body>
</html>

The second argument to @yield is the default used when the child supplies nothing, which is the mechanism for optional regions such as page-specific scripts. An empty @yield('content') with no default renders nothing at all.

Sections are collected on a stack owned by the view environment. The layout is rendered first, the child’s sections are recorded while the child executes, and the layout’s yields pull from that stack. This is why the layout may reference a yield before the section that fills it has been defined.


{{-- THE THREE OPERATIONS. --}}

{{-- (1) DECLARE. The value may be a string or a block: --}}
@section('title', 'Order ' . $order->number)

@section('content')
    <h1>{{ $order->number }}</h1>
@endsection

{{-- (2) YIELD, with a fallback so an undefined section is not
     fatal: --}}
<title>@yield('title', __('app.default_title'))</title>
<main>@yield('content')</main>

{{-- THE RENDER ORDER, which answers "why is my pushed content in
     the wrong place". The child registers; the parent renders; each
     yield emits what the child provided. --}}
@endsection

@section('content')
    {{-- PREPEND: emitted BEFORE the parent's own content in this
         region. --}}
    <x-breadcrumbs :items="$breadcrumbs" />
@append

@section('content')
    {{-- the CHILD's main body --}}
    <h1>{{ $order->number }}</h1>
@append

{{-- and in the PARENT, between two yields of the same section: --}}
<main>@yield('contentBefore')</main>
<main>@yield('content')</main>
<aside>@yield('contentAfter')</aside

{{-- SO THAT child file renders as:

       <breadcrumbs>
       <h1>...</h1>

     ...and NOT in the order the two @section blocks appear in the
     child. Position is decided by the parent. --}}

{{-- THE WARNING, AND WHY IT IS NOT AN ERROR: a child defining a
     section the parent never yields is legal — it just goes
     nowhere. A typo therefore fails silently. --}}
@section('sidebr')      {{-- the parent yields 'sidebar'. This
                             emits nothing and logs a warning
                             naming both. --}}
    <x-filter-panel />
@endsection

{{-- THE DEFENCE: make the parent's set of sections explicit, so a
     typo is a diff rather than a log line nobody reads. --}}
{{-- resources/views/layouts/app.blade.php --}}
@php
    // A single source of truth for what a child may fill. A typo in
    // a section name is then a KeyError at render time rather than
    // a hole in the page.
    $sections = ['title', 'content', 'sidebar', 'footer'];
@endphp

@endsection

@endphp

4.3 Appending with the parent directive

Introduces the parent directive, which lets a child prepend content to an inherited section rather than replacing it entirely.

<h4>Prepending and appending, and the direction is the parent's</h4> <p>Two directives let a child add content to a region rather than replace it. The prepend directive emits before the parent's own content for that section; the append directive emits after. Neither is a block — both are inline expressions whose result is emitted, so a child that writes one emits whatever it returns.</p> <p>The subtlety is that these are two independent pieces of storage. A child that uses prepend twice emits both, in order, before the parent's content. A child that uses append once emits once, after. And a child that uses both gets both. What does not happen is a child that uses append being placed after another child's append, unless the ordering happens to be the render order — which it is, because the sections are stored in the order they were filled, and appends accumulate.</p> <h4>Where the result lands, which is the question people ask</h4> <p>The result lands wherever the parent yields the section, not where the child wrote it. A child that appends to a section the parent yields in the page footer gets its content in the footer, no matter how early the append appears in the child file. This is correct — the parent owns the structure — and it is a surprise the first time, because reading the child file gives no indication of where the output will appear.</p> <h4>When append is the wrong tool</h4> <p>Append is for adding to a region a parent defined. It is not for adding a region the parent does not have, and it is not a substitute for a slot. If two different children need different content in different places, that is a layout composition problem and the answer is a slot in the layout or a component that takes the variation — not two appends into the same section hoping they land correctly.</p>

Prepending and appending, and the direction is the parent's

Two directives let a child add content to a region rather than replace it. The prepend directive emits before the parent's own content for that section; the append directive emits after. Neither is a block — both are inline expressions whose result is emitted, so a child that writes one emits whatever it returns.

The subtlety is that these are two independent pieces of storage. A child that uses prepend twice emits both, in order, before the parent's content. A child that uses append once emits once, after. And a child that uses both gets both. What does not happen is a child that uses append being placed after another child's append, unless the ordering happens to be the render order — which it is, because the sections are stored in the order they were filled, and appends accumulate.

Where the result lands, which is the question people ask

The result lands wherever the parent yields the section, not where the child wrote it. A child that appends to a section the parent yields in the page footer gets its content in the footer, no matter how early the append appears in the child file. This is correct — the parent owns the structure — and it is a surprise the first time, because reading the child file gives no indication of where the output will appear.

When append is the wrong tool

Append is for adding to a region a parent defined. It is not for adding a region the parent does not have, and it is not a substitute for a slot. If two different children need different content in different places, that is a layout composition problem and the answer is a slot in the layout or a component that takes the variation — not two appends into the same section hoping they land correctly.

@extends('layouts.app')

@section('content')
    <p>Every page in this section starts with this banner.</p>
    @parent
    <p>Then the page-specific content follows.</p>
@endsection

@parent inserts the content the parent layout defined for that section. Combined with intermediate layouts it produces the familiar three-tier pattern where a section theme extends an application layout and both contribute to the final output.

Keep inheritance shallow. Three levels is already difficult to reason about. When a layout must be extended twice with different content, prefer passing data or using a layout component with named slots.


{{-- THE TWO DIRECTIVES, and BOTH ARE INLINE EXPRESSIONS rather
     than blocks: whatever they return is emitted. --}}
@section('content')
    <x-breadcrumbs :items="$breadcrumbs" />
@append

@section('content')
    <h1>{{ $order->number }}</h1>
@append

{{-- THE PARENT DECIDES WHERE THEY LAND. The append above appears
     wherever the parent yields 'content' — so this parent: --}}
{{-- resources/views/layouts/app.blade.php --}}
<main>@yield('content')</main>
@stack('scripts')

{{-- ...produces both children in <main>, and this one: --}}
<main>@yield('content')</main>
<footer>@yield('footer')</footer>

{{-- ...produces them in <main> too. The child file gives NO
     indication of which, which is the first-time surprise. --}}

{{-- TWO APPENDS ACCUMULATE, in fill order: --}}
@section('content')
    <p>first</p>
@append
@section('content')
    <p>second</p>
@append
{{-- renders: first, second, then the parent's own content in the
     middle if it has any. --}}

{{-- PREPEND EMITS BEFORE THE PARENT'S CONTENT: --}}
@section('content')
    <x-breadcrumbs :items="$breadcrumbs" />
@prepend

@section('content')
    <h1>{{ $order->number }}</h1>
@append
{{-- renders: <breadcrumbs>, <h1>, and the parent's default
     content for that section between the two if it has any. --}}

{{-- WHERE APPEND IS THE WRONG TOOL. Two children needing
     different content in different places is a COMPOSITION
     problem, and the answer is a slot in the layout — not two
     appends into one section hoping they land correctly. --}}
{{-- resources/views/layouts/reports.blade.php --}}
<x-app-shell :title="$title">
    <x-reports-nav :active="$activeFilter" />

    <x-slot:aside>
        @isset($aside)
            {{ $aside }}
        @else
            <x-reports-help />
        @endisset
    </x-slot:aside>

    {{ $slot }}
</x-app-shell>

{{-- Compare with the append version, where the aside has to be a
     SECTION in every child and the parent has to yield it in the
     right place: --}}
{{-- every child now has to remember: --}}
@section('content')
    <x-reports-body />
@append
@section('aside')
    <x-reports-nav />
@append
{{-- and a child that forgets the aside section renders a page
     with an empty column. --}}


{{-- THE TWO DIRECTIVES, and BOTH ARE INLINE EXPRESSIONS rather
     than blocks: whatever they return is emitted. --}}
@section('content')
    <x-breadcrumbs :items="$breadcrumbs" />
@append

@section('content')
    <h1>{{ $order->number }}</h1>
@append

{{-- THE PARENT DECIDES WHERE THEY LAND. The append above appears
     wherever the parent yields 'content' — so this parent: --}}
{{-- resources/views/layouts/app.blade.php --}}
<main>@yield('content')</main>
@stack('scripts')

{{-- ...produces both children in <main>, and this one: --}}
<main>@yield('content')</main>
<footer>@yield('footer')</footer>

{{-- ...produces them in <main> too. The child file gives NO
     indication of which, which is the first-time surprise. --}}

    <p>first</p>
@append
@section('content')
    <p>second</p>
@append
{{-- renders: first, second, then the parent's own content in the
     middle if it has any. --}}

{{-- PREPEND EMITS BEFORE THE PARENT'S CONTENT: --}}

    <x-breadcrumbs :items="$breadcrumbs" />
@prepend

@section('content')
    <h1>{{ $order->number }}</h1>
@append

     content for that section between the two if it has any. --}}

     SECTION in every child and the parent has to yield it in the

@append
@section('aside')
    <x-reports-nav />
@append
{{-- and a child that forgets the aside section renders a page
     with an empty column. --}}

4.4 Passing data to layouts

Documents the view composer and shared-data mechanisms used to make data available to a layout rendered in a different view context.

Data passed to view() reaches the child template, and because the layout is rendered within the same environment it receives that data too. Where a value must be present regardless of the child, register it through a composer:

<h4>What a layout can see</h4> <p>A layout receives the data passed to its render call, plus anything a view composer or a shared value provides. It does not receive the data passed to the child template, because the child's data belongs to the child. This is worth stating precisely, because the common expectation is the opposite: a layout that needs the order number in its title bar cannot assume the child's data is visible.</p> <p>The mechanism that makes it work is that the child passes its data through, explicitly. A child that receives an order and extends a layout forwards the order to the layout, and the layout declares it as a default so a page rendered without a child still works. That is the whole pattern, and it has a cost worth naming: the data contract of a layout now includes everything any child might forward, which grows.</p> <h4>Values, not just variables</h4> <p>The data passed to a render call need not be an array. Passing a value is what a view model is, and it has one important advantage over an array: the model can compute its own defaults and can enforce a contract in a constructor rather than discovering a missing key three screens down the page. It has one disadvantage: the model is a class, so it needs a namespace and cannot be created in the template.</p> <h4>Shared data, and the coupling it creates</h4> <p>Data can be attached to a view for every render, or shared globally across all views. Both are convenient and both create a dependency that is invisible at the point of use: a layout that reads a globally shared value cannot be rendered without that share being set, which means it cannot be rendered in a test that does not set it, and it cannot be rendered by a console command that did not. The rule that keeps this manageable is that shared data should be configuration-like — a locale, a flag set, the current user — and never data a template branches on for content.</p>

What a layout can see

A layout receives the data passed to its render call, plus anything a view composer or a shared value provides. It does not receive the data passed to the child template, because the child's data belongs to the child. This is worth stating precisely, because the common expectation is the opposite: a layout that needs the order number in its title bar cannot assume the child's data is visible.

The mechanism that makes it work is that the child passes its data through, explicitly. A child that receives an order and extends a layout forwards the order to the layout, and the layout declares it as a default so a page rendered without a child still works. That is the whole pattern, and it has a cost worth naming: the data contract of a layout now includes everything any child might forward, which grows.

Values, not just variables

The data passed to a render call need not be an array. Passing a value is what a view model is, and it has one important advantage over an array: the model can compute its own defaults and can enforce a contract in a constructor rather than discovering a missing key three screens down the page. It has one disadvantage: the model is a class, so it needs a namespace and cannot be created in the template.

Shared data, and the coupling it creates

Data can be attached to a view for every render, or shared globally across all views. Both are convenient and both create a dependency that is invisible at the point of use: a layout that reads a globally shared value cannot be rendered without that share being set, which means it cannot be rendered in a test that does not set it, and it cannot be rendered by a console command that did not. The rule that keeps this manageable is that shared data should be configuration-like — a locale, a flag set, the current user — and never data a template branches on for content.

// app/Providers/AppServiceProvider.php
View::composer('layouts.app', function ($view) {
    $view->with('navigation', Navigation::query()
        ->where('is_active', true)
        ->orderBy('label')
        ->get());
});

The closure receives the view instance and may share data or set it conditionally. A composer is the right place for shared chrome — navigation, tenant settings, unread counts — and is covered in detail in Chapter 6.


{{-- WHAT A LAYOUT RECEIVES: what was passed to the render call,
     plus composers and shared values. NOT the child's data. --}}
{{-- resources/views/layouts/app.blade.php --}}
@props([])   {{-- not a component: a plain layout gets whatever the
                    render call passed --}}

<title>@yield('title', __('app.default_title'))</title>
<h1>{{ $order->number ?? '' }}</h1>
{{-- $order is NOT automatically visible. The child must forward
     it, or the layout must declare it. --}}

{{-- THE FORWARDING PATTERN, which is the whole mechanism: --}}
{{-- resources/views/orders/show.blade.php --}}
@extends('layouts.app')

@section('title', 'Order ' . $order->number)

@section('content')
    <h1>{{ $order->number }}</h1>
@endsection

{{-- The layout needs it too, so the child passes it explicitly: --}}
@extends('layouts.app', ['order' => $order])
{{-- ^ the second argument is data for the LAYOUT. This is
     undocumented in most guides and is the answer to "how does my
     layout get the order number". --}}

{{-- A VALUE INSTEAD OF AN ARRAY — a view model. The advantage is
     that the contract is enforced in a constructor rather than
     discovered three screens down the page. --}}
{{-- resources/views/layouts/app.blade.php --}}
@extends('layouts.app', new AppViewLayoutData(
    title: 'Order ' . $order->number,
    order: $order,
    user:  auth()->user(),
))

{{-- app/View/LayoutData.php --}}
final readonly class LayoutData
{
    public function __construct(
        public string  $title,
        public ?Order  $order = null,     // nullable, because a
                                         // page with no order is
                                         // legitimate — a login
                                         // page, an error page
        public ?User   $user  = null,
    ) {}

    public function heading(): string
    {
        // The layout can COMPUTE without the template doing it,
        // because the computation is a property of the data rather
        // than of the presentation.
        return match (true) {
            $this->order !== null => __('orders.heading', ['n' => $this->order->number]),
            $this->user  !== null => __('dashboard.heading'),
            default               => __('app.welcome'),
        };
    }
}

{{-- AND THE TEMPLATE JUST READS IT: --}}
<h1>{{ $heading }}</h1>

{{-- THE SHARED VALUE, AND THE INVISIBLE DEPENDENCY IT CREATES: --}}
// app/Providers/AppServiceProvider.php
View::share('brands', [
    'name'   => config('app.name'),
    'colour' => config('brand.colour'),
]);
{{-- Convenient. Now consider a test that renders a layout directly:
     it must set this up or the template reads undefined. And a
     console command that renders a PDF must set it up too. --}}

{{-- THE RULE THAT KEEPS IT MANAGEABLE: shared data is
     CONFIGURATION-LIKE — a locale, a flag set, the current user —
     and never data a template branches on for CONTENT. --}}
View::share('locale', app()->getLocale());     {{-- fine --}}
View::share('currentUser', auth()->user());    {{-- borderline:
                                                    fine for a nav
                                                    bar, wrong for
                                                    page content --}}


{{-- WHAT A LAYOUT RECEIVES: what was passed to the render call,
     plus composers and shared values. NOT the child's data. --}}
{{-- resources/views/layouts/app.blade.php --}}
@props([])   {{-- not a component: a plain layout gets whatever the
                    render call passed --}}

<title>@yield('title', __('app.default_title'))</title>
<h1>{{ $order->number ?? '' }}</h1>
{{-- $order is NOT automatically visible. The child must forward
     it, or the layout must declare it. --}}

{{-- THE FORWARDING PATTERN, which is the whole mechanism: --}}
{{-- resources/views/orders/show.blade.php --}}
@extends('layouts.app')

@section('title', 'Order ' . $order->number)

@section('content')
    <h1>{{ $order->number }}</h1>
@endsection

{{-- The layout needs it too, so the child passes it explicitly: --}}
@extends('layouts.app', ['order' => $order])
{{-- ^ the second argument is data for the LAYOUT. This is
     undocumented in most guides and is the answer to "how does my
     layout get the order number". --}}

{{-- A VALUE INSTEAD OF AN ARRAY — a view model. The advantage is
     that the contract is enforced in a constructor rather than
     discovered three screens down the page. --}}
{{-- resources/views/layouts/app.blade.php --}}
@extends('layouts.app', new AppViewLayoutData(
    title: 'Order ' . $order->number,
    order: $order,
    user:  auth()->user(),
))

{{-- app/View/LayoutData.php --}}
final readonly class LayoutData
{
    public function __construct(
        public string  $title,
        public ?Order  $order = null,     // nullable, because a
                                         // page with no order is
                                         // legitimate — a login
                                         // page, an error page
        public ?User   $user  = null,
    ) {}

    public function heading(): string
    {
        // The layout can COMPUTE without the template doing it,
        // because the computation is a property of the data rather
        // than of the presentation.
        return match (true) {
            $this->order !== null => __('orders.heading', ['n' => $this->order->number]),
            $this->user  !== null => __('dashboard.heading'),
            default               => __('app.welcome'),
        };
    }
}

{{-- AND THE TEMPLATE JUST READS IT: --}}
<h1>{{ $heading }}</h1>

{{-- THE SHARED VALUE, AND THE INVISIBLE DEPENDENCY IT CREATES: --}}
// app/Providers/AppServiceProvider.php
View::share('brands', [
    'name'   => config('app.name'),
    'colour' => config('brand.colour'),
]);
{{-- Convenient. Now consider a test that renders a layout directly:
     it must set this up or the template reads undefined. And a
     console command that renders a PDF must set it up too. --}}

{{-- THE RULE THAT KEEPS IT MANAGEABLE: shared data is
     CONFIGURATION-LIKE — a locale, a flag set, the current user —
     and never data a template branches on for CONTENT. --}}
View::share('locale', app()->getLocale());     {{-- fine --}}
View::share('currentUser', auth()->user());    {{-- borderline:
                                                    fine for a nav
                                                    bar, wrong for
                                                    page content --}}

4.5 Inline sections

Documents the single-line section forms that compile to the same stack operations as the block form, and when they are appropriate.

<h4>The two are different mechanisms with overlapping names</h4> <p>A section and a stack both let a child contribute to a parent's output, and they behave differently in the way that matters. A section holds one value: the last writer wins, or with the prepend and append variants the contents are ordered explicitly. A stack accumulates: every push appends to a list and every yield emits the whole list.</p> <p>The consequence is that a stack is the right construct the moment there is more than one contributor, and it is the wrong construct the moment there is exactly one — because a stack cannot be replaced, only appended to. A page that defines a stylesheet in its body and a layout that also defines one produces both, in the order they were pushed, which is occasionally what you wanted and frequently is not.</p> <h4>Where a section defined inline is the answer</h4> <p>Several layouts define a region as an inline section rather than as a slot, and the reason is that the section can be filled with a fallback from inside the layout. A slot cannot: the parent either receives content for it or does not. So a layout that wants to render a default region unless a child overrides it uses an inline section with a conditional, and a layout that wants an empty region unless filled uses a slot.</p> <h4>The cost of inline sections</h4> <p>They are less readable than a slot, because the region's markup is buried in the layout's conditional rather than visible as a named region. They are also more flexible, because the default can be computed. For a region that is usually empty and occasionally filled — a promotional banner, a contextual sidebar — the inline section is the right choice and a slot with a default child is not, because a slot has no notion of "absent".</p>

The two are different mechanisms with overlapping names

A section and a stack both let a child contribute to a parent's output, and they behave differently in the way that matters. A section holds one value: the last writer wins, or with the prepend and append variants the contents are ordered explicitly. A stack accumulates: every push appends to a list and every yield emits the whole list.

The consequence is that a stack is the right construct the moment there is more than one contributor, and it is the wrong construct the moment there is exactly one — because a stack cannot be replaced, only appended to. A page that defines a stylesheet in its body and a layout that also defines one produces both, in the order they were pushed, which is occasionally what you wanted and frequently is not.

Where a section defined inline is the answer

Several layouts define a region as an inline section rather than as a slot, and the reason is that the section can be filled with a fallback from inside the layout. A slot cannot: the parent either receives content for it or does not. So a layout that wants to render a default region unless a child overrides it uses an inline section with a conditional, and a layout that wants an empty region unless filled uses a slot.

The cost of inline sections

They are less readable than a slot, because the region's markup is buried in the layout's conditional rather than visible as a named region. They are also more flexible, because the default can be computed. For a region that is usually empty and occasionally filled — a promotional banner, a contextual sidebar — the inline section is the right choice and a slot with a default child is not, because a slot has no notion of "absent".

@section('title', 'Dashboard')

@section('sidebar')
    <nav>...</nav>
@endsection

@section('footer', 'Literal text')

The single-argument form renders a block; the two-argument form sets a value inline and escapes it. Inline sections are for scalars, not markup: a raw HTML string passed inline is displayed as text rather than rendered.


{{-- A SECTION REPLACES. A STACK ACCUMULATES. That is the whole
     distinction and it decides the mechanism. --}}

{{-- (a) A STACK, WHERE THE CONTRIBUTOR IS DEFINED: --}}
{{-- resources/views/layouts/app.blade.php --}}
<head>
    @stack('head')       {{-- every push is emitted, in push order --}}
</head>
<body>
    ...
    @stack('scripts')
</body>

{{-- (b) AN INLINE SECTION, WHERE THE REGION HAS A DEFAULT: --}}
@hasSection('sidebar')
    <aside>@yield('sidebar')</aside>
@else
    <aside><x-default-nav /></aside>    {{-- the default the slot
                                              form cannot express --}}
@endif

{{-- WHICH IS WHY THE DEFAULT-SIDEBAR PATTERN NEEDS A SECTION: a
     slot has no notion of ABSENT. --}}
{{-- resources/views/layouts/app.blade.php --}}
<x-app-shell>
    @if (isset($withSidebar))
        <x-slot:sidebar>{{ $sidebar }}</x-slot:sidebar>
    @endif
</x-app-shell>
{{-- ^ works, but the "is it filled" question has to be asked
     twice: once for the slot and once for the variable. The inline
     section asks it once. --}}

{{-- A SLOT WITH A DEFAULT IS NOT POSSIBLE, and this is the reason
     many layouts reach for an inline section: --}}
<x-app-shell>
    <x-slot:sidebar>
        {{-- this is CONTENT, not a default: it renders whenever the
             parent has the slot, whether or not a child filled it --}}
        <x-default-nav />
    </x-slot:sidebar>
</x-app-shell>

{{-- THE PAGE THAT DOES NOT WANT A SIDEBAR, with the section form: --}}
{{-- resources/views/dashboard.blade.php --}}
@extends('layouts.app')
@section('content')
    <x-dashboard />
@endsection
{{-- no sidebar section, so the layout's default renders. --}}

{{-- THE SAME PAGE WITH THE SLOT FORM, where the slot must be
     omitted entirely: --}}
<x-app-shell>
    <x-dashboard />
</x-app-shell>
{{-- no x-slot:sidebar, so nothing renders there. Which means the
     layout cannot offer a default, and the page must explicitly
     supply one if it wants a sidebar. --}}


{{-- A SECTION REPLACES. A STACK ACCUMULATES. That is the whole
     distinction and it decides the mechanism. --}}

{{-- (a) A STACK, WHERE THE CONTRIBUTOR IS DEFINED: --}}
{{-- resources/views/layouts/app.blade.php --}}
<head>
    @stack('head')       {{-- every push is emitted, in push order --}}
</head>
<body>
    ...
    @stack('scripts')
</body>

     twice: once for the slot and once for the variable. The inline

     many layouts reach for an inline section: --}}

             parent has the slot, whether or not a child filled it --}}

    </x-slot:sidebar>
</x-app-shell>

     omitted entirely: --}}
<x-app-shell>

</x-app-shell>

     layout cannot offer a default, and the page must explicitly
     supply one if it wants a sidebar. --}}

4.6 Layouts versus layout components

Compares the classic extends-and-section layout with the layout component pattern, and states when each is preferable.

AspectClassic layoutLayout component
Syntax@extends, @section, @yield<x-layouts.app> with slots
DataView composers, global shareAttributes and named slots
Type safetyNone at the call siteConstructor and prop types
Discovery costRuntime section stackRegistered component class
Best forLegacy and package viewsNew code in Laravel 13

New applications should use layout components. They give a typed constructor, an attribute bag, named slots and compile-time discovery, none of which the section stack provides. Classic layouts remain fully supported and remain correct for code that already uses them, as long as each file is internally consistent.

Exercises 4

  1. Build a three-tier layout hierarchy and use the parent directive so each tier contributes to one section.
  2. Replace a layout composer with a layout component attribute and confirm identical output.
  3. Add a default to an optional yield region and verify the fallback renders when the section is absent.
  4. Convert one existing @section template to a layout component and record any data that could not be passed as a prop.
<h4>Two mechanisms for the same problem</h4> <p>Layout inheritance and layout components both solve "the same page structure with varying content". They differ in what varies and in what the output looks like, and the choice between them is worth making deliberately rather than by habit.</p> <p>With inheritance, the child names a parent and fills named regions. The parent's structure is fixed at the point the child extends it, the regions are string-keyed, and the child's own markup outside a region is discarded. With a layout component, the child passes markup as the component's default slot and named regions as named slots; the parent is an ordinary component with typed props, and the child's markup is always inside something.</p> <p>The practical differences follow from that. A section name is a string, so a typo is a warning and a hole. A slot is a component element, so a typo is a parse error or a missing region that a strict component can detect. Sections cannot be given defaults from inside the parent, so a region with a fallback needs an inline conditional. Slots can, because the parent controls the component's internals. And a layout component's props are typed, so a child passing the wrong type fails at render time.</p> <h4>The rule that decides it</h4> <p>Use a component when the layout needs to vary its structure or accept typed data, and use inheritance when the layout is genuinely fixed and you want the child's content to be collected into named regions without passing markup through a slot. A third option exists for the case neither handles well — a page assembled from independently-rendered fragments — and it is a view composer or a dedicated view class, which is worth reaching for when a page has more than about four regions and no two pages share its shape.</p>

Two mechanisms for the same problem

Layout inheritance and layout components both solve "the same page structure with varying content". They differ in what varies and in what the output looks like, and the choice between them is worth making deliberately rather than by habit.

With inheritance, the child names a parent and fills named regions. The parent's structure is fixed at the point the child extends it, the regions are string-keyed, and the child's own markup outside a region is discarded. With a layout component, the child passes markup as the component's default slot and named regions as named slots; the parent is an ordinary component with typed props, and the child's markup is always inside something.

The practical differences follow from that. A section name is a string, so a typo is a warning and a hole. A slot is a component element, so a typo is a parse error or a missing region that a strict component can detect. Sections cannot be given defaults from inside the parent, so a region with a fallback needs an inline conditional. Slots can, because the parent controls the component's internals. And a layout component's props are typed, so a child passing the wrong type fails at render time.

The rule that decides it

Use a component when the layout needs to vary its structure or accept typed data, and use inheritance when the layout is genuinely fixed and you want the child's content to be collected into named regions without passing markup through a slot. A third option exists for the case neither handles well — a page assembled from independently-rendered fragments — and it is a view composer or a dedicated view class, which is worth reaching for when a page has more than about four regions and no two pages share its shape.


{{-- (1) INHERITANCE: the child names a parent; the parent's
     structure is fixed; the regions are string-keyed. --}}
{{-- resources/views/layouts/app.blade.php --}}
<title>@yield('title', 'App')</title>
<main>@yield('content')</main>
<aside>@hasSection('sidebar') @yield('sidebar') @endif</aside>

{{-- resources/views/orders/show.blade.php --}}
@extends('layouts.app')
@section('title', 'Order ' . $order->number)
@section('content') <h1>{{ $order->number }}</h1> @endsection

{{-- WHAT A TYPO IN A SECTION NAME COSTS, and it is the whole
     argument for slots in a layout: --}}
@section('conten')
    <h1>{{ $order->number }}</h1>
@endsection
{{-- renders a page with a HOLE and a warning in the log. No
     exception, no failed test, no diagnostic on the page. --}}

{{-- (2) A LAYOUT COMPONENT: the child passes markup through a
     slot; the parent is an ordinary typed component, so a typo is
     a component that does not exist rather than a string that
     matches nothing. --}}
{{-- resources/views/components/app-shell.blade.php --}}
@props(['title' => null, 'user' => null, 'wide' => false])

<div class="shell {{ $wide ? 'shell-wide' : '' }}">
    <x-app-header :user="$user" />
    <h1>{{ $title ?? __('app.welcome') }}</h1>
    <main>{{ $slot }}</main>
    @isset($aside)
        <aside>{{ $aside }}</aside>
    @endif
</div>

{{-- resources/views/orders/show.blade.php --}}
<x-app-shell :title="__('orders.heading', ['n' => $order->number])"
             :user="auth()->user()" :wide="true">
    <h1>{{ $order->number }}</h1>

    <x-slot:aside>
        <x-order-summary :order="$order" />
    </x-slot:aside>
</x-app-shell


{{-- THE THREE COMPARISONS THAT MATTER, side by side. --}}

{{-- 1. TYPING. A section name is a string; a slot is a component
     element; a component prop is a typed constructor parameter. --}}
@extends('layouts.app')
@section('title', 'Order ' . $order->number)
{{-- $order->status is an ENUM. A section takes a STRING, so a
     status added to the backend and not to the template renders an
     empty page rather than an error. --}}

<x-app-shell :title="__('orders.heading', ['n' => $order->number])" />
{{-- a component's prop is typed: OrderStatus $status, so a value
     that is not a valid case is a TypeError naming the component
     and the parameter. --}}

{{-- 2. DEFAULTS. A section's fallback is written at the yield, so
     every child that omits it gets one automatically. A slot has no
     default mechanism — the parent must ask whether it was filled,
     which it does with @isset. --}}
<title>@yield('title', __('app.welcome'))</title>
{{-- automatic --}}

@isset($aside)
    <aside>{{ $aside }}</aside>
@else
    <aside><x-default-nav /></aside>
@endif
{{-- the parent has to ask, which is more code and more control:
     a default is not just a value, it can be COMPUTED. --}}

{{-- 3. TESTING. A layout with sections is tested by rendering a
     child and asserting on the HTML — so the assertion covers the
     layout AND the child, and a failure does not say which broke.
     A layout component's shell can be asserted on directly. --}}
it('renders the order heading in the shell title', function () {
    $html = $this->render('<x-app-shell :title="Order 42" />');
    expect($html)->toContain('Order 42');
});

it('renders breadcrumbs before the heading', function () {
    // A LAYOUT TEST, where the thing under test is the layout.
    $html = $this->renderView('orders.show', ['order' => $order]);
    expect(strpos($html, 'breadcrumb'))->toBeLessThan(strpos($html, '<h1>'));
});
{{-- the second test is the inheritance form: it asserts an ORDER
     between two things, which is a layout behaviour, but it can
     only be observed by rendering a child as well — so a change
     to the child can fail a layout test. --}}

{{-- THE DECIDING QUESTION, in one line: does the layout need to
     vary its STRUCTURE, or take TYPED DATA? --}}
{{-- structure varies, or props are typed  -> LAYOUT COMPONENT
     structure is fixed, regions are strings -> INHERITANCE
     neither, and the page has four regions no other page shares
                                        -> assemble it explicitly,
                                           a view class returning
                                           rendered fragments --}}


{{-- (1) INHERITANCE: the child names a parent; the parent's
     structure is fixed; the regions are string-keyed. --}}
{{-- resources/views/layouts/app.blade.php --}}
<title>@yield('title', 'App')</title>
<main>@yield('content')</main>
<aside>@hasSection('sidebar') @yield('sidebar') @endif</aside>

{{-- resources/views/orders/show.blade.php --}}
@extends('layouts.app')
@section('title', 'Order ' . $order->number)
@section('content') <h1>{{ $order->number }}</h1> @endsection

{{-- WHAT A TYPO IN A SECTION NAME COSTS, and it is the whole
     argument for slots in a layout: --}}

    <h1>{{ $order->number }}</h1>
@endsection
{{-- renders a page with a HOLE and a warning in the log. No
     exception, no failed test, no diagnostic on the page. --}}

{{-- (2) A LAYOUT COMPONENT: the child passes markup through a
     slot; the parent is an ordinary typed component, so a typo is
     a component that does not exist rather than a string that
     matches nothing. --}}
{{-- resources/views/components/app-shell.blade.php --}}

<div class="shell {{ $wide ? 'shell-wide' : '' }}">
    <x-app-header :user="$user" />
    <h1>{{ $title ?? __('app.welcome') }}</h1>
    <main>{{ $slot }}</main>
    @isset($aside)
        <aside>{{ $aside }}</aside>
    @endif
</div>

{{-- resources/views/orders/show.blade.php --}}

             :user="auth()->user()" :wide="true">
    <h1>{{ $order->number }}</h1>

    <x-slot:aside>
        <x-order-summary :order="$order" />
    </x-slot:aside>
</x-app-shell


{{-- THE THREE COMPARISONS THAT MATTER, side by side. --}}

{{-- 1. TYPING. A section name is a string; a slot is a component
     element; a component prop is a typed constructor parameter. --}}
@extends('layouts.app')

     status added to the backend and not to the template renders an
     empty page rather than an error. --}}

{{-- a component's prop is typed: OrderStatus $status, so a value
     that is not a valid case is a TypeError naming the component
     and the parameter. --}}

{{-- 2. DEFAULTS. A section's fallback is written at the yield, so
     every child that omits it gets one automatically. A slot has no
     default mechanism — the parent must ask whether it was filled,
     which it does with @isset. --}}

    expect($html)->toContain('Order 42');
