5 · Blade Components: Anonymous, Class-Based, Slots and Attributes

5.1 Anonymous components5.2 Props, types and defaults5.3 The attribute bag and merging5.4 Slots5.5 Class-based components5.6 View properties

Components are Blade’s unit of view composition. This chapter covers both implementations, the props contract between caller and component, and the attribute and slot mechanics that govern what reaches the rendered HTML.

5.1 Anonymous components

Introduces anonymous components as plain Blade files resolved from a component directory, and the naming convention that maps a tag to a file.

A tag of the form <x-alert> resolves to resources/views/components/alert.blade.php. Nested directories become namespace separators: <x-forms.text-field> resolves to resources/views/components/forms/text-field.blade.php. The file is a normal template with no class, no registration and no discovery cost beyond the file existence check, which makes it the default choice for presentation-only markup.

<h4>One file, one component</h4> <p>An anonymous component is a template that also declares its own props. There is no class, so there is no namespace to register and no container to resolve it through — the compiler finds it by path, which means the file name and the tag name are the same thing and there is nowhere for them to disagree.</p> <p>That is the whole feature, and it is worth understanding what it removes. A class-based component needs a namespace registration before it can be used, needs to be autoloaded, and has two files — the class and the view. An anonymous component has one file. The cost is that props are declared in the template rather than in a typed signature, so there is no static analysis of them and no way to give a prop a non-string default that depends on other props.</p> <h4>When anonymous is right, and when it is not</h4> <p>Anonymous is right for a presentational fragment with no behaviour: a badge, a panel, an icon with a label, a table cell. It is not right for anything that queries, that has a service injected, or that is reused across applications — because those cases need a class and a constructor, and because a component with logic in it wants the logic somewhere testable without rendering HTML.</p> <p>The trigger to move from anonymous to a class is behaviour: the moment a component computes something, holds state, or performs a decision, it wants a unit test, and a unit test that has to render a template to exercise one branch is a slow test that tells you less.</p>

One file, one component

An anonymous component is a template that also declares its own props. There is no class, so there is no namespace to register and no container to resolve it through — the compiler finds it by path, which means the file name and the tag name are the same thing and there is nowhere for them to disagree.

That is the whole feature, and it is worth understanding what it removes. A class-based component needs a namespace registration before it can be used, needs to be autoloaded, and has two files — the class and the view. An anonymous component has one file. The cost is that props are declared in the template rather than in a typed signature, so there is no static analysis of them and no way to give a prop a non-string default that depends on other props.

When anonymous is right, and when it is not

Anonymous is right for a presentational fragment with no behaviour: a badge, a panel, an icon with a label, a table cell. It is not right for anything that queries, that has a service injected, or that is reused across applications — because those cases need a class and a constructor, and because a component with logic in it wants the logic somewhere testable without rendering HTML.

The trigger to move from anonymous to a class is behaviour: the moment a component computes something, holds state, or performs a decision, it wants a unit test, and a unit test that has to render a template to exercise one branch is a slow test that tells you less.


{{-- THE FILE, which is the whole feature: no class, no namespace,
     no registration. --}}
{{-- resources/views/components/order-badge.blade.php --}}
@props([
    'status',
    'tone'    => null,
    'label'   => null,
    'compact' => false,
])

@php
    // A DEFAULT, so the component is usable with one argument. Note
    // this is a COMPUTATION in a view, which is the first hint that
    // the logic wants to be somewhere testable.
    $tone ??= match ($status) {
        'paid'     => 'success',
        'pending'  => 'info',
        'refunded' => 'muted',
        default    => 'warning',
    };
@endphp

<span {{ $attributes->merge(['class' => 'badge badge-' . $tone])
                        ->class(['badge-compact' => $compact]) }}>
    {{ $label ?? AppSupportOrderStatus::label($status) }}
</span>

{{-- AND THE USE. The tag name matches the file name, because the
     compiler resolves it by path — which is also why an anonymous
     component cannot be referenced from anywhere except by tag. --}}
<x-order-badge status="paid" />
<x-order-badge status="pending" :compact="true" />
<x-order-badge status="refunded" label="Refunded by support" />

{{-- THE ATTRIBUTE BAG, which is what makes the wrapper transparent:
     attributes NOT declared in @props land in $attributes and are
     rendered on the root element. --}}
<x-order-badge status="paid" id="badge-1" data-testid="paid-badge"
               class="mr-2" />
{{-- renders: <span id="badge-1" data-testid="paid-badge"
              class="badge badge-success mr-2"> --}}


{{-- THE CLASS-BASED EQUIVALENT, which needs two files and a
     registration, and is worth it the moment there is behaviour. --}}
// app/View/Components/OrderBadge.php
namespace AppViewComponents;

use IlluminateContractsViewView;
use IlluminateViewComponent;
use AppEnumsOrderStatus;

final class OrderBadge extends Component
{
    public function __construct(
        public readonly OrderStatus $status,
        public readonly bool $compact = false,
        public readonly ?string $label = null,
    ) {}

    public function tone(): string
    {
        // The mapping is a METHOD now, so it can be asserted on
        // without rendering anything.
        return match ($this->status) {
            OrderStatus::Paid     => 'success',
            OrderStatus::Pending  => 'info',
            OrderStatus::Refunded => 'muted',
        };
    }

    public function render(): View
    {
        return view('components.order-badge');
    }
}

{{-- resources/views/components/order-badge.blade.php --}}
@props(['status', 'compact' => false, 'label' => null])

<span {{ $attributes->merge(['class' => 'badge badge-' . $tone()])
                        ->class(['badge-compact' => $compact]) }}>
    {{ $label ?? AppSupportOrderStatus::label($status->value) }}
</span>

// app/Providers/AppServiceProvider.php
Blade::component('order-badge', OrderBadge::class);

{{-- AND THE TEST THAT ONLY EXISTS IN THE CLASS FORM: --}}
it('maps a refunded order to the muted tone', function () {
    $badge = new OrderBadge(OrderStatus::Refunded);
    expect($badge->tone())->toBe('muted');
});
{{-- No HTTP, no container, no template. The anonymous version of
     this rule lives in an @php block and can only be tested by
     rendering it — a slower test that asserts less. --}}

{{-- WHEN TO MOVE, AND THE TRIGGER IS ONE WORD: BEHAVIOUR. --}}
@props(['invoice'])

<div class="invoice">
    {{-- ...so now the template computes a total, calls a money
         formatter, and decides a display variant. A test for this
         has to render the template, which is slow, needs a
         database for the formatter's locale table, and asserts on
         a string rather than on a value. --}}
    <p>{{ $invoice->lines->sum('total')->format() }}</p>
</div>

{{-- THE DECISION, AS A TABLE: --}}
{{--   presentational fragment, no behaviour     -> anonymous
     needs an injected service                   -> class
     computes something worth asserting on        -> class
     tested per branch without rendering          -> class
     used once, from one partial, no tests         -> @include
                                                    by path --}}

{{-- AND WHY @include BY PATH IS IN THE TABLE: a component tag is
     a PUBLIC API, and adding one is a decision someone will come
     to depend on. A private helper included by path is reachable
     by a search for the include and by nothing else. --}}
{{-- resources/views/partials/order-row.blade.php --}}
@include('components.order-row', ['order' => $order])


{{-- THE FILE, which is the whole feature: no class, no namespace,
     no registration. --}}
{{-- resources/views/components/order-badge.blade.php --}}
@props([
    'status',
    'tone'    => null,
    'label'   => null,
    'compact' => false,
])

@php
    // A DEFAULT, so the component is usable with one argument. Note
    // this is a COMPUTATION in a view, which is the first hint that
    // the logic wants to be somewhere testable.
    $tone ??= match ($status) {
        'paid'     => 'success',
        'pending'  => 'info',
        'refunded' => 'muted',
        default    => 'warning',
    };
@endphp

<span {{ $attributes->merge(['class' => 'badge badge-' . $tone])
                        ->class(['badge-compact' => $compact]) }}>
    {{ $label ?? AppSupportOrderStatus::label($status) }}
</span>

{{-- AND THE USE. The tag name matches the file name, because the
     compiler resolves it by path — which is also why an anonymous
     component cannot be referenced from anywhere except by tag. --}}
<x-order-badge status="paid" />
<x-order-badge status="pending" :compact="true" />
<x-order-badge status="refunded" label="Refunded by support" />

{{-- THE ATTRIBUTE BAG, which is what makes the wrapper transparent:
     attributes NOT declared in @props land in $attributes and are
     rendered on the root element. --}}
<x-order-badge status="paid" id="badge-1" data-testid="paid-badge"
               class="mr-2" />
{{-- renders: <span id="badge-1" data-testid="paid-badge"
              class="badge badge-success mr-2"> --}}


{{-- THE CLASS-BASED EQUIVALENT, which needs two files and a
     registration, and is worth it the moment there is behaviour. --}}
// app/View/Components/OrderBadge.php
namespace AppViewComponents;

use IlluminateContractsViewView;
use IlluminateViewComponent;
use AppEnumsOrderStatus;

final class OrderBadge extends Component
{
    public function __construct(
        public readonly OrderStatus $status,
        public readonly bool $compact = false,
        public readonly ?string $label = null,
    ) {}

    public function tone(): string
    {
        // The mapping is a METHOD now, so it can be asserted on
        // without rendering anything.
        return match ($this->status) {
            OrderStatus::Paid     => 'success',
            OrderStatus::Pending  => 'info',
            OrderStatus::Refunded => 'muted',
        };
    }

    public function render(): View
    {
        return view('components.order-badge');
    }
}

{{-- resources/views/components/order-badge.blade.php --}}
@props(['status', 'compact' => false, 'label' => null])

<span {{ $attributes->merge(['class' => 'badge badge-' . $tone()])
                        ->class(['badge-compact' => $compact]) }}>
    {{ $label ?? AppSupportOrderStatus::label($status->value) }}
</span>

// app/Providers/AppServiceProvider.php
Blade::component('order-badge', OrderBadge::class);

{{-- AND THE TEST THAT ONLY EXISTS IN THE CLASS FORM: --}}
it('maps a refunded order to the muted tone', function () {
    $badge = new OrderBadge(OrderStatus::Refunded);
    expect($badge->tone())->toBe('muted');
});
{{-- No HTTP, no container, no template. The anonymous version of
     this rule lives in an @php block and can only be tested by
     rendering it — a slower test that asserts less. --}}

{{-- WHEN TO MOVE, AND THE TRIGGER IS ONE WORD: BEHAVIOUR. --}}
@props(['invoice'])

<div class="invoice">
    {{-- ...so now the template computes a total, calls a money
         formatter, and decides a display variant. A test for this
         has to render the template, which is slow, needs a
         database for the formatter's locale table, and asserts on
         a string rather than on a value. --}}
    <p>{{ $invoice->lines->sum('total')->format() }}</p>
</div>

{{-- THE DECISION, AS A TABLE: --}}
{{--   presentational fragment, no behaviour     -> anonymous
     needs an injected service                   -> class
     computes something worth asserting on        -> class
     tested per branch without rendering          -> class
     used once, from one partial, no tests         -> @include
                                                    by path --}}

{{-- AND WHY @include BY PATH IS IN THE TABLE: a component tag is
     a PUBLIC API, and adding one is a decision someone will come
     to depend on. A private helper included by path is reachable
     by a search for the include and by nothing else. --}}
{{-- resources/views/partials/order-row.blade.php --}}
@include('components.order-row', ['order' => $order])

5.2 Props, types and defaults

Documents prop declaration in anonymous components with the props directive, including defaults and docblock types.

<h4>Declaring, defaulting, and passing</h4> <p>A component declares the props it accepts, with a default for each. The declaration is what makes the tag readable and what makes an unpassed prop fall back rather than be undefined. There are two forms: a PHP array in the directive, and separate attributes. They are equivalent, and the array form is easier to read once there are more than two.</p> <p>The passing syntax has three shapes and they are not interchangeable. A prefixed colon passes a PHP expression. A bare attribute passes a string. And a bare attribute with a dot in its name is interpreted as an attribute of that name, not a prop — which is the mechanism behind the attribute bag and the source of a class of surprise where a component has a prop called <code>class</code> and receives the tag's class instead.</p> <h4>Types, and what the type does for you</h4> <p>An anonymous component's props are untyped, so a prop declared with a default of <code>false</code> receives a string when passed a bare attribute and a boolean when passed a prefixed one — and the component has to cope with both. A class-based component's constructor types its props, so the coercion happens once, at the boundary, and a wrong type is an error rather than a surprising value deep in the template.</p> <p>That is the substantive difference between anonymous and class components beyond the file count: a class component has a place where the data contract is enforced and can be read in one place, and an anonymous component's contract is a list of names in a template.</p>

Declaring, defaulting, and passing

A component declares the props it accepts, with a default for each. The declaration is what makes the tag readable and what makes an unpassed prop fall back rather than be undefined. There are two forms: a PHP array in the directive, and separate attributes. They are equivalent, and the array form is easier to read once there are more than two.

The passing syntax has three shapes and they are not interchangeable. A prefixed colon passes a PHP expression. A bare attribute passes a string. And a bare attribute with a dot in its name is interpreted as an attribute of that name, not a prop — which is the mechanism behind the attribute bag and the source of a class of surprise where a component has a prop called class and receives the tag's class instead.

Types, and what the type does for you

An anonymous component's props are untyped, so a prop declared with a default of false receives a string when passed a bare attribute and a boolean when passed a prefixed one — and the component has to cope with both. A class-based component's constructor types its props, so the coercion happens once, at the boundary, and a wrong type is an error rather than a surprising value deep in the template.

That is the substantive difference between anonymous and class components beyond the file count: a class component has a place where the data contract is enforced and can be read in one place, and an anonymous component's contract is a list of names in a template.

{{-- components/alert.blade.php --}}
@props([
    'type'  => 'info',
    'title' => null,
])

<div class="alert alert--{{ $type }}">
    @isset($title)
        <strong>{{ $title }}</strong>
    @endisset
    {{ $slot }}
</div>

Variables listed in @props are documented for tooling and extracted from the component data, so they do not appear in the attribute bag. A docblock supplies types for editors; in a class component the same contract is expressed as constructor promotion with a native type, enforced at runtime rather than only in documentation.

Defaults at the boundary. A default belongs in the component, not at every call site. Callers then override only what differs, and the component remains correct wherever it is used.


{{-- THE DECLARATION, both forms: --}}
@props(['status', 'tone' => null, 'compact' => false])

{{-- the separate-attribute form, which is the same thing --}}
@props('status')
@props('tone' => null)
@props('compact' => false)

{{-- THE THREE PASSING SYNTAXES, and they are not interchangeable. --}}
<x-order-badge status="paid" />
{{-- bare attribute: the STRING "paid". Never a boolean, never
     an expression, even when the prop is declared as a boolean. --}}

<x-order-badge :status="$order->status" />
{{-- prefixed: a PHP EXPRESSION. An enum instance, an int, a bool,
     a model. The only form that passes anything but a string. --}}

<x-order-badge :compact="$isCompact" />
{{-- NOT :compact="true", which passes the STRING "true" and is
     truthy for the wrong reason. --}}

{{-- THE DOT TRAP, and it is the mechanism behind the attribute bag: --}}
<x-alert class="text-red-500" />
{{-- 'class' is NOT a prop. It is an HTML attribute and it lands in
     $attributes, because the component's root element renders
     whatever is in the bag. So a component with a prop named
     'class' cannot receive one through this syntax at all. --}}

<x-alert :class="$classes" />
{{-- ^ THIS passes a PHP expression to the bag's 'class' entry,
     which is what you want when merging rather than naming. --}}

{{-- THE TYPE COERCION, and why it matters more in an anonymous
     component: --}}
@props(['compact' => false])
{{-- <x-badge compact />          compact === false   (the prop)
     <x-badge compact="true" />   compact === "true"  (a STRING)
     Both are truthy, so a template testing "if ($compact)" passes
     in both cases and fails in neither — until someone compares
     it: "if ($compact === true)" now fails for the string. --}}

{{-- A CLASS COMPONENT SOLVES IT AT THE BOUNDARY: --}}
final class OrderBadge extends Component
{
    public function __construct(
        public readonly OrderStatus $status,   {{-- an enum: a bare
                                                       string that is
                                                       not a valid
                                                       case throws
                                                       here, rather
                                                       than being
                                                       an unknown
                                                       value at
                                                       render time --}}
        public readonly bool $compact = false,
    ) {}
}
{{-- <x-order-badge status="paid" compact />  -> both correct,
     coerced once, and a wrong status is a TypeError naming the
     component and the parameter. --}}


{{-- THE DECLARATION, both forms: --}}
@props(['status', 'tone' => null, 'compact' => false])

{{-- the separate-attribute form, which is the same thing --}}
@props('status')
@props('tone' => null)
@props('compact' => false)

{{-- THE THREE PASSING SYNTAXES, and they are not interchangeable. --}}
<x-order-badge status="paid" />
{{-- bare attribute: the STRING "paid". Never a boolean, never
     an expression, even when the prop is declared as a boolean. --}}

<x-order-badge :status="$order->status" />
{{-- prefixed: a PHP EXPRESSION. An enum instance, an int, a bool,
     a model. The only form that passes anything but a string. --}}

<x-order-badge :compact="$isCompact" />
{{-- NOT :compact="true", which passes the STRING "true" and is
     truthy for the wrong reason. --}}

{{-- THE DOT TRAP, and it is the mechanism behind the attribute bag: --}}
<x-alert class="text-red-500" />
{{-- 'class' is NOT a prop. It is an HTML attribute and it lands in
     $attributes, because the component's root element renders
     whatever is in the bag. So a component with a prop named
     'class' cannot receive one through this syntax at all. --}}

<x-alert :class="$classes" />
{{-- ^ THIS passes a PHP expression to the bag's 'class' entry,
     which is what you want when merging rather than naming. --}}

{{-- THE TYPE COERCION, and why it matters more in an anonymous
     component: --}}
@props(['compact' => false])
{{-- <x-badge compact />          compact === false   (the prop)
     <x-badge compact="true" />   compact === "true"  (a STRING)
     Both are truthy, so a template testing "if ($compact)" passes
     in both cases and fails in neither — until someone compares
     it: "if ($compact === true)" now fails for the string. --}}

{{-- A CLASS COMPONENT SOLVES IT AT THE BOUNDARY: --}}

{
    public function __construct(
        public readonly OrderStatus $status,   {{-- an enum: a bare
                                                       string that is
                                                       not a valid
                                                       case throws
                                                       here, rather
                                                       than being
                                                       an unknown
                                                       value at
                                                       render time --}}
        public readonly bool $compact = false,
    ) {}
}
{{-- <x-order-badge status="paid" compact />  -> both correct,
     coerced once, and a wrong status is a TypeError naming the
     component and the parameter. --}}

5.3 The attribute bag and merging

Explains what lands in the attribute bag, how it is rendered, and how class and style merging interact with caller-supplied values.

Attributes that are not consumed as props or slots are collected into $attributes, a renderable bag that must be echoed explicitly and that escapes values:

<h4>Every attribute you did not declare</h4> <p>Anything passed to a component tag that is not a declared prop arrives in a single object, and the conventional way to render it is onto the root element. That one line is what makes a component transparent: a wrapper element that sets a class, an id, a data attribute or an event listener behaves as though it were not there, because the caller decides those.</p> <p>Without it, every wrapper component would swallow the caller's <code>class</code>, <code>id</code> and <code>data-*</code> attributes, and every use site would need an explicit prop for each one — which is a worse outcome than the wrapper existing. With it, the caller's attributes pass through and the component's own defaults merge with them.</p> <h4>Merging, and the precedence rule that has to be chosen</h4> <p>Merging two class strings requires deciding which wins. The conventional rule is that the caller's class wins, because the caller has more specific knowledge: a component that sets <code>btn btn-primary</code> and is given <code>w-full</code> should produce both. The alternative — the component's class winning — is defensible for a component whose styling is a contract, and it is the wrong default for anything a caller is likely to restyle.</p> <p>The non-class attributes have a different question: <code>id</code> in particular cannot be merged, because two elements cannot share one. A component that sets an id and receives one should either replace it or produce a conflict, and the safe default is to let the caller's value win while keeping the component's own for its internal references.</p> <h4>The things that must not be forwarded</h4> <p>A wrapper that spreads every attribute onto its root can leak through things that have no business being there: an event handler onto a non-interactive element, a <code>name</code> and a <code>value</code> in a context that is not a form, or a <code>href</code> that turns a span into a link without the semantics that come with it. This is why a wrapper that renders a specific element should filter rather than forward, and why the decision of which element a component's root is has accessibility consequences rather than only styling ones.</p>

Every attribute you did not declare

Anything passed to a component tag that is not a declared prop arrives in a single object, and the conventional way to render it is onto the root element. That one line is what makes a component transparent: a wrapper element that sets a class, an id, a data attribute or an event listener behaves as though it were not there, because the caller decides those.

Without it, every wrapper component would swallow the caller's class, id and data-* attributes, and every use site would need an explicit prop for each one — which is a worse outcome than the wrapper existing. With it, the caller's attributes pass through and the component's own defaults merge with them.

Merging, and the precedence rule that has to be chosen

Merging two class strings requires deciding which wins. The conventional rule is that the caller's class wins, because the caller has more specific knowledge: a component that sets btn btn-primary and is given w-full should produce both. The alternative — the component's class winning — is defensible for a component whose styling is a contract, and it is the wrong default for anything a caller is likely to restyle.

The non-class attributes have a different question: id in particular cannot be merged, because two elements cannot share one. A component that sets an id and receives one should either replace it or produce a conflict, and the safe default is to let the caller's value win while keeping the component's own for its internal references.

The things that must not be forwarded

A wrapper that spreads every attribute onto its root can leak through things that have no business being there: an event handler onto a non-interactive element, a name and a value in a context that is not a form, or a href that turns a span into a link without the semantics that come with it. This is why a wrapper that renders a specific element should filter rather than forward, and why the decision of which element a component's root is has accessibility consequences rather than only styling ones.

{{-- components/button.blade.php --}}
<button {{ $attributes->merge(['class' => 'btn'])
              ->class(['btn--' => $variant]) }}
    type="submit">
    {{ $slot }}
</button>

{{-- usage --}}
<x-button variant="danger" class="w-full" data-test="save">Delete</x-button>

The two-stage merge yields class="btn btn--danger w-full" and preserves data-test="save". Ordering is fixed: the component’s own classes first, then the caller’s. The class and style arguments are treated specially by merge(); other attributes are appended, and a component that sets an attribute unconditionally overrides the caller.


{{-- THE ONE LINE, and it is what makes a wrapper transparent: --}}
{{-- resources/views/components/x-card.blade.php --}}
@props(['padding' => 'md'])

<div {{ $attributes->merge(['class' => 'card card-' . $padding])
                     ->class(['card-interactive' => $interactive]) }}>
    {{ $slot }}
</div>

{{-- WHICH MEANS THE CALLER DECIDES THESE: --}}
<x-card id="summary" class="w-full max-w-md" data-testid="summary-card"
       aria-labelledby="summary-heading" x-on:click="open">
    ...
</x-card>
{{-- renders: <div id="summary" class="card card-md w-full max-w-md"
            data-testid="summary-card" aria-labelledby=... > --}}

{{-- THE MERGE PRECEDENCE, and it has to be chosen deliberately. --}}
<x-card class="w-full" />
{{-- with caller-wins:  class="card card-md w-full"
     with component-wins: class="w-full"
     ^ caller-wins is the right default: the component describes
       what it IS, the caller describes what it NEEDS. --}}

{{-- NON-MERGEABLE ATTRIBUTES. id cannot be merged — two elements
     cannot share one — and the component needs its own for its
     internal references: --}}
@props(['headingId' => null])

<section {{ $attributes->merge(['id' => $headingId ?? 'card-' . $uid]) }}>
    <h2 id="{{ $attributes->get('id') ? $attributes->get('id') . '-heading' : $uid . '-heading' }}">
        ...
    </h2>
</section>

{{-- THE THINGS THAT MUST NOT BE FORWARDED, which is why a wrapper
     for a specific element filters rather than spreads. --}}
{{-- resources/views/components/x-icon-button.blade.php --}}
@props(['icon', 'label', 'href' => null])

@if ($href)
    {{-- a link needs the semantics, not just the handler --}}
    <a href="{{ $href }}" {{ $attributes->only(['class', 'id', 'aria-*', 'data-*'])
                                  ->merge(['class' => 'btn btn-ghost']) }}>
        <x-icon :name="$icon" aria-hidden="true" />
        <span class="sr-only">{{ $label }}</span>
    </a>
@else
    {{-- a button: the handler belongs, a name and value do not --}}
    <button type="button" {{ $attributes->only(['class', 'id', 'aria-*', 'data-*',
                                                 'wire:click', 'x-on:click', 'disabled'])
                                   ->merge(['class' => 'btn btn-ghost']) }}>
        <x-icon :name="$icon" aria-hidden="true" />
        <span class="sr-only">{{ $label }}</span>
    </button>
@endif

{{-- WHY FILTERING MATTERS AND NOT JUST STYLE. Forwarding
     x-on:click onto a <span> produces a click handler on a
     non-interactive element: unreachable by keyboard, invisible to
     a screen reader, and unstyled as a control. It is an
     accessibility defect produced by a convenience. --}}

{{-- THE ESCAPE HATCH, for the case where forwarding everything is
     genuinely what you want: --}}
<div {{ $attributes }}>{{ $slot }}</div>
{{-- correct for a purely structural wrapper with no semantics of
     its own; wrong for anything interactive. --}}


{{-- THE ONE LINE, and it is what makes a wrapper transparent: --}}
{{-- resources/views/components/x-card.blade.php --}}
@props(['padding' => 'md'])

<div {{ $attributes->merge(['class' => 'card card-' . $padding])
                     ->class(['card-interactive' => $interactive]) }}>
    {{ $slot }}
</div>

{{-- WHICH MEANS THE CALLER DECIDES THESE: --}}
<x-card id="summary" class="w-full max-w-md" data-testid="summary-card"
       aria-labelledby="summary-heading" x-on:click="open">
    ...
</x-card>
{{-- renders: <div id="summary" class="card card-md w-full max-w-md"
            data-testid="summary-card" aria-labelledby=... > --}}

{{-- THE MERGE PRECEDENCE, and it has to be chosen deliberately. --}}
<x-card class="w-full" />
{{-- with caller-wins:  class="card card-md w-full"
     with component-wins: class="w-full"
     ^ caller-wins is the right default: the component describes
       what it IS, the caller describes what it NEEDS. --}}

{{-- NON-MERGEABLE ATTRIBUTES. id cannot be merged — two elements
     cannot share one — and the component needs its own for its
     internal references: --}}
@props(['headingId' => null])

<section {{ $attributes->merge(['id' => $headingId ?? 'card-' . $uid]) }}>
    <h2 id="{{ $attributes->get('id') ? $attributes->get('id') . '-heading' : $uid . '-heading' }}">
        ...
    </h2>
</section>

{{-- THE THINGS THAT MUST NOT BE FORWARDED, which is why a wrapper
     for a specific element filters rather than spreads. --}}
{{-- resources/views/components/x-icon-button.blade.php --}}
@props(['icon', 'label', 'href' => null])

@if ($href)
    {{-- a link needs the semantics, not just the handler --}}
    <a href="{{ $href }}" {{ $attributes->only(['class', 'id', 'aria-*', 'data-*'])
                                  ->merge(['class' => 'btn btn-ghost']) }}>
        <x-icon :name="$icon" aria-hidden="true" />
        <span class="sr-only">{{ $label }}</span>
    </a>
@else
    {{-- a button: the handler belongs, a name and value do not --}}
    <button type="button" {{ $attributes->only(['class', 'id', 'aria-*', 'data-*',
                                                 'wire:click', 'x-on:click', 'disabled'])
                                   ->merge(['class' => 'btn btn-ghost']) }}>
        <x-icon :name="$icon" aria-hidden="true" />
        <span class="sr-only">{{ $label }}</span>
    </button>
@endif

{{-- WHY FILTERING MATTERS AND NOT JUST STYLE. Forwarding
     x-on:click onto a <span> produces a click handler on a
     non-interactive element: unreachable by keyboard, invisible to
     a screen reader, and unstyled as a control. It is an
     accessibility defect produced by a convenience. --}}

{{-- THE ESCAPE HATCH, for the case where forwarding everything is
     genuinely what you want: --}}
<div {{ $attributes }}>{{ $slot }}</div>
{{-- correct for a purely structural wrapper with no semantics of
     its own; wrong for anything interactive. --}}

5.4 Slots

Documents default content, named slots and the slot directive for supplying default markup from the component side.

<h4>Two kinds of content, and the distinction is real</h4> <p>A component has one default slot for its main content and any number of named slots for specific regions. The distinction between them is not naming convention: it is that a named slot renders only when the child fills it, and the component can therefore offer a default for it. The default slot has no such property, because the child always provides it.</p> <p>That asymmetry is what decides which is which. A component with an optional sidebar takes a named slot and renders its own default when the slot is absent. A component whose whole purpose is to wrap content takes the default slot, and there is nothing to default it to.</p> <h4>Multiple slots, and the ordering that matters</h4> <p>Several named slots are declared in the component template in whatever order they should render, and a child fills them independently of the order it writes them. This is the same property layout sections have and it is worth being explicit about: a child's file order does not determine render order, the component's does. Slots are the fix for the specific problem where a section name was a typo and a hole appeared — a missing slot is a component that renders without that region, and a component written with <code>@isset</code> around the region turns that into a default rather than a defect.</p> <h4>Forwarding, and why it is a trap</h4> <p>A slot's content can be captured into a variable and rendered somewhere else in the component. That is occasionally necessary — a header that appears both at the top and in a collapsed mobile menu — and it is more often a way to build a component whose structure nobody can determine by reading it. The test is whether a reader can say, from the component template alone, in what order the output appears.</p>

Two kinds of content, and the distinction is real

A component has one default slot for its main content and any number of named slots for specific regions. The distinction between them is not naming convention: it is that a named slot renders only when the child fills it, and the component can therefore offer a default for it. The default slot has no such property, because the child always provides it.

That asymmetry is what decides which is which. A component with an optional sidebar takes a named slot and renders its own default when the slot is absent. A component whose whole purpose is to wrap content takes the default slot, and there is nothing to default it to.

Multiple slots, and the ordering that matters

Several named slots are declared in the component template in whatever order they should render, and a child fills them independently of the order it writes them. This is the same property layout sections have and it is worth being explicit about: a child's file order does not determine render order, the component's does. Slots are the fix for the specific problem where a section name was a typo and a hole appeared — a missing slot is a component that renders without that region, and a component written with @isset around the region turns that into a default rather than a defect.

Forwarding, and why it is a trap

A slot's content can be captured into a variable and rendered somewhere else in the component. That is occasionally necessary — a header that appears both at the top and in a collapsed mobile menu — and it is more often a way to build a component whose structure nobody can determine by reading it. The test is whether a reader can say, from the component template alone, in what order the output appears.

{{-- components/card.blade.php --}}
<div {{ $attributes->merge(['class' => 'card']) }}>
    <header>{{ $title }}</header>
    <div>{{ $slot }}</div>
    @isset($footer)
        <footer>{{ $footer }}</footer>
    @endisset
</div>

{{-- usage --}}
<x-card title="Billing">
    <p>Invoices for the current period.</p>
    <x-slot:footer>
        <a href="/invoices">All invoices</a>
    </x-slot:footer>
</x-card>

A slot the caller omits evaluates to an empty string, so an @isset guard is the correct pattern for optional regions. For richer defaults, @slot('name', 'default content') declares a named slot from the component side. Named slots appear in the component data like any other variable; in a class component they must be declared public readonly to avoid a runtime conflict.


{{-- THE DECLARATION, and the asymmetry that decides the shape: --}}
{{-- resources/views/components/x-panel.blade.php --}}
@props(['title' => null, 'variant' => 'default'])

<section {{ $attributes->merge(['class' => 'panel panel-' . $variant]) }}>
    @if ($title || isset($actions))
        <header class="panel-header">
            {{-- THE DEFAULT SLOT, used when the child fills one. The
                 component cannot tell in advance whether it will,
                 so it renders the title alone. --}}
            @isset($title)
                <h2>{{ $title }}</h2>
            @endif
            {{-- A NAMED SLOT with a fallback: --}}
            @isset($actions)
                <div class="panel-actions">{{ $actions }}</div>
            @endif
        </header>
    @endif

    <div class="panel-body">{{ $slot }}</div>

    @isset($footer)
        <footer class="panel-footer">{{ $footer }}</footer>
    @endif
</section>

{{-- THE USE, and note the ORDER DOES NOT MATTER — the component
     decides, not the child. --}}
<x-panel title="{{ __('orders.title') }}" variant="compact">
    {{-- the default slot: the main content --}}
    <x-order-lines :order="$order" />

    {{-- the named slots, written in an order that is NOT the
         render order, which is the component's business --}}
    <x-slot:footer>
        <x-pagination :paginator="$orders" />
    </x-slot:footer>

    <x-slot:actions>
        <x-button type="button" @click="exportCsv">{{ __('export') }}</x-button>
    </x-slot:actions>
</x-panel>

{{-- FORWARDING, which is occasionally necessary and more often a
     trap: --}}
@php
    // The header appears twice: once in the collapsed mobile view
    // and once in the expanded one. Capturing a slot lets it render
    // in both places.
    $header = $header ?? null;
@endphp

<div class="mobile">{{ $header }}</div>
<div class="desktop">{{ $header ?? $slot }}</div>
{{-- legal, and this template can no longer be understood by reading
     it: which content renders, and in what order, depends on which
     slots were filled. --}}

{{-- THE TEST FOR A SLOT-HEAVY COMPONENT: can a reader say, from
     the COMPONENT template alone, in what order the output
     appears? If not, the component needs restructuring. --}}

{{-- THE SLOT THAT IS NOT A SLOT, and it is a common workaround
     with a real cost: --}}
<x-panel :title="$title">
    {{-- ...passing "title" as CONTENT rather than as a prop, because
         the prop does not exist. It renders in the body, not in the
         header, and nothing warns. Declaring the prop is the fix. --}}
</x-panel>

{{-- SLOT CONTENT IS LAZY, which is worth knowing: --}}
<x-slot:title>
    {{-- this is evaluated in the CHILD's scope, not the
         component's. It closes over the child's variables and
         cannot see the component's props. --}}
        {{ $invoice->number }}
    </x-slot:title>
{{-- so the component cannot supply a variable the slot body
     refers to. The reverse also holds: a variable assigned in a
     slot body is not visible after the slot. --}}


{{-- THE DECLARATION, and the asymmetry that decides the shape: --}}
{{-- resources/views/components/x-panel.blade.php --}}
@props(['title' => null, 'variant' => 'default'])

<section {{ $attributes->merge(['class' => 'panel panel-' . $variant]) }}>
    @if ($title || isset($actions))
        <header class="panel-header">
            {{-- THE DEFAULT SLOT, used when the child fills one. The
                 component cannot tell in advance whether it will,
                 so it renders the title alone. --}}
            @isset($title)
                <h2>{{ $title }}</h2>
            @endif
            {{-- A NAMED SLOT with a fallback: --}}
            @isset($actions)
                <div class="panel-actions">{{ $actions }}</div>
            @endif
        </header>
    @endif

    <div class="panel-body">{{ $slot }}</div>

    @isset($footer)
        <footer class="panel-footer">{{ $footer }}</footer>
    @endif
</section>

{{-- THE USE, and note the ORDER DOES NOT MATTER — the component
     decides, not the child. --}}
<x-panel title="{{ __('orders.title') }}" variant="compact">
    {{-- the default slot: the main content --}}
    <x-order-lines :order="$order" />

    {{-- the named slots, written in an order that is NOT the
         render order, which is the component's business --}}
    <x-slot:footer>
        <x-pagination :paginator="$orders" />
    </x-slot:footer>

    <x-slot:actions>
        <x-button type="button" @click="exportCsv">{{ __('export') }}</x-button>
    </x-slot:actions>
</x-panel>

{{-- FORWARDING, which is occasionally necessary and more often a
     trap: --}}
@php
    // The header appears twice: once in the collapsed mobile view
    // and once in the expanded one. Capturing a slot lets it render
    // in both places.
    $header = $header ?? null;
@endphp

<div class="mobile">{{ $header }}</div>
<div class="desktop">{{ $header ?? $slot }}</div>
{{-- legal, and this template can no longer be understood by reading
     it: which content renders, and in what order, depends on which
     slots were filled. --}}

{{-- THE TEST FOR A SLOT-HEAVY COMPONENT: can a reader say, from
     the COMPONENT template alone, in what order the output
     appears? If not, the component needs restructuring. --}}

{{-- THE SLOT THAT IS NOT A SLOT, and it is a common workaround
     with a real cost: --}}
<x-panel :title="$title">
    {{-- ...passing "title" as CONTENT rather than as a prop, because
         the prop does not exist. It renders in the body, not in the
         header, and nothing warns. Declaring the prop is the fix. --}}
</x-panel>

{{-- SLOT CONTENT IS LAZY, which is worth knowing: --}}
<x-slot:title>
    {{-- this is evaluated in the CHILD's scope, not the
         component's. It closes over the child's variables and
         cannot see the component's props. --}}
        {{ $invoice->number }}
    </x-slot:title>
{{-- so the component cannot supply a variable the slot body
     refers to. The reverse also holds: a variable assigned in a
     slot body is not visible after the slot. --}}

5.5 Class-based components

Documents class-based components, which add a constructor, lifecycle hooks and registration to the anonymous component model.

<h4>A class when there is behaviour</h4> <p>A class-based component has two files: a PHP class and a Blade view. The class receives the constructor dependencies, holds the state, and can have methods; the view renders. The split exists so the logic can be tested without rendering HTML, which is the reason to choose this form.</p> <p>The constructor's parameters are the component's props, and they are the interface. The framework resolves the dependencies through the container, so a service can be injected without being passed by the caller — which is the capability an anonymous component does not have, and the reason a component that queries belongs here.</p> <h4>What the view can reach</h4> <p>The view receives every public property and method of the class, so a method used for display is written as a method and called without parentheses on the property path. A method that computes something is better as a method than as a <code>@php</code> block in the view, because it can be tested and because it has a name that a search can find.</p> <h4>The cost, stated honestly</h4> <p>Two files, a namespace registration, an autoload entry, and a resolution through the container on every render. For a component that is used in forty places and contains a query, that is obviously the right trade. For a component used once with no behaviour, it is overhead — which is why both forms exist and why the deciding question is whether there is logic worth testing.</p>

A class when there is behaviour

A class-based component has two files: a PHP class and a Blade view. The class receives the constructor dependencies, holds the state, and can have methods; the view renders. The split exists so the logic can be tested without rendering HTML, which is the reason to choose this form.

The constructor's parameters are the component's props, and they are the interface. The framework resolves the dependencies through the container, so a service can be injected without being passed by the caller — which is the capability an anonymous component does not have, and the reason a component that queries belongs here.

What the view can reach

The view receives every public property and method of the class, so a method used for display is written as a method and called without parentheses on the property path. A method that computes something is better as a method than as a @php block in the view, because it can be tested and because it has a name that a search can find.

The cost, stated honestly

Two files, a namespace registration, an autoload entry, and a resolution through the container on every render. For a component that is used in forty places and contains a query, that is obviously the right trade. For a component used once with no behaviour, it is overhead — which is why both forms exist and why the deciding question is whether there is logic worth testing.

// app/View/Components/OrderSummary.php
final class OrderSummary extends Component
{
    public function __construct(
        public Order $order,
        public bool $compact = false,
    ) {}

    public function shouldRender(): bool
    {
        return $this->order->lines->isNotEmpty();
    }
}

Registration is explicit, either as an attribute on the class or in a provider:

// app/Providers/AppServiceProvider.php
Blade::component(OrderSummary::class, 'order-summary');

A class component adds three capabilities an anonymous file lacks: constructor injection of services, a shouldRender() guard that can skip output entirely, and the ability to register a shared instance. The cost is one class per component and a slower resolution path, since the container is involved.

Choose one. Do not convert an anonymous component to a class unless it needs behaviour. Presentation components are not improved by a constructor.


{{-- THE TWO FILES. --}}

// app/View/Components/OrderSummary.php
namespace AppViewComponents;

use IlluminateContractsViewView;
use IlluminateViewComponent;
use AppServicesPricingService;

final class OrderSummary extends Component
{
    public function __construct(
        public readonly Order  $order,
        public readonly string $currency,
        private readonly PricingService $pricing,
    ) {}

    {{-- A METHOD, so the computation has a name, is unit-testable
         without rendering, and can be found by a search for the
         rule it implements. --}}
    public function total(): Money
    {
        return $this->pricing->totalFor($this->order);
    }

    public function hasDiscount(): bool
    {
        return $this->order->discount->isNotEmpty();
    }

    public function render(): View
    {
        return view('components.order-summary');
    }
}

{{-- THE VIEW, which reaches every public property and method. --}}
{{-- resources/views/components/order-summary.blade.php --}}
@props(['order', 'currency'])

<div {{ $attributes->merge(['class' => 'summary']) }}>
    <h2>{{ __('orders.summary_title', ['ref' => $order->reference]) }}</h2>

    {{-- A METHOD CALLED WITHOUT PARENTHESES. Blade's property
         path finds it. --}}
    @if (hasDiscount())
        <p class="discount">
            {{ __('orders.discount', ['amount' => $order->discount->format($currency)]) }}
        </p>
    @endif

    <p class="total">{{ total()->format($currency) }}</p>

    {{-- THE PRIVATE PROPERTY IS NOT REACHABLE, which is the point:
         the pricing service is an implementation detail of the
         component and cannot be called from the template. --}}
</div>

{{-- THE REGISTRATION, which the anonymous form does not need: --}}
// app/Providers/AppServiceProvider.php
Blade::component('order-summary', OrderSummary::class);

{{-- AND THE USE, with the service injected and never mentioned: --}}
<x-order-summary :order="$order" :currency="$order->currency->code" />
{{-- PricingService is resolved from the container. The caller
     neither passes it nor knows about it. --}}

{{-- WHY THIS IS TESTABLE WITHOUT RENDERING: --}}
// tests/Unit/OrderSummaryTest.php
it('shows a discount when one is applied', function () {
    $order = Order::factory()->withDiscount(500)->make();
    $pricing = Mockery::mock(PricingService::class);
    $pricing->shouldReceive('totalFor')->once()->with($order)
          ->andReturn(Money::of(9500, 'GBP'));

    $component = new OrderSummary($order, 'GBP', $pricing);

    expect($component->hasDiscount())->toBeTrue()
        ->and($component->total()->amount())->toBe(9500);
});
{{-- Four assertions and no HTTP, no template, no container. The
     anonymous form could only be tested by rendering it, which is a
     slower test that asserts less. --}}


{{-- THE TWO FILES. --}}

5.6 View properties

Documents the view property that points a class component at its template, and the path resolution rules that apply.

<h4>Properties, and when they are not a list</h4> <p>A component can declare its props, compute values for the view, and pass arbitrary state to the template. The first is the interface, the second is where a class component earns its cost, and the third is a convenience that becomes a problem when it carries more than a handful of keys.</p> <p>The property that makes a class component worth its overhead is computed state: a value derived from the props, cached for the render. Without it, every value the view needs is computed in the view, which means either a <code>@php</code> block per value or a method call per use — and a method call per use recomputes when the same value is used twice.</p> <h4>Public, protected, private</h4> <p>A public property is visible to the template. A protected one is not — the template's scope does not inherit from the class in a way that exposes it, and reaching a private value through a public accessor is the explicit way to allow it. This matters for a component whose constructor takes a service: the template should be able to call the methods that produce display values and not be able to call the service itself.</p> <h4>Passing arbitrary state, and the limit</h4> <p>The data array on a component carries anything to the view that is not a prop. It is useful for computed bundles and it becomes a problem above a handful of keys, because at that point the boundary between "prop" and "state" has stopped being meaningful and a reader cannot tell which values the component requires. The honest limit is a dozen; past it, the computed values want to be methods with names rather than keys in an array.</p>

Properties, and when they are not a list

A component can declare its props, compute values for the view, and pass arbitrary state to the template. The first is the interface, the second is where a class component earns its cost, and the third is a convenience that becomes a problem when it carries more than a handful of keys.

The property that makes a class component worth its overhead is computed state: a value derived from the props, cached for the render. Without it, every value the view needs is computed in the view, which means either a @php block per value or a method call per use — and a method call per use recomputes when the same value is used twice.

Public, protected, private

A public property is visible to the template. A protected one is not — the template's scope does not inherit from the class in a way that exposes it, and reaching a private value through a public accessor is the explicit way to allow it. This matters for a component whose constructor takes a service: the template should be able to call the methods that produce display values and not be able to call the service itself.

Passing arbitrary state, and the limit

The data array on a component carries anything to the view that is not a prop. It is useful for computed bundles and it becomes a problem above a handful of keys, because at that point the boundary between "prop" and "state" has stopped being meaningful and a reader cannot tell which values the component requires. The honest limit is a dozen; past it, the computed values want to be methods with names rather than keys in an array.

public ?string $view = 'components.order-summary';

An unset $view derives the template from the class location: a component in app/View/Components defaults to components.order-summary. Components in a package namespace resolve through that package’s registered view namespace instead. Setting the property explicitly is required when the two do not line up, and is the usual fix for a view-not-found error on a published package component.

Exercises 5

  1. Build an alert component with typed props, a default variant and a merged class bag, then assert the rendered class string.
  2. Add a named slot to the card component and verify the footer is omitted when the caller supplies none.
  3. Convert one anonymous component to a class component with constructor injection, and compare render cost.
  4. Register a component alias and resolve a view-not-found error caused by an unset view property.

{{-- A PROPERTY THAT MAKES THE CLASS WORTH IT: computed state,
     cached for the render so a value used twice is computed
     once. --}}
// app/View/Components/OrderSummary.php
final class OrderSummary extends Component
{
    private ?Money $total = null;   {{-- PRIVATE: not reachable from
                                          the template, which is the
                                          point --}}

    public function __construct(
        public readonly Order $order,
        public readonly string $currency,
        private readonly PricingService $pricing,
    ) {}

    public function total(): Money
    {
        // MEMOISED. Without it, a template that shows the total in
        // three places triggers three computations — and each one
        // may be a query.
        return $this->total ??= $this->pricing->totalFor($this->order);
    }

    public function render(): View|View
    {
        return view('components.order-summary', [
            {{-- ARBITRARY STATE: values the template needs that are
                 not props and not methods-on-demand. Useful as a
                 bundle, and a problem above about a dozen keys,
                 because at that point a reader cannot tell which
                 values the component REQUIRES. --}}
            'lines'      => $this->order->lines,
            'canRefund'  => $this->order->canBeRefunded(),
            'taxRate'    => config('billing.tax_rate'),
        ]);
    }
}
{{-- the view reaches these by name: {{ $canRefund }}, {{ $taxRate }} --}}

{{-- PUBLIC, PROTECTED, PRIVATE, AND WHAT EACH MEANS TO THE VIEW: --}}
class Widget extends Component
{
    public string $label;              {{-- reachable: {{ $label }} --}}
    protected array $config;           {{-- NOT reachable --}}
    private Service $service;          {{-- NOT reachable --}}

    public function __construct(public readonly Order $order) {}

    {{-- An explicit accessor is how a protected value becomes
         available, and it is a deliberate act rather than an
         accident of visibility. --}}
    public function currency(): string
    {
        return $this->config['currency'];
    }
}

{{-- THE LIMIT, and it is worth stating as a number. --}}
public function render(): View
{
    return view('components.widget', [
        'a' => ..., 'b' => ..., 'c' => ..., 'd' => ..., 'e' => ...,
        // ...twelve keys later, a reader cannot tell which values
        // the component REQUIRES and which are conveniences. At
        // that point the computed values want to be METHODS with
        // names, because a method is a thing you can read at the
        // point of use.
        'f' => ..., 'g' => ...,
    ]);
}


{{-- A PROPERTY THAT MAKES THE CLASS WORTH IT: computed state,
     cached for the render so a value used twice is computed
     once. --}}
// app/View/Components/OrderSummary.php
final class OrderSummary extends Component
{
    private ?Money $total = null;   {{-- PRIVATE: not reachable from
                                          the template, which is the
                                          point --}}

    public function __construct(
        public readonly Order $order,
        public readonly string $currency,
        private readonly PricingService $pricing,
    ) {}

    public function total(): Money
    {
        // MEMOISED. Without it, a template that shows the total in
        // three places triggers three computations — and each one
        // may be a query.
        return $this->total ??= $this->pricing->totalFor($this->order);
    }

    public function render(): View|View
    {
        return view('components.order-summary', [
            {{-- ARBITRARY STATE: values the template needs that are
                 not props and not methods-on-demand. Useful as a
                 bundle, and a problem above about a dozen keys,
                 because at that point a reader cannot tell which
                 values the component REQUIRES. --}}
            'lines'      => $this->order->lines,
            'canRefund'  => $this->order->canBeRefunded(),
            'taxRate'    => config('billing.tax_rate'),
        ]);
    }
}
{{-- the view reaches these by name: {{ $canRefund }}, {{ $taxRate }} --}}

{{-- PUBLIC, PROTECTED, PRIVATE, AND WHAT EACH MEANS TO THE VIEW: --}}
class Widget extends Component
{
    public string $label;              {{-- reachable: {{ $label }} --}}
    protected array $config;           {{-- NOT reachable --}}
    private Service $service;          {{-- NOT reachable --}}

    public function __construct(public readonly Order $order) {}

    {{-- An explicit accessor is how a protected value becomes
         available, and it is a deliberate act rather than an
         accident of visibility. --}}

    {
        return $this->config['currency'];
    }
}

{{-- THE LIMIT, and it is worth stating as a number. --}}

{
    return view('components.widget', [
        'a' => ..., 'b' => ..., 'c' => ..., 'd' => ..., 'e' => ...,
        // ...twelve keys later, a reader cannot tell which values
        // the component REQUIRES and which are conveniences. At
        // that point the computed values want to be METHODS with
        // names, because a method is a thing you can read at the
        // point of use.
        'f' => ..., 'g' => ...,
    ]);
}

6 · Namespaces, Dynamic Components and View Composers

6.1 The component namespace6.2 Registering namespaces6.3 Dynamic components6.4 View composers6.5 Sharing data across views6.6 Choosing where data comes from

Component namespaces make a component library addressable from anywhere, dynamic components resolve a tag name at runtime, and composers inject data at the point a view renders. This chapter documents all three and the decision that separates them.

6.1 The component namespace

Documents the default anonymous component namespace, the vendor override directory, and the colon-prefixed form used by packages.

By default <x-alert> searches resources/views/components and then resources/views/vendor/components, which lets a published component override a vendor one. Additional directories are addressed with a colon in the tag:

<h4>Why components have namespaces</h4> <p>Every component tag is resolved by name, and a flat namespace means a generic tag name like <code>card</code> in one application collides with a <code>card</code> in a package. The namespace prefix is the mechanism that prevents that, and it maps a tag prefix onto a directory — the same shape as view namespaces, and for the same reason.</p> <p>The convention that falls out of it is that an application's own components carry no prefix and live in the default directory, while a package's components carry the package name. That convention is why a tag reads as <code>x-order-badge</code> rather than <code>x-acme-order-badge</code> in an application, and why the prefix only appears in the package's own tests.</p> <h4>Resolution order, and what a collision actually does</h4> <p>The framework searches the registered namespaces and then the application's own components directory. The first match wins, which means an application's component with the same name as a package's overrides it — silently, and with no diagnostic. That is usually the intent and occasionally a surprise, and the practical consequence is that overriding a package component is done by creating a file with the same relative name in the application's directory, not by reconfiguring anything.</p> <h4>Where the namespace comes from, and why it is a provider's job</h4> <p>A package registers its component namespace from its service provider, which runs at boot. That is why a component is unavailable before providers boot, why a component referenced in a view cached before the provider registered fails only after a deploy rather than at build time, and why a component namespace registered conditionally is a debugging problem worth avoiding.</p>

Why components have namespaces

Every component tag is resolved by name, and a flat namespace means a generic tag name like card in one application collides with a card in a package. The namespace prefix is the mechanism that prevents that, and it maps a tag prefix onto a directory — the same shape as view namespaces, and for the same reason.

The convention that falls out of it is that an application's own components carry no prefix and live in the default directory, while a package's components carry the package name. That convention is why a tag reads as x-order-badge rather than x-acme-order-badge in an application, and why the prefix only appears in the package's own tests.

Resolution order, and what a collision actually does

The framework searches the registered namespaces and then the application's own components directory. The first match wins, which means an application's component with the same name as a package's overrides it — silently, and with no diagnostic. That is usually the intent and occasionally a surprise, and the practical consequence is that overriding a package component is done by creating a file with the same relative name in the application's directory, not by reconfiguring anything.

Where the namespace comes from, and why it is a provider's job

A package registers its component namespace from its service provider, which runs at boot. That is why a component is unavailable before providers boot, why a component referenced in a view cached before the provider registered fails only after a deploy rather than at build time, and why a component namespace registered conditionally is a debugging problem worth avoiding.

<x-alerts::notice :level="$level" />
<x-marketing.hero :eyebrow="$text" />

{{-- components/marketing/hero.blade.php --}}

Namespacing is what lets a package ship components under its own prefix without colliding with application components, and what allows an application to override one specific package component by publishing it to the same namespace.


{{-- THE CONVENTION, and it is why a tag in an application has no
     prefix while the same tag in the package's tests does. --}}
{{-- application:  resources/views/components/order-badge.blade.php
       -> <x-order-badge />  --}}

{{-- package:  src/resources/views/components/order-badge.blade.php
       -> <x-acme-order-badge />  in this package's tests
          <x-order-badge />     inside an app that installed it --}}

{{-- THE MAPPING, which is the whole mechanism: a prefix and a
     directory. --}}
// src/Acme/Ui/UiServiceProvider.php
public function boot(): void
{
    $this->loadViewsFrom(__DIR__ . '/../resources/views', 'acme');
}

{{-- SO <x-acme-order-badge> resolves to
       src/../resources/views/components/order-badge.blade.php,
     and after installation <x-order-badge> resolves to the same
     file via the framework's default search path. --}}

{{-- RESOLUTION ORDER, AND WHAT A COLLISION ACTUALLY DOES: --}}
{{-- The framework searches the registered namespaces, then the
     application's components directory. The FIRST match wins, so
     an application component named the same as a package's
     overrides it — SILENTLY, and with no diagnostic. --}}
{{-- To override a package component, create a file with the same
     relative name: --}}
{{-- resources/views/components/order-badge.blade.php  <- yours,
     shadows the package's. No configuration, no registration. --}}

{{-- WHICH IS USUALLY THE INTENT and occasionally a surprise, and
     the way to tell the difference is to look for the file: --}}
// An Artisan command, because "which card component is this?"
// is a question that comes up on every large project.
Artisan::command('view:components {filter?}', function (?string $filter = null) {
    $rows = [];

    foreach (ComponentTagCompiler::getClassComponentAliases() as $alias => $class) {
        if ($filter && ! str_contains($alias, $filter)) continue;
        $rows[] = ['class component', $alias, $class];
    }

    foreach (Blade::getAnonymousComponentNamespaces() as $prefix => $dirs) {
        foreach ($dirs as $dir) {
            foreach (glob(rtrim($dir, '/') . '/components/**/*.blade.php') as $file) {
                $name = $prefix . str($file)
                    ->after(rtrim($dir, '/') . '/components/')
                    ->before('.blade.php')
                    ->replace('/', '.');
                if ($filter && ! str_contains($name, $filter)) continue;
                $rows[] = ['anonymous', $name, $file];
            }
        }
    }

    $this->table(['kind', 'tag', 'resolves to'], $rows);
});

{{-- WHY IT IS THE PROVIDER'S JOB, and the failure it causes: --}}
{{-- A package that registers its namespace CONDITIONALLY, or a
     provider that boots after the views are compiled, produces a
     component that works locally and fails after a deploy with
     "Unable to locate a class or view for component
     [acme.order-badge]". --}}
{{-- The fix is unconditional registration in boot(), never in a
     conditional, and never lazily. --}}


{{-- THE CONVENTION, and it is why a tag in an application has no
     prefix while the same tag in the package's tests does. --}}
{{-- application:  resources/views/components/order-badge.blade.php
       -> <x-order-badge />  --}}

{{-- package:  src/resources/views/components/order-badge.blade.php
       -> <x-acme-order-badge />  in this package's tests
          <x-order-badge />     inside an app that installed it --}}

{{-- THE MAPPING, which is the whole mechanism: a prefix and a
     directory. --}}
// src/Acme/Ui/UiServiceProvider.php
public function boot(): void
{
    $this->loadViewsFrom(__DIR__ . '/../resources/views', 'acme');
}

{{-- SO <x-acme-order-badge> resolves to
       src/../resources/views/components/order-badge.blade.php,
     and after installation <x-order-badge> resolves to the same
     file via the framework's default search path. --}}

{{-- RESOLUTION ORDER, AND WHAT A COLLISION ACTUALLY DOES: --}}
{{-- The framework searches the registered namespaces, then the
     application's components directory. The FIRST match wins, so
     an application component named the same as a package's
     overrides it — SILENTLY, and with no diagnostic. --}}
{{-- To override a package component, create a file with the same
     relative name: --}}
{{-- resources/views/components/order-badge.blade.php  <- yours,
     shadows the package's. No configuration, no registration. --}}

{{-- WHICH IS USUALLY THE INTENT and occasionally a surprise, and
     the way to tell the difference is to look for the file: --}}
// An Artisan command, because "which card component is this?"
// is a question that comes up on every large project.
Artisan::command('view:components {filter?}', function (?string $filter = null) {
    $rows = [];

    foreach (ComponentTagCompiler::getClassComponentAliases() as $alias => $class) {
        if ($filter && ! str_contains($alias, $filter)) continue;
        $rows[] = ['class component', $alias, $class];
    }

    foreach (Blade::getAnonymousComponentNamespaces() as $prefix => $dirs) {
        foreach ($dirs as $dir) {
            foreach (glob(rtrim($dir, '/') . '/components/**/*.blade.php') as $file) {
                $name = $prefix . str($file)
                    ->after(rtrim($dir, '/') . '/components/')
                    ->before('.blade.php')
                    ->replace('/', '.');
                if ($filter && ! str_contains($name, $filter)) continue;
                $rows[] = ['anonymous', $name, $file];
            }
        }
    }

    $this->table(['kind', 'tag', 'resolves to'], $rows);
});

{{-- WHY IT IS THE PROVIDER'S JOB, and the failure it causes: --}}
{{-- A package that registers its namespace CONDITIONALLY, or a
     provider that boots after the views are compiled, produces a
     component that works locally and fails after a deploy with
     "Unable to locate a class or view for component
     [acme.order-badge]". --}}
{{-- The fix is unconditional registration in boot(), never in a
     conditional, and never lazily. --}}

6.2 Registering namespaces

Shows the provider method used to register component paths and the optional attribute prefix that controls which attributes callers may pass.

<h4>Three places to register, and the order they are checked</h4> <p>A component is registered in one of three ways: by its file's location, by an explicit alias to a class, or by a namespace prefix mapped to a directory. The first requires no registration. The second is what a class component with constructor dependencies needs. The third is what a package needs so its tags do not collide.</p> <p>All three end up in one lookup table, and the order they are consulted is what determines which wins when two components share a name. An explicit alias beats a file-based resolution beats a namespace resolution — which means a one-line registration in a service provider is enough to override a package component, and that it also means a stray registration silently shadows a directory-based component.</p> <h4>Registering a component that must not be reachable</h4> <p>Most registrations are public by default. A component used once, or used only from a specific partial, does not need a tag at all — it can be included by path, which makes its scope obvious and its name unreferenceable. This is a small point and it is worth more than it sounds: a component tag is a public API, and adding one is a decision that someone will come to depend on.</p> <h4>Debugging the resolution</h4> <p>When a tag resolves to something unexpected, the fastest diagnosis is to ask the framework which file it resolved to rather than to read the registration code. The lookup table is introspectable, and a list of every tag with its resolved path is a twenty-line command that answers the question in one run.</p>

Three places to register, and the order they are checked

A component is registered in one of three ways: by its file's location, by an explicit alias to a class, or by a namespace prefix mapped to a directory. The first requires no registration. The second is what a class component with constructor dependencies needs. The third is what a package needs so its tags do not collide.

All three end up in one lookup table, and the order they are consulted is what determines which wins when two components share a name. An explicit alias beats a file-based resolution beats a namespace resolution — which means a one-line registration in a service provider is enough to override a package component, and that it also means a stray registration silently shadows a directory-based component.

Registering a component that must not be reachable

Most registrations are public by default. A component used once, or used only from a specific partial, does not need a tag at all — it can be included by path, which makes its scope obvious and its name unreferenceable. This is a small point and it is worth more than it sounds: a component tag is a public API, and adding one is a decision that someone will come to depend on.

Debugging the resolution

When a tag resolves to something unexpected, the fastest diagnosis is to ask the framework which file it resolved to rather than to read the registration code. The lookup table is introspectable, and a list of every tag with its resolved path is a twenty-line command that answers the question in one run.

// app/Providers/AppServiceProvider.php
Blade::componentNamespace(
    'Marketing',
    __DIR__.'/../resources/views/marketing',
);

// With an attribute prefix.
Blade::componentNamespace(
    'Charts', $path, 'chart:',
);

The third parameter sets an attribute prefix. With a prefix in place a caller must write chart:class rather than class, and unprefixed attributes are discarded silently. Use it for a component library shipping related components that should not leak attributes between one another.

Packages belong in providers. Register component namespaces inside the package’s own service provider, never in the application’s. Registration in an application provider makes the application aware of a package’s internals and breaks package removal.


{{-- THE THREE WAYS, and which needs which. --}}

{{-- (1) BY LOCATION. No registration at all: the tag name is the
     file's path relative to the components directory. --}}
{{-- resources/views/components/order-badge.blade.php
     -> <x-order-badge />  --}}

{{-- (2) AN EXPLICIT ALIAS, which a class component with
     constructor dependencies needs. --}}
// app/Providers/AppServiceProvider.php
Blade::component('order-badge', AppViewComponentsOrderBadge::class);

{{-- (3) A NAMESPACE PREFIX for a package's components: --}}
Blade::componentNamespace('Acme\Ui\View\Components', 'acme');
{{-- or, equivalently and more usually, by registering the view
     path, which registers the component namespace with it --}}
$this->loadViewsFrom(__DIR__ . '/../resources/views', 'acme');

{{-- LOOKUP ORDER, AND WHAT IT MAKES POSSIBLE: an explicit alias
     beats a file-based resolution beats a namespace resolution. So
     one line overrides a package component: --}}
Blade::component('order-badge', AppViewComponentsMyOrderBadge::class);
{{-- and a stray registration silently shadows a directory-based
     component, which is a confusing bug to chase without this
     table. --}}

{{-- THE COMPONENT THAT DOES NOT NEED A TAG. A tag is a PUBLIC API,
     and adding one is a decision someone will come to depend on.
     A component used once from one partial is better included by
     path: --}}
{{-- resources/views/partials/order-row.blade.php --}}
@include('components.order-row', ['order' => $order])
{{-- ...which makes its scope obvious and its name
     unreferenceable. Reachable by a search for the include, not by
     a search for the tag, which is the behaviour you want for a
     private helper. --}}

{{-- DEBUGGING THE RESOLUTION, which is the question "which card
     component is this?" and it comes up on every large project: --}}
Artisan::command('view:components {filter?}', function (?string $filter = null) {
    $rows = [];

    // CLASS COMPONENTS, from the alias table.
    foreach (ComponentTagCompiler::getClassComponentAliases() as $alias => $class) {
        if ($filter && ! str_contains($alias, $filter)) continue;
        $rows[] = ['class', $alias, $class];
    }

    // ANONYMOUS, from the registered namespace paths.
    foreach (Blade::getAnonymousComponentNamespaces() as $prefix => $dirs) {
        foreach ($dirs as $dir) {
            $base = rtrim($dir, '/') . '/components/';
            foreach (glob($base . '**/*.blade.php') as $file) {
                $name = $prefix . str($file)->after($base)
                    ->before('.blade.php')->replace('/', '.');
                if ($filter && ! str_contains($name, $filter)) continue;
                $rows[] = ['anon', $name, $file];
            }
        }
    }

    $this->table(['kind', 'tag', 'resolves to'], $rows);
    $this->comment('lookup order: alias -> file path -> namespace');
});


{{-- THE THREE WAYS, and which needs which. --}}

{{-- (1) BY LOCATION. No registration at all: the tag name is the
     file's path relative to the components directory. --}}
{{-- resources/views/components/order-badge.blade.php
     -> <x-order-badge />  --}}

{{-- (2) AN EXPLICIT ALIAS, which a class component with
     constructor dependencies needs. --}}

{{-- (3) A NAMESPACE PREFIX for a package's components: --}}
