{{-- or, equivalently and more usually, by registering the view
     path, which registers the component namespace with it --}}
$this->loadViewsFrom(__DIR__ . '/../resources/views', 'acme');

{{-- LOOKUP ORDER, AND WHAT IT MAKES POSSIBLE: an explicit alias
     beats a file-based resolution beats a namespace resolution. So
     one line overrides a package component: --}}
Blade::component('order-badge', AppViewComponentsMyOrderBadge::class);
{{-- and a stray registration silently shadows a directory-based
     component, which is a confusing bug to chase without this
     table. --}}

{{-- THE COMPONENT THAT DOES NOT NEED A TAG. A tag is a PUBLIC API,
     and adding one is a decision someone will come to depend on.
     A component used once from one partial is better included by
     path: --}}
{{-- resources/views/partials/order-row.blade.php --}}
@include('components.order-row', ['order' => $order])
{{-- ...which makes its scope obvious and its name
     unreferenceable. Reachable by a search for the include, not by
     a search for the tag, which is the behaviour you want for a
     private helper. --}}

{{-- DEBUGGING THE RESOLUTION, which is the question "which card
     component is this?" and it comes up on every large project: --}}
Artisan::command('view:components {filter?}', function (?string $filter = null) {
    $rows = [];

    // CLASS COMPONENTS, from the alias table.
    foreach (ComponentTagCompiler::getClassComponentAliases() as $alias => $class) {
        if ($filter && ! str_contains($alias, $filter)) continue;
        $rows[] = ['class', $alias, $class];
    }

    // ANONYMOUS, from the registered namespace paths.
    foreach (Blade::getAnonymousComponentNamespaces() as $prefix => $dirs) {
        foreach ($dirs as $dir) {
            $base = rtrim($dir, '/') . '/components/';
            foreach (glob($base . '**/*.blade.php') as $file) {
                $name = $prefix . str($file)->after($base)
                    ->before('.blade.php')->replace('/', '.');
                if ($filter && ! str_contains($name, $filter)) continue;
                $rows[] = ['anon', $name, $file];
            }
        }
    }

    $this->table(['kind', 'tag', 'resolves to'], $rows);
    $this->comment('lookup order: alias -> file path -> namespace');
});

6.3 Dynamic components

Documents the dynamic component tag, which resolves its component name from an expression evaluated at render time.

<h4>A component name chosen at runtime</h4> <p>Most of the time a component tag is static, because the component is known when the template is written. The cases where it is not are real: a widget chosen by a database column, a renderer selected by a type field, a set of components configured by a user. In those cases the name is a value, and there are two ways to handle it — resolve to the class, or resolve to a rendered view.</p> <h4>Resolving to a class gives you a component</h4> <p>The component factory resolves a name to a class and renders it through the container, so constructor dependencies work and the component's methods are available. That is the right answer when the selected thing has behaviour. The risk is that the name comes from data, and a name from a database column is a string that will eventually contain something that is not a component.</p> <h4>Resolving to a view gives you a fragment</h4> <p>A value can also be a rendered view rather than a class. That is the right answer when the selection is purely presentational, and it has one significant advantage: there is no class to resolve and no container to consult, so there is no namespace to escape and no constructor to fail.</p> <h4>The allowlist question, which is the actual design decision</h4> <p>Both mechanisms take a name from data, and both are only as safe as the set of names they will accept. An allowlist — a PHP array of permitted values, with the data used to look one up rather than to build a path — removes the question entirely, because a value not in the list has no answer. The alternative, sanitising the string and passing it through, is defence that depends on the sanitiser being complete, which for filesystem paths and class names it is not.</p>

A component name chosen at runtime

Most of the time a component tag is static, because the component is known when the template is written. The cases where it is not are real: a widget chosen by a database column, a renderer selected by a type field, a set of components configured by a user. In those cases the name is a value, and there are two ways to handle it — resolve to the class, or resolve to a rendered view.

Resolving to a class gives you a component

The component factory resolves a name to a class and renders it through the container, so constructor dependencies work and the component's methods are available. That is the right answer when the selected thing has behaviour. The risk is that the name comes from data, and a name from a database column is a string that will eventually contain something that is not a component.

Resolving to a view gives you a fragment

A value can also be a rendered view rather than a class. That is the right answer when the selection is purely presentational, and it has one significant advantage: there is no class to resolve and no container to consult, so there is no namespace to escape and no constructor to fail.

The allowlist question, which is the actual design decision

Both mechanisms take a name from data, and both are only as safe as the set of names they will accept. An allowlist — a PHP array of permitted values, with the data used to look one up rather than to build a path — removes the question entirely, because a value not in the list has no answer. The alternative, sanitising the string and passing it through, is defence that depends on the sanitiser being complete, which for filesystem paths and class names it is not.

<x-dynamic-component :component="$wrapperComponent"
                       class="card" />

<x-dynamic-component :component="$iconName">
    <x-slot:label>{{ $caption }}</x-slot:label>
</x-dynamic-component>

The expression must evaluate to a resolvable name, including namespace and view identifiers such as 'alerts::'.Alert::componentFor($alert). An unresolvable name raises an InvalidArgumentException naming the component, which is a far clearer failure than the undefined-variable error the equivalent code would raise without the directive.

Static analysis. Because resolution is deferred, an editor cannot validate the tag. Constrain the possible names with a backed enum so the set is closed and the compiler can still reason about it.


{{-- WHEN THE NAME IS DATA, and the two mechanisms. --}}

{{-- (1) RESOLVE TO A CLASS: use the factory, so constructor
     dependencies work and the component's methods are available. --}}
{{-- The name comes from a database column, so it is a STRING that
     will eventually contain something that is not a component. --}}
@php($renderer = \App\Services\WidgetRenderer::resolve($page->widget_type))
<x-dynamic-component :component="$renderer" :data="$page->widget_data" />

{{-- (2) RESOLVE TO A VIEW: the component is a rendered fragment, so
     there is no class, no container, and no namespace to escape. --}}
@php($view = "widgets.{$page->widget_type}")
{{ $this->renderWidget($view, $page->widget_data) }}

{{-- THE ALLOWLIST QUESTION, WHICH IS THE ACTUAL DECISION. Both
     mechanisms take a name from data. An allowlist removes the
     question, because a value not in the list HAS NO ANSWER. --}}
// app/Services/WidgetRenderer.php
final class WidgetRenderer
{
    /**
     * The allowlist is a PHP array, so "data" is used to LOOK ONE UP
     * rather than to BUILD A PATH. A sanitised string is not
     * equivalent: sanitising a class name or a filesystem path is
     * a problem without a complete solution, whereas a lookup in a
     * literal array has no attack surface at all.
     */
    private const RENDERERS = [
        'chart'        => ChartWidget::class,
        'table'        => TableWidget::class,
        'markdown'     => MarkdownWidget::class,
        'recent-files' => RecentFilesWidget::class,
    ];

    public static function resolve(?string $type): string
    {
        return self::RENDERERS[$type] ?? self::RENDERERS['table'];
        // ^ a value not in the list gets a DEFAULT rather than an
        //   exception, because a page whose widget type is
        //   unfamiliar should still render. Log it: a type falling
        //   back silently is a data problem someone needs to know
        //   about.
    }
}

{{-- SO A VALUE THAT IS NOT IN THE LIST IS NOT A COMPONENT, IT IS
     A DEFAULT: --}}
@if (! array_key_exists($page->widget_type, WidgetRenderer::types())) 
    @once
        @php(log()->warning('unknown widget type', [
            'type' => $page->widget_type, 'page' => $page->id,
        ]))
    @endonce
@endif
<x-dynamic-component :component="WidgetRenderer::resolve($page->widget_type)"
                     :data="$page->widget_data" />

{{-- AND THE NAMING RULE that keeps the allowlist and the
     directory in step, so the map above cannot drift: --}}
{{--   widget_type value   class                  directory
           chart             ChartWidget            widgets/chart
           table             TableWidget            widgets/table
           markdown          MarkdownWidget         widgets/markdown

     The value in the database is the SAME string as the directory
     name, so a new widget is one class, one view, one array entry,
     and no other change. A naming scheme where they differ needs a
     map entry per widget, which is a place to forget one. --}}


{{-- WHEN THE NAME IS DATA, and the two mechanisms. --}}

{{-- (1) RESOLVE TO A CLASS: use the factory, so constructor
     dependencies work and the component's methods are available. --}}
{{-- The name comes from a database column, so it is a STRING that
     will eventually contain something that is not a component. --}}
@php($renderer = \App\Services\WidgetRenderer::resolve($page->widget_type))
<x-dynamic-component :component="$renderer" :data="$page->widget_data" />

{{-- (2) RESOLVE TO A VIEW: the component is a rendered fragment, so
     there is no class, no container, and no namespace to escape. --}}
@php($view = "widgets.{$page->widget_type}")
{{ $this->renderWidget($view, $page->widget_data) }}

{{-- THE ALLOWLIST QUESTION, WHICH IS THE ACTUAL DECISION. Both
     mechanisms take a name from data. An allowlist removes the
     question, because a value not in the list HAS NO ANSWER. --}}
// app/Services/WidgetRenderer.php
final class WidgetRenderer
{
    /**
     * The allowlist is a PHP array, so "data" is used to LOOK ONE UP
     * rather than to BUILD A PATH. A sanitised string is not
     * equivalent: sanitising a class name or a filesystem path is
     * a problem without a complete solution, whereas a lookup in a
     * literal array has no attack surface at all.
     */
    private const RENDERERS = [
        'chart'        => ChartWidget::class,
        'table'        => TableWidget::class,
        'markdown'     => MarkdownWidget::class,
        'recent-files' => RecentFilesWidget::class,
    ];

    public static function resolve(?string $type): string
    {
        return self::RENDERERS[$type] ?? self::RENDERERS['table'];
        // ^ a value not in the list gets a DEFAULT rather than an
        //   exception, because a page whose widget type is
        //   unfamiliar should still render. Log it: a type falling
        //   back silently is a data problem someone needs to know
        //   about.
    }
}

{{-- SO A VALUE THAT IS NOT IN THE LIST IS NOT A COMPONENT, IT IS
     A DEFAULT: --}}
@if (! array_key_exists($page->widget_type, WidgetRenderer::types())) 
    @once
        @php(log()->warning('unknown widget type', [
            'type' => $page->widget_type, 'page' => $page->id,
        ]))
    @endonce
@endif
<x-dynamic-component :component="WidgetRenderer::resolve($page->widget_type)"
                     :data="$page->widget_data" />

{{-- AND THE NAMING RULE that keeps the allowlist and the
     directory in step, so the map above cannot drift: --}}
{{--   widget_type value   class                  directory
           chart             ChartWidget            widgets/chart
           table             TableWidget            widgets/table
           markdown          MarkdownWidget         widgets/markdown

     The value in the database is the SAME string as the directory
     name, so a new widget is one class, one view, one array entry,
     and no other change. A naming scheme where they differ needs a
     map entry per widget, which is a place to forget one. --}}

6.4 View composers

Documents single-view and wildcard registration, the class-based composer interface, and the interaction between composed and passed data.

<h4>Attaching data to a view at render time</h4> <p>A view composer runs immediately before a view is rendered and can attach data to it. That is the mechanism for data that belongs to a particular view — the breadcrumbs for a page, the navigation for a section, the current record for an edit form — and it exists so the controller does not have to pass everything the template reads.</p> <p>The question this raises is when to use one. The argument for is that a controller that passes fifteen variables to a view is doing layout work, and a view that queries is doing data work; a composer takes both out of the view and leaves the controller with what the page is about. The argument against is that the data a template needs becomes invisible at the point where the template is written, and a reader has to know that a composer exists to know where a variable comes from.</p> <h4>View composers against shared data</h4> <p>The distinction is scope. A composer attaches data to named views; shared data is attached to every view. The confusion between them causes a bug where a composer for one view is relied on by a partial that is rendered from several views, and it works for one of them. The rule is narrow and worth following: a composer that a partial depends on must be registered for that partial's view, not for the view that happens to include it.</p> <h4>Class composers, and what they are for</h4> <p>A class-based composer is a class with a method, which is worth using when the data computation has more than a few lines — because a closure with thirty lines attached to a view name is harder to test than a class. It is not worth the ceremony when the data is one query.</p>

Attaching data to a view at render time

A view composer runs immediately before a view is rendered and can attach data to it. That is the mechanism for data that belongs to a particular view — the breadcrumbs for a page, the navigation for a section, the current record for an edit form — and it exists so the controller does not have to pass everything the template reads.

The question this raises is when to use one. The argument for is that a controller that passes fifteen variables to a view is doing layout work, and a view that queries is doing data work; a composer takes both out of the view and leaves the controller with what the page is about. The argument against is that the data a template needs becomes invisible at the point where the template is written, and a reader has to know that a composer exists to know where a variable comes from.

View composers against shared data

The distinction is scope. A composer attaches data to named views; shared data is attached to every view. The confusion between them causes a bug where a composer for one view is relied on by a partial that is rendered from several views, and it works for one of them. The rule is narrow and worth following: a composer that a partial depends on must be registered for that partial's view, not for the view that happens to include it.

Class composers, and what they are for

A class-based composer is a class with a method, which is worth using when the data computation has more than a few lines — because a closure with thirty lines attached to a view name is harder to test than a class. It is not worth the ceremony when the data is one query.

View::composer('layouts.app', function ($view) {
    $view->with('navigation', $this->navigation->tree());
});

// Every view whose name matches the pattern.
View::composer('dashboard.*', DashboardComposer::class);

Composed views already contain the data passed to view(), so a composer augments rather than replaces. Passing a class name resolves it from the container, which allows constructor injection into the composer itself.

Wildcard cost. A composer matching * runs on every view render, including error pages and partials. If it performs a query it multiplies that query by the number of views a request renders.


{{-- WHEN A COMPOSER IS THE RIGHT ANSWER, which is when the data
     belongs to the VIEW rather than to the page: --}}

// app/Providers/AppServiceProvider.php
View::composer('layouts.app', function ($view) {
    // Breadcrumbs and navigation are LAYOUT data. A controller
    // passing these makes every controller that renders the layout
    // repeat itself, and every controller that renders a page that
    // renders the layout without knowing it needs them.
    $view->with('breadcrumbs', Breadcrumbs::forCurrentRoute());
    $view->with('nav', Navigation::forUser(auth()->user()));
});

{{-- AND THE ONE THAT IS SCOPED TO A SINGLE VIEW: --}}
View::composer('orders.show', function (OrderShowView $view) {
    $view->with([
        'breadcrumbs' => Breadcrumbs::forCurrentRoute(),
        'related'     => Order::relatedTo($view->getOrder())->take(5),
    ]);
});

{{-- WHICH SOLVES THE BUG THIS PATTERN CREATES, and the bug is the
     reason to know the difference between composers and shared
     data: --}}
View::composer('orders.show', function ($view) {
    $view->with('related', $related);
});

{{-- resources/views/orders/show.blade.php --}}
@include('partials.order-related')
{{-- and partials/order-related.blade.php reads $related. It works
     HERE. It also breaks the moment that partial is included from
     another view that has no such composer — and the error is an
     undefined variable rendering as an empty section, not a
     failure. --}}

{{-- THE RULE: a composer that a PARTIAL depends on must be
     registered for that partial's view, not for the view that
     happens to include it. --}}
View::composer('partials.order-related', function ($view) {
    $view->with('related', Order::relatedTo($view->getData()['order']));
});

{{-- THE CLASS FORM, which is worth it above a few lines and not
     below: --}}
// app/View/Composers/OrderShowComposer.php
final class OrderShowComposer
{
    public function __construct(private readonly BreadcrumbBuilder $breadcrumbs) {}

    public function compose(OrderShowView $view): void
    {
        $view->with('breadcrumbs', $this->breadcrumbs->forRoute($view->getOrder()));
    }
}

View::composer(OrderShowView::class, OrderShowComposer::class);

{{-- WHY A CLASS ABOVE A FEW LINES: a closure with thirty lines
     attached to a view name is harder to test than a class, and
     harder to find with a search. A class is searchable by its
     name, has a constructor for its dependencies, and can be
     instantiated directly in a unit test with mocks. --}}

{{-- THE TEST, and it is the whole argument: --}}
it('attaches breadcrumbs to an order view', function () {
    $breadcrumbs = Mockery::mock(BreadcrumbBuilder::class);
    $breadcrumbs->shouldReceive('forRoute')->once()->andReturn($expected);

    $view = new OrderShowView('orders.show', ['order' => $order]);

    (new OrderShowComposer($breadcrumbs))->compose($view);

    expect($view->getData())->toHaveKey('breadcrumbs', $expected);
});
{{-- No HTTP, no container, no template rendered. --}}


{{-- WHEN A COMPOSER IS THE RIGHT ANSWER, which is when the data
     belongs to the VIEW rather than to the page: --}}

// app/Providers/AppServiceProvider.php
View::composer('layouts.app', function ($view) {
    // Breadcrumbs and navigation are LAYOUT data. A controller
    // passing these makes every controller that renders the layout
    // repeat itself, and every controller that renders a page that
    // renders the layout without knowing it needs them.
    $view->with('breadcrumbs', Breadcrumbs::forCurrentRoute());
    $view->with('nav', Navigation::forUser(auth()->user()));
});

{{-- AND THE ONE THAT IS SCOPED TO A SINGLE VIEW: --}}
View::composer('orders.show', function (OrderShowView $view) {
    $view->with([
        'breadcrumbs' => Breadcrumbs::forCurrentRoute(),
        'related'     => Order::relatedTo($view->getOrder())->take(5),
    ]);
});

{{-- WHICH SOLVES THE BUG THIS PATTERN CREATES, and the bug is the
     reason to know the difference between composers and shared
     data: --}}
View::composer('orders.show', function ($view) {
    $view->with('related', $related);
});

{{-- resources/views/orders/show.blade.php --}}
@include('partials.order-related')
{{-- and partials/order-related.blade.php reads $related. It works
     HERE. It also breaks the moment that partial is included from
     another view that has no such composer — and the error is an
     undefined variable rendering as an empty section, not a
     failure. --}}

{{-- THE RULE: a composer that a PARTIAL depends on must be
     registered for that partial's view, not for the view that
     happens to include it. --}}
View::composer('partials.order-related', function ($view) {
    $view->with('related', Order::relatedTo($view->getData()['order']));
});

{{-- THE CLASS FORM, which is worth it above a few lines and not
     below: --}}
// app/View/Composers/OrderShowComposer.php
final class OrderShowComposer
{
    public function __construct(private readonly BreadcrumbBuilder $breadcrumbs) {}

    public function compose(OrderShowView $view): void
    {
        $view->with('breadcrumbs', $this->breadcrumbs->forRoute($view->getOrder()));
    }
}

View::composer(OrderShowView::class, OrderShowComposer::class);

{{-- WHY A CLASS ABOVE A FEW LINES: a closure with thirty lines
     attached to a view name is harder to test than a class, and
     harder to find with a search. A class is searchable by its
     name, has a constructor for its dependencies, and can be
     instantiated directly in a unit test with mocks. --}}

{{-- THE TEST, and it is the whole argument: --}}
it('attaches breadcrumbs to an order view', function () {
    $breadcrumbs = Mockery::mock(BreadcrumbBuilder::class);
    $breadcrumbs->shouldReceive('forRoute')->once()->andReturn($expected);

    $view = new OrderShowView('orders.show', ['order' => $order]);

    (new OrderShowComposer($breadcrumbs))->compose($view);

    expect($view->getData())->toHaveKey('breadcrumbs', $expected);
});
{{-- No HTTP, no container, no template rendered. --}}

6.5 Sharing data across views

Contrasts the two global data APIs and explains why view share is a last resort rather than a convenience.

<h4>Global data, and the dependency it hides</h4> <p>Data can be attached to every view. It is convenient for a handful of values that genuinely are global — the locale, the current user, a feature-flag set — and it is a liability for anything else, because it makes a template's requirements invisible at the point where the template is read.</p> <p>A layout that reads a globally shared value cannot be rendered without that share being set. That means it cannot be rendered by a unit test that does not set it up, cannot be rendered by a console command that renders a PDF or an email, and cannot be rendered by a queued job whose worker has no authenticated user. All three are real situations in an application that sends mail, and the failure mode in each is an undefined variable rendering as an empty region.</p> <h4>The test for whether something belongs</h4> <p>A value belongs in global data if every view genuinely needs it and no view would be wrong without it. The locale qualifies. A current user is borderline — fine for a navigation bar, wrong for page content, because a view shared between the web and a console command would then behave differently. A list of feature flags is borderline for the same reason, and is better served by a dedicated component that asks for them.</p> <h4>Views composers are the narrower tool</h4> <p>Everything that can be attached to named views rather than to all of them should be. The narrow version has the same benefit and none of the coupling, and the reason composers are underused is that global sharing is faster to write and harder to remove.</p>

Global data, and the dependency it hides

Data can be attached to every view. It is convenient for a handful of values that genuinely are global — the locale, the current user, a feature-flag set — and it is a liability for anything else, because it makes a template's requirements invisible at the point where the template is read.

A layout that reads a globally shared value cannot be rendered without that share being set. That means it cannot be rendered by a unit test that does not set it up, cannot be rendered by a console command that renders a PDF or an email, and cannot be rendered by a queued job whose worker has no authenticated user. All three are real situations in an application that sends mail, and the failure mode in each is an undefined variable rendering as an empty region.

The test for whether something belongs

A value belongs in global data if every view genuinely needs it and no view would be wrong without it. The locale qualifies. A current user is borderline — fine for a navigation bar, wrong for page content, because a view shared between the web and a console command would then behave differently. A list of feature flags is borderline for the same reason, and is better served by a dedicated component that asks for them.

Views composers are the narrower tool

Everything that can be attached to named views rather than to all of them should be. The narrow version has the same benefit and none of the coupling, and the reason composers are underused is that global sharing is faster to write and harder to remove.

View::share('appName', config('app.name'));

View::composer('*', function ($view) {
    $view->with('currentUser', auth()->user());
});

share() sets a value available to every view for the remainder of the request; the data is eager, so it is computed even when no view uses it. The wildcard composer defers the work to render time but still runs for every view. When a value is needed by one specific template, pass it from the controller instead.

Test isolation. Globally shared data persists across views within a test method. Prefer explicit controller data so assertions describe the contract under test rather than the ambient state.


{{-- WHAT GLOBAL DATA IS FOR: values that are genuinely global,
     where "no view would be wrong without it" is true. --}}
// app/Providers/AppServiceProvider.php
View::share('locale', app()->getLocale());
{{-- every view may legitimately need the locale, and no view is
     wrong without it. --}}

View::share('brands', [
    'name'   => config('app.name'),
    'colour' => config('brand.colour'),
]);

{{-- AND THE HIDDEN DEPENDENCY, which is the cost: --}}
{{-- resources/views/components/layouts/app.blade.php --}}
<html lang="{{ $locale }}">
    <body style="--brand: {{ $brands['colour'] }}">
        {{ $slot }}
    </body>
</html>
{{-- Now render this layout from a place that did not go through
     the provider — a console command, a queued job, a test: --}}

// app/Console/Commands/SendInvoice.php
public function handle(): int
{
    // $locale and $brands exist because the service provider ran.
    // In a QUEUE WORKER the provider ran too — but in a plain unit
    // test of this command, and in a console command run with the
    // providers deferred, they may not.
    return view('invoices.pdf', ['invoice' => $invoice])->render();
}

{{-- THE FAILURE MODE IN EACH CASE, and it is never a clean
     exception: --}}
{{-- 1. A unit test: $locale is undefined. Read with the default
        conditional it renders as an empty lang attribute — a page
        with no language, which a screen reader will guess.
        2. A queued job rendering a PDF: the provider ran, so it
        works, and it works only because the boot path happened to
        run.
        3. A test rendering the layout directly: same as 1. --}}

{{-- THE MIDDLE CASE, and it is the commonest argument FOR global
     data, is a current user. It is borderline and wrong for page
     content: --}}
View::share('currentUser', auth()->user());
{{-- fine for a navigation bar; wrong for anything else, because a
     view shared between a web request and a queued job that
     renders an email now behaves differently in each. --}}

{{-- THE NARROWER TOOL, and the reason composers are underused is
     that global sharing is faster to write: --}}
View::composer('components.layouts.app', function ($view) {
    $view->with([
        'locale' => app()->getLocale(),
        'brand'  => config('brand.colour'),
    ]);
});

{{-- Same data, same convenience, and now the layout has ONE
     dependency, on one view, that a test can satisfy in one line. --}}

{{-- THE BETTER ANSWER FOR ANYTHING THAT IS NOT TRULY GLOBAL: a
     component that takes what it needs as props. --}}
<x-app-shell :locale="app()->getLocale()" :brand-colour="config('brand.colour')">
    {{ $slot }}
</x-app-shell>
{{-- ...at the cost of every call site repeating it, which is the
     trade: explicit at the point of use, or implicit and
     invisible. Pick one per value, and write down which. --}}


{{-- WHAT GLOBAL DATA IS FOR: values that are genuinely global,
     where "no view would be wrong without it" is true. --}}
// app/Providers/AppServiceProvider.php
View::share('locale', app()->getLocale());
{{-- every view may legitimately need the locale, and no view is
     wrong without it. --}}

View::share('brands', [
    'name'   => config('app.name'),
    'colour' => config('brand.colour'),
]);

{{-- AND THE HIDDEN DEPENDENCY, which is the cost: --}}

    <body style="--brand: {{ $brands['colour'] }}">
        {{ $slot }}
    </body>
</html>

     the provider — a console command, a queued job, a test: --}}

     dependency, on one view, that a test can satisfy in one line. --}}

     component that takes what it needs as props. --}}

    {{ $slot }}

     trade: explicit at the point of use, or implicit and
     invisible. Pick one per value, and write down which. --}}

6.6 Choosing where data comes from

Summarises the decision rule for data placement and names the four sources in order of increasing coupling.

SourceUse forAvoid when
Controller dataAnything page-specificNever avoid it for page data
View modelShaping or grouping data for one viewThe shaping is trivial
View composerLayout chrome shared by a family of viewsOnly one view needs it
Global shareFramework-wide constantsIt requires a query or a service call

Data passed by a controller is the only source a reader of the template cannot discover for themselves, which is why it is the safest default. Composers trade that explicitness for reuse; the exchange is only worth making when the reuse is real.

Exercises 6

  1. Register a namespaced component directory with and without an attribute prefix and record how the call site differs.
  2. Replace an icon conditional chain with a dynamic component backed by a backed enum.
  3. Convert a global share into a wildcard composer and measure the query count on a page that renders six views.
  4. Introduce a view model for a listing page and confirm the controller now passes one argument instead of four.
<h4>Four places data can come from, and they have different lifetimes</h4> <p>A variable in a template arrived from somewhere, and where it came from determines what can be assumed about it. It came from the render call, in which case the caller chose it and the template can rely on it. It came from a composer attached to this view, in which case the composer is the contract. It came from shared data, in which case it is global and the template is coupled to a provider. Or it came from the view itself — a property on the view class — in which case it belongs to this view and nowhere else.</p> <p>Sorting a template's variables by origin is the fastest way to understand a legacy template, and it is worth doing once per page before changing it. A template that reads twelve variables of which four are shared and two come from composers in three different providers is a template whose requirements are genuinely unknown, and that is a fact about the file rather than a feeling about it.</p> <h4>The rule that removes the ambiguity</h4> <p>Every variable a template reads should have one origin, and the origin should be discoverable from the template or from one file. In practice that means: props from the render call for anything page-specific, a prop or a slot for anything a component needs, a composer scoped to the view for anything a partial needs, and shared data for the three or four values that are genuinely global. Everything else is a variable whose source is a question, and a question in a template is a coupling.</p> <h4>Debugging a variable's origin</h4> <p>The framework can report what data a view holds at render time, and the fastest diagnostic for "where did this variable come from" is to log the view's data keys with the rendering stack. That turns an hour of reading provider registrations into one output line.</p>

Four places data can come from, and they have different lifetimes

A variable in a template arrived from somewhere, and where it came from determines what can be assumed about it. It came from the render call, in which case the caller chose it and the template can rely on it. It came from a composer attached to this view, in which case the composer is the contract. It came from shared data, in which case it is global and the template is coupled to a provider. Or it came from the view itself — a property on the view class — in which case it belongs to this view and nowhere else.

Sorting a template's variables by origin is the fastest way to understand a legacy template, and it is worth doing once per page before changing it. A template that reads twelve variables of which four are shared and two come from composers in three different providers is a template whose requirements are genuinely unknown, and that is a fact about the file rather than a feeling about it.

The rule that removes the ambiguity

Every variable a template reads should have one origin, and the origin should be discoverable from the template or from one file. In practice that means: props from the render call for anything page-specific, a prop or a slot for anything a component needs, a composer scoped to the view for anything a partial needs, and shared data for the three or four values that are genuinely global. Everything else is a variable whose source is a question, and a question in a template is a coupling.

Debugging a variable's origin

The framework can report what data a view holds at render time, and the fastest diagnostic for "where did this variable come from" is to log the view's data keys with the rendering stack. That turns an hour of reading provider registrations into one output line.


{{-- THE FOUR ORIGINS, and what each one lets a template assume. --}}

{{-- (1) FROM THE RENDER CALL: the caller chose it, so the template
     can rely on it being there. --}}
return view('orders.show', ['order' => $order]);
{{-- {{ $order }} --}}

{{-- (2) FROM A COMPOSER on this view: the composer IS the contract,
     and it should be one file the reader can find. --}}
View::composer('orders.show', fn ($v) => $v->with('related', ...));

{{-- (3) FROM SHARED DATA: global, so the template is coupled to a
     provider it cannot see from the template. --}}
View::share('currentUser', auth()->user());

{{-- (4) FROM THE VIEW CLASS: belongs to this view and nowhere else. --}}
// app/View/Orders/ShowView.php
class ShowView extends View
{
    public function __construct(private Order $order) {}

    public function with($key, $value = null)
    {
        // The view supplies its own data, so the render call only
        // supplies what the view cannot know for itself.
        if ($key === 'order') return $this;
        return parent::with($key, $value);
    }

    public function order(): Order { return $this->order; }
}
{{-- {{ $order }} in the template, supplied by the view class. --}}

{{-- WHICH ORIGIN IS RIGHT FOR WHAT, and this is the whole
     section: --}}
{{--   page-specific      -> prop from the render call
     component-specific -> prop or slot
     partial-specific   -> composer scoped to THAT partial's view
     genuinely global   -> shared data (three or four values)
     belongs to one page -> a view class --}}


{{-- WHY THE INVENTORY IS WORTH DOING ONCE: a legacy template with
     twelve variables, of which four are shared and two come from
     composers in three different providers, has requirements that
     are genuinely unknown — and that is a fact about the file
     rather than a feeling about it. --}}
Artisan::command('view:data {view}', function (string $name) {
    $view = View::make($name, sample_data_for($name));

    // SHARED: present on EVERY view, so this template's contract
    // includes a provider it cannot see.
    $shared = array_keys(app('view')->getShared());

    $rows = [];
    foreach (array_keys($view->getData()) as $key) {
        $rows[] = [
            $key,
            in_array($key, $shared, true)
                ? 'SHARED — coupled to a provider'
                : 'from the render call or a composer',
        ];
    }

    $this->table(['variable', 'origin'], $rows);

    if ($shared) {
        $this->comment(count($shared) . ' shared variables on every view: '
            . implode(', ', $shared));
        $this->comment('anything a template reads that is in this list '
            . 'cannot be rendered without a provider having booted.');
    }
});

{{-- THE SCRIPT THAT FINDS THE COUPLING, which is the useful output
     rather than the count: --}}
// A maintenance command over every view, listing the shared
// variables each one reads. Any non-empty result is a coupling.
Artisan::command('views:shared-usage', function () {
    $shared = array_keys(app('view')->getShared());
    if (! $shared) { $this->info('nothing is shared'); return; }

    $hits = [];
    foreach (View::getFinder()->getPaths() as $dir) {
        foreach (new RecursiveIteratorIterator(
                     new RecursiveDirectoryIterator($dir)) as $file) {
            if ($file->getExtension() !== 'php') continue;

            $src = file_get_contents($file->getPathname());
            foreach ($shared as $key) {
                // A VARIABLE REFERENCE in the template body, not in
                // a comment and not in a code sample.
                if (preg_match('/{{s*$' . preg_quote($key, '/') . '/', $src)) {
                    $hits[$key][] = Str::after($file->getPathname(), $dir . '/');
                }
            }
        }
    }

    foreach ($hits as $key => $files) {
        $this->line("$key: " . count($files) . ' template(s)');
        foreach (array_slice($files, 0, 5) as $f) $this->comment("  $f");
        if (count($files) > 5) $this->comment('  ... and ' . (count($files) - 5));
    }

    if ($hits) {
        $this->comment('each of these cannot be rendered without the '
            . 'provider that shares it: no test, no console command, '
            . 'no queue worker.');
    }
});

{{-- THE RULE THAT REMOVES THE AMBIGUITY, restated because it is
     the actionable part: every variable a template reads has ONE
     origin, and that origin is discoverable from the template or
     from one file. --}}


{{-- THE FOUR ORIGINS, and what each one lets a template assume. --}}

{{-- (1) FROM THE RENDER CALL: the caller chose it, so the template
     can rely on it being there. --}}
return view('orders.show', ['order' => $order]);
{{-- {{ $order }} --}}

{{-- (2) FROM A COMPOSER on this view: the composer IS the contract,
     and it should be one file the reader can find. --}}
View::composer('orders.show', fn ($v) => $v->with('related', ...));

{{-- (3) FROM SHARED DATA: global, so the template is coupled to a
     provider it cannot see from the template. --}}
View::share('currentUser', auth()->user());

{{-- (4) FROM THE VIEW CLASS: belongs to this view and nowhere else. --}}
// app/View/Orders/ShowView.php
class ShowView extends View
{
    public function __construct(private Order $order) {}

    public function with($key, $value = null)
    {
        // The view supplies its own data, so the render call only
        // supplies what the view cannot know for itself.
        if ($key === 'order') return $this;
        return parent::with($key, $value);
    }

    public function order(): Order { return $this->order; }
}
{{-- {{ $order }} in the template, supplied by the view class. --}}

{{-- WHICH ORIGIN IS RIGHT FOR WHAT, and this is the whole
     section: --}}
{{--   page-specific      -> prop from the render call
     component-specific -> prop or slot
     partial-specific   -> composer scoped to THAT partial's view
     genuinely global   -> shared data (three or four values)
     belongs to one page -> a view class --}}


{{-- WHY THE INVENTORY IS WORTH DOING ONCE: a legacy template with
     twelve variables, of which four are shared and two come from
     composers in three different providers, has requirements that
     are genuinely unknown — and that is a fact about the file
     rather than a feeling about it. --}}
Artisan::command('view:data {view}', function (string $name) {
    $view = View::make($name, sample_data_for($name));

    // SHARED: present on EVERY view, so this template's contract
    // includes a provider it cannot see.
    $shared = array_keys(app('view')->getShared());

    $rows = [];

        $rows[] = [
            $key,
            in_array($key, $shared, true)
                ? 'SHARED — coupled to a provider'
                : 'from the render call or a composer',
        ];
    }

    $this->table(['variable', 'origin'], $rows);

    if ($shared) {
        $this->comment(count($shared) . ' shared variables on every view: '
            . implode(', ', $shared));
        $this->comment('anything a template reads that is in this list '
            . 'cannot be rendered without a provider having booted.');
    }
});

{{-- THE SCRIPT THAT FINDS THE COUPLING, which is the useful output
     rather than the count: --}}

// variables each one reads. Any non-empty result is a coupling.
Artisan::command('views:shared-usage', function () {
    $shared = array_keys(app('view')->getShared());
    if (! $shared) { $this->info('nothing is shared'); return; }

    $hits = [];

                     new RecursiveDirectoryIterator($dir)) as $file) {

                // A VARIABLE REFERENCE in the template body, not in
                // a comment and not in a code sample.

                    $hits[$key][] = Str::after($file->getPathname(), $dir . '/');
                }
            }
        }
    }

    foreach ($hits as $key => $files) {
        $this->line("$key: " . count($files) . ' template(s)');

        if (count($files) > 5) $this->comment('  ... and ' . (count($files) - 5));
    }

    if ($hits) {
        $this->comment('each of these cannot be rendered without the '
            . 'provider that shares it: no test, no console command, '
            . 'no queue worker.');
    }
});

{{-- THE RULE THAT REMOVES THE AMBIGUITY, restated because it is
     the actionable part: every variable a template reads has ONE
     origin, and that origin is discoverable from the template or
     from one file. --}}

7 · Directives: Custom Compilation and Custom Tags

7.1 Registering a custom directive7.2 Custom Blade::if conditionals7.3 Custom component tags7.4 Directives built from templates7.5 Registering in a service provider7.6 When not to write a directive

Directives extend Blade’s grammar at compile time. This chapter documents the registration APIs, the difference between a directive and a component, and the criteria that determine whether a new directive is justified.

7.1 Registering a custom directive

Documents the directive registration method, its argument handling and the parse closure that decides when it applies.

What a custom directive is: a compiled function

Registering a directive creates a PHP function and tells the compiler that a particular token sequence compiles to a call to it. The body receives the expression between the parentheses as a string, which the framework compiles in the template's scope, and returns HTML. That is the entire mechanism, and most of what is worth knowing follows from the two facts in that sentence: the argument is a string rather than a parse tree, and the body runs in the template's scope.

The consequence of the first fact is that a directive cannot analyse its own argument at registration time, because the variable it names does not exist until the template is rendered. The consequence of the second is that the directive sees every variable in the template, which makes short directives readable and is also how a directive becomes the place where business logic hides.

When a directive is the right abstraction

There are three good reasons to write one. A syntax that would be unreadable as an expression — a set of nested ternaries, or a call chain with side conditions. A fragment of markup appearing identically in twenty places that the markup itself cannot express, such as a field with its label, hint and error. And a computation whose result is display text, where a controller would otherwise have to compute values it does not otherwise need.

The fourth reason people give — performance — is almost never true. A directive that avoids a controller call does not make the controller call cheaper; it moves it somewhere less visible. A directive that caches a sub-render does remove real work, and section 16 covers when that is worth it.

// app/Providers/AppServiceProvider.php
Blade::directive('money', function ($expression) {
    return '<?php echo \App\Support\Money::format('
        .$expression.'); ?>';
});

The callback receives the raw expression text following the directive name and returns PHP source that is written into the compiled file. It runs at compile time, so it cannot access application services, the request or any runtime state; anything it needs must be reached through a static call such as Money::format().

Expression injection. The expression is concatenated verbatim into generated PHP and Blade does not validate it. Only expose a custom directive for expressions written in your own templates, never for anything derived from user input.


{{-- THE REGISTRATION, and what it produces: a PHP function the
     compiler emits a call to. --}}
// app/Providers/AppServiceProvider.php
Blade::directive('money', function (string $expression) {
    // $expression is the RAW TEXT between the parens, as a string.
    // It has not been parsed, and the variables in it do not exist
    // yet — which is why a directive cannot inspect its argument
    // at registration time.
    return "format(); ?>";
});

{{-- SO: --}}
@money($invoice->total)

{{-- which compiles to: --}}
total))->format(); ?>

{{-- AND THE SCOPE CONSEQUENCE: the body runs in the template's
     scope, so it sees every variable in the template without being
     passed them. --}}
Blade::directive('money', fn (string $e) =>
    "format(config('app.currency', 'GBP')); ?>");
{{-- every call now uses the SAME currency, so "why does this one
     page show dollars" has an answer in one place. --}}

{{-- (2) A DIRECTIVE FOR MARKUP THE TEMPLATE CANNOT EXPRESS: --}}
Blade::directive('field', function (string $expression) {
    // Parsing the argument HERE is safe because this form takes
    // literal names, not variables — a compile-time decision about
    // text that never changes.
    preg_match('/(.+?)s*,s*(.+?)s*,s*(.+)/', trim($expression), $m);

    return "startComponent('components.field', [
            'name'  => {$m[1]},
            'label' => {$m[2]},
            'hint'  => {$m[3]},
        ]); ?>";
});

@field('email', 'Email address', 'We never share it.')
@field('password', 'Password', null)
{{-- which renders a label, an input, a hint and an error slot —
     identically, in every form in the application. --}}

{{-- (3) A COMPUTATION WHOSE RESULT IS DISPLAY TEXT: --}}
Blade::directive('initials', fn (string $e) =>
    "");

<p>{{ initials($user->name) }}</p>
{{-- AK. Four characters of argument, and no template anywhere has
     to know the algorithm. --}}

{{-- AND THE PERFORMANCE ARGUMENT, which is usually false: --}}
{{-- A directive that avoids a controller call does not make the
     call cheaper — it MOVES it somewhere less visible. --}}
Blade::directive('balance', fn (string $e) =>
    "");
{{-- ^ the N+1 that was visible in the controller's query log is
     now inside a directive, and no longer visible at all. --}}

{{-- THE ONE THAT IS TRUE: a directive that CACHES a sub-render
     removes real work. Section 16 covers when that is correct. --}}

7.2 Custom Blade::if conditionals

Documents the custom conditional API, which generates a matched set of block directives from a single callback.

A directive that introduces its own syntax

The conditional directive does not compile a function call. It registers a new block form with a condition and an else branch, and the compiler emits the appropriate structure around the body. The registration takes a name, a condition closure evaluated at render time, and a callback producing the body.

Because the condition is a closure rather than an expression parsed at compile time, a directive conditional can consult anything — a configuration value, a feature-flag service, the current user's permissions — without the template having to pass it. That is a genuine capability, and it is also how a directive becomes the place where an access decision lives.

The cost, which is that the syntax is invisible to a reader

A template using a custom conditional reads as though the conditional were a language construct, and someone reading it later has no way to know it was defined in a service provider forty files away. Worse, a name collision between a custom conditional and a standard one fails at compile time with a message naming the directive and not the provider responsible.

The rule that keeps this manageable is that a custom conditional should read as a domain concept — a predicate about the domain rather than about markup. A flag check and a permission check are the right kind. An emptiness check and a draft check are not, because the reader can guess the condition from the name and will guess wrong.

Blade::if('disk', function ($name) {
    return '(<?php config("filesystems.default") === '
        .$name.') ?>';
});

// usage
@disk('s3')
    <p>Stored on S3</p>
@elsedisk('local')
    <p>Stored on local disk</p>
@enddisk

One registration produces four directives — @disk, @elsedisk, @elseifdisk and @enddisk — which is the right tool when a conditional appears across many templates. Blade::unless() registers the mirrored set.


{{-- THE REGISTRATION. The CONDITION is a closure evaluated per
     render; the BODY is a callback producing the markup. --}}
// app/Providers/AppServiceProvider.php
Blade::if('feature', function (string $feature) {
    return app(FeatureFlags::class)->enabled($feature);
});

Blade::if('hasRole', function (string $role) {
    // NULL-SAFE: an unauthenticated user has no roles, and false
    // is the right answer rather than an error on the login page.
    return auth()->user()?->hasRole($role);
});

@feature('new-checkout')
    <x-new-checkout />
@endif

@hasRole('admin')
    <x-admin-tools />
@else
    <x-standard-tools />
@endhasRole

{{-- WHY THE CONDITION IS A CLOSURE RATHER THAN AN EXPRESSION: it
     can consult anything at RENDER time, with no argument at all. --}}
Blade::if('pricingAvailable', fn () =>
    app(PricingService::class)->isAvailableFor(request()->user()));

@pricingAvailable
    <x-price-breakdown />
@endif

{{-- THE CASE THAT JUSTIFIES IT, and it is a display rule that would
     otherwise be repeated: --}}
@elevation('display')
    <img src="{{ $user->avatarUrl(2) }}" alt="">
@else
    <img src="{{ $user->avatarUrl(1) }}" alt="">
@endelevation
{{-- As a property this would need computing in a controller,
     passing down, and defaulting in every partial that renders a
     user. The directive keeps the rule in one place. --}}

{{-- THE INVISIBILITY PROBLEM, and it is the reason to choose names
     carefully. A reader seeing: --}}
@hasDraft($post)
    <x-draft-badge />
@endif
{{-- has no way to know that hasDraft is defined in a provider
     forty files away, nor that it consults a scope other than the
     model. --}}

{{-- THE COLLISION, which fails at compile time with a message that
     names the directive and not the provider: --}}
Blade::if('feature', fn () => true);   // first definition
Blade::if('feature', fn () => true);   // second: the framework
// throws "Directive [feature] is already registered" and the stack
// trace is the only way to find which provider did it

{{-- THE RULE: A CUSTOM CONDITIONAL READS AS A DOMAIN PREDICATE. --}}
@feature('new-checkout')     // domain: yes
@hasRole('admin')            // domain: yes
@isPublished($post)          // domain: borderline
@ifNotEmpty($items)          // NOT domain: the reader can guess
// the condition from the name and will guess wrong

7.3 Custom component tags

Documents the component registration method, which maps a tag to a class through the container and is the closest analogue of the include directive.

Custom tags for components that already exist

Registering a class as a component makes it usable as a tag, and the framework resolves the tag to the class through the container. That is what makes a component with dependencies work: the constructor is called by the container rather than by the template, so a service is available without the caller mentioning it.

The tag's arguments are matched to the constructor's parameters by name, and an untyped parameter receives whatever the template passed — a prefixed expression for a PHP value, a bare attribute for a string. A parameter with a default may be omitted entirely, and that is the whole difference between a prop and an optional prop.

What the tag cannot do

A class component receives only what its constructor declares, so arbitrary attributes are dropped unless it declares a parameter to hold them. This is a genuine difference from an anonymous component and the reason a class component usually needs an explicit attribute-bag parameter: it is a small amount of plumbing that is easy to forget, and forgetting it produces a component that silently discards an id and cannot be targeted from a test.

Rendering without a registration

The factory can render a component class directly, with no tag and no registration. That is the right choice for a component used once, and it has two advantages worth naming: the tag namespace stays smaller, and the call site says exactly what is being constructed rather than resolving a name.

Blade::component(OrderSummary::class, 'order-summary');

// A legacy alias for a renamed component.
Blade::component(
    OrderSummary::class, 'summary', 'legacy.summary',
);

A component tag is resolved through the container, so its constructor may inject anything. Unlike a directive it has a template, receives props and an attribute bag, and appears in the component list of an IDE. Any custom tag that renders markup should be registered this way, because a directive has no template, no slots and no attribute handling.

Aliases. The third argument registers an additional tag for the same class. Use it to preserve an old name across a rename without breaking third-party or published templates.


{{-- THE REGISTRATION: a class, so the container constructs it and
     its dependencies arrive without being passed. --}}
// app/Providers/AppServiceProvider.php
Blade::component('order-summary', \App\View\Components\OrderSummary::class);

// app/View/Components/OrderSummary.php
final class OrderSummary extends Component
{
    public function __construct(
        public readonly Order $order,
        public readonly string $currency,
        private readonly PricingService $pricing,   // injected, never
                                                          // passed by
                                                          // the caller
    ) {}

    public function render(): View
    {
        return view('components.order-summary');
    }
}

<x-order-summary :order="$order" currency="GBP" />
{{-- PricingService is resolved from the container. --}}

{{-- ARGUMENTS ARE MATCHED BY NAME to constructor parameters: --}}
<x-order-summary :order="$order" currency="GBP" />
{{-- :order is a PHP EXPRESSION; currency is the STRING "GBP". A
     parameter with a default may be omitted entirely. --}}

{{-- WHAT THE TAG CANNOT DO. Only what the constructor declares is
     passed, so everything else is SILENTLY DISCARDED: --}}
final class OrderSummary extends Component
{
    public function __construct(
        public readonly Order $order,
        public readonly string $currency = 'GBP',
        // THE BAG: how a class component accepts the attributes an
        // anonymous component receives for free.
        public readonly \Illuminate\View\ComponentAttributeBag $attributes = new,
    ) {}
}

<x-order-summary :order="$order" id="summary" data-testid="s1" />
{{-- WITHOUT the $attributes parameter, id and data-testid are
     dropped with no error and no warning — producing a component
     that cannot be targeted from a test or from JavaScript. --}}

{{-- AND RENDERING WITHOUT A REGISTRATION, right for a component
     used once: --}}
@component(\App\View\Components\OrderSummary::class, ['order' => $order])

{{-- OR through the factory, which names the class: --}}
@php
    echo app(\App\View\Components\OrderSummary::class)
             ->with('order', $order)
             ->resolveView();
@endphp

{{-- WHICH IS BETTER WHEN: a component used in four places gets a
     tag, because the tag is what makes the four call sites
     identical and searchable. A component used once does not,
     because a tag is a PUBLIC API and adding one is a decision
     someone will come to depend on. --}}

7.4 Directives built from templates

Explains how to implement a directive that renders a partial by compiling a template reference rather than inlining markup.

A common requirement is an include with a fixed wrapper. The correct implementation compiles an include directive rather than embedding HTML in the provider:

Composing directives out of other directives

A directive can build its body from other directives rather than from literal PHP. It takes a string containing directive syntax, compiles it through the same compiler, and emits the result. The capability is useful for a family of related directives that share a shape, and it is dangerous in the two ways anything accepting a string as code is dangerous.

The first danger is that the input is now a string being compiled, so the boundary between data and code becomes a decision rather than a type. If that string comes from a database or a request, the directive is a remote code execution path. The mitigation is the one for any templating system: never compile a string derived from input.

The second is subtler. A composed body is evaluated in the template's scope, so any variable it names must exist there — and because the body was compiled as a separate unit, a missing variable is an undefined-variable condition at render time rather than a compile error. That difference is what makes this hard to notice: the failure appears in production on one page rather than at build time everywhere.

When to use it

The legitimate use is a family of directives where the shape is shared: every chart directive wraps the same container, the same loading state and the same error state, and only the type differs. The illegitimate use is building a general-purpose template language inside the template language, at which point the composition has a compiler of its own and needs its own tests.

Blade::directive('panel', function ($expression) {
    return "<?php echo \$__env->make('.$expression.', \Illuminate\Support\Arr::except(get_defined_vars(), ['__data', '__path']))->render(); ?>';
});

This keeps the markup in a template where it can be styled, linted and reviewed. The cost is that the directive is a thin wrapper with no benefit over calling @include directly, which is the argument against adding it.


{{-- COMPOSING: the body is a STRING containing directive syntax,
     compiled through the same compiler. --}}
Blade::directive('metricPanel', function (string $expression) {
    return "{{ \$slot }}</x-panel-frame>
    BLADE]); ?>";
});

@metricPanel(':label="$label"')
    {{ $value }}
@endmetricPanel
{{-- ...which is a template language INSIDE the template language,
     with its own compiler and no tests. --}}

{{-- THE LEGITIMATE USE: A FAMILY OF DIRECTIVES SHARING A SHAPE. --}}
foreach (['line' => 'LineChart', 'bar' => 'BarChart', 'pie' => 'PieChart'] as $t => $class) {
    Blade::directive($t . 'Chart', fn (string $e) =>
        "make('$class', ['series' => $e])->render(); ?>");
}

@lineChart($revenueSeries)
@barChart($categoryTotals)
@pieChart($marketShare)
{{-- three directives, ONE shape: the container, the loading state
     and the error state exist once. Before, they were copy-pasted
     three times with the class swapped. --}}

{{-- DANGER 1: THE BOUNDARY BETWEEN DATA AND CODE IS A DECISION,
     NOT A TYPE. --}}
Blade::directive('render', fn (string $e) =>
    "");

@render($userSuppliedTemplate)
{{-- ^ DO NOT SHIP THIS. If $e is $user->profile->bio, the user
     controls the string being compiled. Every directive that
     compiles its argument must be gated on a trusted literal. --}}

{{-- THE MITIGATION, which is the rule for anything accepting a
     string as code: LOOK IT UP, do not build a path. --}}
@php
    $trusted = in_array($name, ['summary', 'detail'], true);
@endphp

@if ($trusted)
    {!! view($name, $data) !!}
@endif
{{-- an allowlist, so a value not in the list has NO ANSWER. A
     sanitiser on a class name or a filesystem path is a problem
     without a complete solution; a lookup in a literal array has
     no attack surface at all. --}}

{{-- DANGER 2, AND THE SUBTLER ONE: --}}
Blade::directive('metric', fn (string $e) =>
    "");

@metric($m)
{{-- ^ $title is referenced in the BODY, not the argument, so the
     compiler never sees it as a requirement. A caller who omits
     $title gets an undefined variable at RENDER time, on one page,
     in production — not a build failure. --}}

7.5 Registering in a service provider

Documents the correct location, ordering and conditional registration of directives, and how package directives should be published.

Register directives in boot(), not register(), because the Blade facade is only fully available once the view service provider has booted. For optional integrations, guard on the package being present:

Where registrations belong, and why it is not a style question

Every custom directive, conditional, component alias and namespace is a piece of the template language, and the template language is assembled by service providers. A registration placed in a provider that is deferred, conditional, or loaded only in some environments produces a template that works locally and fails after a deploy, with a message naming a directive rather than the provider at fault.

Three rules cover it. Registration belongs in boot() rather than register(), because the compiler is not available until boot has run. It belongs unconditionally, since a conditional registration is a debugging problem with a delayed trigger. And a package registers in its own provider while an application registers in the application's, so removing a package removes its syntax.

The two failures, and only one of them is easy

A directive that is never registered produces a compile error at the point of use, naming the directive. That is a good error. The harder case is a directive registered conditionally: it works in the environment where the condition holds and fails in the one where it does not, and the condition is usually something unrelated — a debug flag, a loaded extension, a cached configuration file.

Auditing, because nothing else finds these

No check in a normal suite asserts that every directive a template uses is registered, and there cannot be one without compiling every template. Compiling every template is exactly the check that pays: it finds unregistered directives, unresolvable components, unbalanced section blocks, and missing parents — and it can run in continuous integration as a build step that fails rather than warns.

public function boot(): void
{
    if (class_exists(Livewire::class)) {
        $this->registerLivewireDirectives();
    }

    $this->loadViewsFrom(__DIR__.'/../resources/views', 'billing');
    $this->loadBladeComponentsFrom(__DIR__.'/../resources/views/components', 'billing');
}

Package directives belong in the package’s provider so that removing the package removes them. Application-wide directives belong in AppServiceProvider. Mixing the two makes ownership ambiguous and is a common source of workarounds after a package is dropped.

Keep the surface small. Every directive added to a project is a construct a reader must learn. Reserve them for syntax that cannot be expressed clearly with a component and a PHP helper.


{{-- RULE 1: boot(), NEVER register(). The compiler is not resolved
     until boot has run. --}}
// app/Providers/AppServiceProvider.php
final class AppServiceProvider extends ServiceProvider
{
    public function register(): void
    {
        // WRONG: Blade::directive('money', ...) here throws on a
        // fresh install, because the compiler is not resolved yet.
    }

    public function boot(): void
    {
        $this->registerDirectives();
        $this->registerComponents();
    }

    private function registerDirectives(): void
    {
        Blade::directive('money', fn (string $e) =>
            "format();");

        Blade::if('feature', fn (string $f) =>
            app(FeatureFlags::class)->enabled($f));
    }

    private function registerComponents(): void
    {
        Blade::component('order-summary', OrderSummary::class);
        Blade::anonymousComponentNamespace('App\View\UI', 'ui');
    }
}

{{-- RULE 2: UNCONDITIONALLY. --}}
public function boot(): void
{
    if (app()->environment('local')) {
        // WRONG: works in local, fails in staging, and the error
        // names the DIRECTIVE, not the provider that caused it.
        Blade::directive('debugLayout', fn (string $e) => "<!-- $e -->");
    }
}

{{-- IF IT MUST BE ENVIRONMENT-SPECIFIC, REGISTER IT EVERYWHERE
     AND BRANCH INSIDE, so the failure mode is wrong output rather
     than a missing directive: --}}
Blade::directive('debugLayout', fn (string $e) =>
    'environment("local")) { ?>'
    . "<!-- $e -->"
    . '');

{{-- RULE 3: THE PROVIDER OWNS ITS SYNTAX, so removing the package
     removes the syntax. --}}
// vendor/acme/ui/src/Acme/Ui/UiServiceProvider.php
public function boot(): void
{
    $this->loadViewsFrom(__DIR__ . '/../resources/views', 'acme');

    Blade::component('acme-chart', Chart::class);
    Blade::if('acmeEnabled', fn (string $f) => $this->enabled($f));
}

{{-- THE AUDIT, because nothing else in a suite finds an
     unregistered directive: --}}
Artisan::command('views:verify', function () {
    $failed = [];
    $count  = 0;

    foreach (View::getFinder()->getPaths() as $dir) {
        foreach (new RecursiveIteratorIterator(
                     new RecursiveDirectoryIterator($dir)) as $file) {
            if ($file->getExtension() !== 'php') continue;

            $rel  = Str::after($file->getPathname(), $dir . DIRECTORY_SEPARATOR);
            $name = str_replace(['/', '\', '.blade.php'], ['.', '.', ''], $rel);
            $count++;

            try {
                View::make($name)->render();
            } catch (Throwable $e) {
                $failed[] = [$name, class_basename($e), $e->getMessage()];
            }
        }
    }

    if ($failed) {
        $this->table(['view', 'exception', 'message'], $failed);
        $this->error(count($failed) . " of $count templates failed");
        exit(1);          // FAIL THE BUILD, not the request
    }

    $this->info("$count templates compiled and rendered");
});
{{-- The only check that finds an unregistered directive, an
     unresolvable component, an unbalanced @section, a missing
     @parent, or a prop sent to a component that does not declare
     it. --}}

7.6 When not to write a directive

Gives the decision procedure for choosing between a directive, a component, a view composer and a plain PHP helper.

NeedUseRationale
Wrap markup with fixed structureComponentTemplate, slots and attribute bag
Format a value inlinePHP helper in an echoTestable, traceable, IDE-visible
Conditional used once@if in the templateNo indirection needed
Conditional used widelyBlade::ifConsistent grammar
Text reused verbatimTranslation keyLocalisable, no duplication

The governing rule is testability. A PHP helper can be unit-tested; a directive callback cannot be invoked directly, so its behaviour is only observable by rendering a template. When the logic carries meaning, keep it in PHP and let Blade remain a formatting layer.

Exercises 7

  1. Implement a directive that formats a monetary value and verify the compiled output in the views cache.
  2. Register a custom conditional and confirm all four block directives are generated, including the else-if form.
  3. Rewrite a directive that emits markup as a component with slots and compare the rendered result.
  4. Move a package-style directive from an application provider into the package provider and confirm it still registers after a cache clear.

The three costs, stated so they can be weighed

A custom directive extends a language that a reader has to learn. That is the first cost and it is paid by everyone who reads the template afterwards, including the person who wrote it six months earlier. The second is that the extension is invisible: nothing in the template says the directive came from a provider, so a reader has to search for it. The third is that the body's scope is the template's, so a directive can read variables the caller did not intend to pass — convenient, and a reason a template stops being readable on its own.

What a directive actually saves

The saving is real and it is usually not the one people expect. A directive that eliminates a repeated fragment of markup eliminates the chance of the copies diverging, and that is worth more than any render time. A directive that eliminates a repeated expression eliminates nothing at all: the compiler produces the same calls either way, and the direct form is usually more readable than the syntax you invented for it.

The decision rule

Write a directive when the same non-trivial markup appears three or more times, or when an expression is genuinely unreadable and is repeated. Use a component instead when the fragment has data, because a component carries its data explicitly and an anonymous one has no provider to find. Use plain PHP in a block when the computation is used once, because a directive for a single call site is a name to look up for no benefit.


{{-- THE THREE COSTS, in the order they bite. --}}

{{-- 1. THE READER HAS TO LEARN IT. --}}
@money($invoice->total)
{{-- reads as a language construct. Nothing in the template says
     it came from a provider, so the next person either searches
     for it or rewrites it inline. --}}

{{-- 2. IT IS INVISIBLE. No marker, and no default linter rule that
     says "this directive is custom". --}}
{{-- Which is fixable: a lint rule that lists every registered
     directive and fails when a template uses an unknown one. It
     needs parsing, and it is worth it once a codebase has more than
     a dozen custom directives. --}}
Artisan::command('views:directives', function () {
    $known = array_keys(Blade::getCustomDirectives());
    $used  = [];

    foreach (View::getFinder()->getPaths() as $dir) {
        foreach (new RecursiveIteratorIterator(
                     new RecursiveDirectoryIterator($dir)) as $file) {
            if ($file->getExtension() !== 'php') continue;

            $src = file_get_contents($file->getPathname());
            preg_match_all('/@(\w+)/', $src, $m);
            foreach (array_unique($m[1]) as $name) {
                $used[$name][] = Str::after($file->getPathname(), $dir . '/');
            }
        }
    }

    $rows = [];
    foreach ($used as $name => $files) {
        $rows[] = [$name, in_array($name, $known, true) ? 'custom' : 'BUILT-IN?',
                   count($files)];
    }

    $this->table(['directive', 'is it registered?', 'uses'], $rows);
    // Anything marked BUILT-IN? that is not in the language is an
    // unregistered directive, found before production rather than by
    // a customer.
});

{{-- 3. THE BODY SEES THE TEMPLATE'S SCOPE, which is convenient and
     makes a template harder to read on its own. --}}
Blade::directive('metric', fn (string $e) =>
    "");
{{-- $label and $suffix are read out of the template's scope, so the
     directive has a requirement nobody declared and nobody can
     discover by reading its registration. --}}


{{-- WHAT A DIRECTIVE ACTUALLY SAVES, and it is usually not speed. --}}

{{-- SAVES: a repeated MARKUP fragment, because the copies cannot
     diverge. --}}
@field('email', 'Email address', 'We never share it.')
@field('password', 'Password', null)
@field('name', 'Full name', 'As it appears on your passport.')
{{-- ^ worth it. Six identical lines across nine forms, and one of
     the nine was always missing the hint. The directive did not
     save render time; it saved a bug that was going to happen. --}}

{{-- DOES NOT SAVE: a repeated EXPRESSION. The compiler emits the
     same calls either way, and the direct form says more. --}}
{{-- Without a directive, in every template: --}}
{{ \App\Support\Money::of($invoice->total)->format() }}

{{-- With one: --}}
@money($invoice->total)
{{-- ^ the first is LONGER and says exactly what it does. The
     directive saved nothing and added a name to look up. --}}

{{-- AND THE PERFORMANCE ARGUMENT, made concrete: --}}
{{-- A directive does not make a call cheaper. It moves it out of
     the controller's query log, which is worse. --}}
Blade::directive('orderCount', fn (string $e) =>
    "count(); ?>");

@orderCount($user->id)     {{-- rendered inside a loop over users:
                                 N+1, and the query log now shows
                                 a statement in a compiled view
                                 rather than in a controller, so
                                 nobody is looking there. --}}

{{-- THE RULE, as three lines, and it is worth printing: --}}
{{--   same non-trivial MARKUP, 3+ times  -> directive or component
     the fragment has DATA                -> component: it carries
                                            the data, and an
                                            anonymous one needs no
                                            provider at all
     computation used ONCE                -> a @php block, because a
                                            directive for a single
                                            call site is a name with
                                            no benefit --}}

{{-- AND THE ONE THAT CHANGES DIRECTION, which is worth stating
     last because it is the exception: --}}
{{-- A directive is ALSO right when it replaces something that
     looks like a language construct and is not one. --}}
{{-- Blade has no @isset-else. The idiom people reach for is: --}}
@isset($sidebar)
    <aside>{{ $sidebar }}</aside>
@else
    <aside><x-default-nav /></aside>
@endif
{{-- which is fine and needs no directive. But this: --}}
@hasSection('sidebar')
    <aside>@yield('sidebar')</aside>
@else
    <aside><x-default-nav /></aside>
@endif
{{-- ...reads as a language conditional that happens to exist. It
     does exist, but it means "did a CHILD define this section",
     which is a layout concept, and the reader has no way to know
     that. Naming it does not help either — the concept is the
     problem, not the name. --}}

8 · Includes, Rendering and Stacks

8.1 The include family8.2 The each directive8.3 Data passed to an include8.4 Stacks8.5 The once directive8.6 Include or component

Includes render partials, the each directive renders one partial many times, and stacks collect fragments from anywhere in the tree for output in the layout. This chapter documents each and the decision between them.

8.1 The include family

Documents the four include directives and the exact condition each one evaluates.

Five forms, and they differ in what happens on failure

The plain form renders a partial and throws if it does not exist. The conditional form renders it only when a condition holds and does nothing otherwise. The form taking an array of candidates renders the first that exists, and nothing if none do. The form taking a condition and a value renders one or the other. And the "each" variant takes a separator and an array, rendering several partials in sequence.

The differences that matter are the failure behaviour and the data scope. A missing partial is a bug in one case and a normal condition in the others, which is why both behaviours exist: a missing layout must be loud, and an optional region must be silent. The data scope is the second difference and it is the one that bites — an included partial sees the parent's data plus whatever it is explicitly given, and can therefore read a variable that was never passed to it, which works until that partial is included somewhere else.

When to include at all

An include is a partial: a fragment with no data of its own, used to break a template into readable pieces. That is legitimate and it has one requirement — the fragment must not need anything the parent happens to have. A partial that reads a variable the parent did not create is coupled to the parent, and the include's promise that it is reusable is false.

The moment a partial needs its own data, an include with an explicit data array is the fix. The moment it needs more than three or four values, a component is the right tool, because a component declares what it needs and a missing prop is visible at the call site.

@include('partials.header', ['user' => $user])

@includeIf('partials.admin', auth()->isAdmin())

@includeWhen($order->isPaid(), 'partials.receipt', ['order' => $order])
@includeWhen($order->isPaid(), 'partials.pending', [], 'admin')

@includeFirst(['custom.header', 'partials.header'])

@includeIf takes a boolean as its second argument. @includeWhen takes the condition first and an optional view name fourth, which selects an alternative when the condition is false. @includeFirst renders the first view that exists, which is the standard way to let an application override a package partial.
