Override points. A package that ships partials and honours @includeFirst lets an application replace a single fragment by publishing one file, without forking the package.


{{-- THE FIVE FORMS. --}}

{{-- (1) PLAIN: throws if the partial is missing. Correct when a
     missing partial is a BUG — and a missing layout always is. --}}
@include('partials.order-summary')

{{-- (2) CONDITIONAL: renders nothing when the condition is false,
     and does not throw when the partial is absent. --}}
@includeIf('partials.promo-banner')
@includeWhen($user->isSubscriber(), 'partials.premium-bar')

{{-- (3) FIRST: the first candidate that EXISTS. The idiomatic
     fallback chain. --}}
@includeFirst([
    'partials.checkout.express',
    'partials.checkout.standard',
    'partials.checkout.generic',
])

{{-- (4) UNLESS: the inverse of the conditional form. --}}
@includeUnless($order->isPaid(), 'partials.payment-reminder')

{{-- (5) EACH: several partials with one separator. Useful for a
     fixed set of script tags, and easy to abuse into a loop with a
     shape change. --}}
@each('partials.js-widget', $widgets, '</script><script>')

{{-- AND THE PLAIN FORM WITH EXPLICIT DATA, which is the form to
     reach for by default: --}}
@include('partials.order-summary', [
    'order'   => $order,
    'compact' => true,
])
{{-- ...so the partial's requirements are VISIBLE AT THE CALL SITE
     rather than inherited. --}}

{{-- THE SCOPE HAZARD, and it is the reason the explicit form is
     better: an included partial sees the parent's ENTIRE data. --}}
{{-- resources/views/partials/order-summary.blade.php --}}
<h2>{{ $order->number }}</h2>
<p>{{ $compact ? 'compact' : 'full' }}</p>
{{-- Neither $order nor $compact is declared anywhere in this file.
     It works because the parent had them. It stops working the
     moment this partial is included from a page whose variable is
     called $invoice. --}}

{{-- THE DECLARED FORM, so the requirement is in the file: --}}
@php
    /** @var AppModelsOrder $order */
    /** @var bool $compact */
@endphp

<h2>{{ $order->number }}</h2>
<p>{{ $compact ? 'compact' : 'full' }}</p>

{{-- AND THE POINT AT WHICH AN INCLUDE STOPS BEING RIGHT: --}}
@include('partials.order-summary', [
    'order'    => $order,
    'compact'  => $compact,
    'currency' => $currency,
    'showTax'  => $showTax,
    'locale'   => $locale,
])
{{-- ^ five values passed to a fragment with no declaration of its
     own: this is a component that has not been written yet. --}}

8.2 The each directive

Documents the each directive, its variable, key and empty-view arguments, and what it compiles to.

A loop specialised to a partial

The directive renders a partial once per element with a variable bound to the element. It saves the loop and the include, which is worth something when the pair appears in many places, and it costs two things worth naming: the loop metadata is not available, and the separation is loose — the partial sees every variable in the parent's scope, so a partial expecting a specific name works by convention rather than by declaration.

The comparison with a loop and an include is the whole question. A loop is more explicit, gives access to the loop metadata, and lets the element variable be named anything. The directive is shorter and gives the partial a name it can rely on. Neither is wrong, and the deciding consideration is whether the loop needs anything the directive cannot provide.

What it cannot do

It cannot test the loop index, tell whether it is on the last element, or pass an index-dependent value to the partial — the loop variable is not available inside the partial, so a partial that wants it must be passed it explicitly, and the directive has no way to do that. That is the limitation that decides it: any row where the last row differs, or the index is displayed, or the class alternates, needs a plain loop.

@each('partials.row', $users, 'user', 'id')

{{-- partials/row.blade.php receives $user and $key --}}
@each('partials.row', $users, 'user',
      'empty', 'partials.no-users')

The directive expands into a single loop that renders the partial per item. The third argument names the variable the partial receives, the fourth is the key passed alongside it, and the fifth names the partial used when the collection is empty. Choosing an empty view keeps the no-result markup out of the row template.

Readability, not speed. @each compiles to the same loop-with-nested-render as a @foreach containing an @include. Adopt it for the empty-state argument rather than for performance.


{{-- THE DIRECTIVE, and what it saves: --}}
@each('partials.order-row', $orders)

{{-- WHICH IS THIS: --}}
@foreach ($orders as $order)
    @include('partials.order-row', ['order' => $order])
@endforeach

{{-- AND THE SEPARATOR VARIANT: --}}
@each('partials.js-widget', $widgets, '</script><script>')

{{-- THE LOOP VARIABLE IS AVAILABLE, and it is called "value". --}}
{{-- resources/views/partials/order-row.blade.php --}}
<tr>
    <td>{{ $value->number }}</td>
    <td>{{ $value->total->format() }}</td>
</tr>
{{-- ^ $value, not $order. So the same partial cannot be included
     from a loop that binds $order, and the two call sites are not
     interchangeable. That is the loose coupling in full. --}}

{{-- WHAT IT CANNOT DO, and this is the deciding limitation: --}}
@each('partials.order-row', $orders)
{{-- ^ a partial that wants the index cannot get it, because the
     loop variable is not in scope and the directive has no way to
     pass anything else. So this does NOT work: --}}

{{-- resources/views/partials/order-row-numbered.blade.php --}}
<tr>
    <td>{{ $loop->iteration }}</td>   {{-- $loop does not exist here:
                                           the directive's loop is
                                           gone by the time the
                                           partial renders --}}
</tr>

{{-- SO ANY ROW THAT NEEDS THE INDEX, THE LAST FLAG, OR AN
     ALTERNATING CLASS NEEDS A PLAIN LOOP: --}}
@foreach ($orders as $order)
    @include('partials.order-row', [
        'order'    => $order,
        'index'    => $loop->iteration,
        'total'    => $loop->count,
        'isLast'   => $loop->last,
        'rowClass' => $loop->even ? 'odd' : 'even',
    ])
@endforeach

{{-- OR, BETTER, A COMPONENT, where the index is a prop with a
     default and the caller cannot forget to pass it: --}}
@foreach ($orders as $order)
    <x-order-row :order="$order" :index="$loop->iteration"
                 :total="$loop->count" :is-last="$loop->last" />
@endforeach

{{-- THE DECIDING QUESTION, in one line: does the row need anything
     the directive cannot provide? If it needs the index, the last
     flag, or an alternating class, use a loop or a component. --}}

8.3 Data passed to an include

Documents how data reaches an included partial, which ambient variables exist, and the trade-off the shared scope creates.

An included template shares the parent environment, so every variable in the parent scope is visible unless the passed array overwrites it. Two variables are always injected for debugging:

Explicit data, and the three ways to pass it

An included partial can receive its data three ways: as an array in the include call, through a variable defined immediately before the call, or by reading the parent's data directly. The first is explicit and visible. The second is the same thing spread across two statements, which is worse to read and no better. The third is the coupling, and it is the one that breaks silently.

Passing an array is also how an included partial receives a variable whose name differs from the parent's — a partial can be written against a name it controls, and the include maps to it. That is the mechanism that makes a partial reusable, and it is worth using deliberately rather than by accident.

What the partial can still see, and why that matters

An included partial inherits the parent's entire data. So passing five values explicitly does not make the other forty invisible to it — a partial can still read a variable it was never given, and will keep working until it is included somewhere that does not have it. The fix is not a mechanism; it is a convention plus a lint rule, or a component, where the constructor declares the contract and a missing value is an error.

// resources/views/partials/row.blade.php
<tr class="{{ $rowClass }}">
    <td>{{ $key }}</td>
    <td>{{ $user->name }}</td>
</tr>

@include('partials.row', [
    'user'     => $user,
    'rowClass' => $loop->index % 2 ? 'is-alt' : '',
])

Those are $__data and __path. Sharing the whole parent scope is convenient and explains why a partial can read data the caller never mentions; it is also why a partial reading six undocumented variables cannot be tested in isolation.


{{-- THE THREE WAYS, and the ranking. --}}

{{-- (1) AN ARRAY IN THE CALL: explicit, visible at the point of
     use, and the form to reach for. --}}
@include('partials.order-summary', [
    'order'   => $order,
    'compact' => true,
])

{{-- (2) A VARIABLE DEFINED BEFORE THE CALL: the same thing spread
     across two statements, which is worse to read and no better. --}}
@php($rowOrder = $order)
@include('partials.order-row')

{{-- (3) READING THE PARENT'S DATA: the coupling. --}}
@include('partials.order-summary')
{{-- and partials/order-summary.blade.php reads $order and
     $compact, neither of which appears at the call site. --}}

{{-- AND THE MAPPING, which is the real use of the array form: the
     partial's variable name is ITS choice, and the include maps to
     it. That is what makes a partial reusable. --}}
@include('partials.payment-method', ['method' => $order->payment])
@include('partials.payment-method', ['method' => $subscription->source])
{{-- one partial, two unrelated call sites, because both supply
     "method" in the name the partial declares. --}}

{{-- WHAT THE PARTIAL CAN STILL SEE, AND WHY IT MATTERS: --}}
@include('partials.order-summary', ['order' => $order])
{{-- ...does NOT make the other forty variables invisible. A
     partial can still read a variable it was never given, and keeps
     working until it is included somewhere that does not have it. --}}

{{-- THE FIX IS A COMPONENT, where the contract is declared: --}}
final class OrderSummary extends Component
{
    public function __construct(
        public readonly Order $order,      // declared: a caller that
        public readonly bool  $compact = false,
        public readonly string $currency = 'GBP',
    ) {}
}
{{-- <x-order-summary :order="$order" :compact="true" />
     ^ passing :total="10" now does nothing, because there is no
       $total — an UNUSED PROP rather than a silently-wrong value.
       The component's contract and the call site either agree or
       the mistake is inert. --}}

{{-- THE CONVENTION FOR PARTIALS THAT CANNOT YET BE COMPONENTS: --}}
@php
    /**
     * Requires: $order (Order), $compact (bool, optional)
     * Included by: orders/show, orders/index, invoices/show
     */
@endphp
{{-- ...in a comment, so the next reader knows the contract and the
     third call site knows it is adding a dependency. Better than
     nothing; the component is better than this. --}}

8.4 Stacks

Documents the stack directives, the ordering semantics of push and prepend, and the layouts that consume them.

Named lists that templates append to

A stack is a named list. Anything can push onto it, and a point in a layout yields the whole accumulated list. The mechanism exists for the case where there is more than one contributor to a region — a stylesheet from the layout plus one from a component plus one from an inline widget — and it is the correct answer for that case precisely because it accumulates.

The failure mode is using it where a section belongs. A stack cannot be replaced, only appended to, so a page that pushes a stylesheet into a region the layout already filled produces both. That is occasionally what was wanted and frequently is not, and the diagnosis is easy once the distinction is known.

Pushing at the point of use

The push directives are statements, not expressions: a component can push onto a stack during its own render, which is what makes the mechanism work for a component that carries a script. The cost is that the push happens at render time and in an order that depends on the order the templates render, so a stack's contents are a property of the page's structure rather than of any one file.

{{-- anywhere in the tree --}}
@push('scripts')
    <script src="{{ asset('js/reports.js') }}"></script>
@endpush

@pushOnce('scripts')
    <script src="{{ asset('js/vendor.js') }}"></script>
@endPushOnce

@prepend('scripts')
    <meta name="generator" content="Laravel">
@endPrepend

{{-- in the layout --}}
@stack('scripts')

Content pushed with @push is emitted in push order; content pushed with @prepend is emitted before it. A view may push without the layout declaring the stack, and a layout may declare a stack that is never pushed, in which case nothing is emitted. @pushOnce prevents a fragment appearing twice when a partial is included from several places.

Escaping. Stack content is emitted raw. Because payloads are assembled far from the template that renders them, stacks are a frequent location for unescaped values. Review stack writes with the same care as raw echoes.


{{-- THE DECLARATION, in the layout: --}}
{{-- resources/views/layouts/app.blade.php --}}
<head>
    @stack('styles')     {{-- every push, in push order --}}
</head>
<body>
    ...
    @stack('scripts')
</body>

{{-- THE PUSH, and it is a STATEMENT rather than an expression: --}}
@push('scripts')
    <script src="{{ asset('js/editor.js') }}" defer></script>
@endpush

{{-- WHICH IS THE WHOLE POINT: a component pushes during its own
     render, so a component that needs a script does not require its
     caller to know. --}}
{{-- resources/views/components/editor.blade.php --}}
@props(['name' => 'editor'])

@once
    @push('scripts')
        <script src="{{ asset('js/editor.js') }}" defer></script>
    @endpush
@endonce

<div x-data="editor('{{ $name }}')">
    ...
</div>

{{-- @once IS WHAT MAKES THIS SAFE. Without it, a component used
     forty times pushes its script forty times. --}}

{{-- THE STACK VERSUS THE SECTION, and this is the whole of it: --}}

{{-- SECTION: replaces. Use when there is exactly ONE contributor. --}}
@section('title', $post->title)
@section('content') ... @endsection
{{-- a second @section('title', ...) REPLACES the first. --}}

{{-- STACK: accumulates. Use when there is more than one. --}}
@push('styles') <link rel="stylesheet" href="{{ asset('css/print.css') }}"> @endpush
@push('styles') <link rel="stylesheet" href="{{ asset('css/wide.css') }}"> @endpush
{{-- both render, in push order. --}}

{{-- THE FAILURE THIS PREVENTS: --}}
{{-- WRONG: pushing a stylesheet into a region the layout FILLS
     with a section. --}}
@push('title') <title>Order 42</title> @endpush
{{-- ^ if the layout does @yield('title'), the push is ignored. If
     it does NOT yield, the push renders and there are now two
     titles in the document, and the browser uses the second. --}}

{{-- RIGHT: a section for a region one template owns, a stack for a
     region many contribute to. --}}
<title>@yield('title', config('app.name'))</title>
@stack('styles')

8.5 The once directive

Documents the once directive, its optional condition, and how it differs from pushOnce.

Once per render

The directive removes its body from the output the second time it is reached in a single render. It exists for one purpose: a component rendered many times on a page that needs to emit something once — a script tag, a modal definition, an asset — and where emitting it per instance is wrong.

It is scoped to one render of one template. It is not global, it is not per request, and it does not survive a subsequent include of the same template by another template. That last property surprises people: a partial included from two places within one page emits once, and the same partial included as part of two separately-rendered components also emits once per top-level render, which may be what was wanted or may not.

What it is not

It is not caching. The body is still evaluated each time the directive is reached — the work inside is performed and only the output is discarded — so a directive that queries inside a once block still queries every time. If the intent is to avoid the work, the answer is a different mechanism: a view composer, a cached fragment, or computing the value once outside the loop.

Where it belongs

Inside a component, around a resource declaration. That is the canonical use and it is nearly the only one: a component that needs a script or a style declares it once, and every instance works without the caller knowing.

@once
    <script src="{{ asset('js/editor.js') }}"></script>
@endonce

@once($page === 'edit')
    <script src="{{ asset('js/editor.js') }}"></script>
@endonce

@once tracks whether it has already rendered within the current request and skips subsequent renders; the argument makes that condition dependent on data. It differs from @pushOnce in scope: @once suppresses a repeated block anywhere in the output, while @pushOnce deduplicates a specific fragment inside one named stack.

Prefer libraries. For third-party scripts, a package that registers its own assets through Vite and a stack, combined with @once, removes the need for per-template script tags entirely.


{{-- THE CANONICAL USE, and it is nearly the only one: --}}
{{-- resources/views/components/editor.blade.php --}}
@props(['name' => 'editor'])

@once
    @push('scripts')
        <script src="{{ asset('js/editor.js') }}" defer></script>
    @endpush
@endonce

<div x-data="editor('{{ $name }}')">...</div>

{{-- Without @once, forty editors on a page push forty script tags.
     With it, one. --}}

{{-- SCOPE: ONCE PER RENDER OF ONE TEMPLATE. It is not global, not
     per request, and does NOT survive into a separate render of the
     same template. So a partial included from two places in ONE
     page emits once, and a component rendered in two SEPARATELY
     RENDERED top-level templates — a layout and an email body —
     emits once in each, because each is its own render. --}}

@once
    <div id="modal-definition" hidden>...</div>
@endonce

{{-- WHAT IT IS NOT: CACHING. The body is STILL EVALUATED every time
     the directive is reached; only the OUTPUT is discarded. --}}

@once
    {{-- WRONG: this query runs forty times and produces one output. --}}
    {!! AppSupportChart::svg($expensiveModel) !!}
@endonce

@once
    {{-- RIGHT: the work happens ONCE, outside the directive. --}}
    {!! $chartSvg !!}      {{-- computed by the controller or a
                                    composer, once, for the whole
                                    page --}}
@endonce

{{-- WHICH IS THE ACTUAL ANSWER to "I want this evaluated once": --}}
@php
    // Computed once at the top of the page and read by every
    // instance. This is what "once" actually means.
    $chartSvg = AppSupportChart::svg($expensiveModel);
@endphp

{{-- AND WHERE IT BELONGS: inside a component, around a RESOURCE
     declaration. --}}
@once
    @push('styles')
        <link rel="stylesheet" href="{{ asset('css/datepicker.css') }}">
    @endpush
@endonce

{{-- AND WHAT IT DOES NOT COVER: --}}
@once
    @include('partials.datepicker-assets')
@endonce
{{-- ^ the @once is on the include, so the partial's contents
     render once, and a push inside it is skipped on the second
     call too. What it does NOT cover is a SECOND include of the
     same partial from a different branch of the template tree. --}}

8.6 Include or component

Compares the include directive with the component tag and states the rule that separates them in practice.

AspectIncludeComponent
IsolationShares the parent scopeReceives only its own data
Slot contentNo named slotsDefault and named slots
AttributesNoneEscaped attribute bag with merging
PropsAmbient variablesDeclared, typed, defaulted
Best forFragments of one pageReused across pages

The rule is reuse scope. A fragment rendered only by one page is an include; anything a second page could use is a component, because the moment a second caller appears the shared-scope convenience becomes an undocumented coupling.

Exercises 8

  1. Build an include that can be overridden per application, using @includeFirst, and confirm the override wins.
  2. Replace a @foreach with an @include by an @each with an empty view and confirm identical output.
  3. Include the same partial twice and demonstrate the duplication, then fix it with @once and again with @pushOnce.
  4. Convert an include that two pages share into a component and remove its dependency on the parent scope.

Three mechanisms, and the question that decides

Three things compose a template's fragments: an include, a component, and a layout section. They differ in what they carry. An include carries markup and inherits data. A component carries markup and its own declared data, and renders in an isolated scope. A layout section carries nothing and contributes to a parent's structure.

The question that decides between an include and a component is whether the fragment needs data. A fragment that needs nothing but markup is an include, because a component with no props is a class and a file for something that is only markup. A fragment that needs data is a component, because an include passing that data is doing the component's job without the declaration, the defaults, or the isolation.

The distinction that catches people

An include and a component render at different times and in different scopes. A component is constructed and its props resolved before its template renders; an include's partial renders with whatever the parent has, including things it was not given. So a partial can read a variable a component would have needed a prop for, and moving from an include to a component is a change in behaviour, not a refactor: values that happened to be in scope stop being in scope.


{{-- THE THREE MECHANISMS, and what each carries. --}}

{{-- INCLUDE: markup, and the parent's ENTIRE data. --}}
@include('partials.order-summary', ['order' => $order])

{{-- COMPONENT: markup, and ONLY what it declared. --}}
<x-order-summary :order="$order" :compact="true" />

{{-- LAYOUT SECTION: no markup of its own; contributes to a
     parent's structure. --}}
@section('sidebar') ... @endsection

{{-- THE DECIDING QUESTION: does the fragment need DATA? --}}

{{-- NO DATA — an INCLUDE is right. A component would be a class
     and a file for something that is only markup. --}}
@include('partials.copyright')
{{-- and the exception: ONE piece of data makes it arguably a
     component. The line is around three values. --}}
@include('partials.copyright', ['year' => now()->year])

{{-- DATA — a COMPONENT is right, because an include passing that
     data is doing the component's job without the declaration, the
     defaults, or the isolation. --}}
<x-order-summary :order="$order" :compact="true" />

{{-- WHICH IS WHY A COMPONENT SCALES AND AN INCLUDE DOES NOT: --}}
@include('partials.stat', ['label' => __('stats.orders'),  'value' => $counts->orders])
@include('partials.stat', ['label' => __('stats.revenue'), 'value' => $counts->revenue])
@include('partials.stat', ['label' => __('stats.refunds'), 'value' => $counts->refunds])
{{-- ^ the same include four times, with the data spelled out at
     every call site. Works, and the repetition is in the markup. --}}

<x-stat :label="__('stats.orders')"  :value="$counts->orders" />
<x-stat :label="__('stats.revenue')" :value="$counts->revenue" />
{{-- ^ identical markup at both call sites, and the shape lives in
     one file. The include version also has to be remembered; this
     one is found by searching for x-stat. --}}


{{-- THE ISOLATION POINT, and it is the difference that matters
     when refactoring. --}}
@include('partials.order-summary', ['order' => $order])
{{-- the partial renders with EVERYTHING the parent has. So this
     works: --}}

{{-- resources/views/partials/order-summary.blade.php --}}
<h2>{{ $order->number }}</h2>
<p>{{ $company->name }}</p>
{{-- $company was never passed. It happened to be in scope. --}}

{{-- Move it to a component and $company DISAPPEARS: --}}
// app/View/Components/OrderSummary.php
public function __construct(
    public readonly Order $order,
    // $company is not declared, so it is not passed, and the
    // template now reads an undefined variable — rendering as an
    // empty company name rather than failing.
) {}
{{-- WHICH IS WHY moving an include to a component is a BEHAVIOUR
     CHANGE and not a refactor: values that happened to be in scope
     stop being in scope. Run the tests. --}}

{{-- AND THE TEST THAT CATCHES IT, which is worth having because
     the failure is silent: --}}
it('shows the company name', function () {
    // A component's contract is its constructor, so a variable the
    // template reads without declaring is a missing prop that
    // renders as nothing.
    expect($this->blade('<x-order-summary :order="$order" />'))
        ->toContain('Acme Ltd');
});
{{-- ^ this fails on the refactor, because $company is now gone. It
     would NOT have failed if the template had used an include with
     the parent's scope. --}}

{{-- THE THREE-LINE DECISION: --}}
{{--   needs NO data       -> @include
     needs 1-3 values     -> @include with an array
     needs more, or has
       several callers, or
       needs a default,
       or should be tested -> a component
     contributes to a
       parent's structure -> @section or a layout slot --}}

{{-- AND THE ONE QUESTION THAT OVERRIDES ALL THREE: does the
     fragment need to be CACHED or sent to a QUEUE? Then it is a
     component or a partial with explicit data, never an include
     reading the parent's scope — because a queue worker renders
     with no request and no session, so anything it read from the
     parent is undefined. --}}

9 · Forms: CSRF, Method Spoofing, Old Input and Validation

9.1 CSRF protection9.2 Method spoofing9.3 Repopulating with old input9.4 Rendering validation errors9.5 The model directive9.6 File uploads and enctype

Forms are where Blade meets the request lifecycle. This chapter documents the directives involved, the flash-data mechanism that repopulates a form after a failed submission, and the correct treatment of file inputs.

9.1 CSRF protection

Documents the csrf directive, the session token it emits, and the routes that must be exempted.

What the token is and what it is not

A cross-site request forgery token proves that a request originated from a page this application served. It is a per-session random value, placed in every form as a hidden field, compared against the session on submission, and rotated after a successful submission. The mechanism prevents an attacker's site from making a browser post to yours with the user's session, because the attacker cannot read a token they did not receive.

What it does not do is authenticate the request. A token in a form proves the form came from somewhere; it does not prove who filled it in, and a token that has leaked — through a referer header, a shared cache, or a log — is no protection at all. The companion measure is the cookie flag: a session cookie that is neither readable from JavaScript nor sent on a cross-site request is what actually removes the ambient authority the attack relies on.

Where it is exempt, and why that is dangerous

Read-only routes are exempt, because a GET must not change state. That exemption is the mechanism behind a class of defect: a route that changes state and accepts GET is not protected, and it is also cached, prefetched and triggered by a link. The check that catches it is asserting that every state-changing route requires POST — which is a test rather than a convention, because the convention has never once been followed by hand.

<form method="POST" action="{{ route('orders.store') }}">
    @csrf
    <input type="text" name="reference">
    <button type="submit">Create</button>
</form>

HTML forms cannot send custom headers, so the token travels as a hidden field. The middleware verifies it against the session on every non-read request, and a missing or stale token produces a 419 response, which surfaces as a token mismatch error in Laravel 13 when the session has already expired.

Never hand-write it. The token contains characters that escaping would alter, so the directive emits the field unescaped. Reproducing it manually is both fragile and a security liability.


{{-- THE FORM. The directive emits the hidden field with the current
     session's token. --}}
<form method="POST" action="{{ route('orders.store') }}">
    @csrf

    <input name="email" type="email" value="{{ old('email') }}">
    <button type="submit">{{ __('orders.place') }}</button>
</form>

{{-- WHICH EMITS: an input of type hidden named _token, and the
     SERVER compares it against the session. --}}

{{-- THE MIDDLEWARE, AND WHERE IT SITS: --}}
// bootstrap/app.php
->withMiddleware(function (Middleware $middleware) {
    $middleware->validateCsrfTokens(except: [
        'webhooks/*',        // a webhook cannot carry a token: the
                             // sender has no session. Authenticate
                             // by SIGNATURE instead — which is the
                             // whole point of this exemption.
        'api/*',             // a token-authenticated API is not
                             // vulnerable, because the token is not
                             // sent ambiently by a browser.
    ]);
})

{{-- THE COMPANION MEASURE, which actually removes the ambient
     authority. A token proves the form came from here; the cookie
     flags stop the browser sending credentials to another origin. --}}
// config/session.php
'secure'    => env('SESSION_SECURE_COOKIE'),
'http_only' => true,          // not readable from JavaScript
'same_site' => 'lax',         // not sent on a cross-site POST
{{-- ^ strict would also block a top-level navigation from another
     site, which breaks some legitimate flows; lax is the usual
     choice and it blocks the POST that the attack uses. --}}

{{-- WHERE IT IS EXEMPT, AND WHY THAT IS DANGEROUS. Read-only routes
     are exempt because a GET must not change state — so a route
     that CHANGES STATE AND ACCEPTS GET is unprotected, cached,
     prefetched and triggered by a link in an email. --}}
Route::get('/orders/{order}/cancel', [OrderController::class, 'cancel']);
{{-- ^ no token check. A link in an email cancels the order. --}}

{{-- THE FIX, AND THE TEST THAT PREVENTS IT — because this has never
     once been caught by hand: --}}
it('changes state only on POST, PUT, PATCH or DELETE', function () {
    foreach (Route::getRoutes() as $route) {
        $uri  = $route->uri();
        $verb = $route->methods()[0];

        if (str_starts_with($uri, ['livewire/', 'api/', 'webhooks/'])) continue;

        $middleware = $route->gatherMiddleware();
        if (! in_array('web', $middleware, true)) continue;  // no session
        if (in_array($verb, ['GET', 'HEAD', 'OPTIONS'], true)) continue;

        expect($verb)
            ->not->toBe('GET', "{$verb} {$uri} answers GET and changes state");
    }
});

9.2 Method spoofing

Documents the method directive, which overrides the HTTP verb of a form submission, and the values it accepts.

Browsers support only GET and POST on forms, so the intended verb travels as a hidden field that Laravel reads during route matching:

Faking a verb with a hidden field

Browsers only send GET and POST from a form, and HTML forms only offer those two. The method-spoofing directive is a hidden field naming the intended verb, and middleware that reads it and treats the request as that verb. It is the mechanism by which a form issues a PUT, a PATCH or a DELETE.

The security consequence is specific and worth stating: the framework's CSRF protection keys on the verb. A request whose method was spoofed by a hidden field is not the method the browser actually used, so a route that exempts DELETE from CSRF verification on the grounds that no browser sends DELETE has an exemption an attacker can reach by putting the same field in their form. That is not theoretical — it is the reason the exemption list and the spoofing field must be read together.

Why not just JavaScript

The alternative is to submit by JavaScript, which every modern application does anyway. The hidden field exists so a form works without JavaScript: progressive enhancement, and a form that submits correctly when the script fails to load. That is a real benefit and it is worth keeping, provided the exemption reasoning accounts for it.

<form method="POST" action="{{ route('orders.destroy', $order) }}">
    @csrf
    @method('DELETE')
    <button type="submit">Delete order</button>
</form>

The directive accepts PUT, PATCH and DELETE; any other value throws an InvalidArgumentException at render time, so a typo fails on the page rather than on submission.


{{-- THE FORM. --}}
<form method="POST" action="{{ route('orders.update', $order) }}">
    @csrf
    @method('PUT')

    <input name="reference" value="{{ $order->reference }}">
    <button type="submit">{{ __('orders.save') }}</button>
</form>

{{-- WHICH SENDS: POST with a _method field of PUT in the body. The
     browser sent POST; the middleware rewrites the method. --}}

{{-- THE REWRITE, AND IT IS A GLOBAL TRUST DECISION: --}}
IlluminateHttpRequest::enableHttpMethodParameterOverride();
{{-- ^ enabled by default in a Laravel application, which means ANY
     POST in the application can claim to be ANY verb. --}}

{{-- THE SECURITY CONSEQUENCE, and it is specific. CSRF protection
     keys on the VERB. So this reaches an exempt verb: --}}
// bootstrap/app.php
->withMiddleware(function (Middleware $middleware) {
    $middleware->validateCsrfTokens(except: ['webhooks/*']);
})
{{-- If DELETE were exempted — on the reasoning that "no browser
     sends DELETE" — then this reaches it: --}}
<form method="POST" action="/orders/42" action="/orders/42">
    @csrf
    @method('DELETE')     {{-- an attacker-controlled form on
                               another site. The verb becomes
                               DELETE and the exemption applies. --}}
</form>

{{-- THE RULE: THE EXEMPTION LIST AND THE SPOOFING FIELD MUST BE
     READ TOGETHER. Exempt a verb only if no POST can claim it —
     which, with method overriding enabled, is no verb at all. So
     exempt PATHS, not verbs. --}}
->withMiddleware(function (Middleware $middleware) {
    $middleware->validateCsrfTokens(except: ['webhooks/*']);
})
{{-- ^ a webhook has no session, so it cannot carry a token; it
     authenticates by signature instead. --}}

{{-- WHY KEEP IT ANYWAY: a form that works without JavaScript. --}}
<form method="POST" action="{{ route('profile.destroy') }}">
    @csrf
    @method('DELETE')
    <button type="submit">{{ __('profile.delete') }}</button>
</form>

<script>
    // The progressive path: no hidden field, no method override.
    const res = await fetch(`/profile`, {
        method: 'DELETE',
        headers: {
            'X-CSRF-TOKEN': document.querySelector('meta[name=csrf-token]').content,
            'Accept': 'application/json',
        },
    });
</script>

9.3 Repopulating with old input

Documents the old helper, the flash mechanism behind it, and the keys that must never be echoed back.

After failed validation the controller redirects back and flashes the request input, which Blade retrieves through old():

Repopulating a form after a failed submission

The old-input helper returns the value a user submitted before validation failed, falling back to a default when there was no submission. That is the whole feature, and the correctness of a form built on it depends on three things: the helper is used for the value, the session is flashed so the data survives the redirect, and the password is excluded.

The exclusion is the part people forget and it has two reasons. A password should not survive a round trip through the session store, which may be a cookie, a file, or Redis — anywhere but the process memory it was typed into. And a field repopulated with the old value after a failed login is a field showing a password nobody else can see, on a machine somebody may walk past.

What the helper does not do

It does not distinguish the fields that should be repopulated from those that should not, and it does not remember anything about which fields were submitted. A form with a field the user never filled repopulates with whatever was posted for that name — and a request is attacker-controlled. So a repopulated value is untrusted input and belongs in the page through the escaped form like any other.

<input type="text" name="reference" value="{{ old('reference') }}">

<select name="status">
    @foreach (['draft', 'sent'] as $status)
        <option value="{{ $status }}"
            @selected(old('status') === $status)>{{ $status }}</option>
    @endforeach
</select>

old() returns an empty string when the key is absent, so a failed submit and a first visit produce identical markup. Never render old('_token'): echoing the token as a field value exposes it in the page source.

Excluded keys. The redirect strips the keys listed in except, which removes _token and any password field. If a template needs a hidden field that was excluded, pass it as a separate route default rather than re-flashing it.


{{-- THE HELPER, and the two arguments that matter: --}}
<input name="email" type="email" value="{{ old('email') }}">
<input name="email" type="email" value="{{ old('email', $user->email) }}">
{{-- ^ THE FALLBACK: what to show when there was no submission. So
     an edit form starts populated and becomes the user's input
     after a failure — which is why the fallback is the persisted
     value and not a placeholder. --}}

{{-- AND THE FLASH, which is what makes the data survive the
     redirect. Without it there is nothing to read. --}}
// app/Http/Controllers/ProfileController.php
public function update(Request $request): RedirectResponse
{
    $validated = $request->validate([
        'email' => ['required', 'email', 'max:255'],
        'name'  => ['required', 'string', 'max:255'],
    ]);

    $request->user()->update($validated);

    return back()->with('status', 'profile-updated');
}

{{-- THE PASSWORD, EXCLUDED — and there are TWO reasons. --}}
<form method="POST" action="{{ route('password.update') }}">
    @csrf
    @method('PUT')

    <input name="current_password" type="password" autocomplete="current-password">
    {{-- ^ NEVER old('current_password'). Reason 1: it should not
         survive a round trip through the session store, which may be
         a cookie, a file or Redis: anywhere but the memory it was
         typed into. Reason 2: a password repopulated after a
         failed login is a password shown on screen that nobody else
         can see. --}}

    <input name="password" type="password" autocomplete="new-password">
    <input name="password_confirmation" type="password" autocomplete="new-password">

    <button type="submit">{{ __('password.update') }}</button>
</form>

{{-- WHAT THE HELPER DOES NOT DO, and it is the security half: --}}
<input name="role" value="{{ old('role') }}">
{{-- ^ a field the user never filled repopulates with whatever was
     POSTED for that name. The POST is attacker-controlled, so a
     repopulated value is UNTRUSTED INPUT and belongs in the page
     through the escaped form like any other value. --}}

{{-- THE FORM THAT GETS IT RIGHT, which is three rules: --}}
{{--   1. Use old() for the VALUE.
     2. Always pass a fallback for an EDIT form.
     3. Never use it for a password. --}}

@php
    // Checkboxes: compare against the KNOWN SET rather than
    // casting. "on" is not a value the domain recognises.
    $features = old('features', $user->features);
@endphp

@foreach (['reports', 'exports', 'webhooks'] as $feature)
    <label>
        <input type="checkbox" name="features[]" value="{{ $feature }}"
               @checked(in_array($feature, (array) $features, true))>
        {{ __("features.$feature") }}
    </label>
@endforeach

9.4 Rendering validation errors

Documents both forms of the error directive, including the named bag parameter and the attributes form.

Where validation errors live, and how a template reads them

Errors are flashed to the session by the validation layer and are available to every view in the redirect. The bag is keyed by field name, so a template reads the messages for the field it is rendering and ignores the rest. That is the design, and it is what makes a form template independent of the rules that produced the errors.

The two things worth knowing are the difference between the bag and the first error, and the cost of the flash. The bag holds every message for a field, which matters when a field has several rules and the user fixed none of them; the first-error accessor is a convenience that hides the other messages and is the wrong choice in a form that wants to list them all.

The flash is a session write on every validation failure

Flashing errors means writing the whole bag to the session store, and a form with thirty fields and a message per field writes a few kilobytes per failure. That is usually nothing. It becomes something on a session store that is a cookie, where the session is sent with every subsequent request and the payload is a hard cap — and the failure mode is a truncated session and a lost flash, which appears as a validation error that vanishes on the next failure.

<input type="text" name="reference"
       value="{{ old('reference') }}"
       @error('reference') aria-invalid="true" @enderror>

@error('reference')
    <span class="error">{{ $message }}</span>
@enderror

@error('email', 'login')
    <span class="error">{{ $message }}</span>
@enderror

The block form exposes the message as $message; the attribute form emits attributes only when the key has errors. The second parameter selects a named error bag, which is required when a form posts to a route that validates into anything other than the default bag.

Summaries. The bag is an array keyed by field name, so a summary list can be rendered by looping over it rather than repeating one block per field.


{{-- THE BAG, KEYED BY FIELD NAME. --}}
@if ($errors->has('email'))
    <p class="error" id="email-error">{{ $errors->first('email') }}</p>
@endif

{{-- EVERY MESSAGE for a field, which is what a form that has to
     show them all wants: --}}
@error('email')
    <ul class="errors" role="alert">
        @foreach ($messages as $message)
            <li>{{ $message }}</li>
        @endforeach
    </ul>
@enderror

{{-- AND THE SAME THING SHORTER, which is worth having as a partial
     because the markup appears in every form in the application: --}}
@include('partials.field-error', ['name' => 'email'])

{{-- THE FIRST-ERROR ACCESSOR HIDES: --}}
{{ $errors->first('email') }}
{{-- ^ if 'email' has three failed rules, this shows one message
     and the user fixes that one, submits, and sees the next. The
     bag is the right choice for a form that can show them all. --}}

{{-- ARIA, because an error a screen reader does not announce is an
     error for some users it does not exist for: --}}
<label for="email">{{ __('auth.email') }}</label>
<input id="email" name="email" type="email"
       value="{{ old('email') }}"
       @error('email') aria-invalid="true" aria-describedby="email-error" @enderror>
@error('email')
    <p class="error" id="email-error" role="alert">{{ $message }}</p>
@enderror

{{-- THE FLASH IS A SESSION WRITE ON EVERY FAILURE. Usually
     nothing. It becomes something when the session store is a
     COOKIE, because then the session is sent with every request and
     its payload is a hard cap — and the failure mode is a
     TRUNCATED session and a LOST flash, which appears as a
     validation error that vanishes on the next failure. --}}

// config/session.php
'driver' => env('SESSION_DRIVER', 'database'),   // not 'cookie' for
                                                 // large forms
'cookie_lifetime' => (int) env('SESSION_LIFETIME', 120),

{{-- THE THRESHOLD, as a review question rather than a number: a
     form with more than about twenty validated fields on a cookie
     session is a flash that can be truncated. --}}

{{-- THE SUMMARY DISPLAY, for a form that fails as a whole: --}}
@if ($errors->any())
    <div class="alert alert-error" role="alert">
        <p>{{ __('validation.summary', ['count' => $errors->count()]) }}</p>
        <ul>
            @foreach ($errors->all() as $message)
                <li>{{ $message }}</li>
            @endforeach
        </ul>
    </div>
@endif

9.5 The model directive

Documents the model directive, which populates an entire form from an Eloquent record or a plain object.

Binding a form to a model, and which model the directive means

The directive binds an input's name, value and error state to a model path, so a form does not repeat the field name four times. It resolves a dotted path against an object graph, which means it works with a model, a collection, or a plain array.

What is easy to miss is that there are two different things called "the model" in this area: the object the form is editing, and the class the directive derives the input type and the route from. The directive takes an optional second argument naming the model to derive from, and it defaults to the route's model. A form for a related record — an order's address, a user's profile — therefore needs that argument explicitly, and omitting it produces a form whose names and URLs are built from the wrong record. The result is a form that renders correctly, submits to the wrong route, and fails validation with a message about a field nobody filled.

Binding to a collection

The colon form binds to an element of a collection, which is what a form with a repeating set of rows needs. The difference from the form form is the index, and the index is what distinguishes the rows on submission. A form binding to a collection without stable indices — the loop index rather than the record identifier — produces names the server cannot tell apart, which is the mechanism behind a row update that edits the wrong record.

<form method="POST" action="{{ route('orders.store') }}">
    @csrf
    <input type="text" name="reference">
    <input type="email" name="customer_email">
    <textarea name="notes"></textarea>
    <select name="status">
        <option value="draft">Draft</option>
        <option value="sent">Sent</option>
    </select>
    @error('reference') aria-invalid="true" @enderror

    <button type="submit">Save</button>
@endmodel

The model directive renders the form, then populates each named field from the model’s attributes, including the selected option of a select and the content of a textarea. It also appends a hidden _method field with the spoofed verb and a route field used to resolve the URL from the model. For an existing record the surrounding form must still declare @method('PUT') or @method('PATCH').

Nested keys. Field names must match attribute names exactly. A form field named customer.email requires a matching key on the model, and a value that is not an Eloquent attribute is silently left untouched.


{{-- THE MODEL FORM. Name, value, and error state from one path. --}}
<form method="POST" action="{{ route('profile.store') }}">
    @csrf

    <label for="name">{{ __('profile.name') }}</label>
    <input id="name" name="name" type="text"
           value="{{ old('name') }}"
           @error('name') aria-invalid="true" @enderror>
    @error('name') <p role="alert">{{ $message }}</p> @enderror
</form>

{{-- THE FULL FORM, where a model directive removes the repetition: --}}
@php($formModel = old('profile', $user))
<x-form.model :model="$formModel" action="{{ route('profile.store') }}">
    <x-form.input name="name" label="{{ __('profile.name') }}" />
    <x-form.input name="email" type="email" label="{{ __('profile.email') }}" />
    <x-form.textarea name="bio" rows="4" label="{{ __('profile.bio') }}" />
</x-form.model>
{{-- ...which removes about four repetitions per field, and is the
     right answer for a form with more than a handful of fields. --}}

{{-- THE SECOND ARGUMENT, and it is the part that is easy to miss. --}}
{{-- There are TWO "the model": the object being edited, and the
     class the directive derives the input type and the ROUTE from.
     It defaults to the ROUTE's model. So this form: --}}

<form method="POST" action="{{ route('orders.store') }}">
    @csrf

    <input name="name" value="{{ old('name') }}">
    {{-- ^ renders "name" correctly, derives from Order because the
         route is orders.store, and posts to the WRONG place —
         validation then fails with a message about a field nobody
         filled. The fix is to name the model explicitly. --}}
</form>

{{-- THE CORRECT FORM, with the model named: --}}
<form method="POST" action="{{ route('orders.shippingAddress.store', $order) }}">
    @csrf

    @php($address = old('shippingAddress', $order->shippingAddress))

    <input name="name"  value="{{ $address->name }}">
    <input name="line1" value="{{ $address->line1 }}">
    <input name="city"  value="{{ $address->city }}">
</form>

{{-- THE COLLECTION FORM, and the index is the whole point: --}}
@foreach ($order->lines as $line)
    <input name="lines[{{ $line->id }}][qty]" value="{{ $line->qty }}">
@endforeach
{{-- ^ names by the RECORD ID: stable across a deletion, and the
     server can tell which row is which. --}}

@foreach ($order->lines as $line)
    <input name="lines[{{ $loop->index }}][qty]" value="{{ $line->qty }}">
@endforeach
{{-- ^ names by LOOP INDEX: remove row 1 and row 2's data is now
     submitted as row 1. The server updates the WRONG RECORD,
     silently. --}}

9.6 File uploads and enctype

Documents the encoding requirement for file inputs, the missing-file error case, and how to seed a file field from old input.

A file input requires a multipart encoding, without which the browser submits the filename only:

Two form attributes and a set of rules

An upload needs the encoding set on the form and a multipart content type, and the second is a consequence of the first rather than an independent requirement. The file rule validates the upload itself — presence, size, and MIME type — and the two things people get wrong are the size limit and the MIME type being checked against the browser's declaration rather than the file's contents.

The MIME type a browser sends is a claim, not a measurement, and it is set by the operating system. Renaming a shell script to .jpg changes what the browser says. A rule that validates the declared type is therefore a usability check rather than a security one, and the security check is different: store the upload outside the web root, name it yourself, and serve it with a content type and a content disposition that does not let the browser execute it.

The limit that is not about size

The server's upload limit usually applies before the application code runs, which produces an error that never reaches the validator and is reported as a generic request failure. Two limits have to be raised together — the web server's and PHP's — and a third, the session's, if the upload goes through a validated form whose error bag is flashed.

<form method="POST" action="{{ route('avatars.store') }}"
      enctype="multipart/form-data">
    @csrf
    <input type="file" name="avatar" accept="image/*">
    @error('avatar')
        <p>{{ $message }}</p>
    @enderror
</form>

Browsers cannot repopulate a file field, and browsers and proxies impose size limits independently, so file validation errors are routine. Validate the file type and size on the server, and never rely on the accept attribute as a control.

Exercises 9

  1. Build a form with all three directives and confirm a failed submission repopulates every field.
  2. Verify that old('_token') is absent from the flashed input and explain why it must stay absent.
  3. Render a validation summary by looping over the error bag and compare it with per-field messaging.
  4. Use @model on a create-and-edit pair and confirm the select element preselects the correct option in both modes.

{{-- THE FORM. BOTH attributes, and the second is a consequence of
     the first rather than an independent requirement: --}}
<form method="POST" action="{{ route('documents.store') }}"
      enctype="multipart/form-data">
    @csrf

    <input type="file" name="document" accept=".pdf,.docx">
    <button type="submit">{{ __('documents.upload') }}</button>
</form>

{{-- AND THE VALIDATION, where the size limit and the type check
     are the two things people get wrong: --}}
// app/Http/Requests/StoreDocumentRequest.php
public function rules(): array
{
    return [
        'document' => [
            'required',
            'file',        // the UploadedFile, not a string
            'max:10240',   // KILOBYTES: 10 MB
            // THE MIME CHECK IS A USABILITY CHECK, NOT A SECURITY
            // ONE. What the browser sends is a CLAIM set by the
            // operating system — rename a shell script to .jpg and
            // the browser says image/jpeg.
            'mimes:pdf,docx',
        ],
    ];
}

{{-- THE SECURITY CHECK, and it is not in the rules at all: --}}
// app/Http/Controllers/DocumentController.php
public function store(StoreDocumentRequest $request): RedirectResponse
{
    $file = $request->file('document');

    // 1. STORE OUTSIDE THE WEB ROOT. public/ is served by the web
    //    server; a PHP file there is executed.
    $path = $file->storeAs('documents', Str::uuid() . '.bin', 'local');
    // ^ a name THIS APPLICATION CHOSE, from a UUID: the client's
    //   filename is discarded, so it cannot contain a traversal and
    //   cannot collide.

    // 2. SERVE IT WITH HEADERS THAT PREVENT EXECUTION.
    return response()->download(
        Storage::path($path),
        originalName: $request->input('display_name', 'document'),
        headers: ['Content-Type' => 'application/octet-stream',
                  'X-Content-Type-Options' => 'nosniff'],
    );
    // ^ octet-stream plus nosniff means the browser does not consult
    //   the STORED extension, so a stored .html is downloaded rather
    //   than rendered.

    // 3. NEVER STORE THE ORIGINAL NAME.
}

{{-- THE LIMIT THAT IS NOT ABOUT SIZE. The server's upload limit
     applies BEFORE the application runs, so the error never reaches
     the validator and appears as a generic failure. --}}
# nginx
client_max_body_size 12M;      # must be >= the rule's max
# php.ini
upload_max_filesize = 12M     # AND this one
post_max_size = 13M            # AND this one: exceeding it
                               # silently truncates the body so
                               # $_POST arrives EMPTY with no error
# .htaccess, for Apache
php_value upload_max_filesize 12M
php_value post_max_size 13M

{{-- THE PITFALL THAT PRODUCES AN EMPTY $_POST: post_max_size
     exceeded means PHP discards the whole body INCLUDING the
     tokens, so validation fails on "document is required" and
     "csrf token is missing" at the same time — and the second
     message is the real clue. --}}

{{-- THE MULTIPLE-FILE CASE, and the array shape the validator
     expects: --}}
<input type="file" name="documents[]" multiple>

'documents'   => ['required', 'array', 'max:5'],
'documents.*' => ['file', 'max:5120', 'mimes:pdf'],

10 · Blade and Alpine.js: The Interactivity Boundary

10.1 Where interactivity belongs10.2 Passing server state to Alpine10.3 Alpine inside Blade components10.4 Loops and keys10.5 Alpine plugins10.6 Content security policy

Alpine supplies client-side reactivity without a build step, which makes it the natural counterpart to Blade. This chapter documents the boundary between the two and the patterns that keep it clean.

10.1 Where interactivity belongs

States the division of labour between Blade and Alpine, and the condition under which server state must be replaced by a request.

Blade renders the correct initial state; Alpine manages what happens after the page loads. Anything that requires the database belongs in Blade or Livewire, and anything that manipulates the already-rendered document belongs in Alpine.

Where interactivity belongs, and the line that decides it

A template should contain the behaviour that makes its own markup work — what a disclosure does when clicked, what a combobox does when a key is pressed, what a row does when it is removed. It should not contain the behaviour of the application that supplied the data, because that is the layer's job and putting it in a template means it cannot be tested, cannot be reused, and cannot be reasoned about by anyone reading the controller.

The distinction in practice is between a directive's behaviour and a domain rule's behaviour. Showing a validation message as a field is touched belongs to the template, because it is about the markup. Disabling a submit button while a payment is in flight belongs to the template for the same reason. Rejecting an order whose lines have been removed by another user is a domain rule, and no amount of client-side behaviour makes it safe — the check happens on the server, and the client-side version exists only to avoid a round trip.

The rule that keeps this from eroding

Client-side behaviour is a convenience and never a control. Anything the server does not repeat is not enforced. That is worth writing into a template's design because it is the argument that stops a reviewer from approving a check that exists only in the browser, and it is the argument that stops a template from acquiring the logic it appeared to be avoiding.

<div x-data="{ open: @js($panelOpen) }">
    <button @click("open = !open")>Toggle</button>
    <div x-show="open" x-cloak>
        <p>Rendered by the server, revealed by Alpine.</p>
    </div>
</div>

x-show toggles a CSS property, so the content remains in the HTML and stays reachable to assistive technology and to users without JavaScript. x-if removes the element entirely; reserve it for content that must not exist in the document at all.

x-cloak. Add a rule [x-cloak] { display: none } to the stylesheet so an element governed by Alpine is not briefly visible before Alpine initialises.


{{-- BELONGS IN THE TEMPLATE: behaviour about ITS OWN markup. --}}

{{-- A disclosure. The template knows what a disclosure is. --}}
<x-disclosure>
    <x-slot:trigger>{{ __('orders.show_details') }}</x-slot:trigger>
    <x-slot:content>
        <dl>...</dl>
    </x-slot:content>
</x-disclosure>

{{-- A field that shows its error as soon as it is touched. The
     markup is the template's, so its behaviour is too. --}}
<input x-data="{ touched: false, error: @js($errors->first('email')) }"
       @blur="touched = true"
       :aria-invalid="touched && error ? 'true' : null"
       :class="touched && error ? 'input-error' : ''">

{{-- DOES NOT BELONG IN THE TEMPLATE: the domain rule. --}}
<form x-data="{ submit: false }"
      x-on:submit="if (! lines.length) { $el.preventDefault() }">
    {{-- ^ "do not submit with no lines" in the BROWSER. Correct as
         a courtesy; worthless as a control, because the request can
         be made by anything. The server has to check it again. --}}
</form>

{{-- app/Http/Requests/StoreOrderRequest.php --}}
public function rules(): array
{
    return [
        'lines' => ['required', 'array', 'min:1'],
        'lines.*.qty' => ['required', 'integer', 'min:1'],
    ];
}
{{-- ^ the same rule, ENFORCED. This is the line: client-side
     behaviour is a convenience and never a control; anything the
     server does not repeat is not enforced. --}}

{{-- AND A THIRD CASE, which is the interesting one: a client check
     that the server CANNOT easily repeat. --}}
<button x-on:click="confirm('Delete this order?')"
        x-on:click.away="open = false">
    {{ __('orders.delete') }}
</button>
{{-- ^ the confirmation dialog exists only in the browser, and that
     is CORRECT: the server cannot ask the user a question, so it
     has nothing to enforce. The irreversible action is still the
     server's, and still needs its own authorisation check. --}}

10.2 Passing server state to Alpine

Compares the three ways to hand data to Alpine and the injection that the remaining approach permits.

Passing server state into a client scope

A template that starts a client-side scope has to put server state into it, and the three available mechanisms differ in what they escape and what they produce. A serialised JSON block is the safest and the most verbose. An expression attribute takes a PHP value through a serialiser that also escapes it for the HTML context. And a data attribute is the most robust, because the value is read by the parser rather than by the serialiser.

The choice matters for two values in particular. A string has to survive the HTML parser and the JavaScript parser, so it needs both escapes and the right quoting. A file path or a URL needs a scheme check, because the dangerous part of a URL is not a character. And anything large should not be in the page at all: a megabyte of data in a script block is a megabyte the browser parses before the page can render.

What does not belong in a client scope at all

A secret, a permission decision and a price. A client scope is visible to anyone who opens the developer tools, so a value placed there is public — which makes a permission decision placed there not a decision, and a price placed there not a price the server will honour.

<div x-data="{ options: @js($options) }">
    <template x-for="option in options">
        <span x-text="option.label"></span>
    </template>
</div>

<div x-data="{ config: {{ Js::from($config) }} }">
    <span x-text="config.currency"></span>
</div>

<div x-data='@json($config)'>
    <span x-text="currency"></span>
</div>

@js() is the preferred form: it applies script-safe escaping, handles PHP types the JSON encoder cannot represent, and removes the need to choose an attribute quoting style. @json() inside a single-quoted attribute is the pattern Livewire emits and is correct, but it breaks if the data contains a single quote and the quoting is not chosen to match.

Never concatenate. Writing x-data="{ name: '{{ $name }}' }" is injectable: a value containing ' closes the string. Use @js(), which escapes that sequence.


{{-- (1) A JSON BLOCK. The safest, and the most verbose. --}}
<script type="application/json" id="page-data">
    {!! json_encode($data, JSON_HEX_TAG | JSON_HEX_AMP | JSON_HEX_APOS | JSON_HEX_QUOT) !!}
</script>
<script>
    const data = JSON.parse(document.getElementById('page-data').textContent);
</script>

{{-- (2) AN EXPRESSION ATTRIBUTE, which is the shorthand and which
     serialises AND escapes for the HTML context: --}}
<div x-data="{ userId: @js($user->id), max: @js($limit) }">
{{-- ^ @js returns an object whose __toString does both, so it is
     safe in a double-quoted attribute, a single-quoted one, and a
     script block. Raw json_encode is safe in NONE of those three
     without the flags. --}}

{{-- (3) A DATA ATTRIBUTE, which is the most robust because the HTML
     PARSER handles it rather than a serialiser: --}}
<div id="panel" data-name="{{ $user->name }}"
     data-birthday="{{ $user->birthday->toIso8601String() }}"
     data-endpoint="{{ route('users.show', $user) }}">
    <span x-text="$el.dataset.name"></span>
</div>
{{-- ^ the value never enters the JavaScript source, so there is no
     context to get wrong. The parser has been attacking this for
     thirty years. --}}

{{-- WHICH TO REACH FOR: --}}
{{--   simple scalars       -> @js() on an x-data expression
     anything rendered      -> a data attribute
     a bulk object          -> a JSON block
     over ~100 KB           -> an endpoint, not the page --}}

{{-- WHAT DOES NOT BELONG IN A CLIENT SCOPE AT ALL, because the
     developer tools will show it to anyone who asks: --}}
<div x-data="{ apiKey: @js($tenant->api_key) }">
{{-- ^ NOT A SECRET. Every value in a scope is public. --}}

<div x-data="{ canDelete: @js($user->can('delete')) }">
    <button x-show="canDelete">{{ __('delete') }}</button>
</div>
{{-- ^ NOT A PERMISSION DECISION. Hiding the button is a courtesy;
     the server repeats the check, and a user who finds the endpoint
     gets the same answer either way. --}}

<div x-data="{ price: @js($order->total->amount()) }">
    <button>Pay {{ price }}</button>
</div>
{{-- ^ NOT A PRICE. The server charges what it calculates. A price
     in the page is a display, and a display the client can change
     is not what the user is charged. --}}

10.3 Alpine inside Blade components

Shows the standard pattern for an Alpine root inside a Blade component, and the structural rule that makes it work.

A component that owns an interactive region

A component and a client-side scope compose without special support, because the scope is an attribute and the framework passes attributes through. What needs care is where the scope's data comes from: a component's props are resolved before its template renders, so a scope inside a component can read the props and not anything the parent has.

The second thing that needs care is the boundary of the scope. A scope on the component's root element covers everything the component renders, including any slot content the caller passed — so a caller writing inside a slot is writing inside the component's scope and can see its state. That is occasionally what you want and is worth being deliberate about.

Where a component should not own behaviour

A component that both displays data and changes it is doing two jobs, and the test is whether the behaviour is about the markup or about the domain. A component that owns a disclosure, a menu or a tab set is fine. A component that posts a form and reloads the page is a page, and it will grow a controller's responsibilities in a file that has no place for them.

@props(['options' => [], 'label' => 'Select'])

<div x-data="{ open: false, chosen: null }">
    <button @click="open = !false" class="btn">
        <span x-text="chosen ?? @js($label)"></span>
    </button>
    <ul x-show="open" x-cloak @click.outside="open = false">
        @foreach ($options as $option)
            <li>
                <button type="button" @click="chosen = @js($option->label)">
                    {{ $option->label }}
                </button>
            </li>
        @endforeach
    </ul>
    <input type="hidden" name="option_id" :value="chosen">
</div>

Each component instance owns one x-data scope, so an array of components never shares state. The hidden input bound with :value carries the selection back on submit; without it the interactivity is purely visual and the form transmits nothing.

One root per instance. A component template must emit exactly one root element. Two roots produce two independent scopes and break any state the markup assumes is shared.


{{-- THE COMPONENT, with a scope that reads its OWN props: --}}
{{-- resources/views/components/combobox.blade.php --}}
@props([
    'name',
    'options' => [],
    'value'   => null,
    'label'   => null,
])

{{-- The scope is on the ROOT element, so it covers the whole
     component INCLUDING any slot the caller passes — and the caller
     can see the component's state. Sometimes what you want; be
     deliberate. --}}
<div x-data="{
            open: false,
            query: @js(''),
            selected: @js($value),
            get matches() {
                return @js($options)
                    .filter(o => o.label.toLowerCase()
                                    .includes(this.query.toLowerCase()));
            },
         }"
     @click.outside="open = false"
     {{ $attributes->merge(['class' => 'combobox']) }}>

    <input name="{{ $name }}"
           x-model="query"
           @focus="open = true"
           @keydown.escape="open = false"
           role="combobox" aria-expanded="false">

    <ul x-show="open" x-cloak role="listbox">
        <template x-for="o in matches" :key="o.id">
            <li @click="selected = o.id; open = false" role="option">
                <span x-text="o.label"></span>
            </li>
        </template>
    </ul>

    <input type="hidden" name="{{ $name }}" :value="selected ?? ''">
</div>

{{-- AND THE CALLER, which cannot see the scope's internals and
     does not need to: --}}
<x-combobox name="assignee"
           :options="$users->map(fn ($u) => ['id' => $u->id, 'label' => $u->name])"
           :value="$task->assignee_id"
           class="w-full" />

{{-- x-cloak IS NOT OPTIONAL. Without it, x-show renders its
     element visible for one frame before the framework boots, so
     the combobox flashes open. The CSS hides anything with it
     until the framework removes the attribute. --}}
/* resources/css/app.css */
[x-cloak] { display: none !important; }

{{-- WHERE A COMPONENT SHOULD NOT OWN BEHAVIOUR: --}}
{{-- resources/views/components/save-button.blade.php --}}
<form x-data="{ saving: false }"
      x-on:submit="saving = true"
      method="POST" action="{{ route('tasks.store') }}">
    @csrf
    <input type="hidden" name="title" value="{{ $task->title }}">
    <button :disabled="saving">
        <span x-show="! saving">{{ __('save') }}</span>
        <span x-show="saving" x-cloak>{{ __('saving') }}</span>
    </button>
</form>
{{-- ^ "disable the button while the request is in flight" is about
     the MARKUP, so it belongs here. But this component now creates
     a task, validates it and decides what happens next: that is a
     PAGE, and it will grow a controller's responsibilities in a file
     that has nowhere to put them. The boundary is the test: is the
     behaviour about the markup or about the domain? --}}

10.4 Loops and keys

Explains when to loop in Blade and when to loop in Alpine, and how keyed templates handle DOM identity.

Keys, and what they are for

A client-side list is a live structure, and the framework cannot know which element in the data corresponds to which element on screen. The key attribute states that correspondence, and it has one job: when the list is reordered, filtered or removed from, the framework uses the key to move the existing DOM nodes rather than destroying and recreating them.

The failure is always the same shape. Without a key, filtering a list destroys every node and recreates the survivors, which loses focus, loses scroll position, loses the state of anything inside a row, and resets any transition. With a stable key, the framework moves the three surviving nodes and leaves them alone. With an unstable key — the array index — the framework is told that element 0 is still element 0, so reordering moves the data and leaves the nodes, and the result is a row whose content and identity disagree.

What makes a key stable

A database identifier is stable. A slug is stable until the slug changes. An array index is stable only while the order is and nothing is inserted before. The failure mode of an index key is therefore not the reorder — it is the insert, which is why a list that appends works and a list that prepends does not.

<div x-data="{ rows: @js($rows) }">
    <template x-for="row in rows" :key="row.id">
        <div class="row" x-text="row.label"></div>
    </template>
    <button @click="rows.push({ id: Date.now() })">Add</button>
</div>

Loop in Blade when the data is server-owned and the page reloads: fewer DOM nodes and no client state to synchronise. Loop in Alpine when the client adds, removes or reorders rows without a round trip. In that case always provide :key, because Alpine reuses DOM nodes by key and an unkeyed loop produces duplicated or stale elements after a mutation.


{{-- WITHOUT A KEY: every filter operation destroys and recreates
     every node. --}}
<ul>
    <template x-for="row in rows">
        <li x-text="row.name"></li>
    </template>
</ul>
{{-- ^ type in the filter, and the three surviving rows lose focus,
     lose any open state, and restart their transitions. --}}

{{-- WITH A STABLE KEY: the framework MOVES the surviving nodes. --}}
<ul>
    <template x-for="row in rows" :key="row.id">
        <li x-text="row.name"
            x-data="{ expanded: false }"
            :class="expanded ? 'is-open' : ''">
            <button @click="expanded = ! expanded">{{ row.name }}</button>
            <div x-show="expanded" x-cloak x-collapse>...</div>
        </li>
    </template>
</ul>
{{-- ^ filter, and the survivors are moved rather than rebuilt: the
     expanded one stays expanded and keeps its node. --}}

{{-- WITH AN UNSTABLE KEY, which is worse than no key at all: --}}
<template x-for="(row, i) in rows" :key="i">
    <li x-text="row.name" x-data="{ expanded: false }">
        <button @click="expanded = ! expanded">{{ row.name }}</button>
        <div x-show="expanded" x-cloak>...</div>
    </li>
</template>
{{-- ^ prepend a row and the framework is told element 0 is STILL
     element 0 — so it updates node 0's text in place. The node that
     had the FIRST row's expanded state now belongs to the SECOND
     row, and the button opens the wrong item. Silently. --}}

{{-- WHAT MAKES A KEY STABLE, as a table: --}}
{{--   row.id              stable — use this
     row.slug             stable until the slug changes
     i (the index)        stable ONLY while the order is, and
                          nothing is inserted before
     a random value       stable within one render, so the framework
                          rebuilds every node every cycle — worse
                          than no key --}}

{{-- AND THE INSERT CASE, which is where an index key fails even
     though the list only ever appends elsewhere: --}}
<ul>
    <template x-for="(row, i) in [...newRows, ...rows]" :key="i">
        <li x-text="row.name" x-data="{ expanded: false }">
            <button @click="expanded = ! expanded">{{ row.name }}</button>
        </li>
    </template>
</ul>
{{-- ^ a new row PREPENDED shifts every index, so every existing
     node is told it is a different row. Expanded state and input
     values move to the wrong rows. --}}

{{-- THE SERVER HAS THE SAME PROBLEM, and it is worth stating
     because the two are routinely confused: --}}
@foreach ($tasks as $task)
    <tr wire:key="task-{{ $task->id }}">   {{-- the SERVER loop --}}
        {{-- ...and the client list must use the SAME key. A live
             update that diffs by index while the server renders by
             index produces a list that reorders itself. --}}
    </tr>
@endforeach

10.5 Alpine plugins

Surveys the plugins that solve recurring interface problems, and when a plugin is preferable to custom code.

PluginDirectiveUse for
Collapsex-collapseSmooth height transitions
Intersectx-intersectReveal on scroll, lazy work
Persistx-persistState across navigations
Focusx-trapModal focus management
Anchorx-anchorPositioning to a trigger

Plugins are declared once in the entry point and cost a few kilobytes each. Reach for one before writing custom positioning or transition logic, and prefer a Blade component that wraps the plugin over repeating plugin markup in every template.

Wrap, do not sprinkle. A component that renders a dropdown, a modal or a date picker once removes the plugin markup, the focus management and the transition from every call site.

Extending a client-side library rather than the framework

A plugin is registered once and its behaviour becomes available to any scope that asks for it. This is the right mechanism for three things: a behaviour that has been written already and would otherwise be reimplemented, a behaviour that needs configuration — an endpoint, a format, a set of options — and a behaviour that has to be attached to a class or an attribute rather than to a directive.

What a plugin is not is a place for application logic. A plugin that knows about a specific model or a specific route has become a component with worse ergonomics: it is global, it is registered in a provider, it is invisible at the point of use, and it cannot be tested by rendering the thing that uses it. The test is whether the plugin could be extracted and published as a general-purpose library — if not, it belongs in a component.

The lifecycle problem, and why it is a plugin problem

A plugin that registers a global listener runs on every scope on the page, including the ones on a different component. That is occasionally correct and frequently a performance problem, because the listener runs for scopes that will never use it. A directive that only initialises its own element avoids this, which is the practical argument for preferring a directive to a global plugin when the behaviour is local.


{{-- REGISTERING A PLUGIN, once, globally: --}}
// resources/js/app.js
import { collapse } from '@alpinejs/collapse';

Alpine.plugin(collapse);
Alpine.start();

{{-- A PLUGIN IS THE RIGHT MECHANISM FOR THREE THINGS, and the
     question is which of them applies: --}}
{{--   1. Behaviour that already exists and would otherwise be
         reimplemented — collapse, focus, intersect.
     2. Behaviour that needs CONFIGURATION: an endpoint, a format,
         a set of options.
     3. Behaviour that must attach to a CLASS or an ATTRIBUTE
         rather than to a directive. --}}

{{-- (2) CONFIGURED BEHAVIOUR, which is the case a plugin exists
     for: --}}
Alpine.plugin(apiClient);
function apiClient(Alpine) {
    Alpine.magic('api', () => ({
        // CONFIGURED, so the endpoint is not in a template.
        get: (resourcePath) => fetch('/api/' + resourcePath, {
            headers: {
                'X-CSRF-TOKEN': document
                    .querySelector('meta[name=csrf-token]').content,
                'Accept': 'application/json',
            },
        }).then(r => r.json()),
    }));
}
{{-- ^ `@ is escaped here as ${ so the template literal does not
     interpolate; the shipped page contains the literal text. --}}

{{-- AND USING IT, where the endpoint and the CSRF header exist
     nowhere in the markup: --}}
<div x-data="{ results: [], async search(q) {
        this.results = await $api.get('/`/users/search?q=' + q);
     } }">
    <input @input.debounce.300ms="search($event.target.value)">
    <template x-for="u in results" :key="u.id">
        <p x-text="u.name"></p>
    </template>
</div>

{{-- THE CODE THAT GIVES A PLUGIN AWAY, and it is the plugin that
     already exists: --}}
Alpine.plugin(focus);
Alpine.plugin(intersect);
Alpine.plugin(collapse);
{{-- ^ three installs for three libraries. A plugin for any of
     these is worse: less tested, less maintained, and its
     behaviour will diverge from the library's on the next
     release. --}}


{{-- THE DIRECTIVE FORM, which only initialises its own elements —
     and is the better choice when the behaviour is LOCAL: --}}
Alpine.directive('tooltip', (el, { expression }, { evaluate }) => {
    const text = evaluate(expression);
    const id = 'tip-' + Math.random().toString(36).slice(2);

    el.setAttribute('aria-describedby', id);

    const tip = document.createElement('div');
    tip.id = id;
    tip.className = 'tooltip';
    // textContent, NEVER innerHTML: the expression is a template
    // value and may be untrusted, so a tooltip is the classic
    // injection point in an otherwise careful page.
    tip.textContent = text;
    tip.hidden = true;

    el.after(tip);
