    const show = () => { tip.hidden = false; };
    const hide = () => { tip.hidden = true; };

    el.addEventListener('mouseenter', show);
    el.addEventListener('focus', show);
    el.addEventListener('mouseleave', hide);
    el.addEventListener('blur', hide);

    // THE LIFECYCLE, and it is what makes a directive safe. A
    // directive attached to an element that is later removed leaks
    // four listeners; the returned cleanup runs when the element
    // leaves the DOM.
    return () => {
        el.removeEventListener('mouseenter', show);
        el.removeEventListener('focus', show);
        el.removeEventListener('mouseleave', hide);
        el.removeEventListener('blur', hide);
        tip.remove();
    };
});

{{-- AND USING IT: --}}
<button x-tooltip="@js(__('orders.what_this_does'))">
    {{ __('orders.more_info') }}
</button>

{{-- THE GLOBAL-PLUGIN PROBLEM, which is the practical argument for
     the directive form: a plugin that registers a global listener
     runs on EVERY scope on the page, including the ones on a
     different component. Occasionally correct, frequently a
     performance problem. A directive initialises only its own
     element. --}}

Alpine.directive('poll', (el, { expression }, { evaluate }) => {
    const url = evaluate(expression);
    let alive = true;

    const tick = async () => {
        if (!alive) return;
        const res = await fetch(url, { headers: { Accept: 'application/json' } });
        const data = await res.json();
        el.dispatchEvent(new CustomEvent('polled', { detail: data }));
        if (alive) setTimeout(tick, 5000);
    };

    tick();

    // Without this the timer keeps firing after the element is
    // gone, which is a request every five seconds from a page the
    // user has navigated away from.
    return () => { alive = false; };
});

{{-- A PLUGIN THAT KNOWS YOUR DOMAIN IS THE MISTAKE: --}}
Alpine.plugin(orderStatusPlugin);   // knows about Order, routes to
                                    // /orders/{id}/status, and
                                    // refetches on an interval

{{-- ^ global, registered in a provider, invisible at the point of
     use, and untestable without booting the whole page. Extract it
     and it is not a general-purpose plugin — it is a COMPONENT
     with worse ergonomics. Put it in a component, where its data
     comes in as a prop and its test renders the component. --}}

{{-- THE TEST THAT DECIDES: could this be extracted and published
     as a general-purpose library? If not, it is a component. --}}

10.6 Content security policy

Documents the CSP build of Alpine, the restriction it implies, and how expression-based markup must change.

Three policies, and only one of them is hard

A content security policy states where a page's resources may come from. The permissive form allows a few prefixes and a keyword or two; the hash form names exact scripts; the nonce form names a per-request random value that the template embeds. The first is easy and provides protection against a handful of attacks. The third is the one that actually prevents an injected script from running, and it is the one that has to be generated per request rather than configured statically.

That per-request property is the whole difficulty. A static policy header in a configuration file cannot contain a nonce, because a nonce must be unpredictable and therefore cannot be the same on every response. So the header has to be built at request time from the same value the template embeds, and that is why the policy lives in middleware rather than in a configuration file.

What the policy does not cover

It does not cover a script that is already trusted. An injected script into a page that loads its own bundle with a nonce does not need to guess the nonce — it needs to inject markup, and markup that becomes markup the browser executes is exactly what a nonce prevents. But an injected script that hooks an existing trusted bundle's API is not stopped by the policy, and the mitigation for that is not the policy at all: it is not having a global function that reads a location hash and writes it into the DOM.

// resources/js/app.js
import Alpine from 'alpinejs';

Alpine.setCspNonce(
    document.querySelector('meta[name=csp-nonce]').content,
);

window.Alpine = Alpine;
Alpine.start();

The default Alpine build evaluates expressions through the Function constructor, which a strict policy blocks; the CSP build parses them instead. The same restriction applies to Livewire and Vue. It also means dynamic directive values must arrive as data rather than as expression strings, which reinforces binding server values with @js().

Exercises 10

  1. Build an Alpine-backed combobox component that submits a real value through a hidden input.
  2. Replace every concatenated Alpine expression in a template with @js() and test with a value containing a single quote.
  3. Add a row to a list in Alpine only, reload, and document explicitly that the addition did not persist.
  4. Introduce a strict CSP, register the CSP build of Alpine, and confirm the page works without inline script allowances.

{{-- THE NONCE, generated per request and available to the view: --}}
// app/Http/Middleware/AddContentSecurityHeaders.php
final class AddContentSecurityHeaders
{
    public function handle(Request $request, Closure $next): Response
    {
        // A fresh 16 bytes per REQUEST, base64-encoded. Reusing it
        // across responses defeats the purpose: an attacker who saw
        // one nonce can replay it.
        $nonce = base64_encode(random_bytes(16));

        // Available to every view in this request, and nowhere else.
        View::share('cspNonce', $nonce);

        $response = $next($request);

        // style-src needs 'unsafe-inline' for a framework that sets
        // inline styles, which most do. Remove it by moving styles
        // to a file rather than by pretending.
        $response->headers->set('Content-Security-Policy', implode('; ', [
            "default-src 'self'",
            "script-src 'self' 'nonce-{$nonce}'",
            "style-src 'self' 'unsafe-inline'",
            "img-src 'self' data:",
            "font-src 'self'",
            "connect-src 'self'",           // fetch and XHR endpoints
            "frame-ancestors 'none'",
            "base-uri 'self'",
            "form-action 'self'",
        ]));

        return $response;
    }
}

{{-- AND THE TEMPLATE, which uses the SAME value: --}}
<!doctype html>
<html>
<head>
    <meta name="csrf-token" content="{{ csrf_token() }}">
    @stack('head')
</head>
<body>
    ...
    {{-- EVERY script tag needs the nonce, or the policy blocks it --
}}
    @push('scripts')
        <script nonce="{{ $cspNonce }}" src="{{ asset('js/app.js') }}" defer></script>
    @endpush
</body>
</html>

{{-- WHICH IS WHY THE component that pushes scripts has to read it: --}}
@once
    @push('scripts')
        <script nonce="{{ $cspNonce }}"
                src="{{ asset('js/editor.js') }}" defer></script>
    @endpush
@endonce

{{-- AND A SCRIPT TAG WITHOUT IT IS BLOCKED, which is the policy
     working — and the failure appears in the console as a CSP
     violation rather than as a broken page, so check there first: --}}
<script src="{{ asset('js/legacy-widget.js') }}"></script>   {{-- BLOCKED --}}

{{-- WHAT THE POLICY DOES NOT COVER, and it is the same thing in a
     different form. An injected script that hooks a TRUSTED bundle
     is not stopped by the policy, because it does not need to guess
     the nonce. So the mitigation is not the policy: --}}
<script nonce="{{ $cspNonce }}">
    // Do not build this. It is the injection target the policy
    // cannot reach, because the policy trusts this script.
    window.location.hash && document.write(
        decodeURIComponent(window.location.hash.slice(1)));
</script>
{{-- ^ document.write of a fragment of the URL is unsanitised XSS
     that a nonce cannot prevent, because the script doing it is
     trusted. The fix is that no trusted script ever treats a URL
     fragment as markup. --}}

11 · Blade and Livewire: Where Each Belongs

11.1 Two rendering models11.2 Anatomy of a Livewire component11.3 The Livewire view is a Blade view11.4 Layout integration11.5 Performance discipline11.6 Choosing between them

Livewire keeps rendering on the server while making components interactive over HTTP. Because its templates are Blade views, the boundary between the two tools is a design decision rather than a technical one.

11.1 Two rendering models

Contrasts a conventional Blade request with a Livewire round trip and identifies what each costs.

AspectBlade requestLivewire round trip
HTTP requestsOne per navigationOne per interaction
PayloadFull documentComponent diff only
StateServer sessionSigned client snapshot
Best forContent pagesForms, filters, live panels

A Blade page is one request producing one document. A Livewire interaction is a separate AJAX request that re-executes a component method and returns an HTML diff. Livewire therefore adds a request per interaction and moves component state into a client-held snapshot that must be verified before it is trusted.

Two ways to make a page interactive

There are two models in common use, and they differ in where the state lives. A component library keeps the page's state on the client and talks to endpoints for the data it needs. A server-driven model keeps the state on the server and re-renders a fragment when it changes, with the client sending the actions and receiving the resulting HTML.

That single difference explains everything else about them. A client-state model needs a client-side store, a routing convention for its endpoints, and a way to reconcile the server's answer with what the user was looking at. A server-state model needs a session, a request cycle per interaction, and a way to diff the resulting HTML against what is already on the page.

The cost each one moves

The client-state model moves rendering cost to the browser and keeps interactivity genuinely immediate: a drag, a keystroke-driven filter, a reorder, none of which involve a request. The server-state model keeps the rendering where the data and the logic already are, so a component that shows an order does not need a second copy of the order's display rules in JavaScript — but every interaction is a round trip, and an application that feels sluggish is the normal consequence.

What actually decides it

How much of the page is genuinely interactive. A form with a dependent dropdown and a wizard is one thing; a dashboard with drag-to-reorder columns, an inline-editable table and a live-updating chart is another. The first is a good fit for the server-driven model and the second is not, because every drag would be a request.


{{-- CLIENT STATE: the page's data lives in the browser, the server
     serves JSON. --}}
{{-- resources/views/dashboard.blade.php --}}
<div x-data="dashboard(@js($initial), @js($endpoints))" x-init="load()">
    <template x-for="row in rows" :key="row.id">
        <div draggable="true"
             @dragstart="drag($event, row.id)"
             @dragover.prevent
             @drop="drop($event)">
            {{ row.name }}
        </div>
    </template>
</div>
{{-- Every interaction is local: the drag runs at frame rate with
     no request at all. The server is an API, and the rules about
     what a row means live in two places — the API for fetching and
     persisting, and the template for displaying. --}}

{{-- SERVER STATE: the state is on the server, the client sends an
     action and receives the re-rendered HTML. --}}
{{-- resources/views/livewire/order-manager.blade.php --}}
<div>
    @foreach ($orders as $order)
        <div wire:key="order-{{ $order->id }}"
             wire:draggable="true"
             wire:sort="reorder($event.detail.value)">
            {{ $order->number }}
        </div>
    @endforeach
</div>

// app/Livewire/OrderManager.php
final class OrderManager extends Component
{
    public function reorder(array $ids): void
    {
        // The rule about what a priority MEANS lives here, once,
        // beside the query that persists it — not in a JavaScript
        // file that has to be kept in step with it.
        foreach ($ids as $position => $id) {
            Order::findOrFail($id)->update(['position' => $position]);
        }
    }
}
{{-- Every interaction is a request, and the display rules exist
     ONCE. A drag is a round trip, which is what makes a live
     dashboard feel wrong under this model. --}}


{{-- THE THREE THINGS THAT DECIDE, as a table: --}}
{{--   mostly forms and wizards      -> server state. The rules
                                         live where the data does.
   mostly local interaction on real
     data: drag, sort, live filter    -> client state. Round trips
                                         are invisible in the
                                         interaction.
   a mix, and the local part is
     presentational                   -> client state for the
                                         presentational part, and
                                         keep the rules on the
                                         server either way --}}

{{-- AND THE SHAPE THAT LIVES IN BOTH, which is the common case and
     is worth naming: --}}
{{-- resources/views/orders/show.blade.php --}}
<x-layout>
    {{-- SERVER-RENDERED, because the display rules for an order
         already exist in Blade and rewriting them in JavaScript
         would be a second copy to maintain. --}}
    <livewire:order-panel :order="$order" />
</x-layout>

{{-- resources/views/livewire/order-panel.blade.php --}}
<div>
    <livewire:status-toggles :order="$order" />

    {{-- ...and the ONE region on the page that genuinely needs
         continuous manipulation is a client library with its own
         mount point, calling the server once when the drag ends.
         No overlap with the component above. --}}
    <div x-data="reorder(@js($order->attachments),
                         @js(route('orders.reorder', $order)))"
         @dragend="save">
        <template x-for="a in items" :key="a.id">
            <div draggable="true">{{ a.filename }}</div>
        </template>
    </div>
</div>

{{-- THE DECIDING QUESTION IS NOT "WHICH IS BETTER" — IT IS "WHAT
     IS THE INTERACTION". A form is a request either way. A change
     to a view is a request either way, and the difference is
     latency. Only a CONTINUOUS manipulation separates them, and
     only that case is worth a second display language. --}}

11.2 Anatomy of a Livewire component

Shows the class and template pair that form a component, and identifies which properties form the client-visible state.

A class, a view, and a name

A server-driven component is a class with public properties and public methods, and a Blade view that displays them. The properties are the state the client can read and the client can change through its binding; the methods are the actions the client can invoke. The view is an ordinary Blade view, which is the point of the whole design: the display rules for a component are the same rules as for a page.

The wire between them is generated from those two things. A property's name becomes a key on the server and a variable on the client; a method's name becomes an endpoint the client can call. Nothing else is exposed, which is what means the component's public surface is its public properties and methods and nothing else.

The property rules, because they are not obvious

A public property is part of the component's wire format. It can be initialised in the constructor, set from a mounted call, or bound from the client — and a client may set anything of a type it can produce. That makes the type load-bearing: a public property is an input to the component, and it is an input that arrives over a network from code the component does not control.

// app/Livewire/OrderFilter.php
final class OrderFilter extends Component
{
    use WithPagination;

    public string $search = '';
    public int $perPage = 15;

    public function render(): View
    {
        return view('livewire.order-filter', [
            'orders' => Order::query()
                ->when($this->search,
                    fn ($q) => $q->where('ref', 'like', '%'.$this->search.'%'))
                ->latest()
                ->paginate($this->perPage),
        ]);
    }
}

Public properties form the state transferred to the client and back; only those are writable from the browser. Private and protected properties are server-side only, which is exactly why an authorisation check belongs in the class rather than in the template.

Never trust the snapshot. The checksum detects tampering, but each public property must still be validated or authorised before use. A tampered snapshot is rejected, not sanitised.


{{-- THE CLASS: public properties are STATE, public methods are
     ACTIONS. Nothing else is exposed. --}}
// app/Livewire/OrderFilters.php
namespace AppLivewire;

use LivewireComponent;

final class OrderFilters extends Component
{
    {{-- PUBLIC PROPERTIES ARE THE WIRE FORMAT. Each is readable and
         writable by the client, and each is therefore an INPUT
         that arrives over a network from code the component does
         not control. --}}
    public string $search   = '';
    public string $status   = 'any';
    public ?int    $customer = null;

    {{-- NOT public, so NOT on the wire and NOT reachable by the
         client. A private property cannot be used in the view, which
         is the trade. --}}
    private array $applied = [];

    public function mount(): void
    {
        $this->applied = $this->rules();
    }

    {{-- PUBLIC METHODS ARE THE ACTIONS. The client can call any of
         them with any arguments it can produce. --}}
    public function updated(string $field): void
    {
        // Hook for when a bound property changes. This is where a
        // dependent query belongs — and it is a SERVER method, so
        // it runs with the session and the auth context.
        $this->applied = $this->rules();
    }

    public function clear(): void
    {
        $this->reset('search', 'status', 'customer');
        $this->applied = $this->rules();
    }

    private function rules(): array
    {
        // The QUERY lives here, not in the view. Which is the whole
        // argument for the server-state model: the rule about what
        // "pending" means is beside the query that uses it.
        return Order::query()
            ->when($this->search !== '',
                   fn ($q) => $q->where('number', 'like', "%{$this->search}%"))
            ->when($this->status !== 'any',
                   fn ($q) => $q->where('status', $this->status))
            ->when($this->customer,
                   fn ($q) => $q->where('customer_id', $this->customer))
            ->latest()
            ->paginate(25);
    }

    public function render()
    {
        return view('livewire.order-filters', [
            'orders'  => $this->applied,
            'statuses' => OrderStatus::cases(),
        ])
        ->layout('layouts.app');   // the component renders INSIDE a
                                   // layout, and the layout is
                                   // rendered ONCE
    }
}

{{-- AND THE VIEW, which is an ordinary Blade view. The bindings are
     the wire: --}}
{{-- resources/views/livewire/order-filters.blade.php --}}
<div>
    <input type="search" wire:model.live.debounce.300ms="search"
           placeholder="{{ __('orders.search') }}">

    <select wire:model="status">
        <option value="any">{{ __('orders.any_status') }}</option>
        @foreach ($statuses as $s)
            <option value="{{ $s->value }}">{{ __("orders.status.$s->value") }}</option>
        @endforeach
    </select>

    <button type="button" wire:click="clear">{{ __('orders.clear') }}</button>

    @foreach ($orders as $order)
        <div wire:key="order-{{ $order->id }}">{{ $order->number }}</div>
    @endforeach

    {{ $orders->links() }}
</div>

11.3 The Livewire view is a Blade view

Shows the template side and confirms which Blade features remain available inside it.

The view is a Blade view, with two additions

There is nothing special about a component's view: it is a Blade view rendered with data, and it can include partials, use components, extend a layout and declare sections. That is the design goal, and it is worth checking a project against — the moment a component's view stops being readable as Blade, the value has gone.

The two additions are the wire directives on elements and the modifier syntax on bindings. The directives declare which properties and methods the client may touch; the modifiers say when a binding takes effect. Neither changes the template's semantics, which is why a component's view is genuinely the same artefact as a page's view.

The modifier syntax, and why it matters

A binding is either immediate or deferred, and the modifiers control which. A field bound live re-renders on every keystroke, which is a request per keystroke. A field bound normally re-renders when the element loses focus. The two extra modifiers are a delay and a blur-only form. Choosing between them is the difference between an interface that feels immediate and one that feels like a web page from 2005.

<div>
    <input type="search" wire:model.live.debounce.300ms="search">
    <span wire:loading>Searching</span>

    <table>
        @foreach ($orders as $order)
            <tr wire:key="order-{{ $order->id }}">
                <td>{{ $order->reference }}</td>
                <td>{{ $order->status->label() }}</td>
            </tr>
        @endforeach
    </table>

    {{ $orders->links() }}
</div>

Everything in the preceding chapters still applies inside a Livewire template: escaping remains automatic, components and includes still work, and the same N+1 and layering rules hold. The difference is that this template renders once per interaction rather than once per request.

Key every loop row. wire:key is the DOM identity the diff relies on. Omitting it on a loop produces duplicated or misplaced rows when the collection reorders between interactions.


{{-- IT IS A BLADE VIEW. Includes, components, sections, layout
     directives — all of it works. --}}
{{-- resources/views/livewire/order-filters.blade.php --}}
<div class="filters">
    @include('partials.filter-label', ['for' => 'q'])

    <input id="q" type="search"
           wire:model.live.debounce.300ms="search"
           value="{{ $search }}">

    <select wire:model.live="status">
        @foreach ($statuses as $s)
            <option value="{{ $s->value }}">{{ __("orders.status.$s->value") }}</option>
        @endforeach
    </select>

    {{-- A COMPONENT inside a component's view. Nothing special. --}}
    <x-status-pill :status="$status" />

    @foreach ($orders as $order)
        <x-order-row :order="$order" />
    @endforeach

    {{ $orders->links() }}
</div>

{{-- THE TWO ADDITIONS, and nothing else: --}}

{{-- (1) WIRE DIRECTIVES DECLARE THE SURFACE. --}}
<div wire:key="filters"          {{-- identity for the DOM diff.
                                          MANDATORY in a loop --}}
     wire:loading                 {{-- a class or attribute while a
                                          request is in flight --}}
     wire:loading.remove.attr="disabled" wire:target="save">

<button wire:click="clear" wire:loading.attr="disabled">
    {{ __('orders.clear') }}
</button>

<input wire:model.live.debounce.300ms="search">

{{-- (2) THE MODIFIER SYNTAX, which is where the feel of the
     interface is decided. --}}
<input wire:model="field">
{{-- ^ DEFAULT: the value is sent with the NEXT request, and the
     binding takes effect when the element loses focus. One request
     per field, not per keystroke. --}}

<input wire:model.live="field">
{{-- ^ EVERY KEYSTROKE is a request. Right for a select. Wrong for
     a text field on a large table. --}}

<input wire:model.live.debounce.300ms="search">
{{-- ^ the right answer for a text field: at most one request every
     300 ms of continuous typing, plus one when typing stops. --}}

<input wire:model.live.debounce.500ms="bio">
{{-- ^ a LONGER debounce for a field that triggers an expensive
     query. The number is a decision about latency, not about
     correctness. --}}

<input wire:model.blur="draft"    {{-- on focus loss ONLY --}}
     wire:model.change="tags"    {{-- on change only --}}
>
{{-- .blur and .change are the escape hatches for a field that must
     not be sent on every keystroke and should not wait for blur. --}}

11.4 Layout integration

Documents the required stack directives and why each must be present exactly once.

The layout is rendered once, and this is worth being precise about

A server-driven component renders inside a layout, and the layout is rendered once — at the initial page load, and then never again for that component. Only the component's own output changes. That is a genuine advantage over a full page reload, and it is also the source of two common misunderstandings: a component cannot add to the layout's head or push to a stack, and a layout cannot read a component's state.

The mechanism is worth understanding because it explains both. The component's HTML is inserted into a placeholder in an already-rendered document, and the component has no access to the document around that placeholder. So a script it pushes is pushed at a point where the framework's head is already closed.

What a component uses the layout for

The layout is where the page's chrome lives — navigation, the flash messages, the footer — and it is the right place for it. What the layout must not contain is anything derived from the component's state, because the layout will not re-render when that state changes. A page title derived from a component's current filter is the symptom: correct on load, stale after every interaction.

<head>
    @livewireStyles
    @stack('head')
</head>
<body>
    {{ $slot }}

    @livewireScripts
    @stack('scripts')
</body>

@livewireStyles injects the component stylesheet and @livewireScripts the runtime script; both must be present exactly once per document, with the scripts at the end of the body so the DOM exists when the runtime boots. A page that renders a Livewire component without either directive fails quietly: the markup appears, but nothing responds.

Once per page. Because the asset payload is inlined by default, a layout rendering several full-page components still emits the runtime once — but a hand-rolled partial that re-declares the directives will emit it repeatedly.


{{-- THE LAYOUT IS RENDERED ONCE. This is the whole mechanism and
     it explains both limitations below. --}}
// app/Livewire/OrderFilters.php
public function render()
{
    return view('livewire.order-filters')->layout('layouts.app');
}

{{-- (1) A COMPONENT CANNOT PUSH TO THE LAYOUT'S HEAD, because the
     head is already closed when the component's HTML is inserted
     into the page. --}}
@push('scripts')
    <script src="{{ asset('js/filters.js') }}"></script>
@endpush
{{-- ^ inside a component's view: the script tag is inserted into
     the BODY, where it still executes — but it is not in the head,
     not in the asset manifest, and not ordered by the policy. The
     workarounds are all worse than the obvious thing, which is a
     script tag in the layout itself. --}}

{{-- (2) A LAYOUT CANNOT READ A COMPONENT'S STATE, so anything
     derived from it goes STALE on every interaction. --}}
{{-- resources/views/layouts/app.blade.php --}}
<title>@yield('title')</title>
{{-- ^ correct on load. After the user changes the filter, the
     title still says "All orders" — and nothing warns, because the
     layout simply did not re-render. --}}

{{-- THE FIX, and it is the right one: the title belongs to the
     COMPONENT, which re-renders. --}}
// app/Livewire/OrderFilters.php
public function render()
{
    $orders = $this->rules();

    return view('livewire.order-filters', [
        'orders'   => $orders,
        'statuses' => OrderStatus::cases(),
    ])
    ->layout('layouts.app')
    ->title($this->status === 'any'
        ? __('orders.all_title')
        : __("orders.status_title.{$this->status}"));
    // ^ the component's render() returns a title, and the layout's
    //   <title>@yield('title')</title> picks it up on EVERY
    //   re-render. The layout is written once and the component
    //   supplies the value each time.
}

{{-- WHAT THE LAYOUT IS FOR: the page's chrome, which does not
     change per interaction. --}}
{{-- resources/views/layouts/app.blade.php --}}
<body>
    <x-app-header />
    <x-flash-messages />        {{-- flash data IS in the session, so
                                      a flash rendered by a component
                                      works; a flash rendered only by
                                      the layout does not update
                                      without a reload. --}}
    {{ $slot }}
    <x-app-footer />
</body>

11.5 Performance discipline

Lists the modifiers and properties that control how often a component talks to the server.

  • Model binding modifiers. wire:model.live.debounce.300ms batches keystrokes; wire:model.blur waits for a blur event. Both avoid a request per character.
  • Loading states. wire:loading and wire:dirty give feedback that prevents duplicate submissions without disabling the interface.
  • Computed values. #[Computed] evaluates once per request and caches the result on the instance, unlike a public accessor evaluated per access.
  • Locked properties. #[Locked] prevents a client request from changing a property, which is the correct guard for an identifier or a tenant id.
  • URL state. #[Url] keeps a property in the query string so a filtered view is linkable and survives a reload.

Defer by default. Default wire:model to deferred behaviour and opt into .live only where immediate feedback is required. The default costs far fewer requests than the exception.

Every interaction is a request, so count them

The performance model is simple: an interaction costs a round trip plus a server render. That makes the cost per interaction the only number that matters, and the two things that inflate it are a re-render that touches more of the page than changed, and a query inside the render that was avoidable.

The re-render size is the one that surprises people. A component that renders a table of twenty-five rows re-renders all of them when the filter changes, even if one row changed — because the model is "re-render this component's output", not "diff at row level". So a component that renders a thousand rows is a component that renders a thousand rows on every keystroke that reaches the server.

The three things that actually help

Rendering less, querying less, and sending less. Rendering less means splitting a component so that the part that changes is smaller than the part that does not. Querying less means checking the query log for the component's render specifically, because a paginator's count query is a common and invisible cost. Sending less means the same thing as rendering less, since the HTML is what is sent.

What does not help

Debouncing the binding past the point where the interface feels broken helps nothing, and neither does caching a component's own output — the component's whole purpose is that its output changes. Caching belongs to the queries and to fragments that genuinely do not change, and section 16 covers that.


{{-- MEASURE THE RENDER, because the model says the interaction
     cost IS the render cost: --}}
// app/Livewire/OrderFilters.php
public function render()
{
    $start = hrtime(true);
    $orders = $this->rules();

    Log::debug('order-filters render', [
        'ms'      => (hrtime(true) - $start) / 1e6,
        'queries' => count(DB::getQueryLog()),
    ]);

    return view('livewire.order-filters', ['orders' => $orders]);
}

{{-- THE RE-RENDER SIZE PROBLEM, and it is the usual one: --}}
@foreach ($orders as $order)
    <x-order-row :order="$order" />
@endforeach
{{-- ^ THIS RE-RENDERS ON EVERY KEYSTROKE THAT REACHES THE SERVER,
     all twenty-five rows. The model is "re-render this component's
     output", not "diff at row level". --}}

{{-- THE FIX IS SPLITTING, and it is the only fix that works: the
     part that changes is smaller than the part that does not. --}}
<div>
    {{-- The filter form depends on $status but not on the list, so
         it does not need to re-render with the list. --}}
    <livewire:filter-form :status="$status" :search="$search" />

    {{-- The list depends on the result, so it does. --}}
    <livewire:order-list :orders="$orders" :key="$listKey" />
</div>

{{-- THE PAGINATOR, which has a cost nobody sees: --}}
public function rules(): array
{
    return Order::query()
        ->when($this->status !== 'any', fn ($q) => $q->where('status', $this->status))
        ->latest()
        ->paginate(25);   // ^ ONE count query on every render, plus
                          //   the page query. The count is usually
                          //   the slower of the two and it changes
                          //   with every filter keystroke.
}


{{-- SEND LESS, which is the same as rendering less, and it is the
     one most applications miss: --}}
@if ($orders->hasMorePages())
    <button wire:click="$set('page', $page + 1)" wire:loading.attr="disabled">
        {{ __('orders.more') }}
    </button>
@endif
{{-- ^ APPEND a page rather than replacing twenty-five rows with
     twenty-five different rows. The DOM diff then has twenty-five
     nodes it can keep instead of twenty-five to destroy. --}}

{{-- AND THE HYDRATION COST, which is per component on the page and
     is paid at first load: --}}
{{-- resources/views/dashboard.blade.php --}}
<div>
    {{-- ^ three components on a page: three round trips of state
         at load, and three sets of render work before the page is
         interactive. --}}
    <livewire:order-filters />
    <livewire:order-list :orders="$orders" />
    <livewire:revenue-chart :data="$revenue" />
</div>
{{-- ONE component with three parts re-renders all three when any
     changes. Three components re-render independently. The trade is
     hydration cost at load against re-render size on interaction,
     and which one matters depends on whether the user types more
     often than the page loads. --}}

{{-- WHAT DOES NOT HELP, and it is worth being explicit: --}}
{{-- Debouncing past the point where the interface feels broken
     trades a real problem for a different real problem.
     Caching a component's own output defeats the component, whose
     entire purpose is that its output changes.
     A CDN in front of a component's HTML does nothing: the HTML is
     per-request by construction. --}}

{{-- THE NUMBER TO PUT IN A REVIEW, which is per interaction and
     not per page: --}}
@php
    // A budget, not an observation. A page that re-renders in 400 ms
    // feels broken; 120 ms feels responsive. Put the number in the
    // component so a regression is visible in a test.
    $budgetMs = 120;
@endphp

it('re-renders the filter within the budget', function () {
    $start = hrtime(true);
    Livewire::test(OrderFilters::class)->set('status', 'paid')->call('render');
    $ms = (hrtime(true) - $start) / 1e6;

    expect($ms)->toBeLessThan(120);
});
{{-- ^ slow in CI and fast on a laptop, so the threshold needs
     slack; the point is that a change which turns 40 ms into 300 ms
     fails a test rather than being noticed six weeks later. --}}

11.6 Choosing between them

Summarises the decision procedure and the cases where Blade alone is the correct tool.

SituationToolReason
Static content pageBladeNo interaction to support
SEO-critical indexBladeFull HTML in the first response
Filter, search or wizard formLivewirePartial updates beat a reload
Toggle, tab or disclosureAlpineNo server round trip needed
Chart or dashboard panelLivewireServer-side aggregation

Choose Blade when the page is content and the interaction is cosmetic. Choose Livewire when a user action genuinely requires fresh server data. When both apply to one page, render the static parts as Blade and isolate the interactive region as a single Livewire component — the diff is computed against that component’s markup only, so a large page costs no more than the region that actually changed.

Exercises 11

  1. Build a filter component and measure request volume with and without the debounce modifier.
  2. Add a locked attribute to a tenant identifier and demonstrate that a tampered request is rejected.
  3. Convert a computed property accessor into a computed attribute and compare query counts per render.
  4. Embed one Livewire region in a large static Blade page and confirm the diff payload covers only the region.

The question that decides, stated without the slogans

Ask what the interaction is. If it is a form — data in, validation, a save — both models work and the server-driven one is less code, because the display rules already exist. If it is a change to a view — a filter, a sort, a tab — both work and the difference is a round trip per change. If it is a continuous manipulation — a drag, a resize, a keystroke-driven reorder — the server-driven model is wrong, because the round trip is per frame of the interaction.

The migration cost, which is the deciding factor for an existing application

Moving an existing page to a client-state model means rewriting its display rules in a second language, because the server-rendered version no longer produces the markup. Moving it to the server-driven model means adding a class and moving the query out of the controller. The second is an afternoon; the first is a rewrite of every conditional in the template. This asymmetry is why most applications that start with server rendering and need more interactivity end up with both rather than replacing one with the other.

What both models have in common

Neither is a framework for the whole application, and the interesting decision is where the boundary goes. The pragmatic arrangement is: server rendering for pages, the server-driven model for forms and filters, and a client-side library for the few regions that genuinely need continuous manipulation — each with its own mount point and no overlap.


{{-- THE QUESTION, with all three answers. --}}

{{-- 1. A FORM. Both work; the server-driven one is less code,
     because the display rules already exist. --}}
<form wire:submit="save">
    <input wire:model="form.name">
    <button wire:loading.attr="disabled">{{ __('save') }}</button>
</form>

{{-- 2. A CHANGE TO A VIEW. Both work; the difference is a round
     trip per change. --}}
<select wire:model.live="status">
    <option value="any">{{ __('orders.any_status') }}</option>
    <option value="paid">{{ __('orders.paid') }}</option>
</select>

{{-- 3. A CONTINUOUS MANIPULATION. The server-driven model is
     WRONG here, because the round trip is per frame. --}}
{{-- WRONG for a server-driven component: --}}
<div wire:draggable="reorder($event.detail.value)">
    @foreach ($columns as $column)
        <div wire:key="col-{{ $column->id }}">{{ $column->name }}</div>
    @endforeach
</div>
{{-- ^ dragging is CONTINUOUS. Every frame that crosses the wire is
     a request, and the interaction becomes a slideshow. --}}


{{-- RIGHT for a client-side library, scoped to one region: --}}
<div x-data="kanban(@js($columns), @js($reorderUrl))">
    <template x-for="col in columns" :key="col.id">
        <div class="col" @dragover.prevent @drop="drop($event, col.id)">
            <template x-for="card in col.cards" :key="card.id">
                <div draggable="true" @dragstart="drag($event, card)">
                    <span x-text="card.title"></span>
                </div>
            </template>
        </div>
    </template>
</div>
{{-- ^ the drag is local, and the SERVER is called once when the
     drag ENDS. The rule about what a card may be dropped onto is
     on the server, where the data is. --}}

{{-- THE MIGRATION COST, which is the deciding factor for an
     existing application. SERVER-DRIVEN is a class and a moved
     query — an afternoon: --}}
// app/Livewire/OrderManager.php
final class OrderManager extends Component
{
    public string $status = 'any';

    public function render()
    {
        return view('livewire.order-manager', [
            'orders' => Order::query()
                ->when($this->status !== 'any',
                       fn ($q) => $q->where('status', $this->status))
                ->paginate(25),
        ]);
    }
}
{{-- The template's conditionals stay exactly where they were. --}}

{{-- CLIENT STATE is a REWRITE in a second language, because the
     server-rendered version no longer produces the markup: --}}
<div x-data="orders(@js($initial), @js('/api/orders'))" x-init="load()">
    <template x-if="status !== 'any'">
        <p x-text="__('orders.filtered')"></p>
    </template>
    <template x-for="o in orders" :key="o.id">
        <x-order-row :order="o" />   {{-- a Blade component still
                                         works — but only where the
                                         markup is static --}}
    </template>
</div>
{{-- ^ the server-rendered version is still there for the first
     paint, and the two now have to agree. That duplication is the
     real migration cost, and it is not the same as rewriting the
     template: it is MAINTAINING TWO OF THEM. --}}

{{-- WHAT BOTH HAVE IN COMMON: neither is a framework for the whole
     application. The decision is where the boundary goes. --}}
{{--   pages                 -> server rendering
     forms and filters      -> the server-driven model
     continuous manipulation -> a client library, scoped to one
                                 region, with its own mount point
                                 and no overlap --}}

12 · Conditional Rendering and Its Cost

12.1 Guard clauses in templates12.2 Empty and loading states12.3 Early exit with an optional section12.4 What a conditional actually costs12.5 Nesting depth and readability12.6 Who decides what is rendered

Conditional rendering is the most common directive in a Blade codebase and the one most often used to compensate for a controller that returns too much data. This chapter examines the patterns, their cost, and where the decision to render belongs.

12.1 Guard clauses in templates

Documents the guard-clause pattern, where the condition is resolved early so the body of the template stays unnested.

Early exit, and why a template benefits from it

A template that renders a header, a guard condition, and then the body has two problems. The reader has to scroll past the header to find out whether the body renders at all. And the body is indented one level for no reason, so its nesting depth says something the code does not.

An early exit fixes both: render the header, test the condition, return if it is false, and render the body at the original indentation. The mechanism is a conditional directive that contains the rest of the template, which reads oddly the first time and is the clearest form after that.

The guard clause and the empty state are different

A guard exits when the condition is false and renders nothing. An empty state renders something explaining that there is nothing. Conflating them is common and produces the specific bad outcome where a user who filtered to zero results sees a blank panel with no indication of whether the filter worked.

@extends('layouts.app')

@section('content')
    @unless ($order)
        <p>Order not found.</p>
    @else
        @include('orders._summary', ['order' => $order])
        @include('orders._lines', ['order' => $order])
    @endunless
@endsection

Where this guard genuinely belongs is the route: binding a model that does not exist raises a 404 before any view is resolved. A template guard is for cases the route cannot express — an empty collection, an expired link, a feature flag hiding a section.

Prefer route model binding. A typed route parameter 404s on a missing record automatically. Every template guard that duplicates this is a guard that can be forgotten.


{{-- WITHOUT A GUARD: the header is above the decision, so the
     reader scrolls past it to learn whether anything renders. --}}
<x-panel title="{{ __('orders.title') }}">
    @if ($orders->isEmpty())
        <x-empty-state :title="__('orders.none')" />
    @else
        @foreach ($orders as $order)
            <x-order-row :order="$order" />
        @endforeach
    @endif
</x-panel>
{{-- ^ a third level of indentation for a conditional that is one
     line, and the @else branch is the common case so the reader
     scans past it to find it. --}}

{{-- WITH A GUARD: the empty case returns, and the body sits at the
     original indentation. --}}
<x-panel title="{{ __('orders.title') }}">
    @if ($orders->isEmpty())
        <x-empty-state
            :title="__('orders.none')"
            :action="route('orders.index')" />
    @else
        @foreach ($orders as $order)
            <x-order-row :order="$order" />
        @endforeach
    @endif
</x-panel>
{{-- still three levels — the point is that the GUARD is a return,
     not an if/else. The template form of one: --}}

{{-- THE TEMPLATE FORM: an @if with no @else that CONTAINS the rest
     of the template. It reads oddly once and is the clearest form
     afterwards. --}}
<x-panel title="{{ __('orders.title') }}">

    {{-- THE GUARD. Everything after this is inside it. --}}
    @if ($orders->isEmpty())
        <x-empty-state
            :title="__('orders.none')"
            :action="route('orders.index')" />
    @else

        {{-- ...and the body is at the ORIGINAL indentation, because
             it is inside an @else rather than a nested @if. --}}
        @foreach ($orders as $order)
            <x-order-row :order="$order" />
        @endforeach

    @endif
</x-panel>

{{-- AND WHERE A GUARD BELONGS: at the top of a partial whose whole
     purpose is a list, so the partial becomes self-contained and
     does not need its caller to check first. --}}
{{-- resources/views/partials/order-table.blade.php --}}
@if ($orders->isEmpty())
    <x-empty-state :title="__('orders.none')" />
@else
    <table>
        @foreach ($orders as $order)
            <tr><td>{{ $order->number }}</td><td>{{ $order->total->format() }}</td></tr>
        @endforeach
    </table>
@endif
{{-- ^ and the CALLER stops needing to know: --}}
@include('partials.order-table', ['orders' => $orders])
{{-- rather than @if ($orders->count()) @include(...) @endif --}}

{{-- AND THE DISTINCTION WORTH KEEPING SEPARATE: a guard EXITS, an
     empty state EXPLAINS. Conflating them produces the specific bad
     outcome where a user who filtered to zero results sees a blank
     panel with no indication of whether the filter worked. --}}

12.2 Empty and loading states

Documents the empty-state patterns and the requirement that an empty state offer a next action.

Three states, and they are not variations of one another

A region that fetches data has three states, and the design question is that all three need a design. There is no data yet, there is data, and the request failed. A template that handles only the middle one produces an empty region during the request and an identical empty region after a failure — which is the specific outcome that makes an error indistinguishable from an empty list.

The second thing worth separating is "empty because nothing matched" from "empty because you have not looked yet". They are different messages with different actions: one offers a way to clear the filter, the other offers a way to start. Conflating them produces "No results" on a page the user has not searched yet.

Loading is a state of the template, not of the page

The tempting approach is a global spinner around the page while any request is in flight. The better approach is that each region has its own loading state, because a page where one region is loading and another is not should show that, and a global spinner destroys the information. This is a design decision made in markup, and it is why the loading markup belongs in the component rather than in a layout.

@forelse ($orders as $order)
    <tr>
        <td>{{ $order->reference }}</td>
        <td>{{ $order->status->label() }}</td>
    </tr>
@empty
    <tr>
        <td colspan="2">
            <p>No orders yet.</p>
            <a href="{{ route('orders.create') }}">Create the first order</a>
        </td>
    </tr>
@endforelse

An empty state that only reports the absence of data is a dead end. Every empty state in production should state what would fill it and offer the route that fills it, because an empty table is also how a user learns that a permission, a filter or a date range is excluding everything.

Distinguish three empties. No records exist, none match the filter, or still loading. The first wants a create action, the second wants a clear-filters action, the third wants a skeleton. One generic message cannot serve all three.


{{-- THE THREE STATES, and the failure mode of handling only the
     middle one: --}}
@if ($loading)
    <x-skeleton :rows="5" />
@elseif ($error)
    <x-error-state :message="$error" :retry="fn () => $load()" />
@elseif ($orders->isEmpty())
    <x-empty-state :title="__('orders.none')" />
@else
    @foreach ($orders as $order)
        <x-order-row :order="$order" />
    @endforeach
@endif
{{-- ^ without the @elseif ($error), a failed request renders the
     SAME empty state as a request that succeeded with no rows — and
     the user is told there are no orders when in fact the query
     failed. --}}

{{-- THE FOURTH STATE, which is the one that gets missed: EMPTY
     BECAUSE NOTHING MATCHED is not the same as EMPTY BECAUSE YOU
     HAVE NOT LOOKED YET. --}}
@if (! $hasSearched)
    <x-empty-state :title="__('orders.search_first')"
                   :hint="__('orders.search_hint')" />
@elseif ($orders->isEmpty())
    <x-empty-state :title="__('orders.none_matching')"
                   :action="fn () => clearFilters()" />
@endif
{{-- ^ different messages with different actions: one offers a way
     to START, the other offers a way to CLEAR THE FILTER. --}}

{{-- LOADING IS A STATE OF THE REGION, NOT OF THE PAGE: --}}
{{-- WRONG: a global spinner, because a page where one region is
     loading and another is not should SHOW that, and a global
     spinner destroys the information. --}}
<div x-data="{ loading: false }" x-on:...>
    <div x-show="loading" class="page-spinner"></div>   {{-- ^ --}}
    ...
</div>

{{-- RIGHT: each region carries its own loading state, which is why
     the loading markup belongs in the COMPONENT rather than in a
     layout. --}}
<x-order-panel :orders="$orders" :loading="$loading" />

{{-- resources/views/components/order-panel.blade.php --}}
@props(['orders', 'loading' => false, 'error' => null])

<div>
    @if ($loading)
        <x-skeleton :rows="5" />
    @elseif ($error)
        <x-error-state :message="$error" />
    @elseif ($orders->isEmpty())
        <x-empty-state :title="__('orders.none')" />
    @else
        @foreach ($orders as $order)
            <x-order-row :order="$order" />
        @endforeach
    @endif
</div>

{{-- AND THE ARIA, because a loading state announced silently is a
     loading state nobody knows about: --}}
<div aria-busy="{{ $loading ? 'true' : 'false' }}"
     aria-live="polite">
    @if ($loading)
        <span class="sr-only">{{ __('orders.loading') }}</span>
        <x-skeleton :rows="5" />
    @else
        ...
    @endif
</div>

{{-- AND THE SKELETON'S SHAPE SHOULD MATCH THE CONTENT'S, so the
     layout does not jump when the data arrives: --}}
<x-skeleton :rows="5" :row-height="'2.5rem'" />
{{-- ^ a spinner where a table will be produces a visible reflow on
     every load. The skeleton is not decoration; it is a
     reservation of the space the content will occupy. --}}

12.3 Early exit with an optional section

Shows how an unset section keeps the document free of meaningless markup, and how the layout decides the fallback.

The early exit, and what it costs

The mechanism is a conditional that contains the rest of the template, with nothing after it. That is the whole idea, and it has two benefits and one cost that has to be understood before adopting it.

The benefits are that the reader finds the decision near the top rather than scrolling past content that may not render, and that the body sits at the original indentation rather than one level in. The cost is that the template now ends inside a conditional, which means adding markup after the conditional requires noticing that it will be inside it. That is a real cost, and it is why the pattern works best in a partial whose whole purpose is the guarded body rather than in a full page.

Where it is genuinely better

In a partial. A partial that renders a list should be self-contained: it should decide whether there is anything to render, and its caller should not have to know. A partial that needs a guard clause in the caller is a partial whose contract includes a condition it does not enforce, and the second caller will forget.

@extends('layouts.app')

@section('sidebar-actions')
    @if ($order->canBeCancelled())
        <form method="POST" action="{{ route('orders.cancel', $order) }}">
            @csrf
            @method('PATCH')
            <button>Cancel order</button>
        </form>
    @endif
@endsection

A section whose body produces nothing leaves the layout’s default in place, so a layout declaring @yield('sidebar-actions', '') emits no element at all. This beats rendering an empty container that still occupies space, because the document contains only elements that mean something.

Authorisation. @if ($user->can(...)) hides a link; it does not prevent the action. The same check must exist in the policy the controller calls. Template conditionals are presentation, never enforcement.


{{-- THE PATTERN: a conditional with NO @else that CONTAINS the rest
     of the template. --}}
{{-- resources/views/partials/order-table.blade.php --}}

@if ($orders->isEmpty())
    <x-empty-state
        :title="__('orders.none')"
        :action="route('orders.create')" />
@else
    <table>
        <thead>
            <tr><th>{{ __('orders.number') }}</th><th>{{ __('orders.total') }}</th></tr>
        </thead>
        <tbody>
            @foreach ($orders as $order)
                <tr>
                    <td>{{ $order->number }}</td>
                    <td>{{ $order->total->format() }}</td>
                </tr>
            @endforeach
        </tbody>
    </table>
@endif

{{-- AND THE CALLER IS SIMPLER, WHICH IS THE POINT: --}}
<section>
    <h2>{{ __('orders.title') }}</h2>
    @include('partials.order-table', ['orders' => $orders])
</section>
{{-- ^ rather than: --}}

{{-- THE VERSION THAT PUTS THE BURDEN ON THE CALLER, and the second
     caller forgets: --}}
<section>
    @if ($orders->isNotEmpty())
        @include('partials.order-table', ['orders' => $orders])
    @else
        <x-empty-state :title="__('orders.none')" />
    @endif
</section>
{{-- ^ and somewhere else in the application: --}}
@include('partials.order-table', ['orders' => $orders])
{{-- ^ no guard, so the table renders with a header and no rows. --}}

{{-- THE COST, and it is worth understanding before adopting it:
     the template now ENDS INSIDE a conditional. --}}
<div>
    @if ($showFooter)
        <p>{{ __('orders.footer') }}</p>
    @endif

    {{-- ...so a developer adding markup HERE is adding it INSIDE
         the conditional, and it will silently disappear whenever
         $showFooter is false. --}}
</div>

{{-- WHICH IS WHY IT WORKS BEST IN A PARTIAL whose whole purpose is
     the guarded body, rather than in a full page: --}}
{{-- resources/views/partials/sidebar-filters.blade.php --}}
@if ($filters->isEmpty())
    <p class="muted">{{ __('filters.none') }}</p>
@else
    <ul>
        @foreach ($filters as $filter)
            <li><a href="{{ $filter->url }}">{{ $filter->label }}</a></li>
        @endforeach
    </ul>
@endif
{{-- ...and in a full page it is usually worse, because the page has
     content after the list that does not belong inside the guard. --}}

12.4 What a conditional actually costs

Quantifies the runtime cost of a conditional and identifies the cases where the cost is not negligible.

A compiled @if is a PHP if. Evaluating one is a comparison, effectively free, and a page with fifty conditionals spends microseconds on all of them. The cost of conditional rendering is never the directive itself; it is what the condition guards.

What a conditional actually costs

Almost nothing, and the reason is worth being precise about because people add conditionals defensively. A conditional compiles to an ordinary conditional in PHP, the compiler may remove the branch entirely if the condition is a compile-time constant, and an untaken branch is not evaluated. There is no measurable cost to an untaken conditional and a small one to a taken branch.

The costs that are real are elsewhere. A conditional inside a loop is evaluated per iteration, so a condition that calls a method is a method call per row. A conditional whose condition queries is a query per row, which is the classic N+1 and the reason a template should never be the place a query happens. And a deeply nested conditional is a cost in reading, not in running — which is the one worth designing against.

The exception worth knowing

One case is genuinely expensive: a conditional wrapping a large block of markup that is evaluated and then discarded. That is what a "hide if empty" wrapper does when the block is large — the whole block is built and then thrown away. It is rare and it is easy to avoid by moving the condition inside.

// Free: a comparison on already-loaded data.
@if ($order->status === OrderStatus::Paid)

// Expensive: a query inside the condition.
@if ($order->invoices()->count() > 0)

A query, a service call or an authorisation check inside a template condition executes once per render, per component instance and per Livewire interaction. These are the patterns to audit, not the conditionals themselves.

Hoist the work. If a condition needs a count, add withCount() to the query in the controller and read the loaded attribute. The work happens once, in the query, instead of once per render.


{{-- WHAT IT COMPILES TO. An ordinary conditional: --}}
{{-- resources/views/orders/show.blade.php --}}
@if ($order->isPaid())
    <x-paid-badge />
@endif
{{-- compiles to: --}}
isPaid())): ?>
    make('components.paid-badge', ...)->render(); ?>


{{-- ^ so the cost is a branch, and an UNTANEN branch is not
     evaluated at all. There is no measurable cost to a
     conditional that is not taken. --}}

{{-- THE COST THAT IS REAL, and it is per ITERATION: --}}
@foreach ($orders as $order)
    @if ($order->total->isOverThreshold())
        <x-warning-row :order="$order" />
    @endif
@endforeach
{{-- ^ $order->total->isOverThreshold() runs 25 times for 25 rows.
     Cheap IF it is a property. NOT cheap if it is a method that
     queries. --}}

{{-- THE N+1, which is the version that turns a template into the
     slowest part of a page: --}}
@foreach ($orders as $order)
    @if ($order->customer->isOverdue())   {{-- a RELATION access. On
                                                 an unloaded model
                                                 this is a QUERY, so
                                                 25 rows means 26
                                                 queries. --}}
        <x-overdue-row :order="$order" />
    @endif
@endforeach
{{-- ^ THE FIX IS NOT IN THE TEMPLATE. A template that reads a
     relation is doing the controller's job, and the fix is to
     eager-load in the controller or the query. --}}

// app/Http/Controllers/OrdersController.php
public function index(): View
{
    return view('orders.index', [
        // loadMissing is not optional here: the template reaches
        // $order->customer and $order->total, so those are this
        // view's requirements and they belong in this file.
        'orders' => Order::query()
            ->with(['customer', 'lines.product'])
            ->paginate(25),
    ]);
}

{{-- THE ONE GENUINELY EXPENSIVE CASE, and it is avoidable: --}}
@if ($orders->isEmpty())
    ...
@else
    <div class="grid">
        @foreach ($orders as $order)
            <x-order-card :order="$order" />   {{-- 8 KB each --}}
        @endforeach
    </div>
@endif
{{-- ^ fine, because the branch is outside the loop. --}}

@if ($showGrid)
    @foreach ($orders as $order)
        <x-order-card :order="$order" />   {{-- 8 KB each --}}
    @endforeach
@endif
{{-- ^ 25 cards, 200 KB of HTML, BUILT AND THEN DISCARDED when
     $showGrid is false. The fix is the same condition, moved: --}}

{{-- THE READING COST, which is the one worth designing against,
     and no tool measures it: --}}
@if ($a)
    @if ($b)
        @if ($c)
            <x-thing :variant="$variant" />
        @endif
    @endif
@endif
{{-- ^ four levels of nesting to express what is one expression: --}}

@if ($a && $b && $c)
    <x-thing :variant="$variant" />
@endif

12.5 Nesting depth and readability

Sets a workable depth limit for conditional nesting and demonstrates the extraction that resolves a deep branch.

Nesting is a budget, and there is no tool to enforce it

A template nests for two different reasons, and only one of them costs anything. Nesting because of the document's structure — a table inside a form inside a section — mirrors the output and is fine. Nesting because of conditionals — a conditional inside a loop inside a conditional — is a control-flow structure, and that is the kind that becomes unreadable.

The useful rule is to count conditional nesting, not element nesting, and to hold the conditional depth to two. Two is enough for every real case: a loop containing a condition, or a condition containing a loop. Three is where the reader has to hold two conditions in mind at once and remember which is which, which is the point at which extracting a component or a partial pays for itself.

The extraction that works

The fix for a deeply nested conditional is almost always a component whose existence makes the condition a prop rather than a branch. A component that renders nothing when its list is empty removes both the nesting and the empty state, because the condition moves from the call site into the component's own template.

@if ($user->isAdmin())
    @if ($order->isPaid())
        @if ($invoice->isOverdue())
            @if ($user->can('invoice.waive'))
                <button>Waive</button>
            @endif
        @endif
    @endif
@endif

// Extracted: the same decision, one level deep.
@include('invoices._waive-button', [
    'invoice' => $invoice,
    'allowed' => $user->can('invoice.waive'),
])

Beyond two levels, the reader must hold several conditions in mind simultaneously and the risk of a missing @endif grows. Extraction into a partial or a component flattens the branch and gives the decision a name that can be tested directly.


{{-- NESTING FOR DOCUMENT STRUCTURE IS FINE — it mirrors the output: --}}
<section>
    <form method="POST" action="{{ route('orders.store') }}">
        <fieldset>
            <legend>{{ __('orders.details') }}</legend>
            <div>
                <label for="n">{{ __('orders.number') }}</label>
                <input id="n" name="number">
            </div>
        </fieldset>
        <div>
            <table>
                <tbody>
                    <tr><td>{{ __('orders.total') }}</td><td>{{ $total }}</td></tr>
                </tbody>
            </table>
        </div>
        <div><button type="submit">{{ __('save') }}</button></div>
    </form>
</section>

{{-- NESTING FOR CONTROL FLOW IS THE KIND THAT COSTS. Count the
     CONDITIONAL depth, not the element depth: --}}
@if ($user->isAdmin())
    @foreach ($sections as $section)
        @if ($section->isVisibleTo($user))
            <x-section :section="$section" />
        @endif
    @endforeach
@endif
{{-- ^ TWO conditions plus a loop. At the limit. --}}

@if ($user->isAdmin())
    @foreach ($sections as $section)
        @if ($section->isVisibleTo($user))
            @if ($section->hasContent())
                <x-section :section="$section" />
            @endif
        @endif
    @endforeach
@endif
{{-- ^ THREE. The reader now holds two conditions plus a loop, and
     the answer to "when does this render" requires reading all
     three. --}}

{{-- THE FIX, and it is almost always a component rather than a
     partial: the condition becomes a PROP rather than a branch. --}}
@foreach ($sections as $section)
    <x-section :section="$section" :user="$user" />
@endforeach
{{-- ^ one level at the call site, and the conditions moved into
     resources/views/components/section.blade.php --}}

{{-- resources/views/components/section.blade.php --}}
@props(['section', 'user'])

{{-- The component is the clearest form of these four conditions,
     because each one is a named early exit at one indentation
     level rather than a nested branch. --}}
@unless ($section->isVisibleTo($user))
    {{-- nothing: this is the common case for a non-admin --}}
@elseif (! $section->hasContent())
    <x-empty-section :section="$section" />
@elseif ($section->isLocked())
    <x-locked-section :section="$section" />
@else
    <x-section-body :section="$section" />
@endunless

{{-- AND THE NUMBER WORTH WRITING DOWN: conditional depth of TWO.
     Two is enough for every real case — a loop containing a
     condition, or a condition containing a loop. Three is where
     the reader has to hold two conditions in mind at once. --}}

{{-- A QUICK WAY TO SEE IT, since no tool enforces it: --}}
// A maintenance command that reports the worst conditional depth in
// each view, so a refactor has a target.
Artisan::command('views:depth {file?}', function (?string $file = null) {
    $files = $file
        ? [resource_path('views/' . $file)]
        : View::getFinder()->getPaths();

    foreach ($files as $dir) {
        $it = $file ? [$dir]
             : new RecursiveIteratorIterator(new RecursiveDirectoryIterator($dir));

        foreach ($it as $f) {
            if (! $f instanceof SplFileInfo || $f->getExtension() !== 'php') continue;

            $depth = 0; $max = 0; $at = null;
            foreach (file($f->getPathname()) as $n => $line) {
                if (preg_match('/@(if|foreach|forelse|for|while|unless|isset|empty|switch)\b/', $line)) {
                    $depth++;
                    if ($depth > $max) { $max = $depth; $at = $n + 1; }
                }
                if (preg_match('/@(end\w+)|@else|@elseif/', $line)) {
                    $depth = max(0, $depth - 1);
                }
            }

            if ($max > 2) {
                $this->warn(sprintf('%-45s depth %d at line %d',
                    Str::after($f->getPathname(), $dir . '/'), $max, $at));
            }
        }
    }
});

12.6 Who decides what is rendered

Summarises the division of responsibility and the failure each misplaced decision produces.

DecisionOwnerFailure if misplaced
Record existsRoute bindingTemplate guard on every page
What data is loadedController or view modelQuery inside a conditional
Whether a link appearsTemplatePolicy check in the markup
Whether an action is allowedPolicyHiding a control as security
Markup structureComponentMarkup duplicated per page

Every conditional in a template is a question about data or permission that has already been answered elsewhere. Reading a template should confirm decisions, not reveal them. When a template guard appears on most pages, the answer belongs in the route, the controller or the view model instead.

Exercises 12

  1. Audit every conditional in one page and classify it as a route decision, a controller decision or a presentation decision.
  2. Replace a query inside a conditional with a withCount() and confirm the query count falls to one.
  3. Extract a four-level nested branch into a component and confirm identical output.
  4. Replace a generic empty state with three distinct messages and confirm each renders only in its own situation.

Who decides what is rendered, and why the question has three answers

Every conditional in a template is a decision made in the wrong place. Some of them are display decisions and belong in the template. Some of them are access decisions and belong in a policy. Some of them are data decisions and belong in the controller, because the value the template branches on should not have needed branching at all.

The middle case is the one with a security consequence: a template that checks a permission is not enforcing a permission, it is hiding a button. The check must be repeated where the action is performed, and the template check exists for usability. Recognising the difference between those two — a check that hides and a check that refuses — is the useful skill here.

The third case, and it is the one people argue about

A template that branches on a computed display value is doing its job, provided the value arrived as data. A template that computes the condition is doing the controller's job. The line is not about what is written but about where the truth came from: a boolean the controller supplied is a display value, and a boolean the template derived is a decision that has leaked.


{{-- 1. DISPLAY DECISION — the template's job. --}}
@if ($order->isOverdue())
    <x-overdue-badge />
@endif
{{-- ^ the template branches on a fact the domain supplied and
     chooses a presentation for it. Correct. --}}

{{-- 2. ACCESS DECISION — a policy's job, and the template's check
     HIDES rather than REFUSES. --}}
@can('delete', $order)
    <form method="POST" action="{{ route('orders.destroy', $order) }}">
        @csrf
        @method('DELETE')
        <button type="submit">{{ __('orders.delete') }}</button>
    </form>
@endcan
{{-- ^ This does not enforce anything. It hides a button. The
     request is a POST to a route that must check again: --}}

// app/Http/Controllers/OrderController.php
public function destroy(Order $order): RedirectResponse
{
    // THE CHECK THAT ENFORCES. Without this, the template's @can is
    // decoration: remove it and nothing changes for anyone.
    $this->authorize('delete', $order);

    $order->delete();
    return back();
}

{{-- THE ASYMMETRY, and it is worth stating because it surprises
     people: --}}
@can('delete', $order)
    <button>{{ __('orders.delete') }}</button>
@endcan
{{-- ^ hiding is a USABILITY feature. Refusing is a SECURITY
     feature. Only the second belongs in the request path, and only
     the second is ever tested — because a test for the first is a
     test that the button is absent, which passes the moment the
     button is gone for the wrong reason. --}}


{{-- 3. DATA DECISION — the controller's job, and the test is
     whether the value could have arrived as data. --}}

{{-- WRONG: the template derives the condition, so the truth about
     what the user may see lives in a view. --}}
@if (auth()->user()->orders->count() > 10 && ! auth()->user()->isTrial())
    <x-upsell />
@endif
{{-- ^ two facts the template should not know, combined into a third
     it invents. And it reads $user->orders, which is a QUERY in a
     view if the relation is not loaded. --}}

{{-- RIGHT: the controller supplies the DECISION as a value. --}}
// app/Http/Controllers/DashboardController.php
public function __invoke(Request $request): View
{
    $user = $request->user();

    return view('dashboard', [
        'user'       => $user,
        'showUpsell' => Order::where('user_id', $user->id)->count() > 10
            && ! $user->isTrial(),
    ]);
}

{{-- dashboard.blade.php --}}
@if ($showUpsell)
    <x-upsell />
@endif
{{-- ^ one condition, one fact, and the rules live beside the data
     they are about. A test asserts on showUpsell without rendering
     anything and without a query. --}}

{{-- THE THREE ANSWERS AS A REVIEW QUESTION: --}}
{{--   "which presentation?"  -> the template. Correct here.
     "may this user do it?"   -> a policy. The template hides; the
                                  policy refuses.
     "what data do I need?"   -> the controller. If a template
                                  derives a condition from two
                                  facts, it is a controller written
                                  in the wrong place. --}}

{{-- AND THE FOURTH CASE, which nobody lists: a condition on
     something the template CANNOT know, where hiding is the whole
     point and there is nothing to refuse. --}}
@if ($user->emailVerified())
    <x-verified-badge />
@else
    <p>{{ __('profile.verify_prompt') }}</p>
@endif
{{-- ^ no enforcement is possible or wanted: the user's email state
     is not an action. The distinction is whether the server would
     REJECT the thing if the user bypassed the template. If yes, it
     is access; if there is no action to reject, it is display. --}}

13 · Localization: Translating from Blade

13.1 The translation directives13.2 Pluralization and choice13.3 Placeholders and replacements13.4 JSON translation files13.5 Switching the locale per request13.6 Right-to-left locales

Localization touches every template that contains user-facing text. This chapter documents the translation directives, the pluralisation forms, and the request-level locale resolution that determines which strings a template produces.

13.1 The translation directives

Documents the lang directive, its helper equivalents, and the lookup order that resolves a key.

The four directives, and what each is for

A translation directive takes a key and returns the string; a choice directive takes a key, a count, and an array of forms; a replacement directive returns a string with placeholders substituted; and the raw form returns a string without escaping. Four mechanisms for what is conceptually one operation, and they differ in ways worth knowing: the replacement directive fails loudly on a missing placeholder while the choice directive falls back, and the raw form is a hole in the escaping guarantee.

The reason for four is historical and structural. Replacements are applied after the string is fetched, so a missing placeholder is detectable. Pluralisation needs the count before the key is chosen. And the raw form exists because a translated string legitimately contains markup — a link, an emphasis — and escaping the whole string would break it.

Keys, and what they should be

A key should be a sentence fragment, not an identifier. A key like order_total_label forces a translator to guess what the sentence is and where it appears; a key like orders.total_with_date with a placeholder names the case. The practical test is whether a translator who has never seen the page can produce a natural sentence from the key and its placeholders alone.

<h1>@lang('orders.title')</h1>
<h1>{{ __('orders.title') }}</h1>
<h1>{{ trans('orders.title') }}</h1>

@lang is the directive form and is equivalent to __(), which returns a string that may be composed. Both escape their output through {{ }}, so a translated value containing markup renders as text unless it is echoed raw.

Resolution proceeds through the active locale’s PHP file in lang/, then a JSON file for that locale, then the fallback locale. A key that resolves nowhere returns the key itself, which is deliberate: the untranslated key is visible in the interface and greppable in the codebase.

Missing keys should be visible. Returning the key rather than an empty string means an untranslated page is obvious in review. Never supply a default argument that hides an absent key.


{{-- THE FOUR DIRECTIVES. --}}

{{-- (1) GET A STRING. --}}
<h1>{{ __('orders.title') }}</h1>
<button>{{ __('orders.place_order') }}</button>

{{-- (2) PLURALISE, on the count. --}}
<span>{{ trans_choice('orders.item', $count) }}</span>
{{-- the key names the SINGULAR. The plural forms live beside it in
     the language file, which is why a translator never has to
     invent a key for the plural case. --}}

{{-- (3) REPLACE PLACEHOLDERS. --}}
<p>{{ __('orders.placed_on', ['date' => $order->placedAt->isoFormat('D M Y')]) }}</p>
{{-- ^ ":date" in the string, "date" in the array. The framework
     leaves a placeholder it cannot fill as the literal ":date", so
     a missing replacement is VISIBLE rather than silently empty. --}}

{{-- (4) RAW, for a translated string that legitimately contains
     markup. --}}
{!! __('emails.welcome', ['name' => $user->name]) !!}
{{-- ^ use ONLY for strings in your OWN language files, and only
     where they contain markup you wrote. A translation is a string
     a translator controls; a translated string placed with the raw
     form is a string a translator can put markup into. --}}

{{-- THE SHORTHAND, which reads better in a template and means the
     same thing: --}}
{{-- `__()` is the common case; `trans()` is the older name and is
     an alias. There is no difference in behaviour. --}}

{{-- AND THE PHP FUNCTIONS, which are what to reach for OUTSIDE a
     template — in a controller, a class, a validation message: --}}
// app/Http/Controllers/OrderController.php
return redirect()
    ->route('orders.show', $order)
    ->with('status', __('orders.placed_successfully'));
{{-- ^ the message is assembled here, where the event happened, and
     the template only displays it. Putting the translation call in
     the template means the message's wording is duplicated if it is
     needed in an email, a log line, or an API response. --}}

{{-- WHAT EACH RESOLVES TO, which is worth knowing for a template
     that has to render outside a request: --}}
// resources/js/app.js
import { trans } from './i18n';
window.__ = trans;     // so a JS-side message can use the same
                       // catalogue as the page

13.2 Pluralization and choice

Documents the transchoice directive, the pipe-delimited plural forms and the parameter that selects among them.

Plural rules are not a boolean

The reason pluralisation is a separate mechanism rather than a conditional is that plural is a category with more than two members in a great many languages. English has two. Polish has four. Arabic has six. Russian has three and selects on the last digit rather than on whether the number is greater than one, so the rule that works for English is wrong for Russian.

So the count is not used to choose between two strings by a comparison. It is used to look up a plural form by index, and each language file declares how many forms it has and — optionally — how to pick between them. That is why a translation key names the singular form and why the other forms sit beside it: the file is the place where the language's rules live.

The explicit-range form

Most languages have cases that a count alone cannot express: "few", "many", "zero", or a specific numeric range like "2 to 5". The explicit-range form exists for those, and it takes a value rather than a count, which lets a range be expressed directly. A language file that uses it is expressing a rule that no comparison can.

@lang_choice('orders.count', $count)

{{ trans_choice('orders.count', $count) }}

// lang/en/orders.php
return [
    'count' => '{0} No orders|{1} :count order|[2,*] :count orders',
];
