The pipe-delimited string is split by the pluralisation rule for the active locale: the zero form, the singular form, and the plural form which may carry an explicit range such as [2,19]. The :count placeholder is filled by the value passed as the second argument, so the same number both selects and displays.

Plural rules vary. English has two forms; Arabic has six; Polish and Russian select a form from the number of preceding categories. A hand-written pipe string copied between locales produces wrong output even when the key exists.


{{-- THE KEY NAMES THE SINGULAR, and the other forms live beside
     it: --}}
<span>{{ trans_choice('messages.apples', $count) }}</span>
{{-- count === 1  ->  "one apple"
     count === 0  ->  "no apples"
     count === 5  ->  "5 apples"  (from :count) --}}

{{-- THE LANGUAGE FILE, and this is where the rules live: --}}
{{-- lang/en/messages.php --}}
return [
    'apples' => '{0} There are none|{1} There is one|[2,*] There are :count',
    //        ^ EXPLICIT RANGES, which is how :count gets into the
    //          string at all. The framework does not invent a
    //          count placeholder.
    'unread' => '{0} No unread messages|{1} :count unread message|[2,*] :count unread messages',
];

{{-- SO THE FRAMEWORK DOES NOT PRODUCE THE NUMBER. A translation
     saying "5 apples" is a string the template must supply, and
     the range is how you ask for it. --}}
<span>{{ trans_choice('messages.apples', $count) }}</span>

{{-- WHICH MEANS THE PLAIN FORM NEEDS THE COUNT SEPARATELY: --}}
{{-- lang/en/messages.php --}}
'apples' => '{0} There are none|{1} There is one|[2,*] There are :count',

{{-- THE LANGUAGES WHERE TWO FORMS ARE NOT ENOUGH, and this is the
     reason the mechanism exists at all: --}}
{{-- lang/pl/messages.php — FOUR forms --}}
'apples' => '{0} Brak jabłek|{1} Jest {count} jabłko|[2,4] {count} jabłka|[5,*] {count} jabłek',
//         zero         one              few            many

{{-- lang/ru/messages.php — and it selects on the LAST DIGIT, not
     on whether the number is greater than one: --}}
'apples' => '{0} нет яблок|{1} {count} яблоко|[2,4] {count} яблока|[5,*] {count} яблок',
//         11 яблок is the MANY form, not the FEW form, and no
//         comparison on "greater than one" gets that right.

{{-- AND THE RANGE FORM, for the cases a count cannot express: --}}
{{ trans_choice('messages.apples', $count, ['zero' => 'no apples at all']) }}
{{-- ^ supplies a value for :zero explicitly, because zero is a
     CATEGORY in some languages rather than the absence of one. --}}

13.3 Placeholders and replacements

Documents the placeholder syntax, the replacement argument and the capitalisation directive form.

Named placeholders, and the failure mode that makes them worth having

A placeholder in a string is a name with a colon before it; a replacement is an array mapping that name to a value. The pairing is what makes the mechanism safe: the translation states which value belongs in which position, and the code supplies values by name, so inserting a second placeholder into a translation cannot shift the arguments.

That is the whole reason to use named replacements rather than the positional form. A positional replacement is a string with numbered slots, and adding one to a translated string shifts every later value — so the bug is introduced by a translator, in a string nobody in engineering reads, and it surfaces as a sentence with a date where a name belongs.

What happens to a placeholder with no replacement

The default is to leave the placeholder in the output, which means a missing replacement is visible in the page rather than silently empty. That is the correct default and it is worth stating because the alternative — an empty string — produces a sentence that reads correctly and is wrong.

@lang('welcome.greeting', ['name' => $user->first_name])

// lang/en/welcome.php
return [
    'greeting' => 'Hello, :name!',
    'messages' => 'There is one issue|There are :count issues',
];

Named replacements match their token exactly and are substituted across the whole string. Capitalisation directives are appended after a pipe inside the translation file, which keeps the transform with the translator rather than in the template — a translator working in a language whose rules differ from English should not have to reason about English sentence structure inside a Blade file.

Whole strings, not fragments. Translating a sentence fragment forces English word order onto the target language. Translate complete sentences and place dynamic content inside them.


{{-- THE PAIRING, and why it is named rather than positional. --}}
{{-- lang/en/orders.php --}}
return [
    'placed_on' => 'Order :number was placed on :date',
];

<p>{{ __('orders.placed_on', [
        'number' => $order->number,
        'date'   => $order->placedAt->isoFormat('D M Y'),
    ]) }}</p>
{{-- ^ the translation states WHICH value belongs WHERE, and the
     code supplies values BY NAME. Insert a second placeholder into
     the translation and nothing shifts. --}}

{{-- THE POSITIONAL FORM, and the bug a translator introduces: --}}
{{-- WRONG: positional --}}
{{-- lang/en/orders.php --}}
'placed_on' => 'Order %s was placed on %s',
'greeting'  => 'Hello %s',

{{ __('orders.placed_on', [$number, $date]) }}
{{ __('orders.greeting', [$name]) }}
{{-- ^ works. Then a translator improves one string: --}}

'placed_on' => 'Hi %s, order %s was placed on %s',
{{-- ^ and now EVERY call site for that key passes three arguments
     where the template passes two. The date is rendered as the
     number, the sentence reads plausibly, and the bug is in a
     string no engineer reads. --}}

{{-- WHAT HAPPENS TO A PLACEHOLDER WITH NO REPLACEMENT. --}}
{{ __('orders.placed_on', ['number' => $order->number]) }}
{{-- renders: "Order 42 was placed on :date"
                  ^ the placeholder is LEFT IN. Which is the
                    correct default: a missing replacement is
                    VISIBLE rather than silently empty. --}}

{{-- AND THE CASE THAT PROVES IT: --}}
{{ __('greeting', ['user_name' => $user->name]) }}
{{-- renders: "Hello :user_name"
                  ^ a typo in the key is a visible defect rather
                    than a greeting with a hole in it. --}}

{{-- AND THE PLACEHOLDER THAT IS ITSELF A TRANSLATION: --}}
<p>{{ __('orders.status_line', [
        'status' => __("orders.status.{$order->status->value}"),
        'date'   => $order->updatedAt->diffForHumans(),
    ]) }}</p>
{{-- ^ the replacement is a translation itself, which is correct:
     the placeholder's CONTENT is localised because the value it is
     given is already localised. Do not build a translated string
     by concatenation — see the JSON form in 13.4. --}}

13.4 JSON translation files

Documents the JSON translation file format, its flat key structure, and when it is preferable to grouped PHP files.

Serialising a catalogue, and why it exists

A JavaScript file that needs a translated string cannot call the server's translation helper, so the catalogue has to be serialised into the page. The framework offers a directive for that, and it applies the escaping that section 2.6 covered: the script block is JSON, encoded with the flags that stop a string containing a closing tag from ending the block early.

The purpose is narrower than it looks. It is for a small number of strings needed by client-side code — a validation message, a button label, a confirmation prompt. It is not for the catalogue: a page carrying the full set of translations is a page carrying every string in every language file the application has, and a single page at forty thousand strings is a page nobody can make fast.

The alternative that is usually better

Pass the message from the server as data. The template already knows which language it is rendering in, and a value the server computed arrives already translated — so the client-side code handles a string it was given rather than looking one up. The catalogue exists for the cases where the client genuinely does not know which key it needs, and those are rarer than they look.

// lang/ar.json
{
    "Welcome": "مرحبا",
    "Save": "حفظ"
}

// usage
<button>{{ __('Save') }}</button>

JSON keys are flat and therefore cannot express nesting; a group becomes part of the key text. This makes JSON the correct choice for short, context-free interface strings — the buttons and labels a translator needs to see on one screen — and the wrong choice for anything that benefits from grouping or from comments in the file. Both formats may coexist in the same application.

Flat keys do not compose. A JSON file cannot express plurals, because a value is a single string with no variants. Any string needing pluralisation must live in a PHP file.


{{-- THE SERIALISED CATALOGUE, with the flags that matter: --}}
<script type="application/json" id="translations">
    {!! json_encode(trans('js'), JSON_HEX_TAG | JSON_HEX_AMP | JSON_HEX_APOS | JSON_HEX_QUOT) !!}
</script>

{{-- OR THE DIRECTIVE, which is the same thing with the escaping
     already applied: --}}
@json(trans('js'))

{{-- AND READING IT ON THE CLIENT, which must not be a global: --}}
<script>
    // One read, no global. A global catalogue is a global
    // namespace collision waiting to happen with another bundle.
    const translations = JSON.parse(
        document.getElementById('translations').textContent
    );
</script>

{{-- THE PURPOSE IS NARROWER THAN IT LOOKS, and this is the rule: --}}
{{--   a validation message the client re-renders  -> serialise
     a button label                              -> pass it from
                                                    the server
     a confirmation prompt                        -> pass it from
                                                    the server
     anything the catalogue happens to contain    -> nothing --}}
<div x-data="{ saveLabel: @js(__('orders.save')) }">
    <button x-text="saving ? @js(__('orders.saving')) : saveLabel">
        {{ __('orders.save') }}
    </button>
</div>
{{-- ^ three strings, passed as data. The client-side code handles
     a string it was GIVEN rather than looking one up, and the page
     carries three strings rather than a catalogue. --}}

{{-- AND THE CASE THE CATALOGUE IS FOR, where the client genuinely
     does not know which key it needs until the user acts: --}}
@json(trans('validation'))

<script>
    const messages = JSON.parse(
        document.getElementById('validation').textContent
    );
    // The client picks the key at RUNTIME, from the field name in a
    // response it did not have when the page was rendered. This is
    // the one case that cannot be solved by passing a value.
    showError(field, messages[field + '.required']);
</script>

{{-- WHY NOT THE WHOLE CATALOGUE: a page carrying the full set of
     translations carries every string in every language file the
     application has, and a single page at forty thousand strings is
     a page nobody can make fast. --}}
{{-- ^ the rule for the size, which is worth writing down: --}}
{{--   under ~20 keys   -> serialise the group
     over 20            -> pass the value from the server
     over 100           -> an endpoint that returns one group --}}

13.5 Switching the locale per request

Documents the middleware that resolves the request locale and the three sources it consults, in order.

Where the locale is decided, and what follows from it

The locale is a property of the application instance, set once during the request. Two things depend on it: which language file is consulted, and which formatting rules apply to dates, numbers and currency. Both are the reason a per-request switch must happen before anything is formatted, and the reason a locale set halfway through a request is a bug rather than a feature.

The scope rule matters in an application that serves several locales from one host. Setting the locale per request means every concurrent request must have its own value, which is what the framework's scoped binding is for — a global static would be correct for one language and a race for several.

What a locale does not do

It does not translate a value already rendered, and it does not affect a string already in the database in one language. Content stored as translated text — a product description supplied by a translator rather than by the locale — is a different problem with different storage, and mixing the two produces a database where some columns follow the locale and some do not.

// app/Http/Middleware/HandleLocalization.php
final class HandleLocalization
{
    public function handle(Request $request, Closure $next): Response
    {
        app()->setLocale($request->user()?->locale
            ?? $request->session()->get('locale')
            ?? config('app.locale'));

        return $next($request);
    }
}

Avoid routing on the accept-language header alone: it makes output vary without the user choosing it, so cached responses fragment unpredictably. Resolve the locale from an explicit user preference, fall back to the session, then to the configured default, and set it once per request before any view renders.

Cache fragmentation. If full-page responses are cached, the cache key must include the locale, the permission level and any other input that changes the rendered output. Forgetting the locale is the most common instance of this bug.


{{-- WHERE IT IS DECIDED, and the rule that makes it safe: --}}
// app/Http/Middleware/SetLocale.php
final class SetLocale
{
    public function handle(Request $request, Closure $next): Response
    {
        // FROM THE ROUTE PREFIX, which is deterministic and
        // cacheable. The alternatives are all worse.
        //
        // FROM A COOKIE: one language per browser, so a shared link
        //   opens in the reader's language rather than the author's.
        // FROM ACCEPT-LANGUAGE: a negotiation on every request, and
        //   the result differs per visitor, so the page cannot be
        //   cached at all without varying on the header.
        $locale = $request->route('locale')
            ?? $this->negotiate($request->header('Accept-Language'));

        app()->setLocale($this->supported($locale));

        // FORMATTING RULES FOLLOW. A locale is not only a language:
        // it selects the date format, the number separators and the
        // currency format. Changing the locale changes all of them.
        Carbon::setLocale(app()->getLocale());

        return $next($request);
    }

    private function supported(string $locale): string
    {
        // AN ALLOWLIST, because an unvalidated locale is a path
        // traversal: ../../lang/en/secret is a locale string.
        return in_array($locale, config('app.supported_locales'), true)
            ? $locale
            : config('app.fallback_locale');
    }
}

// routes/web.php
Route::prefix('{locale}')->where(['locale' => '[a-z]{2}(-[A-Z]{2})?'])
    ->middleware(SetLocale::class)
    ->group(function () {
        Route::get('/orders', [OrderController::class, 'index'])
            ->name('orders.index');
    });

{{-- AND WHAT A LOCALE DOES, in a template — every one of these
     changes with it, which is why they belong to the locale rather
     than to a format string in the view: --}}
<p>{{ $order->placedAt->isoFormat('D M Y') }}</p>      {{-- date --}}
<p>{{ $order->total->format() }}</p>                  {{-- currency --}}
<p>{{ number_format($order->weight, 1) }}</p>          {{-- decimal
                                                           separator --}}
<p>{{ trans_choice('messages.apples', $count) }}</p>    {{-- plural --}}
<p>{{ $user->preferredLocale }}</p>                    {{-- language --}}

{{-- AND WHY IT MUST BE SET BEFORE ANY FORMATTING, which is the
     bug the middleware's position exists to prevent: --}}
public function show(Order $order): View
{
    // WRONG: anything formatted before setLocale keeps the default
    // formatting, and the page is half in one language.
    return view('orders.show', ['placed' => $order->placedAt->format('d/m/Y')]);
}

{{-- RIGHT: the middleware has already run, so every format call in
     the controller and in the template uses the request's locale. --}}

{{-- WHAT A LOCALE DOES NOT DO: --}}
{{-- It does not translate a value ALREADY RENDERED, and it does
     not affect a string already stored in one language. --}}
{{ __('orders.title') }}   {{-- reads lang/es/orders.php --}}
{{ $product->description }}  {{-- reads the DATABASE, which is
                                   whatever language whoever
                                   entered it used. --}}
{{-- ^ these two look identical in the template and are entirely
     different systems. A product description is CONTENT; an order
     title is CHROME. --}}

{{-- THE RULE THAT PREVENTS THE MIX-UP: content that varies by
     language is stored per language, with a locale column, and the
     query selects it. --}}
$product = Product::select(['id', 'name'])
    ->with(['translations' => fn ($q) => $q->where('locale', app()->getLocale())])
    ->first();

13.6 Right-to-left locales

Documents the direction attribute, logical CSS properties and the layout mirroring that right-to-left support requires.

Right-to-left is a layout change, not a text change

A right-to-left locale changes the direction of the flow, not the characters. The consequences reach well past an attribute on the container: alignment defaults, the side a scrollbar appears on, the order of a flex row, the direction of an arrow glyph, and the meaning of a shadow. Each of these has to be handled deliberately, and none of them is handled by setting one attribute.

The mechanical approach uses logical properties — the ones that mean "the start side" rather than "the left side" — so a single declaration is correct in both directions. The cost is that logical properties are not supported everywhere the physical ones are, so an application with older-browser support needs both, and the both has to be written by hand.

The cases that are not about layout

Three things need more than a direction attribute. A number in an Arabic locale uses different digits, and the digit choice follows the locale rather than the text's direction. A date's field order follows the locale. And a string that contains a fragment in the other direction — a product name in Latin script inside an Arabic sentence — needs bidirectional isolation around it or the punctuation moves to the wrong end of the phrase.

<html lang="{{ app()->getLocale() }}"
      dir="{{ in_array(app()->getLocale(), ['ar', 'he', 'fa'], true) ? 'rtl' : 'ltr' }}">

/* Use logical properties, not left and right. */
.order__actions {
    margin-inline-start: 1rem;
    padding-inline:      2rem;
    text-align:          start;
    border-inline-end:   1px solid;
}

Setting dir="rtl" on the root element lets the browser resolve directional typography, punctuation and text input for the direction. CSS authored with margin-left, padding-right or text-align: left does not mirror, so a right-to-left locale must arrive alongside a sweep to logical properties rather than as a toggle.

Exercises 13

  1. Extract every user-facing string from one page into a language file and confirm the page renders identically.
  2. Add a pluralised count string for a locale with more than two plural forms and verify each form is selected correctly.
  3. Implement the localization middleware with an explicit preference precedence and confirm a query parameter cannot override a saved preference.
  4. Add the direction attribute, then replace physical CSS properties with logical ones across one page and compare both directions.

{{-- THE ONE NECESSARY ATTRIBUTE, and everything that follows from
     it: --}}
<html lang="{{ str_replace('_', '-', app()->getLocale()) }}"
      dir="{{ app()->getLocale() === 'ar' ? 'rtl' : 'ltr' }}">

{{-- THE LANG ATTRIBUTE IS NOT OPTIONAL. A screen reader picks its
     pronunciation from lang, so an Arabic page with lang="en" is
     read in English with Arabic syllables. --}}

{{-- LOGICAL PROPERTIES, and this is the whole approach: --}}
.panel {
    /* PHYSICAL: correct in one direction, wrong in the other */
    margin-left:  1rem;
    padding-right: 0.5rem;
    text-align:   left;
    border-left:  3px solid var(--rule);

    /* LOGICAL: correct in both, because "inline-start" means the
       start side of the writing direction */
    margin-inline-start:  1rem;
    padding-inline-end:   0.5rem;
    text-align:           start;
    border-inline-start:  3px solid var(--rule);
}

{{-- ^ so ONE rule covers both directions. The cost: logical
     properties need a fallback for older browsers, and the both
     has to be written by hand. --}}

{{-- FLEX ROWS, where the ORDER REVERSES and logical properties do
     not help because order is not a property: --}}
.row { display: flex; gap: 0.5rem; }
{{-- ^ in RTL the first child renders on the RIGHT. Which is
     usually what you want — and sometimes is not. --}}
.row--ltr { direction: ltr; }
{{-- ^ for a row whose order is MEANINGFUL, such as a date range
     or a breadcrumb, pin it explicitly. --}}

{{-- THE CASES THAT ARE NOT ABOUT LAYOUT: --}}

{{-- (1) DIGITS. An Arabic locale uses different digits, and the
     choice follows the LOCALE rather than the text direction. --}}
<p>{{ number_format($amount) }}</p>
{{-- ^ formats with the locale's digits and separators, which for
     an Arabic locale are not the ASCII ones. Passing an explicit
     format string overrides this, so do not. --}}

{{-- (2) DATE FIELD ORDER, which follows the locale. --}}
{{ $order->placedAt->isoFormat('D M Y') }}   {{-- correct --}}
{{ $order->placedAt->format('d/m/Y') }}     {{-- WRONG in an
                                                   Arabic locale:
                                                   the field order
                                                   is fixed by the
                                                   format string
                                                   rather than by
                                                   the locale --}}

{{-- (3) BIDIRECTIONAL ISOLATION, and this is the one that produces
     visible corruption: --}}
<p>{{ __('orders.from', ['name' => $seller->name]) }}</p>
{{-- ^ if the seller's name is in Latin script inside an Arabic
     sentence, the TRAILING PUNCTUATION moves to the wrong end of
     the phrase without isolation. --}}

{{ bdi dir="auto" }}{{ $seller->name }}{{ endbdi }}
{{-- ^ the isolation element. "dir=auto" derives the direction from
     the CONTENT, so a Latin name reads left-to-right inside an
     Arabic sentence and the punctuation stays with it. --}}

{{-- AND WHERE IT GOES: anywhere a value from the database can be
     in a script the surrounding text is not. --}}
<x-money :amount="$order->total" />
{{-- inside the component: --}}
<span>{!! $amount->format() !!} <bdi dir="auto">{{ $currency->code }}</bdi></span>

{{-- THE MIXED-DIRECTION MARKER, for the rare case a string needs
     to be RTL even when its content is not. The mark is the
     Unicode character U+200E, LEFT-TO-RIGHT MARK: --}}
<p>Some text in an Arabic page: ‎{{ $name }}‎</p>
{{-- ^ the marks force the surrounding neutral characters to be
     read as part of the Latin run. Prefer bdi over the marks: the
     element carries the intent and the marks are invisible
     control characters. Written as a numeric character reference
     because the character itself is invisible in a source file and
     a reader cannot see what is there. --}}

14 · Conditional Classes and Attributes

14.1 The class directive14.2 Conditional class tokens14.3 The Tailwind purge problem14.4 Conditional attributes14.5 Inline styles14.6 Arbitrary values and variants

Conditional styling is where Blade meets the class-based stylesheet. This chapter documents the directives for conditional classes and attributes, and the purge behaviour that makes a dynamically composed class name disappear from the compiled CSS.

14.1 The class directive

Documents the class directive, its argument forms and the array-key condition syntax.

Concatenating class names, and the three sources

A class attribute in a template has up to three sources: a fixed base, a conditional set, and whatever the caller passed. Concatenating them by hand produces a string, and a string cannot be merged — so a caller's class either overwrites the component's or is appended to it with a rule nobody wrote down. The directive class exists so the merge is a merge.

The three forms differ in what they do with a falsy value. A plain expression is included or not. The conditional form includes the base and the conditional value together, and omits both when the condition is false. And the array form omits any key whose value is falsy, which is the form that scales to a component with a half-dozen conditions.

Why a string is not enough

Because a component's class is part of its contract. If a caller can override it, the component cannot guarantee the class its behaviour depends on — a disclosure whose class supplies the hidden attribute is a disclosure that does not work when a caller writes a class. The merge, with the caller's value winning for the classes both supply and the component's value winning for the ones only it supplies, is the rule that makes the contract hold.

<div class="@class(['alert', 'alert--danger' => $level === 'danger'])">
    <span>{{ $message }}</span>
</div>

<div @class([
    'btn',
    'btn--primary' => $variant === 'primary',
    'btn--ghost'   => $variant === 'ghost',
    'btn--block'   => $block,
])>
    Save
</div>

An array element with no condition contributes its value unconditionally; an element whose key is a boolean expression contributes the value when that expression is truthy. A plain string passed as the argument is emitted unchanged. Values are escaped, so the directive is safe with interpolated data.

Keep conditions outside the class token. Write 'btn--danger' => $level === 'danger', never "btn--{$level}". The first survives the purge step described in 14.3; the second does not.


{{-- HAND CONCATENATION, and the two bugs in one line: --}}
<div class="btn {{ $variant }} {{ $attributes->get('class') }}">
{{-- ^ (1) $attributes->get('class') overwrites nothing, it
     APPENDS, so a caller who passes btn-lg gets "btn btn-lg"
     twice or "btn btn-lg" in an order nobody controls.
     (2) If $variant is null, the class attribute has a double
     space and the markup is untidy for no reason. --}}

{{-- (1) PLAIN EXPRESSION: the class and its condition, together. --}}
<div class="btn {{ $variant ? 'btn-' . $variant : '' }}">
{{-- ^ simple, and correct — but a caller still cannot contribute. --}}

{{-- (2) THE CONDITIONAL FORM, which merges rather than
     concatenates: --}}
<div {{ $attributes->merge(['class' => 'btn btn-' . $variant])
                     ->class(['btn-lg' => $large]) }}>
{{-- ^ merge() takes the component's default and lets the CALLER'S
     value win for the classes both supply. class() then adds the
     conditional ones on top, with the same precedence. --}}

{{-- (3) THE ARRAY FORM, for a component with several conditions: --}}
@props(['variant' => 'primary', 'size' => 'md', 'disabled' => false])

<div {{ $attributes->merge([
        'class' => [
            'btn',
            'btn-' . $variant,
            'btn-' . $size,
            'btn-disabled:opacity-50',
            'btn-disabled:pointer-events-none',
            // FALSY VALUES ARE OMITTED, so a disabled false does
            // not leave "btn-disabled" in the class attribute.
            'is-disabled' => $disabled,
        ],
    ]) }}>
{{-- ^ any key whose value is falsy is dropped, so the array can be
     written as a list of conditions with no conditionals in it.
     This is the form that scales. --}}

{{-- AND THE ARRAY FORM IS ALSO THE PLACE FOR TOOLING: --}}
class="{{ implode(' ', array_filter([
        'btn',
        'btn-' . $variant,
        $large ? 'btn-lg' : null,
        $attributes->get('class'),
])) }}"
{{-- ^ written out by hand, and note what it loses: it does not
     DEDUPLICATE, and two sources both supplying "text-red-500"
     produces it twice. merge() resolves the conflict; a string
     cannot. --}}

14.2 Conditional class tokens

Explains why splitting a class into a base and modifiers improves reuse, and how state classes fit the same pattern.

Conditional tokens without a conditional

The pattern that works well is a list of class names where a falsy entry disappears. It reads as data rather than as logic, it deduplicates naturally if the list is filtered, and it is the shape a reviewer can check without evaluating anything.

The array form in the directive does this, and the benefit over a hand-written ternary chain is not brevity — it is that the merge happens once, in one place, so the caller's class and the component's conditional classes are resolved by the same rule rather than by two.

Where a token becomes a problem

A class that exists only for a framework to find it. The common form is a token whose only consumer is a stylesheet rule that sets a custom property, which is a reasonable thing to do and worth being deliberate about — because the alternative is an inline style, and an inline style cannot be overridden by a class without !important.

<button @class([
    'tab',
    'tab--active'    => $isActive,
    'tab--disabled' => $disabled,
    'tab--muted'    => !$hasPermission,
]) aria-current="{{ $isActive ? 'page' : 'false' }}">
    {{ $label }}
</button>

A single class such as tab-danger-active-disabled cannot express combinations: every additional state needs another class and the stylesheet grows combinatorially. A base class plus one modifier per state keeps the stylesheet linear and lets any combination render correctly.

Prefer CSS over conditionals for structure. A striped table row needs no conditional class when the stylesheet uses :nth-child(even). Reserve conditional classes for states that change semantics, not for patterns a selector can express.


{{-- THE SHAPE, and it reads as DATA rather than as logic: --}}
@props(['variant' => 'primary', 'active' => false, 'disabled' => false])

<span {{ $attributes->merge([
        'class' => [
            'badge',
            'badge-' . $variant,
            'badge-active' => $active,
            'badge-disabled' => $disabled,
        ],
    ]) }}>
    {{ $slot }}
</span>
{{-- ^ three conditions, no conditionals. A reviewer can read the
     whole set of states the component can be in, which is the thing
     a chain of ternaries hides. --}}

{{-- WHERE THE PRIORITY IS DECIDED, and it is worth knowing: --}}
<div {{ $attributes->merge(['class' => 'p-4 text-red-500'])
                     ->class(['text-blue-500' => $highlight]) }}>
{{-- ^ merge() lets the CALLER win for classes both supply, and
     class() then adds the component's conditional ones with the
     same precedence. So a caller's text-red-500 beats a
     component's text-blue-500, and both appear when the caller
     supplies neither. --}}

{{-- AND WHAT HAPPENS WHEN TWO SOURCES SUPPLY THE SAME ONE: --}}
<x-badge class="badge-lg" />
{{-- with size === 'lg' — the class appears twice. merge() does
     not deduplicate, and this is fine: the CSS is identical and a
     browser does not care. What it does NOT do is let one source
     silently WIN, which is what a naive string replacement would
     do and what makes that approach wrong. --}}

{{-- THE VARIANT THAT IS WORTH NAMING: a token that exists only so
     a stylesheet can find it. --}}
<div {{ $attributes->merge([
        'class' => [
            'panel',
            'panel-tone-' . $tone,      {{-- consumed by a rule that
                                                 exists only to set
                                                 a custom property --}}
        ],
    ]) }}
     style="--panel-tone: var(--tone-{{ $tone }})">
{{-- ^ this is legitimate and it is NOT an inline style in the
     usual sense: the value comes from a custom property, so the
     component still decides the presentation. What it must not
     become is a component that sets presentation directly, because
     a class can then never override it without !important. --}}

{{-- THE VERSION THAT HAS GONE WRONG: --}}
<div class="panel" style="background: {{ $colour }}">
{{-- ^ the component now decides presentation, a caller's class
     cannot change it without !important, and a dark-mode variant
     requires a conditional in the template. --}}

14.3 The Tailwind purge problem

Explains how the Tailwind content scanner builds the stylesheet and why a dynamically composed class name is removed from it.

Tailwind generates only the utility classes it finds as literal strings in the configured content paths. A class assembled at runtime is not present as a literal, so it is never generated:

Purging by scanning, and what it cannot see

A utility framework's production build removes every class it cannot find in the source. That is what makes the stylesheet small, and it works by scanning files for strings that look like class names. The consequence is that a class assembled at run time is invisible to the scanner, and the style silently disappears from the production build while working perfectly in development.

The two forms of this are equally common and equally invisible. A class built by concatenation exists only as pieces in the source. And a class supplied as a prop — a component that takes a variant name and prefixes it — is built at run time by construction, because the complete string never appears anywhere.

Where the fix belongs

Not in the component that needs the class, but at the boundary where the variant is named. The complete class must appear as a literal somewhere the scanner will read, which in practice means a map from variant name to class in a file the build includes. The variant is then a key rather than a prefix, and the scanner sees the class.

/* Removed by the scanner: no literal class exists. */
<div class="bg-{{ $color }}-500"></div>

/* Retained: each literal appears in the source file. */
<div @class([
    'bg-blue-500' => $theme !== 'dark',
    'bg-gray-500' => $theme === 'dark',
])></div>

The second form works because the scanner sees bg-blue-500 and bg-gray-500 as literals. When a value arrives from the database or a configuration file it appears in no scanned file, so it must either be safelisted or mapped to a literal inside the template.

Silent failure. A purged class produces no error and no warning — the element simply renders unstyled. Treat "correct in development, unstyled in production" as a purge candidate before investigating the template.


{{-- THE MECHANISM, and why a run-time class vanishes: --}}
{{-- resources/views/components/badge.blade.php --}}
<span class="badge badge-{{ $variant }}">
{{-- ^ the production build scans this file and finds "badge" and
     "badge-" — NOT "badge-success", "badge-warning" and the rest,
     because those strings appear nowhere. The CSS for those
     variants is REMOVED, and the badges render unstyled in
     production while working perfectly in dev. --}}

{{-- THE FIX AT THE BOUNDARY, not at the point of use: the
     complete class appears as a literal the scanner will read. --}}
{{-- resources/views/components/badge.blade.php --}}
@props(['variant' => 'neutral'])

@php
    // THE MAP. Every complete class is a literal here, so the
    // scanner finds it. The variant is a KEY rather than a prefix,
    // which is the whole fix.
    $tones = [
        'neutral' => 'badge-neutral',
        'success' => 'badge-success',
        'warning' => 'badge-warning',
        'danger'  => 'badge-danger',
    ];
@endphp

<span {{ $attributes->merge([
        'class' => ['badge', $tones[$variant] ?? $tones['neutral']],
    ]) }}>
    {{ $slot }}
</span>
{{-- ^ now every class is a string in the source, and the build
     keeps all of them. The map is the price, and it is worth
     paying once per component. --}}

{{-- THE SAFEST CONFIGURATION, which is worth considering on a
     project that has been bitten: --}}
// tailwind.config.js
module.exports = {
    content: [
        './resources/**/*.blade.php',
        './resources/**/*.js',
        './app/**/*.php',
        {{-- AND A SAFELIST for anything that is assembled outside
             the scanned paths: a vendor package, a generated
             class, a configuration value. --}}
        safelist: [
            // Every status tone, in every component that uses one.
            { pattern: /badge-(neutral|success|warning|danger)/ },
            // Anything that arrives as a database value.
            { pattern: /theme-.*/ },
        ],
    ],
};
{{-- ^ a safelist is a list of things the scanner cannot find. It
     is the honest escape hatch, and a pattern is better than a
     literal list because it covers the variants nobody has used
     yet. --}}

{{-- THE TEST THAT CATCHES IT, because nothing else does: a class
     that works in dev and not in prod fails no test and shows no
     warning. --}}
it('keeps every variant class in the production build', function () {
    $css = file_get_contents(public_path('build/assets/app.css'));

    foreach (['badge-neutral', 'badge-success', 'badge-warning',
              'badge-danger'] as $class) {
        expect($css)->toContain('.' . $class);
    }
});
{{-- ^ this runs against the BUILT stylesheet, so it fails if the
     purge removed a class. It is the only check that does. --}}

14.4 Conditional attributes

Documents conditional attributes in Blade and in Alpine, and the difference in escaping behaviour between them.

Attributes that may or may not be there

A boolean attribute is either present or absent, and its presence is the value — so it cannot be produced by interpolating a boolean into a string, because a false boolean becomes the literal text "false", which is a present attribute. Every framework's mechanism for this exists because of that one detail.

The three forms available here all handle it: the spread directive renders nothing for a null or false value, the ternary form produces the whole attribute including its name, and the explicit-attribute directive omits the attribute entirely when its value is falsy. They differ in what they emit for an empty string, which is the case that is genuinely ambiguous.

The three-value problem

An attribute has three interesting states: present with a value, present with no value, and absent. A boolean attribute collapses the first two, which is correct for it and wrong for everything else. For a non-boolean attribute, an empty string is usually not the same as absent, and the mechanism has to distinguish them or the markup ends up with attributes that carry no information.

<input type="text"
       @required($field->isRequired())
       @disabled($field->isLocked())
       @error($field->name()) aria-invalid="true" @enderror>

/* Alpine binds the same attributes on the client. */
<input type="checkbox" :checked="selected.includes({{ $id }})">

The Blade form compiles to an attribute present only when the expression is true, emitting nothing otherwise. The Alpine form uses a bind directive whose value is an expression rather than markup. Blade escapes attribute values; Alpine expressions are evaluated as code and must never interpolate raw user data.

Accessibility is not decoration. aria-expanded and aria-hidden carry meaning for assistive technology and should follow the same condition as the class, not merely the visual treatment.


{{-- WHY THE INTERPOLATION FORM IS WRONG, and it is not a style
     preference: --}}
<div class="{{ $disabled ? 'opacity-50' : '' }}" aria-disabled="{{ $disabled }}">
{{-- ^ aria-disabled="false" is NOT the same as omitting the
     attribute. A screen reader announces "dimmed" either way,
     because it reads the attribute's PRESENCE, not its value.
     The correct output is no attribute at all. --}}

{{-- (1) THE SPREAD DIRECTIVE, which omits null and false: --}}
<div {{ $attributes->merge(['class' => 'panel'])
                     ->class(['opacity-50' => $disabled]) }}
     {{ $disabled ? 'aria-disabled' : '' }}>
{{-- ^ the spread renders nothing for a falsy value, so the
     attribute is absent rather than empty. --}}

{{-- (2) THE EXPLICIT-ATTRIBUTE DIRECTIVE, which omits the WHOLE
     attribute when the value is falsy: --}}
<div @disabled($disabled) @readonly($readonly) aria-disabled="{{ $disabled ? 'true' : null }}">
{{-- ^ @disabled emits disabled="" for true and NOTHING for false.
     aria-disabled uses the third form, because that attribute
     carries a value and "false" is meaningful there. --}}

{{-- THE THREE-VALUE PROBLEM, which is the one that needs thought: --}}
<input value="{{ $value }}">
{{-- ^ an EMPTY STRING produces value="", which the browser
     reports as an empty field. If "absent" and "present and
     empty" mean the same thing to your form, say so explicitly. --}}

<input value="{{ $value === '' ? '' : null }}">
{{-- ^ null renders no attribute at all, which is a DIFFERENT
     state: the browser then uses its own default, and for a
     text input that default is also empty. --}}

{{-- SO WHICH DO YOU WANT? --}}
{{--   present-and-empty   -> value=""     (a field the user must
                                              clear, or a value that
                                              IS the empty string)
     absent               -> no attribute  (use the default,
                                              which may be non-empty)
     present-with-a-value -> the value
     ^ a form that conflates the first two sends something the user
       did not type. --}}

{{-- AND THE ARIA VERSIONS, where the distinction matters more than
     anywhere else: --}}
<div aria-hidden="{{ $hidden ? 'true' : 'false' }}">
{{-- ^ correct: this attribute is always present and its value
     matters. --}}

<div aria-hidden="{{ $hidden ?: null }}">
{{-- ^ WRONG: aria-hidden with an EMPTY value is aria-hidden="",
     which assistive technology treats as INVALID and may ignore
     entirely — so the element is announced when it should not be. --}}

14.5 Inline styles

Documents the style directive and the cases where an inline value is genuinely required.

Inline styles, and the three things that justify one

An inline style is a presentation decision made in the markup, and it beats a class in three specific cases. A value that is only known at run time — a width computed from a percentage, a colour derived from data. A value that is per-element by design, such as a bar chart's height. And a custom property whose value comes from a theme the user selected.

Everything else is a class. The reason is not aesthetic: an inline style has a higher priority than any class in the cascade, so a component that sets one cannot be restyled by a caller without !important, and cannot respond to a media query, a hover state, or a dark-mode variant.

Custom properties are the interesting case

A custom property set inline is different in kind from a presentation property set inline, because the property itself does not decide anything — it supplies a value that a class uses. That makes it a theme rather than an override, and it is the right mechanism for a per-instance theme: a chart's palette, a user's accent colour, a status colour from data.

<div @style([
    'width' => '50%',
    'opacity' => $muted ? '0.5' : '1',
])>

<div style="background-image: url('{{ $image->url() }}')"></div>

<div @style([
    '--progress' => $percent.'%',
])>
    <div class="bar"></div>
</div>

Inline style is appropriate where the value is genuinely computed at render time — a chart geometry, a progress width, a user-supplied accent colour. It is inappropriate for anything that a stylesheet could express, because inline styles cannot be overridden by a class without !important, and they fragment the styling across two systems.

Custom properties. Setting a CSS custom property with the style directive and consuming it in the stylesheet keeps computed values dynamic while the visual definition stays in CSS.


{{-- (1) A VALUE KNOWN ONLY AT RUN TIME. This is the legitimate
     case, and there is no alternative: --}}
<div class="bar"
     style="width: {{ round($value / $max * 100, 2) }}%">
{{-- ^ 47.31% is not in the source, so no class can express it. --}}

<div class="bar" data-value="{{ $value / $max }}"
     x-bind:style="'width:' + $el.dataset.value * 100 + '%'">
{{-- ^ and the same value computed on the client, which is the
     version that does not need a server round trip. --}}

{{-- (2) A PER-ELEMENT VALUE BY DESIGN: --}}
<div class="spark" style="--v: {{ $v }}"></div>
{{-- ^ a custom property, and the class does the presentation. --}}

.bar::before { width: calc(var(--v) * 100%); }

{{-- (3) A THEME THE USER SELECTED. Also legitimate, and the reason
     custom properties exist: --}}
<div style="--accent: {{ $user->accentColour }}">
{{-- ^ a theme, not an override. The class decides what --accent
     does; this only says what it is. --}}

.cta { background: var(--accent); }

{{-- EVERYTHING ELSE IS A CLASS, and the reason is the cascade: --}}
<div style="background: {{ $colour }}">
{{-- ^ an inline style beats every class, so:
     - a caller cannot restyle it without !important
     - it cannot respond to hover, a media query or a dark mode
     - it cannot be overridden by a user stylesheet
     - it does not appear in the class list, so a developer looking
       for why this element is blue will not find it. --}}

{{-- THE VARIABLE FORM, which is the correct answer to all four: --}}
<div class="cta" style="--cta-bg: {{ $colour }}"></div>
.cta { background: var(--cta-bg); }
.cta:hover { background: color-mix(in srgb, var(--cta-bg) 85%, black); }
{{-- ^ a caller CAN restyle it, it responds to hover, and the class
     name is searchable. --}}

{{-- THE ONE PLACE AN INLINE STYLE IS ALWAYS WRONG, and it is a
     security question rather than a style one: --}}
<div style="background: url({{ $userSuppliedUrl }})">
{{-- ^ a url from a user is a url the browser will FETCH, and the
     inline style bypasses every class-based content policy. --}}
<div class="avatar" style="--src: url("{{ $avatarUrl }}")">
{{-- ^ still fetches it. The fix is not a different property: it
     is to validate the URL, or to serve the image from a domain
     that only serves images. --}}

14.6 Arbitrary values and variants

Documents arbitrary values, how to keep them literal for the scanner, and the accessibility cost of colour-only state indication.

Values that only exist once

An arbitrary value is a way to express a CSS declaration inside a class name, for the cases where a token does not exist and adding one is not worth it. That is a genuine use: a one-off spacing value, a colour derived from data, a grid template for a page with no other page.

The cost is that arbitrary values are invisible to the design system. A spacing token is named, so a reader knows the intent and a change propagates. An arbitrary value is a number in a class name, so nobody knows whether it is deliberate or a typo, and nothing propagates. The rule that keeps it useful is that an arbitrary value is a signal to add a token, and the count of them in a codebase is the measure of whether the token set is adequate.

The purge interaction, which is the practical trap

An arbitrary value containing a space becomes a class with underscores in place of spaces, and the scanner sees the assembled string only if it appears literally. A value computed at run time vanishes from the production build exactly as a concatenated variant name does, and the symptom is identical: works in development, missing in production.

<div class="w-[{{ $width }}px]"></div>

<div class="w-[237px]"></div>

@foreach ($columns as $n)
    <div class="grid grid-cols-{{ $n }}"></div>
@endforeach

<div @class([
    'text-red-600'  => $error,
    'text-slate-900' => !$error,
    'font-medium'     => $error,
])>
    {{ $label }}
</div>

Two failures recur with Tailwind in Blade. An interpolated arbitrary value such as w-[{{ $width }}px] is invisible to the scanner, so it must be written as a literal. And an interpolated grid column count removes the whole class, so the variants must be enumerated rather than interpolated.

Never signal state by colour alone. A red border with unchanged text conveys failure to a sighted user and nothing at all to many others. Pair colour with an icon, a text change or an accessible name.

Exercises 14

  1. Replace every interpolated class name in a template with the class directive and confirm production styling is unchanged.
  2. Enumerate a dynamic grid column set as literals, then delete the safelist entry that was masking the problem.
  3. Add a computed progress bar using a custom property and confirm the value stays in CSS.
  4. Audit one component for colour-only state indication and add a non-colour signal to each case.

{{-- THE LEGITIMATE USE: a value no token covers. --}}
<div class="mt-[13px] grid-cols-[minmax(0,1fr)_320px]">
{{-- ^ 13px because the spec says 13. And a grid with one wide
     column and one fixed sidebar, which is the actual layout of
     this page and of no other page in the application. --}}

{{-- THE SIGNAL TO ADD A TOKEN, and it is worth being explicit: --}}
<div class="mt-4">          {{-- a TOKEN: named, so a reader knows
                                 the intent, and a change to the
                                 spacing scale propagates here --}}
<div class="mt-[13px]">     {{-- an ARBITRARY VALUE: a number, so
                                 nobody knows whether 13 is
                                 deliberate or a typo, and nothing
                                 propagates. --}}

{{-- SO THE RULE: an arbitrary value is fine ONCE. The second one in
     the same component is a signal that the token set is missing
     something, and the count of them is the measure. --}}
// A command that counts them, because a design system degrades one
// arbitrary value at a time and nothing notices.
Artisan::command('views:arbitrary', function () {
    $rows = [];
    foreach (View::getFinder()->getPaths() as $dir) {
        foreach (new RecursiveIteratorIterator(
                     new RecursiveDirectoryIterator($dir)) as $file) {
            if ($file->getExtension() !== 'php') continue;

            preg_match_all('/class="([^"]*\[[^"]*)"/',
                file_get_contents($file->getPathname()), $m);

            foreach ($m[1] as $match) {
                $rows[] = [Str::after($file->getPathname(), $dir . '/'),
                           $match];
            }
        }
    }

    foreach (collect($rows)->countBy(0)->sortDesc() as $file => $n) {
        $this->line(str_pad($n, 4) . $file);
    }
    $this->info(count($rows) . ' arbitrary value(s) across '
        . count(collect($rows)->pluck(0)->unique()) . ' template(s)');
});

{{-- THE PURGE INTERACTION, which is the practical trap and is the
     same failure as a concatenated variant name: --}}
<div class="w-[calc(100%-2rem)]"></div>
{{-- ✓ the complete string is in the source, so the scanner finds
     it and the CSS survives the production build. --}}

<div class="w-[calc(100%-var(--{{ $gutter }})]"></div>
{{-- ✗ assembled at RUN TIME. The scanner sees "w-[calc(100%-var(--"
     and nothing else, the rule is removed, and the element has no
     width in production while working perfectly in dev. --}}

{{-- THE FIX: the map, at the boundary. --}}
@php
    // Every complete class is a literal, so the scanner keeps it.
    $widths = [
        'flush' => 'w-[calc(100%-0rem)]',
        'gutter'=> 'w-[calc(100%-2rem)]',
        'wide'  => 'w-[calc(100%-4rem)]',
    ];
@endphp
<div class="{{ $widths[$variant] ?? $widths['gutter'] }}"></div>

15 · Performance: What Compiles Once and What Runs Every Request

15.1 Work that happens once15.2 Work that happens per request15.3 The N+1 problem in templates15.4 Query patterns for listings15.5 Include and component overhead15.6 Measuring render time

A Blade template’s cost is dominated by the work it triggers, not by its own syntax. This chapter separates one-off compilation cost from per-request rendering cost and documents the query patterns that dominate the latter.

15.1 Work that happens once

Enumerates the work performed during deployment or on first render, and why it does not affect throughput.

The following costs are paid once per template per deployment and are covered by php artisan view:cache:

  • Reading the source file and scanning it for directives.
  • Compiling each directive to PHP and writing the artifact.
  • Component discovery, performed once and cached by component:cache.

After pre-compilation a render performs no compilation work at all: the engine includes a PHP file opcache already holds.

Pre-compile in CI. Running view:cache during the build moves this cost off the first request after a deploy and makes the build fail loudly if any template cannot compile.

Once per process, not once per request

Some work genuinely happens once for the lifetime of a process: compiling the container, resolving the framework's own configuration, building a list of available commands. In a long-running worker that work is amortised over thousands of requests, and in a traditional request lifecycle it happens on every request and is worth doing once per process rather than once per request.

The distinction that matters is whether the work is per-request or per-process. A template that computes a constant at parse time is per-process, because the compiled view is compiled once and included many times. A helper called inside a loop is per-iteration, and making it per-process does not reduce anything — it produces a value that is stale after the first request in a worker.

The cost of getting it wrong in the other direction

A cached value that depends on the request — the current user, the locale, the URL — computed once at boot and reused is worse than no cache: it is correct in the first request and wrong in all the others, and it fails on the second user rather than the first. This is the single most common bug in a long-running worker, and it is worth knowing that every framework's own per-request state is reset at the start of each request precisely because it is easy to get wrong.


{{-- PER-PROCESS, and the marker that makes it obvious: the call
     is at the TOP LEVEL of the file, outside any function and
     outside any loop, so it runs once when the file is included. --}}
@php
    {{-- ONCE PER COMPILED VIEW. A view is compiled once and
         included on every request, so a top-level @php block runs
         once per process in a worker and once per compile
         otherwise. --}}
    $companyName = AppSupportCompany::current()->name;
@endphp

<h1>{{ $companyName }}</h1>
{{-- ^ correct for a value that is genuinely constant. --}}

{{-- AND NOT THIS, which is the bug in the other direction: --}}
@php
    {{-- WRONG: $user is per-request, and this block is not. In a
         worker, the first request's user is displayed for every
         subsequent request. --}}
    $userName = auth()->user()->name;
@endphp

<h1>{{ $userName }}</h1>

{{-- THE RULE, and it is worth writing down because it is the whole
     distinction: --}}
{{--   constant for the process  -> a top-level block, or a config
                                     value, or a static
   depends on the request       -> inside the function or the loop,
                                     every time
   depends on the USER          -> per request, always --}}


{{-- AND WHAT "RESET PER-REQUEST STATE" MEANS, because it is the
     mechanism that stops the bug above from being everybody's. --}}
// app/Providers/AppServiceProvider.php
$this->app->scoped(RequestContext::class, fn ($app) =>
    new RequestContext($app->make(Auth::class)->user()));
{{-- SCOPED, not singleton: resolved once per REQUEST and flushed
     afterwards, so a worker that handles a thousand requests has a
     thousand contexts and none of them leaks into the next. --}}

final class RequestContext
{
    public function __construct(public readonly ?User $user) {}
}

{{-- THE TEMPLATE READS IT rather than caching it: --}}
@php($ctx = app(AppSupportRequestContext::class))

<h1>{{ $ctx->user?->name }}</h1>
{{-- ^ one resolution per request, correct in a worker, and flushed
     before the next request. --}}

{{-- AND A STATIC, which is the subtle one: --}}
@php
    // static inside a view survives every render in a worker. The
    // value LOOKS constant because it is assigned once — and it is
    // constant for the wrong reason.
    static $locale = null;
    $locale ??= request()->getPreferredLanguage();
@endphp
{{-- ^ request 2 renders request 1's language. The fix is to read
     the value from the request-scoped context rather than from a
     static, so its lifetime is the request's. --}}

15.2 Work that happens per request

Lists the operations a template incurs on every render, ordered by cost.

OperationRelative costControl
Database queryHighestEager load, cache, aggregate in SQL
Model accessor or castMediumRead loaded attributes
Authorisation checkMediumCache the policy result
Component renderLowExtract only when reused
Echo and escapeNegligibleNo action needed

Escaping output is not a meaningful cost: e() is one function call over a short string. Replacing {{ }} with {!! !!} saves nothing measurable and discards the security guarantee.

Measure before optimising. A template is fast or slow because of the queries and service calls it triggers. Profiling the echo path is effort spent on the wrong layer.

Work that happens once per request

Some work is genuinely per-request and must be repeated every time: reading the session, resolving the authenticated user, determining the locale, parsing the request body. Doing any of it once per process is the bug described in the previous section, and doing it once per call is a measurable cost on a page that resolves the same value fifty times.

The mechanism that resolves this is a request-scoped binding: resolved once per request, reused within that request, discarded afterwards. That is exactly the shape the work has, and the cost of getting it wrong in either direction is a correctness bug rather than a performance one — so it is worth being deliberate even when the performance difference is invisible.

The test that distinguishes the two

Ask what a second request could change. A value derived only from configuration and code cannot change between requests in a worker, so caching it per process is correct. A value derived from the session, the user, the URL or the request body can change on every request, so caching it anywhere but in the request is wrong. There is no third category, and a value that seems to be in the first while reading something in the second is the bug.


{{-- THE FOUR, and they are all per-request by construction: --}}
// bootstrap/app.php
$middleware = [
    IlluminateCookieMiddlewareEncryptCookies::class,
    IlluminateSessionMiddlewareStartSession::class,
    IlluminateViewMiddlewareShareErrorsFromSession::class,
    IlluminateFoundationHttpMiddlewareValidateCsrfToken::class,
];
{{-- ^ StartSession runs once per request and the session object is
     shared with everything downstream. Auth, localisation and the
     error bag all read it. --}}

{{-- THE REQUEST-SCOPED BINDING, which gets the lifetime right: --}}
// app/Providers/AppServiceProvider.php
$this->app->scoped(ViewComposer::class, fn ($app) =>
    new ViewComposer(
        $app->make(BreadcrumbBuilder::class),
        $app->make(Auth::class)->user(),     // resolved ONCE per
                                              // request, from the
                                              // session middleware
                                              // that already ran
    ));

{{-- WHY SCOPED AND NOT SINGLETON, and this is the bug worth
     naming: --}}
$this->app->singleton(CurrentUser::class, fn () => ...);
{{-- ^ SINGLETON in a long-running worker: resolved on the FIRST
     request and never again. Request 1 is for Alice, request 2 is
     for Bob, and CurrentUser still says Alice. --}}

$this->app->scoped(CurrentUser::class, fn () => ...);
{{-- ^ SCOPED: resolved once per request, flushed when the request
     ends. Correct in a worker, identical in a traditional
     lifecycle. --}}


{{-- WHERE A TEMPLATE READS IT, which is the point of the exercise: --}}
{{-- resources/views/components/user-menu.blade.php --}}
@php($viewer = app(AppSupportCurrentUser::class)->resolve())
{{-- ^ ONE resolution per request: not one per render, and not one
     per process. --}}

@if ($viewer)
    <x-avatar :user="$viewer" />
    <x-menu>
        <x-menu.item href="{{ route('profile.edit') }}">
            {{ __('profile.title') }}
        </x-menu.item>
    </x-menu>
@endif

{{-- THE TEST THAT DISTINGUISHES THE TWO CATEGORIES, and it is one
     question: what could a second request change? --}}
{{--   derived from config and code only  -> cache per process is
                                              CORRECT
   derived from session, user, URL or
     body                                 -> cache per request, and
                                              nowhere else
   reads one and assumes the other        -> this is the bug --}}

{{-- AN EXAMPLE OF THE LAST, which is subtle because it LOOKS like
     a per-process constant: --}}
@php
    // Reads the URL — per request — but caches it per process.
    // WRONG: request 2 renders request 1's path in the breadcrumb.
    static $path = null;
    $path ??= request()->path();
@endphp
<p>You are viewing {{ $path }}</p>

{{-- AND THE COMPOSITE CASE, which is even harder to spot because
     both halves look fine: --}}
@php
    static $categories = null;
    static $currency   = null;

    $categories ??= Category::orderBy('name')->get();  {{-- per
                                                              process:
                                                              correct --}}
    $currency   ??= request()->user()->currency;      {{-- per
                                                              request:
                                                              WRONG --}}
@endphp
{{-- ^ the first is right and the second is not, in adjacent lines,
     in the same block, looking identical. --}}

15.3 The N+1 problem in templates

Shows how lazy loading triggered from a template produces one query per rendered row, and where the fix belongs.

The template is where the N+1 becomes visible

An N+1 is a query inside a loop, so the number of queries grows with the number of rows. The template is where it happens, and it happens because the template reads a relation on a model that was not loaded. The fix is not in the template: it is in the file that produced the collection.

There are three things that make this survive. The first is that the development environment often has a small dataset, so a loop of three rows produces three queries and nobody notices. The second is that eager loading is correct by convention rather than by construction — a developer adds an access to the template and does not add it to the query. The third is that the relation may be loaded on some rows and not others, so the count is not even proportional to the loop.

Why eager loading is not optional

Lazy loading is a convenience for a script and a hazard in a request. In a request there is no loop guard, so a relation access inside a loop is one query per iteration with no default protection. The framework can be told to throw on lazy loading outside development, which converts the defect from a slow page into an exception with a stack trace naming the template and the line.

{{-- 1 query for orders, then 1 per order for customer, then per line. --}}
@foreach ($orders as $order)
    <tr>
        <td>{{ $order->customer->name }}</td>
        <td>{{ $order->lines->sum('amount') }}</td>
    </tr>
@endforeach

The fix belongs in the controller. A template cannot eager load without issuing a query per render, which is the problem being solved:

$orders = Order::query()
    ->with('customer')
    ->withSum('lines', 'amount')
    ->paginate(25);

withCount() and withSum() compute aggregates in the original query and expose them as attributes, removing both the N+1 and the per-row collection load.

Never eager load in a view. A call to load() inside a template triggers a query on first access and hides the relationship from the log summary. Fix the controller.


{{-- THE N+1, and it is invisible in a loop of three: --}}
{{-- resources/views/orders/index.blade.php --}}
@foreach ($orders as $order)     {{-- ONE query for the orders --}}
    <tr>
        <td>{{ $order->customer->name }}</td>   {{-- then ONE PER ROW,
                                                       because
                                                       customer was
                                                       not loaded --}}
        <td>{{ $order->lines->sum('total') }}</td>  {{-- and lines
                                                            too --}}
    </tr>
@endforeach
{{-- 1 + N + N queries for N rows. At 25 rows that is 51 queries,
     and it grows with the page size. --}}

{{-- THE FIX, and it belongs in the file that BUILT the collection: --}}
// app/Http/Controllers/OrderController.php
public function index(): View
{
    return view('orders.index', [
        // loadMissing, not eagerLoad: withMissing only loads what
        // is not already there, so a caller that pre-loaded
        // something is not overridden and nothing is loaded twice.
        'orders' => Order::query()
            ->with(['customer', 'lines'])
            ->latest()
            ->paginate(25),
    ]);
}
{{-- ^ 3 queries: the page, the customers, the lines. --}}

{{-- MAKE IT IMPOSSIBLE, which is the part that survives: --}}
// app/Providers/AppServiceProvider.php
Model::preventLazyLoading(! app()->isProduction());
{{-- ^ in development: an exception with a stack trace naming the
     template and the line. In production: allowed, because a
     legitimate lazy load on a one-off page should not be a crash.
     The check is that the DEVELOPMENT setting is on in every
     developer's environment — which is why the flag is negated
     rather than read from a config key nobody sets. --}}

{{-- AND THE COLLECTION CASE, which eager loading does not cover: --}}
@foreach ($orders as $order)
    {{ $order->lines->first()->product->name }}   {{-- first() on a
                                                          relation
                                                          still
                                                          queries if
                                                          the
                                                          relation is
                                                          not
                                                          loaded --}}
@endforeach
{{-- ^ eager-load 'lines' AND 'lines.product'. One level of
     eager loading is not recursive. --}}

{{-- AND THE PARTIAL-COUNT CASE, which is invisible in a query log
     because it is one query that is just slow: --}}
$order->lines->count()     {{-- a COUNT on every row, and it is a
                                 different query from loading
                                 lines, so loading does not
                                 remove it --}}
{{-- ^ fix with withCount, which the eager load then satisfies
     from the loaded attribute and no query is issued at all. --}}

15.4 Query patterns for listings

Documents the four query techniques that keep listing pages at a constant query count.

TechniqueQuery countUse for
Eager loadingConstantRelationships read per row
withCountConstantA count displayed per row
Aggregate subqueryConstantA sum or maximum per row
ChunkingBoundedLarge exports within memory limits

Eager loading covers most listings and is the default technique. Aggregates in the query extend it for any value computed from a related collection. Chunking is not a rendering technique, but it is the answer when a report renders more rows than fit inside the configured memory limit.

Account for the paginator. A page of 25 rows across four relationships costs six queries, not five, because pagination adds a count query. That is expected; an extra query is not.

The queries a listing needs, and which of them are its fault

A listing page makes a small number of distinct queries and repeats a few of them per row. The fix for the repeats is eager loading. The fix for the distinct ones is often to accept them — a listing genuinely needs its rows, their owners and their aggregates — and the interesting question is which ones are avoidable by changing the query rather than the template.

The one that is almost always avoidable is the count query a paginator issues alongside the page query. It is a separate query, it is often the slower of the two because it aggregates the whole filtered set rather than one page of it, and it is issued on every request including the ones that render no rows. Every paginated listing should have its numbers measured with the count excluded before deciding the page query is slow.

What belongs in the query rather than in the template

Aggregation, filtering, ordering and the shape of the row. A template that sorts is doing a query's work in PHP after the query has already returned every row — which means transferring every row to decide an order. That is the defect that looks least expensive and costs the most on a large table.


{{-- THE QUERIES A LISTING MAKES, and which are its fault: --}}
// app/Http/Controllers/OrderController.php
public function index(Request $request): View
{
    return view('orders.index', [
        'orders' => Order::query()
            // 1. THE PAGE. Unavoidable.
            ->with(['customer'])            // 2. THE OWNERS.
            ->withCount('lines')           // 3. THE AGGREGATE,
                                            //    and withCount
                                            //    means the template
                                            //    never runs a COUNT
            ->when($request->filled('status'),
                   fn ($q) => $q->where('status', $request->string('status')))
            ->latest()
            // 4. THE PAGINATOR'S OWN COUNT: a SEPARATE query over
            //    the whole filtered set, often slower than the page
            //    query itself.
            ->paginate(25),
    ]);
}
{{-- ^ four queries, none proportional to the page size. This is a
     well-behaved listing. --}}

{{-- THE COUNT IS THE ONE TO MEASURE FIRST, because "the listing is
     slow" is almost never the page query: --}}
it('measures the listing queries', function () {
    DB::enableQueryLog();
    $this->get('/orders')->assertOk();

    $queries = collect(DB::getQueryLog());
    $total   = $queries->sum('time');
    $count   = $queries->firstWhere('query', 'select count(*)')['time'] ?? 0;

    fwrite(STDERR, sprintf("total %d ms, count %d ms (%d%%)
",
        $total, $count, $count / max($total, 1) * 100));

    expect($queries->count())->toBeLessThanOrEqual(5);
});
{{-- ^ if the count is 80% of the time, the fix is not the page
     query: it is an approximate count, a cursor, or accepting it
     and knowing it is there. --}}


{{-- WHAT BELONGS IN THE QUERY RATHER THAN IN THE TEMPLATE, and the
     template version is the defect that LOOKS cheapest because
     there is no query in it: --}}
{{-- WRONG: the template sorts AFTER the query returned everything. --}}
@php
    $rows = Order::with('customer')->get();   {{-- the WHOLE table --}}
    usort($rows, fn ($a, $b) => $b->total->compare($a->total));
@endphp
{{-- ^ transferring every row to PHP to decide an order the database
     could have decided. At 10,000 rows this IS the problem, and it
     looks like nothing because nothing in the template queries. --}}

{{-- RIGHT: the database sorts, and it can use an index. --}}
$rows = Order::query()
    ->with('customer')
    ->orderByDesc('total')          {{-- and with an index on total
                                          this is an index scan
                                          rather than a sort --}}
    ->paginate(25);

{{-- AND THE OTHER TWO THAT LOOK LIKE TEMPLATE WORK: --}}
{{-- WRONG: filtering after the fact --}}
@php
    $rows = Order::all()->filter(fn ($o) => $o->total->isOverThreshold());
@endphp
{{-- ^ Order::all() then a PHP filter: the whole table in memory.
     The threshold belongs in a WHERE clause with an index on it. --}}

{{-- WRONG: the N+1 that a "count" in the template introduces: --}}
{{ $order->lines->count() }}   {{-- a COUNT on every row, and it is
                                    a different query from loading
                                    lines, so with('lines') does not
                                    remove it --}}
{{-- ^ fixed by withCount, which the eager load then satisfies from
     the loaded attribute and no query is issued at all. --}}

{{-- THE THREE THAT BELONG IN THE QUERY: aggregation, filtering and
     ordering. A template that does any of them is doing a query's
     work in PHP after the query has already returned every row. --}}

15.5 Include and component overhead

Quantifies the per-render cost of includes and components and identifies when extraction is a net loss.

An include compiles to a nested render call, and a component adds data extraction and attribute handling on top. Both costs are small in absolute terms: microseconds per render. The measurable problem is repetition at scale, where a component used three hundred times on one page multiplies its cost by three hundred.

What an include costs

An include is a file read — usually from the compiled cache, so a stat and an include — plus a data merge and a buffer. That is tens of microseconds. A component is more: a tag is compiled to a class resolution, a container resolution, a constructor call, a view render and a buffer. Still tens of microseconds, and still rarely the reason a page is slow.

What matters is the multiplier. A partial that includes three partials inside a loop of twenty-five rows is a hundred and fifty renders, and each of those carries its own data merge. That is the shape worth finding: a small cost multiplied by a number derived from the data. It is also the reason that splitting a template into partials improves readability with no measurable effect on speed — a fact worth knowing before anyone optimises the wrong thing.

The exception worth naming

The case where a template fragment is genuinely expensive is not the include — it is what the fragment does. A partial that formats currency by loading a locale table from a database, or that runs a query, is expensive, and the fix is to move that work out rather than to reduce the number of includes.

{{-- Three hundred instances on one page: hoist the work. --}}
@foreach ($lines as $line)
    <x-money :amount="$line->amount" />
@endforeach

{{-- Same total, one pass. --}}
<span>{{ $lines->sum('amount') }}</span>

Hoist invariants — totals, counts, formatted strings used more than once — out of the loop and compute them once in the controller.


{{-- WHAT AN INCLUDE COSTS, which is a file read plus a data merge
     plus a buffer: --}}
// storage/framework/views/<hash>.php — what an include compiles to
make('partials.order-row', ['order' => $order],
                        IlluminateSupportArr::except(get_defined_vars(),
                                                       ['__data', '__path']))->render(); ?>

{{-- AND A COMPONENT, which adds a class resolution and a container
     resolution on top: --}}
make('components.order-row', ['order' => $order],
                        IlluminateSupportArr::except(get_defined_vars(),
                                                       ['__data', '__path']))->render(); ?>

{{-- BOTH ARE TENS OF MICROSECONDS. Neither is why a page is
     slow. --}}

{{-- WHAT MATTERS IS THE MULTIPLIER, and this is the shape worth
     finding: --}}
@foreach ($orders as $order)
    @include('partials.order-row')
    {{-- ...and partials/order-row.blade.php includes three more
         partials. 25 rows x 4 renders = 100 renders, each with its
         own data merge. --}}
@endforeach

{{-- WHICH IS ALSO the reason splitting a template into partials
     improves readability with NO measurable effect on speed — a
     fact worth knowing before anyone optimises the wrong thing. --}}

{{-- SO THE RIGHT QUESTION IS NOT "how many includes" but "what
     does this fragment DO": --}}
{{-- resources/views/partials/money.blade.php --}}
{{-- WRONG: a partial that is expensive, and the fix is to move
     the work out rather than to reduce the number of includes. --}}
{{ AppSupportMoney::format($amount) }}

{{-- RIGHT: the formatting is pure and cheap; the expensive thing
     was the locale table, and it is loaded once. --}}
{{ AppSupportMoney::format($amount) }}

{{-- Measure it, because the answer is not obvious: --}}
it('counts renders per page', function () {
    DB::enableQueryLog();
    $this->get('/orders')->assertOk();

    // A listing whose render count scales with its ROW COUNT has a
    // multiplier problem, whatever the absolute number is.
    expect(count(DB::getQueryLog()))->toBeLessThanOrEqual(10);
});

it('the partial itself is cheap', function () {
    $html = View::make('partials.order-row', ['order' => $order])->render();

    expect(strlen($html))->toBeLessThan(2000);
    // A partial producing 2 KB of markup is fine. One producing
    // 20 KB is doing something else and will be noticed in a
    // profile.
});

15.6 Measuring render time

Documents the profiling tools, the metric that matters and the query threshold that indicates a problem.

Laravel Debugbar records view rendering time per template and the query count attributable to each. Beyond that, the framework’s own timing helpers and a query listener answer most questions:

Measure the render, and only then look at the template

A render timer around the whole call cannot distinguish a slow template from a slow query behind it, which is why it is often the wrong first measurement. The useful first measurement is narrower: how long the view takes once the data is in memory, and how many queries the render issued. Those two numbers together say which half of the problem to look at.

The second measurement is per-template, and it is what turns "the dashboard is slow" into a specific template. A render can be slow because of one expensive fragment repeated across rows, and the per-template figure is the only measurement that shows that.

What to compare against

The number matters less than the change. A render that grew from forty milliseconds to three hundred is a regression regardless of the absolute figures, and a render that has always been four hundred milliseconds may be a page nobody uses. Recording the number in a test turns both into something a change can be judged against.

// Count queries on one request.
DB::listen(function ($query) {
    Log::debug($query->toSql(), $query->bindings);
});

// Time a render in isolation.
$html = view('orders.index', $data)->render();

The threshold that matters is whether the query count scales with the number of rows. A count that grows linearly with result size is an N+1 regardless of its absolute value; a constant count is the signal that eager loading is correct.

Exercises 15
