  1. Record the query count for a listing page at 10, 50 and 200 rows and identify the scaling behaviour.
  2. Replace two per-row aggregates with a subquery and confirm the count becomes constant.
  3. Hoist a repeated formatted value out of a loop and compare render time over five hundred iterations.
  4. Run view:cache in a build pipeline and measure first-request latency against an unoptimised release.

{{-- THE FIRST MEASUREMENT, and it is the one that says which half
     of the problem to look at: --}}
// app/Providers/AppServiceProvider.php
View::composer('*', function ($view) {
    $view->with(['__render_start' => hrtime(true)]);
});

View::composer('*', function ($view) {
    $data = $view->getData();
    $ms = (hrtime(true) - $data['__render_start']) / 1e6;

    if ($ms > 50) {          {{-- A THRESHOLD, so this is data
                                     rather than noise --}}
        Log::notice('slow view', [
            'view'    => $view->name(),
            'ms'      => round($ms, 1),
            'queries' => count(DB::getQueryLog()),
            // ^ THE QUERY COUNT BESIDE THE TIME. A 40 ms render
            //   with one query is fine. A 12 ms render with 300
            //   queries is a page that will be slow on a different
            //   database, and the time alone does not show it.
            'user'    => auth()->id(),      // for a report you can
                                            // reproduce
        ]);
    }
});

{{-- THE SECOND MEASUREMENT: per template, which turns "the
     dashboard is slow" into a specific file. --}}
// A command that renders every view and reports the slowest.
Artisan::command('views:profile {filter?}', function (?string $filter = null) {
    $rows = [];

    foreach (View::getFinder()->getPaths() as $dir) {
        foreach (new RecursiveIteratorIterator(
                     new RecursiveDirectoryIterator($dir)) as $file) {
            if ($file->getExtension() !== 'php') continue;

            $rel  = Str::after($file->getPathname(), $dir . DIRECTORY_SEPARATOR);
            $name = str_replace(['/', '\', '.blade.php'], ['.', '.', ''], $rel);
            if ($filter && ! str_contains($name, $filter)) continue;

            $t0 = hrtime(true);
            try {
                View::make($name)->render();
            } catch (Throwable $e) {
                $rows[] = [$name, '-', class_basename($e)];
                continue;
            }
            $rows[] = [$name, round((hrtime(true) - $t0) / 1e6, 2), 'ok'];
        }
    }

    usort($rows, fn ($a, $b) => ($b[1] ?? 0) <=> ($a[1] ?? 0));
    $this->table(['view', 'ms', 'status'], array_slice($rows, 0, 20));
});

{{-- AND WHAT TO COMPARE AGAINST. The number matters less than the
     CHANGE: --}}
// A render that grew from 40 ms to 300 ms is a regression regardless
// of the absolute figures, and a render that has always been 400 ms
// may be a page nobody uses.
it('renders the dashboard within budget', function () {
    $start = hrtime(true);
    $this->get('/dashboard')->assertOk();
    $ms = (hrtime(true) - $start) / 1e6;

    // Slack for CI, which is slower than a laptop. The point is
    // that a change which turns 40 ms into 300 ms fails a test
    // rather than being noticed six weeks later.
    expect($ms)->toBeLessThan(400);
});

{{-- AND THE MEASUREMENT THAT LOCATES THE COST, which is the one
     that answers "is it the template or the query": --}}
// bootstrap/app.php
DB::listen(function ($query) {
    // Only slow queries, and only outside production.
    if ($query->time >= 50 && ! app()->isProduction()) {
        Log::notice('slow query', [
            'sql'  => $query->sql,
            'ms'   => $query->time,
            'bindings' => $query->bindings,
            // ^ THE BINDINGS, which is where the parameter that made
            //   it slow is. A query that is slow with a 3-character
            //   parameter is slow for a reason the SQL alone does
            //   not show.
        ]);
    }
});

16 · Caching Fragments: When a Cached Block Is Correct

16.1 The cache directive16.2 Cache keys and dependencies16.3 When fragment caching is correct16.4 When fragment caching is wrong16.5 TTLs, eviction and stampede16.6 Alternatives to fragment caching

Caching a rendered fragment trades freshness for throughput, and the trade is correct only when the fragment is identical for every request that shares the key. This chapter documents the directive and the conditions under which it is safe.

16.1 The cache directive

Documents the cache block syntax, its default key and the parameters available for expiry and tagging.

Caching a fragment, and what the key has to include

The cache directive renders its body once and stores the resulting HTML, keyed by whatever you name. The mechanism is straightforward; everything worth knowing is about the key. A key that does not include everything the fragment depends on produces a page that is correct for the first visitor and wrong for every one after, and the failure is silent.

So the key must include every input the fragment reads: the identifiers of the records it displays, the locale, the authenticated user's permissions if the fragment varies by them, and anything from configuration that affects its output. That list is longer than it first appears, which is why the useful practice is to derive the key from a value the component already has rather than from a hand-written string.

When a fragment is a good candidate

When it is expensive and its inputs change rarely. A footer with a link list, a marketing banner, a rendered markdown document. It is a bad candidate when its inputs change per request — a user's name in a header is per request, and caching it means one user sees another user's name.

@cache('nav.categories')
    <nav>
        @foreach ($categories as $category)
            <a href="{{ route('categories.show', $category) }}">
                {{ $category->name }}
            </a>
        @endforeach
    </nav>
@endcache

@cache('nav.categories', 3600)
    ...
@endcache

@cache('panel.stats', ['team' => $team->id], 300)
    ...
@endcache

The block key is the first argument. A second argument sets the TTL in seconds; a third supplies the parameters bound into the key, so the same block with different parameters yields different entries. Without a TTL the entry does not expire.

Scope leaks silently. A block rendered for one user and served to another leaks data across accounts. Before caching any fragment, list every variable it reads and confirm each is either in the key or genuinely identical for every reader.


{{-- THE MECHANISM, and everything worth knowing is about the KEY: --}}
@cache('nav.footer', 3600)
    <nav>
        @foreach ($categories as $category)
            <a href="{{ route('categories.show', $category) }}">{{ $category->name }}</a>
        @endforeach
    </nav>
@endcache
{{-- ^ keyed by a string and a TTL. Renders once, then serves the
     stored HTML until the key expires. --}}

{{-- A KEY THAT DOESN'T INCLUDE ITS INPUTS, which is the bug the
     mechanism invites: --}}
@cache('order-summary', 300)
    <p>{{ $order->customer->name }} — {{ $order->total->format() }}</p>
@endcache
{{-- ^ correct for the FIRST visitor and wrong for every one after.
     Every order shares the key, so the second customer sees the
     first customer's name and the first customer's total. Silent,
     because nothing failed. --}}

{{-- AND THE KEY WITH THE INPUTS, and the version worth copying
     because the inputs are DERIVED from what the fragment reads
     rather than hand-listed: --}}
@php
    // Everything the fragment below reads, and nothing else:
    // the order, its customer's name, the locale, and the currency
    // formatting depends on.
    $key = 'order-summary:' . $order->id
         . ':' . $order->updated_at->timestamp
         . ':' . $order->customer->name
         . ':' . app()->getLocale();
@endphp

@cache($key, 300)
    <p>{{ $order->customer->name }} — {{ $order->total->format() }}</p>
@endcache
{{-- ^ the updated_at makes it invalidate when the order changes,
     without a cache-invalidation call anywhere. The name and the
     locale are in because the fragment displays them. --}}

{{-- AND WHEN A FRAGMENT IS A GOOD CANDIDATE: --}}
{{--   expensive to render, inputs change RARELY -> cache it
     cheap, inputs change PER REQUEST          -> do not
     depends on auth()                          -> almost never,
                                                   because the key
                                                   then has to
                                                   include the user
                                                   and every user
                                                   gets their own
                                                   entry, which is
                                                   a cache of size
                                                   n_users --}}

16.2 Cache keys and dependencies

Explains how the key is derived and why a fragment depending on mutable data must be invalidated explicitly.

The generated key combines the block name with the supplied parameters, hashed by the store. Anything the fragment reads that is not in the key is assumed constant, so a fragment displaying a category list stays stale after a rename unless something removes the key.

Dependencies, and the two ways to invalidate

A cached fragment is stale when one of its inputs changes. There are two ways to handle that: put the input's version in the key, so the key changes when the input does; or invalidate explicitly when the input changes. The first needs no cooperation from anything else and is what most applications should use.

The second is worth knowing because it is more efficient — one delete rather than a new entry — and it requires remembering to call it. A forgotten invalidation call is a stale fragment that no key scheme would have saved you from, because the key was correct and the fragment was not refreshed.

What belongs in a key

Everything the fragment reads, and nothing else. The second half is the half people get wrong: including something that changes per request turns one entry into one entry per request, which is a cache with a one-hundred-percent miss rate and the memory cost of a cache. A timestamp that changes every second, a random nonce, an identifier that is unique per user — each of these in a key is a cache that stores every render separately.

$category->save();

Cache::forget('nav.categories');

// Invalidate every key carrying a tag.
Cache::tags(['navigation'])->flush();

Invalidation on write is reliable; a TTL is a fallback. A block cached for an hour with no invalidation path serves stale data for up to an hour after every change — acceptable for a marketing banner, unacceptable for a price.

Cache the data, not the markup. Caching with Cache::remember() in the controller keeps the fragment cacheable regardless of layout and keeps invalidation tied to the model rather than to a template file.


{{-- (1) VERSION IN THE KEY. No cooperation needed, and it is what
     most applications should use. --}}
@php
    $key = 'invoice:' . $invoice->id
         . ':' . $invoice->updated_at->getTimestamp();
@endphp

@cache($key, 3600)
    <x-invoice :invoice="$invoice" />
@endcache
{{-- ^ the updated_at changes whenever the invoice does, so the key
     changes with it and the old entry is simply never read again.
     Nothing has to remember to invalidate anything. --}}

{{-- (2) EXPLICIT INVALIDATION, which is more efficient — one delete
     rather than a new entry — and requires remembering: --}}
// app/Observers/InvoiceObserver.php
final class InvoiceObserver
{
    public function updated(Invoice $invoice): void
    {
        Cache::forget('invoice:' . $invoice->id . ':1');
        // ^ a FORGOTTEN CALL is a stale fragment that no key scheme
        //   would have saved you from, because the key was correct
        //   and the fragment was never refreshed. --}
    }
}

{{-- (3) TAGGED CACHES, which is the answer to "one fragment out of
     four hundred is wrong": --}}
// app/Providers/AppServiceProvider.php
Cache::tags(['invoices'])->remember('list', 3600, fn () =>
    Invoice::query()->latest()->limit(20)->get());

// When ONE invoice changes:
Cache::tags(['invoices'])->flush();
{{-- ^ removes every invoice fragment in one call. The cost is that
     a tagged cache is not shared across machines on a file or
     database driver, so this only works on a store that supports
     tags — Redis, Memcached, or DynamoDB. --}}

{{-- WHAT BELONGS IN A KEY, and what does not: --}}
@php
    // GOOD: the identifiers and versions of what the fragment
    // actually reads.
    $key = 'invoice:' . $invoice->id . ':' . $invoice->updated_at->timestamp;
@endphp

@php
    // BAD: a value that changes on every render. One entry per
    // render, which is a cache with a 100% miss rate and the memory
    // cost of a cache.
    $key = 'invoice:' . $invoice->id . ':' . microtime();
@endphp

@php
    // BAD: a per-request identifier. One entry per user, which is a
    // cache of size n_users — and if the fragment does not actually
    // vary by user, every user's copy is identical.
    $key = 'dashboard:' . auth()->id();
@endphp

{{-- THE RULE: everything the fragment reads, and nothing else. The
     second half is the half people get wrong. --}}

16.3 When fragment caching is correct

Enumerates the properties a fragment must have for its cached output to be indistinguishable from a freshly rendered one.

Fragment caching is correct when all of the following hold:

  • The output depends only on data captured in the cache key.
  • That data changes rarely enough that staleness is acceptable for the TTL.
  • The fragment contains no user-specific content: no name, no permission state, no CSRF token, no flash data.
  • Invalidation on write exists, or the TTL is short enough to be the policy.
  • The fragment is worth the complexity — typically a navigation tree, a static footer, a category list.

Note the third condition. A fragment rendering a CSRF token or an error summary is uncacheable by construction, however it is keyed.

Layouts stay outside. A cached block that extends a layout would nest whole documents. Cache the partial only and let the layout render around it.

What makes fragment caching correct

Three conditions, and all three must hold. The fragment must be a function of its inputs — given the same key it must produce the same output, every time, on every machine. The inputs must be in the key. And the fragment must not have side effects that something else depends on.

The first condition is the one that is usually violated by accident. A fragment that reads the current time, a random value, the request's hostname, or a counter produces different output for the same key — which means a user who loads the page twice sees two different fragments, and neither is wrong. That is not a cache bug; it is a fragment that was never a pure function.

The cost side, which is often the deciding factor

A cached fragment is a stored string, and storing a thousand of them is a thousand strings in a cache with an eviction policy that may evict the one your next request wants. So fragment caching trades compute for memory, and the trade is only worth making when the compute is large relative to the size. A fragment whose HTML is 400 bytes and whose render takes 30 milliseconds is a good candidate; one whose HTML is 40 KB and whose render takes 2 milliseconds is not.


{{-- THE THREE CONDITIONS, and all three must hold. --}}
@php
    $key = 'nav:' . app()->getLocale() . ':' . $viewer->role->value;
@endphp

@cache($key, 3600)
    <nav>
        @foreach ($navItems as $item)
            <a href="{{ $item->url }}">{{ $item->label }}</a>
        @endforeach
    </nav>
@endcache
{{-- ^ (1) a function of its inputs: navItems, locale and role
     only. (2) all three in the key. (3) no side effects. --}}

{{-- (1) VIOLATED BY ACCIDENT, and the usual culprit is time: --}}
@cache('footer', 3600)
    <footer>(c) {{ now()->year }} {{ config('app.name') }}</footer>
@endcache
{{-- ^ now() is read INSIDE the fragment. The first render stores a
     year; the cached copy is served until the TTL expires, so the
     footer says the wrong year for up to an hour — and nobody ever
     sees a bug, because both years look plausible. --}}

{{-- THE FIX: everything the fragment reads is OUTSIDE it, and the
     time is in the key. --}}
@php
    $year = now()->year;
    $key  = 'footer:' . $year;
@endphp

@cache($key, 86400)
    <footer>(c) {{ $year }} {{ config('app.name') }}</footer>
@endcache
{{-- ^ now() is called once per request to build the key, and the
     FRAGMENT reads only the value. Two users loading on the same
     day share the entry; a user on 1 January gets a new one. --}}


{{-- THE OTHER IMPURE READS, all with the same effect: --}}
@cache('welcome', 600)
    <p>Hello, {{ auth()->user()->name }}. Your session started
       {{ time() }}.</p>
@endcache
{{-- ^ three impure reads in one fragment: the user (so every user
     shares one entry), the clock, and a session id that changes
     constantly. All three are invisible and all three are wrong. --}}

{{-- AND THE COST SIDE, which is often the deciding factor: --}}
{{--   400 bytes of HTML, 30 ms to render   -> cache it
     40 KB of HTML, 2 ms to render         -> do not: the memory and
                                              the eviction pressure
                                              cost more than the
                                              compute you saved
     depends on auth()                      -> one entry per user,
                                              which is a cache of
                                              size n_users --}}

{{-- THE MEASUREMENT, because "expensive" is a number and this is
     how to get it: --}}
it('a cached fragment is worth caching', function () {
    $view = View::make('partials.expensive-fragment', sample_data());

    // The render, timed.
    $t0 = hrtime(true);
    $html = $view->render();
    $renderMs = (hrtime(true) - $t0) / 1e6;

    // And the SIZE, which is the number people forget. 40 KB of
    // stored HTML is a different proposition from 400 bytes.
    $kb = strlen($html) / 1024;

    // The ratio decides it. Below roughly 20:1 the memory and
    // eviction cost is real and the caching is marginal.
    $ratio = $renderMs / max($kb, 0.001);

    expect($ratio)->toBeGreaterThan(20,
        sprintf('render %.1f ms for %.1f KB — ratio %.0f:1',
            $renderMs, $kb, $ratio));
});

{{-- SO THE RULE: cache when the compute is large relative to the
     size, and when the inputs are in the key. Everything else is
     a cache that costs memory and evicts entries. --}}

16.4 When fragment caching is wrong

Lists the fragments that must never be cached, and explains the failure each produces.

FragmentFailure
Anything reading auth()One user sees another’s interface
Error summary or validation outputErrors leak between unrelated submissions
Anything using old()One user’s input appears in another’s form
Fragment with an unbounded keyCache grows without limit and evicts everything
Fragment that must be real-timeCorrectness requirement, not a preference

The third row is the most frequently violated. Because old() reads the flashed session input, a cached form fragment shows the previous submitter’s values to the next visitor.

What the four have in common

Every one of them produces HTML that looks correct to the person looking at it. The first shows a user's name — the wrong one, but a name. The second shows a form that works. The third shows a document that reads correctly and is out of date. And the fourth shows a fragment that is fresh every time. There is no error, no warning, and no exception, which is why all four survive a review and reach production.

Why the second is intermittent and the others are not

A side effect inside a cached fragment happens once — for whoever loaded the page first — and never again. So the analytics report is correct for one impression per hour and wrong for every other, and the error is invisible because the number that is wrong is an aggregate. A stale document is wrong for everyone and consistently wrong, which is easier to notice. The consistency difference is worth knowing: an intermittent defect is usually an aggregate of a per-request one.

The review question, which is four yes-or-no questions

Does the fragment read the authenticated user? Does it have a side effect? Does it read a configuration value? And does its key vary per request? Three of the four have one answer that is wrong and one that is right, and the right answer is always the same: move the read outside the fragment and put its value in the key. The fourth is wrong either way — a key that varies per request is not a cache, it is a place to store things.

Four ways fragment caching goes wrong

Caching something that varies per request, so one user sees another's data. Caching something with a side effect, so an action happens once and then never again. Caching something with a build-time input, so a deploy does not change it until the TTL expires. And caching something whose key is too broad, so every user shares one entry.

The first is a security defect and it is the one worth checking for. The second is a correctness defect that appears intermittently, because the side effect happened for whoever loaded the page first. The third is a deploy problem that looks like a caching problem. And the fourth is the one that produces a cache with a hundred-percent miss rate while reporting a hit rate of zero and being blamed for nothing.

What to do instead

Each of the four has a different answer. Per-request data is not cached at all, or is cached under a key that includes the user's identity — which is the same as not caching it, more expensively. Side effects happen before the cache boundary. Build-time inputs are part of the key. And a key that varies per request is not a key.


{{-- (1) PER-REQUEST DATA. A security defect, and the one worth
     checking for. --}}
@cache('dashboard-welcome', 3600)
    <h1>Welcome back, {{ auth()->user()->name }}</h1>
@endcache
{{-- ^ the first user to load the page in an hour populates the
     entry, and every user after sees THEIR name. --}}

{{-- THE FIX IS A KEY THAT INCLUDES THE USER, and the cost of that
     is the point: --}}
@php
    $key = 'dashboard-welcome:' . auth()->id();
@endphp

@cache($key, 3600)
    <h1>Welcome back, {{ auth()->user()->name }}</h1>
@endcache
{{-- ^ one entry per user. For ten thousand users that is four
     megabytes and a cache with a 100% miss rate, because every
     request is a first request for that user. Which is worse than
     not caching. --}}

{{-- (2) SIDE EFFECTS, a correctness defect that appears
     INTERMITTENTLY: --}}
@cache('newsletter-impression', 3600)
    @php AppServicesAnalytics::track('newsletter_impression'); @endphp
    <x-newsletter-signup />
@endcache
{{-- ^ the analytics call happens ONCE — for whoever loaded the page
     first — and never again. The report says one impression per
     hour rather than one per view, and nobody can work out why. --}}


{{-- THE FIX: the side effect happens BEFORE the cache boundary. --}}
@php
    AppServicesAnalytics::track('newsletter_impression');
@endphp

@cache('newsletter-form', 3600)
    <x-newsletter-signup />
@endcache
{{-- ^ the tracked impression is now per request and the FORM is
     cached, which is what was wanted. --}}

{{-- (3) BUILD-TIME INPUTS, a deploy problem that looks like a
     caching problem: --}}
@cache('terms', 604800)
    <x-legal-terms :version="config('app.terms_version')" />
@endcache
{{-- ^ config() is read INSIDE the fragment. Deploy a new terms
     version and every cached copy serves the OLD one for a week.
     The deploy succeeded and the page is wrong. --}}

@php
    // The version is in the KEY, so a deploy changes the key.
    $key = 'terms:' . config('app.terms_version');
@endphp

@cache($key, 604800)
    <x-legal-terms :version="config('app.terms_version')" />
@endcache

{{-- (4) A KEY THAT VARIES PER REQUEST: --}}
@php
    $key = 'panel:' . microtime(true) . ':' . request()->header('x-request-id');
@endphp
{{-- ^ one entry per render. A cache with a 100% miss rate that
     reports itself as working, holding every fragment ever rendered
     until the eviction policy removes them. --}}

{{-- THE FOUR, AS A CHECKLIST FOR A REVIEW: --}}
{{--   does it read auth()?              -> the key needs the user,
                                           or do not cache
   does it have a side effect?           -> move it outside
   does it read config()?                -> the config key belongs
                                           in the cache key
   does its key vary per request?        -> it is not a cache --}}

16.5 TTLs, eviction and stampede

Discusses how to choose an expiry, how to keep cache size bounded, and the stampede problem at expiry.

Choose a TTL from the rate of change, not from a default: a marketing block may cache for a day, a feature flag for a minute. When the number of possible keys is large — one per user, per tenant, per filter combination — an unbounded cache will evict the working set; bound it with a prefix and an explicit eviction policy rather than a TTL alone.

At expiry every concurrent request may miss simultaneously and each render the fragment, which is a cache stampede. The store’s atomic lock or the atomic counter facility prevents this: the first process acquires the lock and renders, the others wait or serve the previous value.

Never cache error responses. A fragment that throws while the cache is cold can be stored by a naive wrapper. Ensure the cached path cannot persist a 500, and warm the cache during the build where possible.

Three numbers, and they answer different questions

A time to live says how long an entry may be served. It is the staleness bound and it is the only one of the three that is about correctness. An eviction policy says what happens when the store is full: least recently used, least frequently used, or a random sample. It is about memory. And a stampede protection says what happens when an entry expires and a thousand requests arrive at once.

The third is the one that bites. Without protection, an entry expiring under load produces a thundering herd: every request misses, every request recomputes, and the thing that was cached precisely because it was expensive is now computed a thousand times at once — which is worse than never caching it. The fix is deliberately slow: on a miss, compute the value, and if another process has computed it in the meantime, use theirs.

What to choose for the time to live

The staleness the fragment's consumers can tolerate, not a round number. A price display tolerates seconds; a legal document tolerates a deploy. And the key already handles the case where an input changes — so the time to live is only covering the inputs the key does not know about, which is usually a change made by an administrator rather than by a user.


{{-- THE THREE NUMBERS, and they answer different questions. --}}

{{-- 1. THE TTL IS THE STALENESS BOUND. It is the only one of the
     three that is about CORRECTNESS. --}}
@cache($key, 300)     {{-- five minutes of staleness --}}

{{-- 2. THE EVICTION POLICY is about MEMORY, and it is a store
     configuration rather than a call-site decision: --}}
// config/database.php — for a redis store
'options' => [
    'default' => [
        'maxmemory'        => '256mb',
        // LEAST RECENTLY USED. The alternative is least frequently
        // used, which keeps entries nobody has touched for a month
        // and evicts the ones a busy page needs.
        'maxmemory-policy' => 'allkeys-lru',
    ],
],
{{-- ^ allkeys rather than volatilekeys: with volatilekeys an entry
     is removed only when it EXPIRES, so an entry with a long TTL
     is never evicted under pressure. For a cache — where every
     entry is reconstructible — allkeys is correct. --}}

{{-- 3. STAMPEDE PROTECTION, which is the one that bites: --}}
{{-- WITHOUT IT: an entry expires under load, every request misses,
     every request recomputes, and the thing cached BECAUSE it was
     expensive is now computed a thousand times at once. That is
     worse than never caching it. --}}


{{-- THE FIX, and it is deliberately slow: on a miss, compute the
     value, and if another process has computed it in the meantime,
     use theirs. --}}
// app/Support/CacheStampede.php
final class CacheStampede
{
    public static function remember(
        string $key, int $ttl, Closure $compute
    ): mixed {
        if (($value = Cache::get($key)) !== null) return $value;

        // A LOCK with a SHORT TTL, so a holder that crashes does not
        // block the key for the rest of its life.
        if (! Cache::add('lock:' . $key, true, 10)) {

            // SOMEONE ELSE IS COMPUTING IT. Wait briefly for their
            // result rather than computing it ourselves — and the
            // wait is deliberately SHORT, so a compute that fails
            // does not become a hang.
            for ($i = 0; $i < 20; $i++) {
                usleep(50_000);                   // 50 ms
                if (($value = Cache::get($key)) !== null) return $value;
            }

            // Their compute failed or is very slow. Fall through
            // and do the work ourselves rather than erroring.
        }

        $value = $compute();
        Cache::put($key, $value, $ttl);
        Cache::forget('lock:' . $key);

        return $value;
    }
}

@php($tree = AppSupportCacheStampede::remember(
    'nav:categories', 3600,
    fn () => Category::with('children')->get()->toTree(),
))
{{-- ^ one process computes; the other nine hundred wait fifty
     milliseconds and read its result. --}}

{{-- WHAT TO CHOOSE FOR THE TTL, and it is not a round number: --}}
{{--   the staleness the fragment's CONSUMERS can tolerate,
     not a round number.
     A price display tolerates seconds.
     A legal document tolerates a deploy.
     The key already handles an input changing, so the TTL is only
     covering the inputs the key does NOT know about — usually a
     change made by an administrator rather than by a user. --}}

16.6 Alternatives to fragment caching

Compares the caching strategies available in a Laravel application and the situation each addresses best.

TechniqueGranularityPrefer when
remember() in controllerQuery resultData is slow, markup is not
@cache fragmentRendered HTMLMarkup itself is expensive
Model observer to bust keysInvalidationData changes at runtime
Full-page HTTP cacheWhole documentPublic pages, CDN in front
Octane or opcacheProcessAlways — it removes boot cost

The usual correct answer is to cache the query in the controller and let the template render from memory. Fragment caching adds a second invalidation surface tied to markup; reserve it for the cases where the render itself is genuinely expensive.

Exercises 16

  1. Cache a navigation fragment with an explicit invalidation call in a model observer and confirm updates appear immediately.
  2. Demonstrate the cross-user leak by caching a fragment that reads the authenticated user, then fix it.
  3. Bound a per-user cached fragment with a prefix and an eviction policy, and measure the effect on hit rate.
  4. Compare total response time for a fragment-cached page and a controller-cached page, and explain any difference.

Four alternatives, and which one the problem actually calls for

Fragment caching is the most powerful tool in this area and the most likely to be the wrong one. Four alternatives cover most of what people reach for it for: eager-loading the data, caching the query rather than the HTML, caching at the HTTP layer, and making the fragment cheaper.

The choice is usually determined by what makes the fragment slow. If it is slow because it queries, cache the query — the fragment stays dynamic and the database does less work. If it is slow because the HTML is large, cache at the HTTP layer and let a CDN hold the whole page. If it is slow because the same computation runs a thousand times in a loop, make it run once. And if it is slow because it does genuinely expensive work that depends on data which changes rarely, fragment caching is right and nothing else is.

The question to ask first

What would have to change for this to be fast? If the answer is "nothing, it is already doing the minimum work", then the cost is inherent and the only lever is to do it less often — which means caching at whatever layer lets you skip the most work for the least risk. If the answer is "it should not be doing that", then caching hides a defect rather than fixing one.


{{-- (1) EAGER-LOADING. The fix when a fragment is slow because it
     QUERIES, and it is the right one: the fragment stays dynamic
     and the database does less work. --}}
{{-- WRONG: a fragment that queries per row --}}
{{ $order->customer->name }}      {{-- one query per row --}}

{{-- RIGHT: eager-load in the query that BUILT the collection --}}
'orders' => Order::query()->with('customer')->paginate(25),
{{-- ^ 2 queries instead of 26, and the fragment is now fast
     enough that it does not need caching at all. --}}

{{-- (2) CACHE THE QUERY, not the HTML. The fragment stays
     dynamic; the database does less work. --}}
$categories = Cache::remember('nav:categories', 3600,
    fn () => Category::with('children')->get());

{{-- THE DIFFERENCE, and it is why this is usually preferable: a
     cached query produces FRESH HTML with the fragment's own logic
     still applied. A cached fragment produces HTML that has to be
     discarded whenever the fragment's logic changes — so every
     change to the fragment costs every user a cache miss. --}}

{{-- (3) CACHE AT THE HTTP LAYER, when the whole PAGE is cacheable
     and the HTML is large. --}}
Route::get('/about', [AboutController::class, 'index'])
    ->middleware(['cache.headers:public;max_age=3600']);
{{-- ^ a CDN holds the whole page, so the origin sees one request
     per edge per hour rather than one per visitor. The right tool
     when the page is the same for everyone and the response is
     large — a marketing page, not an order. --}}


{{-- (4) MAKE THE FRAGMENT CHEAPER, when the same computation
     runs in a loop. --}}
{{-- WRONG: --}}
@foreach ($orders as $order)
    {{ AppSupportMoney::format($order->total) }}   {{-- the
         locale table is loaded on EVERY call --}}
@endforeach

{{-- RIGHT: --}}
@php
    $format = AppSupportMoney::formatter(app()->getLocale());
@endphp
@foreach ($orders as $order)
    {{ $format($order->total) }}
@endforeach
{{-- ^ the locale table is loaded once per render rather than once
     per row. No cache, no staleness, and the cost is proportional
     to the page rather than to the page times the rows. --}}

{{-- THE QUESTION TO ASK FIRST: what would have to change for this
     to be fast? --}}
{{--   "it should not be doing that"  -> fix it. Caching HIDES a
                                          defect rather than fixing
                                          one, and the defect is
                                          still there for the next
                                          caller.
   "it is already doing the minimum" -> the cost is INHERENT, and
                                          the only lever is to do
                                          it less often, which means
                                          caching at whichever
                                          layer skips the most work
                                          for the least risk. --}}

{{-- AND THE DECISION, AS A TABLE: --}}
{{--   slow because it queries      -> eager-load. Cheapest, no
                                       staleness, and the fragment
                                       stays dynamic.
   slow because the same computation runs per row
                                    -> hoist it out of the loop.
   slow because the data changes rarely AND the computation is large
                                    -> cache the QUERY, which keeps
                                       the fragment's logic live.
   slow because the output is large and identical for everyone
                                    -> cache at the HTTP layer.
   slow because it does genuinely expensive work on rarely-changing
     data                          -> cache the FRAGMENT. This is
                                       the only case where the last
                                       option is right. --}}

17 · Security: Unescaped Output and How to Audit It

17.1 The default-safe invariant17.2 Untrusted data sources17.3 Legitimate raw output17.4 Sanitising on write17.5 Attribute and URL injection17.6 Auditing a template

Blade escapes by default, which makes a safe template the default outcome and an unsafe one a deliberate act. This chapter documents the threat model, the legitimate uses of raw output, and a repeatable audit procedure.

17.1 The default-safe invariant

Restates the compiler guarantee and the exact conditions under which it does not apply.

Blade compiles {{ }} to an escaped echo and {!! !!} to a raw echo. The safe form is therefore the default at the syntax level; producing an injection requires a developer to choose the unsafe form or to bypass the escaping helper.

The invariant, stated as a rule rather than a hope

An invariant is a property that holds at every point where the program can be observed. In a template the observable points are narrow — the moment the browser receives the output — and that narrowness is what makes an invariant possible: nothing the template does is observable until it has finished.

The useful discipline is to write the invariant down as a sentence that a reviewer can check, and then to choose the escaping mechanism that enforces it. Escaping by default is one such choice, and it is worth noticing what makes it work: the safe case requires no decision, so the number of decisions that have to be made correctly is one rather than the number of interpolations.

What the invariant does not cover

It does not cover what the template is given. A value that has been sanitised before it reaches a template is safe by the sanitiser's policy, not by the template's — and a reviewer looking at the template cannot verify the first without reading the second. So the invariant worth stating is about the template's own behaviour, and the sanitisation is a separate claim with a separate owner.

// Escaped by the compiler. Safe for any text content.
{{ $comment->body }}

// Raw echo. Safe only for markup you produced.
{!! $invoice->rendered_html !!}

// Escaping bypassed explicitly.
@php echo $value; @endphp

Three mechanisms bypass the guarantee and all three must be found in review: the raw echo, an explicit echo inside a @php block, and an object whose toHtml() returns unescaped markup. Everything else inherits the invariant.

Htmlable is a trust boundary. Returning an object that implements Htmlable from an accessor silently disables escaping for that value. Treat the implementation as security-relevant code.


{{-- THE INVARIANT, STATED SO A REVIEWER CAN CHECK IT: --}}
<div class="note">
    <strong>Invariant.</strong> Every interpolation in this template
    escapes. The only exceptions are the raw forms listed below, each
    of which names its source and why that source is trusted.
</div>

{{-- WHICH IS CHECKABLE, because it is a property of the file and
     not of the data: --}}
<p>{{ $user->name }}</p>          {{-- escaped --}}
<p>{{ $order->total->format() }}</p>  {{-- escaped --}}

{{-- THE EXCEPTION LIST, and the rule that makes an exception
     reviewable: it NAMES ITS SOURCE. --}}
{!! $invoice->pdfHtml !!}
{{-- Source: InvoiceLine::pdfHtml(), which returns a fragment built
     by AppSupportPdf::render() from a template with no user
     input. Narrowed in section 17.3. --}}

{{-- AND WHAT THE INVARIANT DOES NOT COVER, which is worth being
     explicit about: --}}
{{ $product->description }}
{{-- ^ escaped HERE, and safe here only if the value was escaped
     ON THE WAY IN. The template cannot verify that, and a reviewer
     looking at this line cannot either. So the claim about that
     value belongs to whoever stores it, and section 17.4 is about
     them. --}}

{{-- THE TEST THAT ENFORCES THE INVARIANT, which is worth having
     because a convention is not: --}}
it('every raw interpolation in a view has a justification', function () {
    $unjustified = [];

    foreach (View::getFinder()->getPaths() as $dir) {
        foreach (new RecursiveIteratorIterator(
                     new RecursiveDirectoryIterator($dir)) as $file) {
            if ($file->getExtension() !== 'php') continue;

            $src = file_get_contents($file->getPathname());

            // Every raw form in the file.
            preg_match_all('/{!!(.*?)!!}/s', $src, $raw);

            foreach ($raw[1] as $expression) {
                // Is there a documented source within three lines
                // above it? Not "does it look safe" — a written
                // justification, which is what a reviewer reads.
                $pos  = strpos($src, '{!!' . $expression);
                $near = substr($src, max(0, $pos - 400), $pos);

                if (! preg_match('/Source:|Trusted|sanitis|generated/i', $near)) {
                    $unjustified[] = [
                        Str::after($file->getPathname(), $dir . '/'),
                        trim($expression),
                    };
                }
            }
        }
    }

    expect($unjustified)->toBeEmpty();
});
{{-- ^ the only check that finds a raw form nobody thought about.
     It does not prove the raw form is safe — nothing does — but it
     makes every one of them require a written reason. --}}

17.2 Untrusted data sources

Enumerates the inputs that must be treated as hostile regardless of where they appear in the template.

  • Any field a user submitted, including through an authenticated interface.
  • Request parameters, headers and the path, all of which are attacker-controlled.
  • Flash data, which may itself originate from user input.
  • Third-party content: imported feeds, payment descriptions, review text, uploaded filenames.
  • Model values seeded or imported without validation.

The distinguishing question is not who typed the value but whether an adversary could have. A value only an administrator can enter is still reachable by an attacker who compromises an administrator account or exploits an injection in the admin interface.

Authenticate, do not exempt. "Only staff can post this" is not a sanitisation argument. Treat every stored value as untrusted regardless of the interface that produced it.

Trust is a property of a writer, not a field

The classification people actually want is "is this safe", and the honest answer is that safety is not a property of the value — it is a property of what will be done with it and who else will see it. A user's own name displayed back to that user is harmless; the same value displayed on a page another user reads is not. The variable, its column and its model are identical in both cases.

So the classification has to be made per output rather than per value, and that is why the escaping rule is not "escape untrusted values" but "escape every value, always". The rule has no exceptions to reason about, which is the property that makes it hold on the four hundredth interpolation rather than only on the ones somebody thought about.


{{-- THE FOUR, and they are not equally dangerous. --}}

{{-- (1) A VISITOR TYPING THEIR OWN NAME. Cannot attack anyone but
     themselves — UNLESS the value is displayed to somebody else. --}}
<p>{{ $request->get('name') }}</p>
{{-- ^ safe: it appears on one visitor's page only. What makes a
     value dangerous is not the source but WHERE IT LANDS. --}}

{{-- (2) A USER WRITING A COMMENT. Displayed to OTHER users, so it
     is a stored-XSS vector even though the author is known. --}}
<div class="comment">{{ $comment->body }}</div>
{{-- ^ escaped, which is the whole defence. --}}

{{-- (3) DATABASE CONTENT A USER WROTE AT SOME POINT. The author is
     a customer; the audience is everybody. --}}
<div>{{ $review->body }}</div>
{{-- ^ escaped. The value is a string in the database, which is
     exactly what a reader's comment is, and it is displayed in
     exactly the same place. --}}

{{-- (4) ADMINISTRATOR-WRITTEN CONTENT. Trusted, but the trust is
     ADMINISTRATIVE rather than structural. --}}
{!! $page->body !!}
{{-- ^ a reasonable choice. It stops being one the day an
     administrator account is compromised, or the day a
     non-administrator is given content-editing rights, and nothing
     in the template distinguishes those cases. --}}


{{-- THE CLASSIFICATION IS A TRAP, because it is asked ONCE PER
     VIEW rather than once per VALUE: --}}
@php
    /** @var AppModelsPage $page */
    $page->body;          {{-- trusted, because admins write it --}}

    $page->seo_title;     {{-- ALSO trusted? It was set by a form an
                               admin filled in, and the form
                               validated its length. Fine. --}}

    $page->embed_code;    {{-- ALSO trusted? It is a SCRIPT TAG
                               stored as a string, and any admin can
                               paste one. "Trusted" is doing a lot
                               of work here. --}}
@endphp
{{-- ^ three fields of one model, three different answers, and a
     model-level judgement gets two of them wrong. --}}

{{-- WHICH IS WHY THE FOUR-WAY ANALYSIS BELONGS ON THE VALUE, AND
     WHICH IS WHY IT CANNOT BE WRITTEN DOWN: --}}
@php
    // A per-field declaration, so the judgement is made once per
    // field rather than once per model or once per view.
    // @var 'html:sanitised:admin'  $page->body
    // @var 'text:plain'            $page->seo_title
    // @var 'html:raw:admin'        $page->embed_code
    //          ^ raw and admin-written: trusted only while
    //            content editing is restricted to
    //            administrators, which is a POLICY decision rather
    //            than a fact about the field.
@endphp

{{-- THE ESCAPED OUTPUT MAKES BOTH CASES SAFE WITHOUT DISTINGUISHING
     THEM, which is why the distinction matters for the raw forms
     and not for the rest: --}}
{!! AppSupportHtml::sanitiseRichText($page->body) !!}
{!! AppSupportHtml::allowEmbeds($page->embed_code) !!}
{{-- ^ two sanitiser policies rather than one judgement. The first
     drops scripts; the second permits a script but only from a list
     of known embed hosts. Both are allowlists, so both fail safe
     when the language gains a feature. --}}

17.3 Legitimate raw output

Lists the cases where a raw echo is correct and the obligation each case creates.

ContentRaw echoObligation
Paginator linksCorrectNone — framework output
Validation summaryCorrectNone — framework output
Sanitised rich textCorrectSanitise on write
Markdown rendered on outputCorrectRender with a sanitiser
Comment body from a userIncorrectEscape, or sanitise first
Any value from the requestIncorrectEscape

The pattern that satisfies both requirements is to escape by default and convert to a sanitised Htmlable object explicitly where rich text is produced:

What to do when you find one that is not justified

Remove it. Almost every unjustified raw form exists because somebody needed to output something the escaping would have broken, and the thing they were outputting can nearly always be expressed without markup — a class name, a style, a flag — which is exactly what a class and an attribute are for. A component that needed a raw form to render a background colour needed a class; one that needed it to render a user-supplied style needed a sanitiser.

The exceptions are real and they are worth naming precisely: markup generated by a renderer you control and can test, a translation string from your own language files that contains markup you wrote, and an allowlist sanitiser's output. Each of those has a property that can be checked by reading something other than the template, which is what makes it defensible. Everything else is a raw form that has not yet been given a reason.

The failure mode that is not a raw form

It is worth naming the neighbouring case, because it looks safe and is not. A value is not dangerous because of where it came from; it is dangerous because of what it is. A value that is a URL is dangerous when its scheme is unchecked, regardless of whether an administrator or a visitor typed it — and the whole population of URL values has that property, so a rule about who may write a field does not address it. The four-way classification is about writers, and it says nothing at all about the two injection techniques in the next section, which are properties of the value and not of its provenance.

That is the reconciliation, and it is the reason both halves exist: the classification tells you where to be careful, and the escaping rules tell you what to do about the cases that are dangerous whoever wrote them. Neither substitutes for the other, and a codebase that has only one of them has a class of defect it cannot see.

The number worth keeping

A well-maintained codebase has a handful of raw forms, and the handful is small enough to read aloud in a review. That is the target, and it is worth stating as a number rather than as a principle — because a principle about escaping is either obeyed or not, whereas a count can be watched.

When the count grows, the cause is nearly always the same: a value arrives at a template already containing markup and there is no mechanism that makes it safe to output, so the raw form is used at the point of use. The fix is to add the mechanism rather than to argue about the raw form — a sanitiser with an explicit policy, or a wrapper type that the escaping function recognises. Both move the decision to one place, and the count falls.

What to do when you find one that is not justified

Remove it. Almost every unjustified raw form exists because somebody needed to output something the escaping would have broken, and the thing they were outputting can nearly always be expressed without markup — a class name, a style, a flag — which is exactly what a class and an attribute are for. The exceptions are rare, and each of them wants a written reason.

final readonly class SanitisedHtml implements Htmlable
{
    public function __construct(private string $html) {}

    public function toHtml(): string
    {
        return $this->html;
    }
}

{!! $post->body !!}   {{-- body is a SanitisedHtml --}}

{{-- THE STRUCTURAL FIX, which makes case (2) not need a raw form
     at all: make the trusted value a TYPE. --}}
// app/Support/Html.php
final class Html
{
    /**
     * @param  string  $input  UNTRUSTED
     */
    public static function sanitiseRichText(string $input): SanitisedHtml
    {
        return new SanitisedHtml(
            // AN ALLOWLIST, not a blocklist. A blocklist of tags and
            // attributes has been bypassed repeatedly over the life
            // of this problem; an allowlist fails safe when the
            // standard gains a new tag.
            (new HtmlSanitizer(config('security.sanitiser.allow')))
                ->sanitize($input)
        );
    }
}

final readonly class SanitisedHtml implements Htmlable
{
    public function __construct(private string $html) {}
    public function toHtml(): string { return $this->html; }
}


{{-- AND THEN THE OUTPUT IS THE ORDINARY INTERPOLATION, because the
     escaping function passes a trusted value through unchanged. --}}
{{ $comment->bodyHtml }}
{{-- ^ compiles to e($comment->bodyHtml), and e() returned it
     unchanged because it implements Htmlable. So the escaped form
     is correct whether or not the value was sanitised: a value
     that forgot to be sanitised renders as VISIBLE MARKUP rather
     than executing, which is a cosmetic failure rather than a
     compromise.

     And in this design there is no reason for a raw interpolation
     to exist anywhere in the codebase. --}}

{{-- SO THE RAW FORM SURVIVES ONLY WHERE THE VALUE IS TRUSTED BY A
     DIFFERENT MECHANISM — and each of these is worth naming,
     because they are the cases where the type is not enough: --}}

{{-- (1) MARKUP YOU GENERATED, which is a value a RENDERER
       produced. The mechanism is the renderer, and it is testable:
       a test asserting on its output is a stronger claim than a
       comment saying it is safe. --}}
{!! $chart->toSvg() !!}
{{-- a test: expect($chart->toSvg())->toStartWith('<svg') --}}

{{-- (2) A TRANSLATION FROM YOUR OWN LANGUAGE FILES. The mechanism is
       REVIEW: the strings are in version control and a change to
       one is a diff somebody sees. --}}
{!! __('emails.welcome_body', ['name' => $user->name]) !!}

{{-- (3) A RAW BLOCK WITH NO MECHANISM, which is the case that is
       not worth having: --}}
{!! $user->bio !!}
{{-- ^ nothing here makes the source safe. It works today. The
     failure is scheduled for the day a user is allowed to set a
     bio, or an import is added, or a plugin is installed — and
     none of those is a code change, so none of them is a review. --}}

17.4 Sanitising on write

Documents where sanitisation belongs, why a regular expression is not a sanitiser, and the backfill obligation for existing rows.

Sanitisation belongs on write, in a mutator or a cast, so that every read path — Blade, API, export, email — receives the same value. Applying it in the template leaves every other read path unprotected.

Sanitising on write, and the property it gives you

Storing the sanitised form rather than the submitted one means the work happens once rather than on every render, and it means the output side has nothing to decide. That is a genuine simplification, and it is the reason it is the usual recommendation.

It has a cost that is worth stating before choosing it: the sanitiser's policy becomes permanent. A value stored sanitised by one policy and rendered under a different one is a value that was silently rewritten, and if the policy is later relaxed there is no record that the original text ever existed. For content that is never displayed in a second context — a comment, a review — that is fine. For content that might be exported, re-imported, or displayed in a richer context than the sanitiser knew about, it is not.

The other cost, which is the one that bites

An HTML sanitiser's policy is written against HTML. A value that is not HTML — a CSV cell, a JSON field, a plain-text email body — is mangled by an HTML sanitiser, because it decodes entities and re-encodes them. Applying a markup sanitiser to plain text is a common mistake with a visible symptom and an invisible cause.

final class Post extends Model
{
    protected function casts(): array
    {
        return [
            'body' => static fn (mixed $value): HtmlString =>
                new HtmlString(Purifier::clean((string) $value)),
        ];
    }
}

Sanitisation is an allow-list operation: it removes every element and attribute not explicitly permitted rather than searching for known-bad patterns. Regex approaches fail against event handler attributes, javascript: URLs, CSS expressions and encoding variations.

Backfill first. Rows written before sanitisation existed still contain raw markup. Run the sanitiser over existing content before enabling any raw echo that depends on it.


{{-- SANITISE ON WRITE, and the property it gives: --}}
// app/Http/Controllers/CommentController.php
public function store(StoreCommentRequest $request): RedirectResponse
{
    $comment = $request->user()->comments()->create([
        // SANITISED BEFORE STORAGE. So the output side has nothing
        // to decide, and e() is enough.
        'body' => Html::sanitiseRichText($request->string('body')),
        // ^ the plain-text body is NOT run through the HTML
        //   sanitiser. It is a text field, and an HTML sanitiser
        //   would decode and re-encode its entities — which for a
        //   value that was never markup is mangling, not
        //   sanitising.
        'excerpt' => Str::limit($request->string('body'), 200),
    ]);

    return back();
}

<div>{{ $comment->body }}</div>
{{-- ^ escaped, and it is ALREADY sanitised. Two layers, and the
     outer one is the one that guarantees safety. --}}

{{-- THE COST, and it is worth stating before choosing this: the
     sanitiser's policy becomes PERMANENT. --}}
// A value stored sanitised by one policy and rendered under a
// different one is a value that was silently rewritten. If the
// policy is later relaxed there is NO RECORD that the original text
// ever existed.
Html::sanitiseRichText($input);   {{-- <b>bold</b> kept,
                                         <script> removed --}}
// ...and the original <script> is gone. Not recoverable from the
// database, not in a log, not anywhere.

{{-- SO IT IS FINE FOR CONTENT NEVER DISPLAYED IN A SECOND CONTEXT,
     and not fine otherwise: --}}
{{--   a comment, a review, a bio          -> sanitise on write
   anything exported or re-imported       -> store the original and
                                             sanitise on read
   anything displayed in two different
     contexts                             -> store the original;
                                             sanitise per context --}}

{{-- THE SANITISER'S POLICY, and it is a configuration value rather
     than a decision buried in a controller: --}}
// config/security.php
'sanitiser' => [
    // AN ALLOWLIST, because a blocklist fails open every time the
    // language gains a feature.
    'allow' => [
        'p', 'br', 'strong', 'em', 'a', 'ul', 'ol', 'li',
        'blockquote', 'code', 'pre', 'h2', 'h3', 'h4',
    ],
    'allow_attributes' => [
        'a' => ['href', 'title', 'rel', 'target'],
    ],
    // EVERY LINK GETS rel="noopener", which is not optional:
    // target="_blank" without it hands the opened page a reference
    // to your window.
    'force_attributes' => ['a' => ['rel' => 'noopener noreferrer']],
    // AND THE SCHEME ALLOWLIST on href, which is the XSS that has
    // been in every HTML sanitiser ever written.
    'allow_schemes' => ['http', 'https', 'mailto'],
],
{{-- ^ href="javascript:alert(1)" has no angle brackets and no
     quotes, so no amount of character escaping stops it. It is a
     SCHEME allowlist that stops it, and it has to be there. --}}

17.5 Attribute and URL injection

Covers the contexts where HTML escaping is applied but the value is still dangerous, and the validation each context requires.

The two injections, and neither of them needs an angle bracket

Attribute injection needs a quote character and nothing else. A value that closes the attribute and adds an event handler contains no angle brackets in the position that matters, so an escaper handling only angle brackets passes it through. URL injection needs nothing at all: a scheme is not a character, so a URL of javascript: survives every form of escaping.

Both are prevented the same way, and the way is not an escaping function. The first is prevented by always quoting the attribute and always using the escaped interpolation, because the framework's escape function handles both quote characters regardless of which one delimits the attribute. The second is prevented by validating the URL rather than escaping it, since the dangerous part of a URL is what it points at rather than what it contains.

The third case, which is the one that is missed

An unquoted attribute. A value containing a space becomes two attributes, and a value containing an equals sign supplies its own. It is a defect that survives review because unquoted attributes are rare enough that a reader does not notice one, and it is fixed by a habit rather than by a function.

/* Text and quoted attributes: escaping is sufficient. */
<p>{{ $bio }}</p>
<div title="{{ $bio }}"></div>

/* URL attributes: escaping does not neutralise the scheme. */
<a href="{{ $user->website }}">Site</a>

/* Inline script: use JSON encoding, not interpolation. */
<script>window.user = @js($user);</script>

HTML escaping neutralises <, >, &, " and '. It leaves the javascript: scheme intact, so a value like javascript:alert(1) survives escaping unchanged and remains executable in a link.

Allow-list the scheme. Parse user-supplied URLs with parse_url(), accept only http and https, and fall back to a safe placeholder otherwise. Apply this wherever a URL originates outside your code.


{{-- (1) ATTRIBUTE INJECTION. No angle brackets needed: --}}
<div id='{{ $id }}'>
{{-- ^ SAFE, and it is safe because e() uses ENT_QUOTES and
     escapes BOTH quote characters, whichever one delimits the
     attribute. Most people do not know that, and write their own
     escaper that does not. --}}

<div id={{ $id }}>
{{-- ^ UNQUOTED, and broken three separate ways: "a b" becomes
     two attributes, "a=b" supplies its own, and a quote ends it.
     Always quote. --}}

{{-- (2) URL INJECTION. Needs NOTHING. A scheme is not a
     character, so no amount of escaping stops it: --}}
<a href="{{ $user->website }}">
{{-- ^ a value of javascript:alert(1) contains no angle brackets
     and no quotes. It survives every escaping function there is. --}}

{{-- THE FIX IS VALIDATION, not escaping: --}}
// app/Support/SafeUrl.php
final class SafeUrl
{
    public static function isSafe(?string $url): bool
    {
        if (! $url) return true;              // null is not a URL
        if (str_starts_with($url, '/')) return true;   // relative

        $scheme = strtolower((string) parse_url($url, PHP_URL_SCHEME));

        // A SCHEME ALLOWLIST. Everything else is rejected, which
        // means javascript: and data: and vbscript: are all out
        // without a blocklist of dangerous schemes — a blocklist of
        // schemes fails open every time one is invented.
        return in_array($scheme, ['http', 'https', 'mailto'], true);
    }
}

@if (AppSupportSafeUrl::isSafe($user->website))
    <a href="{{ $user->website }}" rel="noopener noreferrer">
        {{ $user->website }}
    </a>
@endif
{{-- ^ rejected rather than escaped. An escaping function cannot
     help here, which is the point. --}}

{{-- AND THE SANITISER'S href, which has the same hole and the same
     fix: --}}
'sanitiser' => [
    'allow_schemes' => ['http', 'https', 'mailto'],
],

{{-- (3) THE THIRD CASE, which is missed because unquoted attributes
     are rare enough that a reader does not notice one: --}}
<div class={{ $classes }}>   {{-- "btn btn-primary" becomes TWO
                                  attributes --}}
<input value={{ $value }}>   {{-- "a b" becomes a value AND a
                                  second attribute named b --}}

{{-- THE FIX IS A HABIT, not a function: --}}
<div class="{{ $classes }}">
<input value="{{ $value }}">
{{-- ^ always quote, always escaped, and the two together make
     every attribute-injection case impossible. --}}

17.6 Auditing a template

Provides a repeatable review procedure and a mechanical search that finds candidate raw-echo sites.

Auditing, and the question of what a grep finds

A search for the raw interpolation form finds every instance in the codebase, and that is the property worth relying on. The audit is not the search — it is reading the results and deciding, per instance, whether the justification is real. A codebase with four raw forms can be read in a minute; a codebase with four hundred cannot be, and the four hundred are the reason nobody reads them.

So the audit has a second half, which is reducing the count. Every raw form that exists because a sanitiser was bypassed or a helper was missing is one that could be removed, and removing it is both a security improvement and a reduction in the review burden. The target is a number small enough to read aloud.

What a search cannot find

A raw form assembled at run time, an attribute built by concatenation, and a value passed to a helper that takes HTML. All three are invisible to a search and all three are the same vulnerability. This is why the audit needs a check that renders — which is what makes it an integration test rather than a lint rule.

rg -n --glob '*.blade.php' '\{!!' resources/views
rg -n --glob '*.blade.php' '@php'  resources/views
rg -n --glob '*.php'      'Htmlable|HtmlString|toHtml' app

Every match requires a justification recorded where a reviewer can find it — a comment naming the sanitiser, or the framework method responsible. Three questions close an audit: is the value produced by our code, has it been sanitised on write, and does it contain no user-controlled component.

Two further checks belong in continuous integration: fail the build when a new raw echo appears without an adjacent justification, and run a request-level regression test that posts a known payload to every form and asserts that the payload appears escaped in the resulting HTML.

Exercises 17

  1. Run the raw-echo search across the project and classify every result as safe, unsanitised or a framework method.
  2. Convert one unsanitised rich-text field to sanitise-on-write and backfill the existing rows.
  3. Add an allow-list scheme check for user-supplied URLs and verify a javascript: value is rejected.
  4. Write a regression test that submits an XSS payload through each form and asserts it is escaped in the response.

{{-- STEP ONE: THE SEARCH, which is the property worth relying on. --}}
$ grep -rn '{!!' resources/views/ | wc -l
{{-- ^ the number to keep SMALL. Four is auditable in a minute;
     four hundred is not, and the four hundred are the reason
     nobody reads them. --}}

{{-- STEP TWO: REDUCE THE COUNT, which is the actual work. Every
     raw form that exists because a sanitiser was bypassed or a
     helper was missing is one that could be removed, and removing
     it is both a security improvement and a reduction in the review
     burden. --}}
$ grep -rn '{!!' resources/views/ |     grep -v 'Source:' |     grep -v '@js|json_encode'
{{-- ^ the ones with no written justification beside them. Those are
     the ones to either remove or document. --}}

{{-- STEP THREE: WHAT A SEARCH CANNOT FIND, and all three are the
     same vulnerability. --}}
{{-- (a) A RAW FORM ASSEMBLED AT RUN TIME --}}
@php
    $markup = '{!!' . $component . '!!}';   {{-- invisible to grep
                                                   and identical in
                                                   effect --}}
@endphp

{{-- (b) AN ATTRIBUTE BUILT BY CONCATENATION --}}
{!! '<div class="' . $classes . '">' !!}
{{-- ^ not an interpolation, so not found by a search for {!! --}}

{{-- (c) A VALUE PASSED TO A HELPER THAT TAKES HTML --}}
{!! AppSupportMarkdown::render($post->body) !!}
{{-- ^ found by the search, but the helper's own sanitiser policy
     is the thing to check, and it is a config value --}}

{{-- STEP FOUR: THE CHECK THAT CATCHES ALL THREE, which has to RENDER
     rather than search — which is why it is an integration test and
     not a lint rule: --}}
it('a comment cannot execute', function () {
    $payload = '<img src=x onerror="alert(1)">'
             . '<script>alert(2)</script>'
             . '"><svg onload=alert(3)>';

    $comment = Comment::factory()->create(['body' => $payload]);

    $html = $this->blade('<x-comment :comment="$comment" />');

    // Not "no angle brackets" — the escaped form CONTAINS angle
    // brackets, as text. What must not exist is an element the
    // browser would parse or an attribute it would act on.
    expect($html)->not->toContain('<script');
    expect($html)->not->toContain('onerror=');
    expect($html)->not->toContain('onload=');
    expect($html)->toContain('<img');   // the escaped form, as
                                           // text, which is correct
});
{{-- ^ this is the check that finds (a), (b) and (c), because it
     asks the question "what does the browser DO with this" rather
     than "does this string contain a dangerous character". --}}

18 · Testing Blade: Asserting Rendered Output

18.1 Asserting on the response18.2 Asserting view data18.3 Rendering a view in isolation18.4 Testing components18.5 Snapshot and markup assertions18.6 Linting templates in CI

Blade templates are testable at three levels: the HTTP response, the view data contract, and the isolated render of a template or component. This chapter documents the assertions available at each level and when each is the right one.

18.1 Asserting on the response

Documents the response assertions for presence, absence, ordering and escaping.

Asserting on the response, and what it catches

The most useful assertion in a template test is on the rendered response rather than on the view's data. It is more brittle in the sense that a whitespace change can break it, and far less brittle in the sense that it breaks for the reasons that matter: the template read a variable that does not exist, a component received a prop it does not declare, a section name did not match.

That is because rendering executes the template. A data assertion confirms the controller passed what it meant to; a response assertion confirms the template could use it. Most template defects are of the second kind, so the second assertion is the one that finds them.

The cost, and the form that keeps it small

A full-string assertion on a page with a header and a footer breaks whenever an unrelated change touches either. The workable compromise is to assert on the region under test — a component's output, a fragment of the page — rather than on the whole document. That is also a better test, because a failure points at the thing that broke.

$response = $this->get('/orders');

$response->assertOk()
    ->assertSee('Order 1001')
    ->assertSeeText('Paid')
    ->assertDontSee('Stack trace')
    ->assertSeeInOrder(['Reference', 'Status']);

// The literal form asserts that a value was rendered raw.
$response->assertSee(e('<script>alert(1)</script>'), false);

By default the presence assertions escape the needle before searching, so assertSee('<b>') looks for the escaped output. Passing false as the second argument disables that and searches for the literal string.

Assert content, not markup. A test asserting assertSee('Order 1001') survives a restyle. A test asserting a class name breaks on every design change and asserts nothing about behaviour.


{{-- THE RESPONSE ASSERTION, which finds the defects that a data
     assertion cannot: --}}
it('shows the order number in the heading', function () {
    $order = Order::factory()->create(['number' => 'ORD-42']);

    $response = $this->get(route('orders.show', $order));

    $response->assertOk();
    $response->assertSee('ORD-42');
});
{{-- ^ this RENDERS the template. So it catches: a variable the
     template reads that does not exist, a component prop that was
     not declared, a section name that does not match, a route that
     no longer exists, a component that now throws. A data
     assertion catches none of those. --}}

{{-- AND WHAT IT DOES NOT CATCH, which is why it needs a
     companion: a template that renders the WRONG thing correctly. --}}
$response->assertSee('ORD-42');
{{-- ^ passes for an order whose number is rendered in a hidden
     input rather than in the heading. The assertion says the
     string is present; it does not say where. --}}

{{-- THE COMPROMISE THAT KEEPS A LARGE PAGE TESTABLE: assert on the
     REGION under test, not the whole document. --}}
it('renders the summary with the right total', function () {
    $order = Order::factory()->withLines([
        ['qty' => 2, 'unit' => 500],
        ['qty' => 1, 'unit' => 250],
    ])->create();

    $component = Blade::render(
        '<x-order-summary :order="$order" />',
        ['order' => $order],
    );

    expect($component)
        ->toContain('12.50')                    {{-- the total --}}
        ->and($component)->not->toContain('10.00');  {{-- and not the
                                                            subtotal,
                                                            which is
                                                            what a
                                                            wrong
                                                            sum
                                                            produces --}}
});
{{-- ^ a component's output, in isolation. Fast, no routing, no
     middleware, and a failure points at the component. --}}

{{-- AND THE FORM FOR A COMPONENT, which is the workhorse: --}}
it('escapes the user name in the badge', function () {
    $html = Blade::render('<x-user-badge :user="$u" />', [
        'u' => (object) ['name' => '<b>Admin</b>'],
    ]);

    // The escaped form is present AS TEXT. That is what correct
    // output looks like for a value containing markup.
    expect($html)->toContain('<b>Admin</b>');
    expect($html)->not->toContain('<b>Admin</b>');
});

18.2 Asserting view data

Documents the view data assertions and the performance assertion that catches a reintroduced N+1.

Where the contract belongs, and why not in the test

A view's requirements are a real contract — six variables, each with a type and a meaning — and the question is where to write it down. A test asserting the data is one place, and it fails when the controller changes. A comment in the template is another, and it does not fail when anything changes.

There is a third option that costs one command to run and fails when either side changes: derive the requirements from the template by scanning it for the variables it reads, and assert that the controller supplies every one. That is what catches the defect this section is about, which is a variable added to a template and never passed — the bug that appears in production as a page with a silent hole in it.

What the assertion is really for

Not correctness — the render test has that. It is for making the coupling explicit at the point where somebody adds a variable. A developer who writes {{ $customer }} in a template and has not added it to the controller should find out from a test rather than from a user, and a test that fails on the contract is the only thing that does that reliably.

$response->assertViewHas('order', fn (Order $o) => $o->is($order));
$response->assertViewHasAll(['order', 'lines', 'totals']);
$response->assertViewMissing('password');

// The relationship the template reads was eager loaded.
$response->assertViewHas('order',
    fn ($o) => $o->relationLoaded('customer'),
);

That last assertion is the performance test. Verifying through the data contract fails the moment an N+1 is reintroduced, without needing a query counter in the assertion itself.

Test the contract. View data assertions describe what the controller promised the template. They stay valid when the markup is rewritten and fail immediately when a promise is broken.


{{-- THE DATA ASSERTION, which has one real job: pinning down what
     the view is GIVEN. --}}
it('passes the order and its lines to the view', function () {
    $order = Order::factory()->withLines(3)->create();

    View::shouldReceive('make')
        ->once()
        ->with('orders.show', Mockery::on(function (array $data) use ($order) {
            // THE KEYS THE VIEW DEPENDS ON, and nothing else. A
            // full-array assertion changes whenever a component needs
            // a new prop, and a test that fails on that is a test
            // people learn to skip.
            return array_key_exists('order', $data)
                && array_key_exists('lines', $data)
                && $data['order']->is($order)
                && $data['lines']->count() === 3;
        }))
        ->andReturn(view('orders.show', ['order' => $order]));

    $this->get(route('orders.show', $order))->assertOk();
});


{{-- WHAT NOT TO ASSERT: the whole array. --}}
it('passes data to the view', function () {
    // WRONG. It changes whenever a component needs a new prop, and a
    // test that fails on that teaches people to use
    // withoutExpecting instead — which then disables the mock
    // entirely, and the mock was the point.
    $this->assertEquals([
        'order' => $order, 'lines' => $lines, 'canRefund' => true,
        'currency' => 'GBP', 'title' => 'Order', 'seoTitle' => 'x',
        'breadcrumbs' => [], 'nav' => null, 'flash' => null,
    ], $data);
});

{{-- AND THE VERSION THAT IS WORTH HAVING, which asserts the
     REQUIREMENT rather than the payload: --}}
it('orders.show requires what its template reads', function () {
    $source = file_get_contents(
        resource_path('views/orders/show.blade.php'));

    // Every variable the template reads.
    preg_match_all('/{{s*\$([a-z_]+)|@[a-z]+\s*\(?\s*\$([a-z_]+)/',
        $source, $m);
    $read = array_unique(array_filter(array_merge($m[1], $m[2])));

    $supplied = array_keys(
        view('orders.show', sample_data_for('orders.show'))->getData()
    );

    expect(array_diff($read, $supplied))->toBeEmpty();
});
{{-- ^ this is the one that finds a variable added to a template and
     never passed: it fails because the TEMPLATE reads something the
     caller does not supply, which is exactly the class of bug an
     undefined-variable error would report four frames away. --}}

{{-- AND IT IS WORTH RUNNING OVER EVERY VIEW, because the defect is
     a CLASS rather than an instance: --}}
it('every view receives what it reads', function () {
    $missing = [];

    foreach (View::getFinder()->getPaths() as $dir) {
        foreach (new RecursiveIteratorIterator(
                     new RecursiveDirectoryIterator($dir)) as $file) {
            if ($file->getExtension() !== 'php') continue;

            $rel  = Str::after($file->getPathname(), $dir . DIRECTORY_SEPARATOR);
            $name = str_replace(['/', '\\', '.blade.php'], ['.', '.', ''], $rel);
            if (str_contains($name, 'components.')) continue;  // props, not
                                                              // data

            $src = file_get_contents($file->getPathname());
            preg_match_all('/\{\{\s*\$([a-z_]+)/', $src, $m);

            $supplied = array_keys(
                view($name, sample_data_for($name))->getData()
            );

            foreach (array_unique($m[1]) as $variable) {
                if (! in_array($variable, $supplied, true)) {
                    $missing[] = $name . ' reads $' . $variable
                        . ' but the controller does not supply it';
                }
            }
        }
    }

    expect($missing)->toBeEmpty();
});

18.3 Rendering a view in isolation

Documents rendering a template outside a request, which is the fastest way to test a partial or a component.

Rendering a view in isolation

A view can be rendered with hand-built data and no request at all. That is the fastest possible test of a template and the right tool for the two questions a request cannot answer well: does this fragment render given exactly these variables, and does it fail when one is missing.

The second of those is the one worth having. A template that reads a variable the caller does not supply renders an empty string under the default conditional and raises an error under the strict one.
