A test that asserts the error is raised catches the class of bug that appears in production as a page with a silent hole in it.

What isolation costs

Composers do not run unless you run them, shared data is whatever the test bootstrap set, and any view that extends a layout needs the layout rendered too. So an isolated render is not the same as a request — it is a narrower thing, and the narrower thing is the point: it removes the routing, the middleware and the database from the question "does this template work".

$html = view('partials.summary', [
    'order' => Order::factory()
        ->hasLines(3)
        ->create(['total' => 15000]),
])->render();

$this->assertStringContainsString('150.00', $html);
$this->assertStringContainsString(e('Order & Co'), $html);

Isolated rendering avoids booting the router, middleware and response stack, which keeps a template suite fast. It requires supplying every variable the template reads, which is exactly the discipline that makes a template portable.

A missing variable is a defect. An isolated render that reads an undefined variable emits a notice and renders nothing, where a production request may supply it from a view composer. Pass the data explicitly.


{{-- THE ISOLATED RENDER, with hand-built data and no request: --}}
it('renders the partial with the data it declares', function () {
    $html = View::make('partials.order-row', [
        'order'   => Order::factory()->make(['number' => 'ORD-1']),
        'compact' => false,
    ])->render();

    expect($html)->toContain('ORD-1');
});
{{-- ^ no routing, no middleware, no database — the factory makes
     the model without saving it. Milliseconds. --}}

{{-- THE TEST WORTH HAVING, which isolation makes possible: --}}
it('fails loudly when a required variable is missing', function () {
    // The STRICT conditional is what makes this an error rather
    // than an empty string.
    expect(fn () => View::make('partials.order-row', [
        // 'order' is deliberately absent
        'compact' => false,
    ])->render())->toThrow(InvalidArgumentException::class);
});
{{-- ^ THIS is the test that finds the class of bug that appears
     in production as a page with a silent hole in it. A template
     that reads a variable the caller does not supply renders an
     empty string under the default conditional — so the bug is
     invisible until somebody switches the strict mode on. --}}

{{-- AND THE COMPONENT FORM, which is the same isolation with the
     props resolved for you: --}}
it('renders the badge with a status', function () {
    expect(Blade::render('<x-order-badge status="paid" />'))
        ->toContain('badge-success');

    expect(Blade::render('<x-order-badge status="refunded" />'))
        ->toContain('badge-muted');
});
{{-- ^ two asserts, no container setup, no database, and a failure
     names the variant that broke. --}}

{{-- WHAT ISOLATION COSTS, and it is worth stating: --}}
it('shows what isolation does NOT give you', function () {
    // A view composer does NOT run unless you run it.
    View::composer('partials.order-row', fn ($v) => $v->with('currency', 'GBP'));

    $html = View::make('partials.order-row', [
        'order' => Order::factory()->make(),
    ])->render();
    // ^ $currency is UNDEFINED, because the composer was not
    //   registered before the render. This is the trap: a test that
    //   passes in isolation and fails in a request, or the reverse.

    // Shared data is whatever the bootstrap set, not what a request
    // would provide. And a view that extends a layout needs the
    // LAYOUT rendered too — which is why testing an extending view
    // in isolation needs its section data supplied explicitly.
});
{{-- SO AN ISOLATED RENDER IS NOT A REQUEST. It is a NARROWER
     thing, and the narrower thing is the point: it removes the
     routing, the middleware and the database from the question
     "does this template work". --}}

18.4 Testing components

Documents the component test helpers and the three properties a component test should assert.

Testing a component, and the three things worth asserting

A component is a template with a declared interface, and testing it is mostly a matter of exercising that interface. The three assertions worth having are: it renders with only its required props, it renders the right variant for each value its logic distinguishes, and it omits or escapes what it should.

The second is the one that pays, and the reason is that a component's conditional logic is its whole contract. A badge with a match expression over four statuses needs four tests, not one, because the failure mode is a variant silently falling through to the default — which looks correct and is wrong for exactly the case nobody tested.

The isolation test, which is the interesting one

Rendering a component with a prop it does not declare should do nothing rather than fail. That is the behaviour anonymous components have and the one class components do not, and it is worth asserting deliberately: it is the property that lets a caller pass an attribute that the component does not use without an error, and a test that pins it means a future change to the merge behaviour is caught.

view('components.alert', [
    'type'  => 'danger',
    'title' => 'Disk full',
    'slot'  => 'Delete old files.',
])->assertSee('alert--danger')
  ->assertSeeInOrder(['Disk full', 'Delete old files.']);

A component test should assert that the variant reaches the class attribute, that slot content renders in the expected position relative to surrounding markup, and that omitted optional props produce no markup. The third assertion is the one most often missing and the one most likely to regress silently.

Assert the merge. Attribute merging is library behaviour, so test that a caller-supplied class survives alongside the component’s own. That contract breaks on a framework upgrade.


{{-- (1) IT RENDERS WITH ONLY ITS REQUIRED PROPS: --}}
it('renders with the minimum', function () {
    $html = Blade::render('<x-order-summary :order="$o" />', [
        'o' => Order::factory()->make(),
    ]);

    expect($html)->toContain('summary');
});

{{-- (2) THE RIGHT VARIANT FOR EACH VALUE ITS LOGIC DISTINGUISHES,
     which is the one that pays: --}}
it('renders the right tone for every status', function (string $status, string $tone) {
    $html = Blade::render('<x-order-badge status="' . $status . '" />');

    expect($html)->toContain('badge-' . $tone);
})->with([
    ['paid',     'success'],
    ['pending',  'info'],
    ['refunded', 'muted'],
    // ^ FOUR tests, not one. The failure mode is a status silently
    //   falling through to the DEFAULT, which looks correct and is
    //   wrong for exactly the case nobody tested. --]
    // A fifth row here, for a status added to the enum last month,
    // is the test that catches the fallback.
]);

{{-- (3) IT ESCAPES WHAT IT SHOULD: --}}
it('escapes a user name in the badge', function () {
    $html = Blade::render('<x-user-badge :user="$u" />', [
        'u' => (object) ['name' => '<script>alert(1)</script>'],
    ]);

    expect($html)->not->toContain('<script>');
    expect($html)->toContain('<script>');
});

{{-- THE ISOLATION TEST, which is the interesting one: --}}
it('ignores a prop it does not declare', function () {
    $html = Blade::render('<x-order-summary :order="$o" :nonsense="1" />', [
        'o' => Order::factory()->make(),
    ]);

    expect($html)->toContain('summary');
    // ^ passes, and the attribute is DISCARDED. That is the
    //   behaviour an anonymous component has, and it is worth
    //   asserting deliberately: it is the property that lets a
    //   caller pass an attribute the component does not use without
    //   an error, and a test that pins it means a future change to
    //   the merge behaviour is caught. --}
});

{{-- AND THE ATTRIBUTE-BAG TEST, which is the one a component gets
     wrong most often: --}}
it('forwards the id to the root element', function () {
    $html = Blade::render('<x-card id="summary" class="w-full" />');

    expect($html)->toContain('id="summary"');
    // A component that does not spread $attributes DROPS both, and
    // the rendered page still looks correct — nothing is visibly
    // broken, the element is simply not targetable from a test or
    // from JavaScript.
});

18.5 Snapshot and markup assertions

Explains when snapshot testing of rendered output is useful, and the failure mode that limits it.

Snapshot testing stores the rendered HTML and fails on any difference. It is valuable for a component whose exact structure is a public contract — a payment form, an email body, a document export — because any unintended structural change becomes visible in the diff.

Snapshot tests, and the two things they are good at

A snapshot test records the output and fails when it changes. It is excellent at two things and poor at a third: it catches an unintended change to markup, and it makes a deliberate one visible as a reviewable diff. It is poor at telling you whether the new output is correct — a snapshot accepts whatever it is shown.

The failure mode is well known and worth naming: a snapshot that is regenerated without being read becomes a change-detector that approves everything. The discipline that prevents it is to require the diff to be reviewed, which is a process rather than a tool, and the tool-level mitigation is to snapshot small units so a diff is readable.

What to snapshot and what not to

Snapshot the things whose markup is a contract: a component's output, a rendered email, a PDF. Do not snapshot a whole page, because the diff then contains the header and the footer and nobody reads it. And never snapshot a value containing a timestamp, an identifier or a random string, because every run differs and the snapshot becomes noise.

// A narrow structural assertion beats a whole-page snapshot.
$html = view('components.badge', ['status' => 'paid'])->render();

$dom = new DOMDocument;
@$dom->loadHTML('<meta charset="utf-8"><div>'.$html.'</div>');

// assert the badge element carries the expected role and text

Whole-page snapshots are counter-productive in a Blade codebase: they fail on every intentional design change, train reviewers to accept regenerated snapshots without reading the diff, and say nothing about behaviour. Prefer the narrow structural assertions above, and reserve snapshots for output consumed by a system you do not control.


{{-- WHERE A SNAPSHOT IS EXCELLENT: an unintended change to markup,
     and a deliberate one as a reviewable diff. --}}
it('matches the snapshot', function () {
    $html = Blade::render('<x-invoice :invoice="$i" />', [
        'i' => Invoice::factory()->make([
            'number'  => 'INV-1',
            'total'   => 1250,
            // ^ EVERY FIELD PINNED. A snapshot containing a
            //   timestamp, a generated identifier or a random
            //   value differs on every run, so the test is noise
            //   and people stop reading the diffs. --]
        ]),
    ]);

    expect($html)->toMatchSnapshot();
});

{{-- WHAT NOT TO SNAPSHOT: a whole page. --}}
it('matches the dashboard snapshot', function () {
    // WRONG. The diff now contains the header, the footer and every
    // other component, so a one-character change somewhere is
    // buried and the snapshot is regenerated unread. That is how a
    // snapshot becomes a change-detector that approves everything.
    $this->get('/dashboard')->assertMatchesSnapshot();
});

{{-- RIGHT: a small unit, whose diff is readable. --}}
it('renders the badge', function () {
    expect(Blade::render('<x-order-badge status="paid" />'))
        ->toMatchSnapshot();
});

{{-- AND THE THING THAT MAKES SNAPSHOTS REVIEWABLE, which is that
     the snapshot is COMMITTED: --}}
tests/__snapshots__/BadgeTest__it_renders_the_badge.snap
┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ <span class="badge badge-success">Paid</span>              │
└────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
{{-- ^ a failure prints the diff, and the fix is either to revert
     or to update the snapshot deliberately. There is no third
     option — which is what stops "regenerate and move on", because
     the regeneration is a COMMIT that somebody has to look at. --}}

{{-- THE HYBRID, which is the form that works in practice: a
     snapshot for the markup, an EXPLICIT assertion for the things
     a snapshot cannot judge. --}}
it('renders the badge', function () {
    $html = Blade::render('<x-order-badge status="paid" />');

    // Explicit: the things a snapshot accepts without judgement.
    expect($html)->toContain('badge-success')      {{-- the variant --}}
         ->and($html)->toContain('Paid')           {{-- the label --}}
         ->and($html)->not->toContain('&');       {{-- nothing
                                                       escaped that
                                                       should not
                                                       be --}};

    // Snapshot: everything else, so an unintended change to the
    // structure is still visible.
    expect($html)->toMatchSnapshot();
});

18.6 Linting templates in CI

Documents the static checks that catch template defects before deployment.

Linting templates, and what only a linter can find

A template can be checked for things that are not its correctness but are its maintainability: a raw interpolation with no justification, a comment longer than the code it explains, a conditional nested more than two deep, an arbitrary utility class where a token belongs. None of these is a defect, and all of them are the things a reviewer spends time on instead of on the defects.

There is one class of finding that is a real defect and that only a linter finds: a directive used that was never registered. It fails at render time, on one page, in production — so it is worth a check that renders every template at build time. Everything else is a review aid.

The cost of a linter in a team

A linter with rules nobody agrees on is noise, and a team will route around it. The rules worth having are the ones where the correct answer is unambiguous: a raw form needs a justification, a directive must exist, a nesting depth has a limit. A rule about naming is a matter of taste and will be ignored.

// Every template compiles.
php artisan view:cache

// Blade-aware formatting and static analysis.
./vendor/bin/pint --test resources/views

// Structural checks.
./vendor/bin/phpstan analyse resources/views --level=5

view:cache in continuous integration catches syntax errors, unclosed directives and missing component views before a merge reaches a shared branch. Pair it with a formatter and an analyser, and with a grep for raw echoes as described in Chapter 17.

Fail the build, not the page. Every check documented here is cheap to run and expensive to discover in production. None of them require a request.

Exercises 18

  1. Write a feature test asserting the contents of an order listing, using only content assertions.
  2. Write an isolated render test for a component that has never been requested through a route.
  3. Add an assertion that a relationship read by the template is eager loaded, then break the controller and confirm the test fails.
  4. Add view:cache and a raw-echo grep to the CI pipeline and verify both catch an intentional defect.

{{-- WHAT ONLY A LINTER FINDS, and it is a real defect: a directive
     used that was never registered. It fails at RENDER time, on
     one page, in production. --}}
Artisan::command('views:verify', function () {
    $failed = [];
    $count  = 0;

    foreach (View::getFinder()->getPaths() as $dir) {
        foreach (new RecursiveIteratorIterator(
                     new RecursiveDirectoryIterator($dir)) as $file) {
            if ($file->getExtension() !== 'php') continue;

            $rel  = Str::after($file->getPathname(), $dir . DIRECTORY_SEPARATOR);
            $name = str_replace(['/', '\', '.blade.php'], ['.', '.', ''], $rel);
            $count++;

            try {
                // RENDERING, not parsing: an unregistered directive
                // is a runtime failure, so a parse-only check misses
                // it entirely.
                View::make($name)->render();
            } catch (Throwable $e) {
                $failed[] = [$name, class_basename($e), $e->getMessage()];
            }
        }
    }

    if ($failed) {
        $this->table(['view', 'exception', 'message'], $failed);
        exit(1);          // FAIL THE BUILD, not a customer's request
    }

    $this->info("$count templates compiled and rendered");
});

{{-- AND THE RULES WORTH HAVING, chosen by ONE criterion: the
     correct answer is UNAMBIGUOUS. --}}
Artisan::command('views:lint', function () {
    $findings = [];

    foreach (View::getFinder()->getPaths() as $dir) {
        foreach (new RecursiveIteratorIterator(
                     new RecursiveDirectoryIterator($dir)) as $file) {
            if ($file->getExtension() !== 'php') continue;
            $rel = Str::after($file->getPathname(), $dir . '/');

            $src = file_get_contents($file->getPathname());

            // 1. A RAW FORM WITH NO JUSTIFICATION. Unambiguous: the
            //    correct answer is a written reason.
            preg_match_all('/{!!(.*?)!!}/s', $src, $raw, PREG_OFFSET_CAPTURE);
            foreach ($raw[1] as [$expr, $off]) {
                $near = substr($src, max(0, $off - 400), $off);
                if (! preg_match('/Source:|Trusted|sanitis|generated/i', $near)) {
                    $findings[] = [$rel, 'raw form without a reason', trim($expr)];
                }
            }

            // 2. CONDITIONAL DEPTH OVER TWO. Unambiguous: a limit.
            $depth = 0; $max = 0;
            foreach (file($file->getPathname()) as $line) {
                if (preg_match('/@(if|foreach|forelse|for|unless|isset|empty|switch)/', $line)) {
                    $depth++; $max = max($max, $depth);
                }
                if (preg_match('/@(endw+)|@else|@elseif/', $line)) $depth = max(0, $depth - 1);
            }
            if ($max > 2) $findings[] = [$rel, 'conditional depth ' . $max, ''];

            // 3. AN ARBITRARY VALUE, because it is a signal to add a
            //    token and the count is the measure.
            if (preg_match('/class="[^"]*[[^"]*]/', $src)) {
                $findings[] = [$rel, 'arbitrary value', ''];
            }
        }
    }

    if ($findings) {
        $this->table(['template', 'finding', 'detail'], $findings);
        $this->comment(count($findings) . ' finding(s). Rules 1 and 2 are '
            . 'defects; rule 3 is a signal.');
    } else {
        $this->info('clean');
    }
});

{{-- WHAT NOT TO WRITE, and the criterion that excludes it: --}}
{{-- "use kebab-case for components"  -> a MATTER OF TASTE, so the
     team will route around the rule and the linter becomes noise.
     A linter with rules nobody agrees on is worse than no linter,
     because it teaches people to ignore the output. --}}

19 · Conventions and Maintainability

19.1 Logic-free templates19.2 View models and presenters19.3 Choosing component boundaries19.4 Naming and file layout19.5 View composers and shared data19.6 A review checklist

Maintainability in Blade is a consequence of a small number of structural decisions. This chapter states those decisions, the conventions that follow from them, and a review checklist that makes them enforceable.

19.1 Logic-free templates

States what may appear in a template, what must not, and the practical test that separates them.

A template may contain presentation logic: a conditional choosing a class, a loop over a collection, a formatted value. It must not contain domain logic: queries, calculations that decide anything, authorisation decisions, or calls into services.

The practical test is whether a reader can determine what the page shows without knowing how the data was produced. A template containing @if ($order->lines->sum('amount') > 500) forces the reader to understand aggregation, Eloquent and currency handling to know what the page displays.

The smell. Reaching for a relation, a helper or a service inside a template means the template is doing something a class should be doing. The fix is always to move the work and pass the result.

Why the rule is worth having, stated as a cost

The benefit of a logic-free template is not purity. It is that the logic has somewhere to be tested. A domain rule in a template is tested by rendering it, which means the test needs a container, a request, and a database — so it is slow, so it is written rarely, so it is written for the obvious case only. The same rule in a class is four lines of a unit test with mocks.

The second benefit is that the rule has one home. A refund rule in a template exists once per page that shows a refund button, and the console command that also refunds will not consult it. In a class it exists once, and every caller — the view, the endpoint, the command, the queued job — gets the same answer.

Where the line is genuinely fuzzy

Formatting is the honest example. A template that calls a formatter with the user's currency is arguably making a display decision, and in a small application it will never be the problem. The test that settles it is the queue: if a job renders this template, the user is not there, so a value read from the user's settings is unavailable and the template must take a formatted value instead. That single question resolves most of the arguable cases.


{{-- PRESENTATION LOGIC. Answers "which presentation". --}}
@if ($order->status === OrderStatus::Paid)
    <x-badge tone="success">{{ __('orders.paid') }}</x-badge>
@elseif ($order->status === OrderStatus::Pending)
    <x-badge tone="info">{{ __('orders.pending') }}</x-badge>
@endif
{{-- ^ the template branches on a fact the domain supplied and
     chooses a presentation for it. Correct. --}}

{{-- DOMAIN LOGIC. Answers "what is true", and it does not belong
     here even though it reads the same. --}}
@if ($order->status === OrderStatus::Paid && ! $order->refunded_at
      && $order->lines->sum('total') > 0
      && $user->hasPermission('orders.refund'))
    <form method="POST" action="{{ route('orders.refund', $order) }}">
        @csrf
        <button type="submit">{{ __('orders.refund') }}</button>
    </form>
@endif
{{-- ^ FOUR facts combined into one decision, and this one is
     needed in an API response, in a console command that refunds an
     order, and in a queued job. Three copies of a refund rule is
     how one of them ends up wrong. --}}


{{-- RIGHT, AND IT IS ALSO SHORT: --}}
// app/Policies/OrderRefundPolicy.php
final class OrderRefundPolicy
{
    public function canBeRefunded(Order $order): bool
    {
        return $order->status === OrderStatus::Paid
            && $order->refunded_at === null
            && $order->lines->sum('total') > 0;
        // ^ ONE place, used by the template, the API, the console
        //   command and the queue worker.
    }
}

// app/Policies/OrderPolicy.php — the permission half
final class OrderPolicy
{
    public function refund(User $user, Order $order): bool
    {
        return $user->hasPermission('orders.refund')
            && app(OrderRefundPolicy::class)->canBeRefunded($order);
    }
}

{{-- orders/show.blade.php --}}
@can('refund', $order)
    <form method="POST" action="{{ route('orders.refund', $order) }}">
        @csrf
        <button type="submit">{{ __('orders.refund') }}</button>
    </form>
@endcan
{{-- ^ the template states the INTENT; the policy decides. And @can
     HIDES rather than refuses — the route repeats the check, which
     is the only thing that enforces it. --}}

{{-- AND THE FORMATTING CASE, which is the one most templates get
     wrong: --}}
{{ $order->total->format() }}     {{-- RIGHT: a value arrives
                                         formatted, from the
                                         controller or a view
                                         model. --}}
{{ AppSupportMoney::format($order->total) }}   {{-- WRONG: the
         template has chosen a currency and a locale, and those are
         domain concerns. The rendered value depends on a per-user
         setting, and a template reading that setting is a
         component that cannot be rendered in a queue. --}}

19.2 View models and presenters

Documents the view model pattern for preparing template data and the discipline that makes it worth the extra class.

A view model, and what it buys

A view model is an object whose only job is to give a template what it needs. Its benefit is not the object — it is that the computations move out of the template into something that can be tested without rendering HTML, and that a template receiving one object has one declared requirement rather than six implicit ones.

The distinction between a view model and a presenter matters less than it appears. Both wrap data for display; the difference is whether they wrap one record or many, and whether they hold the data or read it. Holding the data makes them constructors of test fixtures, which is convenient. Reading it makes them thinner and keeps the query in one place, which is usually better.

What does not belong in one

A query that is specific to one template's display is fine, because the query exists to render. A decision that a second caller will also need is not — it is domain logic that happens to have been put in a presentation class, and it will be duplicated the moment a console command needs it.

final readonly class OrderPage
{
    public function __construct(
        public Order $order,
        public string $totalFormatted,
        public bool $canCancel,
        public array $statusOptions,
    ) {}
}

The discipline: a view model performs work and prepares scalars and collections, and it exposes no behaviour the template must call. A template invoking $page->totalFormatted() on each render is doing the work in the template instead, once per pass.

When it pays. A view model earns its place when a page prepares more than a handful of values, when the same preparation serves several templates, or when it is expensive enough to cache. A single formatted string does not justify a class.


{{-- THE VIEW MODEL, and what it actually buys: the computation
     moves out of the template into something testable without
     rendering HTML. --}}
// app/View/Models/OrderListView.php
final readonly class OrderListView
{
    public function __construct(
        private Order $order,
        private OrderPresenter $presenter,
    ) {}

    public function number(): string
    {
        return $this->order->number;
    }

    public function total(): string
    {
        // FORMATTING WITH A CURRENCY, which the template must not
        // choose: a rendered value depends on a per-user setting,
        // and a template reading that setting is a component that
        // cannot be rendered in a queue.
        return $this->presenter->money($this->order->total);
    }

    public function statusBadge(): StatusView
    {
        // A PRESENTER OBJECT rather than a string, so the template
        // chooses a presentation and the model chooses the value.
        return match ($this->order->status) {
            OrderStatus::Paid    => new StatusView('success', __('orders.paid')),
            OrderStatus::Pending => new StatusView('info',    __('orders.pending')),
            OrderStatus::Refunded=> new StatusView('muted',   __('orders.refunded')),
        };
    }

    public function canRefund(): bool
    {
        // Hmm: a permission check in a presenter. Usually RIGHT —
        // it is a display question ("should this button show") —
        // and sometimes WRONG, when the same rule is needed by the
        // controller and ends up duplicated.
        return Gate::allows('refund', $this->order);
    }
}

{{-- THE TEMPLATE, which now has ONE requirement rather than six: --}}
@foreach ($orders as $order)
    <tr>
        <td>{{ $order->number() }}</td>
        <td>{{ $order->total() }}</td>
        <td>
            @php($badge = $order->statusBadge())
            <x-badge :tone="$badge->tone">{{ $badge->label }}</x-badge>
        </td>
    </tr>
@endforeach

{{-- AND THE TEST, which is the whole benefit: --}}
it('labels a refunded order as refunded', function () {
    $order  = Order::factory()->refunded()->make();
    $view   = new OrderListView($order, new OrderPresenter(Money::GBP));
    $badge  = $view->statusBadge();

    expect($badge->tone)->toBe('muted')
        ->and($badge->label)->toBe(__('orders.refunded'));
});
{{-- ^ no HTTP, no container, no template rendered. The same
     assertion as a rendering test, at a fraction of the cost and
     with a failure that names the model rather than a byte offset
     in a string. --}}

{{-- WHAT DOES NOT BELONG IN ONE: a decision a second caller will
     also need. --}}
final readonly class OrderListView
{
    public function canRefund(): bool
    {
        // WRONG: this rule is ALSO needed by the refund endpoint
        // and by the console command that refunds on request. Two
        // copies of a refund rule is how one of them ends up wrong.
        return $this->order->status === OrderStatus::Paid
            && $this->order->refunded_at === null
            && Gate::allows('refund', $this->order);
    }
}
{{-- ^ in the template instead: --}}
@can('refund', $order)
    <form method="POST" action="{{ route('orders.refund', $order) }}">
        @csrf
        <button type="submit">{{ __('orders.refund') }}</button>
    </form>
@endcan
{{-- ^ which delegates to a policy — ONE place, used by the view,
     the endpoint and the command. --}}

19.3 Choosing component boundaries

Provides the tests that decide whether markup should become a component, and the failure modes of over- and under-extraction.

QuestionYesNo
Is the markup used twice?ExtractLeave in place
Does it carry its own props?ExtractLeave in place
Would a change need two edits?ExtractLeave in place
Does it need its own test?ExtractLeave in place

Over-extraction is the more common error. A component used once adds an indirection, a file and a context switch while removing nothing. Under-extraction duplicates markup that must then change in several places, which is how an accessibility fix reaches some pages and misses others.

Three is the signal. When you notice yourself copying the same block for the third time, extract it. Before that, duplication is often clearer than indirection.

What the prop count actually measures

It measures how many things a caller can get wrong, which is not the same as how many things the component varies by. A component with two props and forty boolean combinations is a worse component than one with eight props and one combination, and the difference is that the eight are individually nameable while the forty are combinations nobody has thought about.

That is the real form of the rule: a component should vary along axes a caller can name. A variant name, a slot, a slot's content — those are nameable. A set of boolean flags crossed with each other is a set of forty states described by eight bits, and the states that were never intended are indistinguishable from the ones that were.

The test that finds the mis-split component

Two regions inside one component that are never configured together. If no call site sets both, they are two components with a shared wrapper, and the wrapper should be a slot. The practical way to find this is to list the prop combinations actually used — the framework can log them, or a script can grep the call sites — and to look for the flags that never appear alongside each other.


{{-- WHAT CHANGES TOGETHER BELONGS TOGETHER, and what does not: --}}
{{-- resources/views/components/order-row.blade.php --}}
@props(['order', 'showCustomer' => false, 'showStatus' => false,
        'dense' => false, 'selectable' => false, 'highlight' => false])

<tr class="{{ $dense ? 'row-dense' : '' }}"
           {{ $selectable ? 'data-selectable' : '' }}
           @if ($highlight) data-highlighted @endif>
    @if ($showCustomer)<td>{{ $order->customer->name }}</td>@endif
    @if ($showStatus)<td>{{ $order->status->label() }}</td>@endif
    <td>{{ $order->total->format() }}</td>
</tr>

{{-- ^ SEVEN PROPS, and this is the signal. Each is an independent
     flag, so each is a variation some call site wants, and the
     component is now a table with switches. --}}

{{-- THE TEST: do these two things change for the same reason? --}}
@props(['order', 'variant' => 'default'])

<tr class="order-row order-row--{{ $variant }}">
    <td>{{ $order->number }}</td>
    <td>{{ $order->total->format() }}</td>
    <td>{{ $order->customer->name }}</td>
</tr>

<x-order-row :order="$o" />                      {{-- default --}}
<x-order-row :order="$o" variant="compact" />    {{-- what the list
                                                        page wants --}}
<x-order-row :order="$o" variant="printable" />  {{-- what the PDF
                                                        wants --}}
{{-- ^ three props become one, and the variants are defined ONCE
     with their markup rather than composed from flags. The
     variation is now a NAME, which means it can be a map, can be
     extended, and can be listed. --}}


{{-- WHEN THINGS CHANGE INDEPENDENTLY, THEY BELONG APART — because a
     component containing two independently-varying things has a
     prop per thing and is really TWO components with a shared
     wrapper: --}}
{{-- WRONG: a "page" component that is really three --}}
<x-orders-page :orders="$orders" :filters="$filters"
               :pagination="$orders->links()" :sidebar="$sidebar"
               :toolbar="$toolbar" :empty="$emptyState" />
{{-- ^ six props, each a separate region of the page that a
     different call site might want to replace. And it is really a
     wrapper with a slot. --}}

{{-- RIGHT: the wrapper takes a slot, and the regions are the
     CALLER's: --}}
<x-orders-page :filters="$filters">
    <x-orders-toolbar :selected="count($selected)" />

    <x-orders-table :orders="$orders" />

    {{ $orders->links() }}
</x-orders-page>
{{-- ^ one prop, and the three regions are the caller's code, which
     is the point: a caller that needs a different table writes a
     different table rather than setting a flag. --}}

{{-- AND THE PROPS BUDGET, restated as the evidence that a split is
     overdue rather than as a style rule: --}}
{{--
   0-3 props   a component is easy to call correctly
   4-7 props   every call site is a chance to get one wrong
   8+ props    the probability that any call site is wrong is high
               enough that the component should be split, and the
               prop count is the EVIDENCE

   This is not style. It is the argument that a component with
   fifteen props is fifteen separate components wearing a shared
   wrapper, and a caller cannot tell which of the fifteen it forgot.
--}}

19.4 Naming and file layout

Documents the naming conventions that keep a template directory navigable and the resolution rules that depend on them.

Naming, and why it is not style

Template naming is not a matter of taste, because a name is a lookup key and a lookup key that does not predict its target is a lookup that fails. Three conventions carry most of the value: a partial named for what it renders rather than where it is used, a component named as an adjective-plus-noun so the tag reads as markup, and a view named for the resource it belongs to rather than the page it appears on.

The second convention has a specific benefit that is worth stating: a component named as an adjective reads correctly in the rendered output. <x-order-row> is a row; <x-order-row-thing> is not anything. That is not vanity — it is the test that the name describes the thing rather than the file.

The convention that goes wrong most

Naming a view after the page it appears on. A dashboard view that is rendered by the reports page is a name that will be wrong the moment the report is also embedded somewhere else, and the wrongness is invisible until somebody searches for the wrong name.

resources/views/
  layouts/app.blade.php
  components/forms/text-field.blade.php
  orders/index.blade.php
  orders/show.blade.php
  orders/_summary.blade.php

/* view('orders.index') -> orders/index.blade.php */

Names map to paths through dot notation, so the directory structure is the index. Keeping pages in a domain directory with underscore-prefixed partials keeps related markup together and makes it obvious which files are meant to be included rather than routed to.

Keep the trees separate. Reusable components belong under components/; domain pages belong under their domain. Mixing them produces directories that are neither a design system nor a feature.


{{-- THREE CONVENTIONS, and none of them is taste. --}}

{{-- (1) A PARTIAL NAMED FOR WHAT IT RENDERS, not where it is
     used. --}}
{{-- RIGHT --}}
@include('partials.order-row')
@include('partials.pagination')
@include('partials.user-avatar')

{{-- WRONG: the name says where it is used, so it is wrong the
     moment it is used somewhere else --}}
@include('partials.dashboard-row')       {{-- now on the orders
                                                 page too --}}
@include('partials.modal-footer')        {{-- now in a drawer too --}}

{{-- (2) A COMPONENT NAMED AS AN ADJECTIVE PLUS A NOUN, so the tag
     READS AS MARKUP. This is the convention with a benefit that is
     not vanity: it is the test that the name describes the thing
     rather than the file. --}}
<x-order-row :order="$order" />       {{-- reads: an order row --}}
<x-user-avatar :user="$user" />       {{-- reads: a user avatar --}}
<x-empty-state :title="..." />        {{-- reads: an empty state --}}

<x-order-row-thing :order="$order" /> {{-- reads: nothing --}}
<x-component-thing />                 {{-- reads: nothing --}}

{{-- (3) A VIEW NAMED FOR THE RESOURCE, not the page it appears on. --}}
{{-- RIGHT: the resource owns the view --}}
resources/views/orders/show.blade.php
resources/views/orders/index.blade.php
resources/views/orders/_form.blade.php

{{-- WRONG: named after the page, and wrong the moment it is used
     somewhere else --}}
resources/views/dashboard.blade.php    {{-- now also embedded in
                                              the reports page, and
                                              still called
                                              "dashboard" --}}
resources/views/modal.blade.php       {{-- now in a drawer too --}}

{{-- AND THE DIRECTORY STRUCTURE THAT FOLLOWS FROM (3), which is the
     part that pays: --}}
{{--
resources/views/
    orders/
        show.blade.php
        index.blade.php
        _form.blade.php              a partial of this resource
    components/
        order-row.blade.php           reusable across resources
    partials/
        pagination.blade.php          generic, no resource
    layouts/
        app.blade.php

     The two underscores and two directories carry meaning: a file
     beginning with an underscore is a partial and never rendered on
     its own, and a file inside a resource directory belongs to that
     resource. --}}

{{-- THE TEST FOR A NAME, which is worth applying in review: does
     the name describe the THING or the FILE? --}}
{{--   order-row        the thing   RIGHT
     dashboard-row     the file    WRONG
     order-row-thing   neither     WRONG
     x-order-row       the thing   RIGHT
     x-component-thing neither     WRONG --}}

19.5 View composers and shared data

Reviews the composer and share mechanisms and states the rule that keeps data discoverable.

A composer is justified when the same data is needed by a family of views — a layout and the pages that use it — and the data is not naturally the responsibility of any one controller. Beyond that case, pass data from the controller.

Composers and shared data, and the line between them

A composer attaches data to named views. Shared data is attached to every view. The distinction is scope, and the confusion between them produces a bug that has a specific shape: a composer for one view is relied upon by a partial that is rendered from several views, and it works for one of them.

The rule that prevents it is narrow and worth following exactly: a composer that a partial depends on must be registered for that partial's view, not for the view that happens to include it. That sounds pedantic and it is the difference between a partial that works everywhere and one that works in the place it was written.

Shared data, and the three values that earn it

The locale, the current user, and a flag set. All three are genuinely global, and the test is whether any view would be wrong without them. A fourth is the application's name and configuration-derived brand values, which are configuration rather than data. Everything else belongs on a named view.

// AppServiceProvider — shared, request-wide, computed once.
View::composer('layouts.app', NavigationComposer::class);

// Never: a query executed on every view, including error pages.
View::composer('*', function ($view) {
    $view->with('carts', Cart::query()->count());
});

The wildcard composer is the pattern to avoid: it couples every view in the application to one query, including error pages rendered after an exception. When a value is genuinely global — the application name, the active theme — set it once at boot; when it is data, pass it explicitly.


{{-- THE LINE, and the bug the confusion produces: --}}
View::composer('orders.show', function ($view) {
    $view->with('related', Order::relatedTo($view->getData()['order']));
});

{{-- resources/views/orders/show.blade.php --}}
@include('partials.order-related')
{{-- and partials/order-related.blade.php reads $related. It works
     HERE. It also breaks the moment that partial is included from
     another view with no such composer — and the error is an
     UNDEFINED VARIABLE rendering as an empty section, not a
     failure. --}}

{{-- THE RULE: a composer that a PARTIAL depends on must be
     registered for that PARTIAL's view. --}}
View::composer('partials.order-related', function ($view) {
    $view->with('related', Order::relatedTo($view->getData()['order']));
});
{{-- ^ pedantic, and it is the difference between a partial that
     works everywhere and one that works where it was written. --}}

{{-- SHARED DATA, and the THREE VALUES that earn it — chosen by one
     test: would any view be WRONG without them? --}}
// app/Providers/AppServiceProvider.php
View::share('locale', app()->getLocale());     {{-- every view may
                                                      legitimately
                                                      need it, and
                                                      none is wrong
                                                      without it --}}
View::share('currentUser', auth()->user());    {{-- borderline --
                                                      fine for a nav
                                                      bar, wrong for
                                                      page content --}}
View::share('flags', FeatureFlags::all());     {{-- borderline --
                                                      and better
                                                      served by a
                                                      component that
                                                      asks for them --}}
View::share('brand', config('brand'));         {{-- configuration,
                                                      not data: fine --}}

{{-- AND THE COST, which is the thing shared data hides: --}}
{{-- resources/views/components/layouts/app.blade.php --}}
<html lang="{{ $locale }}">
    <body style="--brand: {{ $brand['colour'] }}">
        {{ $slot }}
    </body>
</html>
{{-- ^ NOW RENDER THIS FROM A PLACE THAT DID NOT GO THROUGH THE
     PROVIDER: a console command, a queued job, a test. In a plain
     unit test $locale is undefined, and read with the default
     conditional it renders as an EMPTY lang attribute — a page
     with no language, which a screen reader will guess. --}}

{{-- THE NARROWER TOOL, and the reason composers are underused is
     that global sharing is faster to write: --}}
View::composer('components.layouts.app', function ($view) {
    $view->with([
        'locale' => app()->getLocale(),
        'brand'  => config('brand.colour'),
    ]);
});
{{-- ^ same data, same convenience, and now the layout has ONE
     dependency on ONE view that a test can satisfy in one line. --}}

19.6 A review checklist

Consolidates the guide into a sequence of questions that can be applied to any template during review.

  1. Does the template query, calculate or authorise anything it should be given?
  2. Is every untrusted value rendered with the escaped echo?
  3. Is every raw echo justified, with a named sanitiser or framework origin?
  4. Are relationships read in the loop eager loaded in the controller?
  5. Does each component have one root element and a documented props contract?
  6. Does each cached fragment contain nothing user-specific?
  7. Are user-facing strings translated as whole sentences?
  8. Will a failure in this template name the template rather than a compiled file?

The eighth item is satisfied by ensuring the template compiles cleanly during the build, so the first person to see a syntax error is the developer, not a customer.

Automate what you can. Questions two, three, six and eight are all decidable by a grep or a build step. Reserve human review for questions one and four, which require understanding intent.

Exercises 19

  1. Apply the checklist to three templates of differing quality and record which questions each fails.
  2. Extract the work from one template into a view model and confirm the template contains no domain logic.
  3. Replace a wildcard view composer with an explicit controller dependency and measure the difference on an error page.
  4. Add the automated checks from questions two, three, six and eight to the pipeline and record the run time.

Why the checklist is ordered by cost

A reviewer with fifteen minutes should look at the things that cost the most when they are wrong, and the ordering is deliberately not the order in which the defects appear in the file. A missing eager load costs a page its response time on every request, forever, and it is invisible in a diff. A missing alt attribute costs one screen-reader user one image, once. Both are absences, and the first is the one worth the reviewer's time.

The two questions at the end — is every relation loaded and where is that decided, and what makes every raw form's source trusted — are the ones that find the most, because they are the two places where a template depends on a fact that lives in another file. Everything else on the list is either visible in the diff or cheap to fix afterwards. Those two are not, which is why they are first.

The part a checklist cannot do

It cannot tell you whether the eager load is the right one, whether the cache key is complete, or whether the component boundary is in the right place. Those are judgement, and a checklist can only tell you that they were looked at. The mechanical half — the directives, the raw forms, the nesting, the compilation — belongs in the two commands, because those are checks rather than opinions.


{{-- A CHECKLIST, ordered by the COST of the defect rather than by
     position in the file. --}}

{{-- 1. EVERY RELATION READ IN THE TEMPLATE. Is it loaded, and in
       which file is that decided? The template cannot answer this
       and a reader of it cannot either — which is why it is first. --}}
{{ $order->customer->name }}
{{--       loaded in the controller? eager loading is NOT recursive
           --}}                      ^ "lines" is loaded but
                                          "lines.product" is not

{{-- 2. EVERY RAW INTERPOLATION. What makes its source trusted, and
       where is that stated? --}}
{!! $page->body !!}
{{--       justified within three lines above it? --}}

{{-- 3. EVERY PROP THE COMPONENT REQUIRES. Is it passed at every
       call site? --}}
<x-order-summary :order="$o" />
{{--       currency has a default; customer does not --}}

{{-- 4. EVERY QUERY. Is it inside a loop? --}}
@foreach ($orders as $o)
    {{ $o->lines()->count() }}      {{-- one COUNT per row --}}
@endforeach

{{-- 5. EVERY FORM. Is the action the verb the route accepts? --}}
<form method="POST">                 {{-- the route wants DELETE,
                                           and the form does not
                                           say so --}}

{{-- 6. EVERY CONDITIONAL ON AUTH. Does the route repeat it? --}}
@can('delete', $order)              {{-- a hiding check is not an
                                           enforcing one --}}


{{-- 7. EVERY PLACEHOLDER. Is it a translation key or a literal
       English string? --}}
<p>{{ __('orders.title') }}</p>      {{-- good --}}
<p>Order details</p>                  {{-- not translated --}}

{{-- 8. EVERY DATE AND NUMBER. Does it use a format the LOCALE
       should decide? --}}
{{ $order->created_at->format('d/m/Y') }}   {{-- the field order is
                                                   fixed by the
                                                   format string
                                                   rather than by
                                                   the locale --}}

{{-- 9. EVERY CONDITIONAL NESTING. Over two levels? --}}

{{-- 10. EVERY FRAGMENT. Does it read a variable the parent did not
        pass? --}}
@include('partials.order-row')

{{-- 11. EVERY CACHED FRAGMENT. Does its key include everything it
        reads? --}}
@cache('summary', 300)

{{-- 12. EVERY INTERACTIVE REGION. Does it work without JavaScript,
        or is that a deliberate choice somebody made? --}}

{{-- AND THE ONE THAT FINDS THE MOST, stated as two questions: --}}
{{--   every relation read  -> is it loaded, and where is that
                               decided?
     every raw form        -> what makes its source trusted, and
                               where is that stated?
   ^ Both are the same shape: a fact the template depends on that
     lives in another file, and a reader of the template cannot see
     it. That is why a review that reads for style finds none of
     them. --}}

{{-- AND THE ONE TO RUN WHEN THERE IS TIME, because it is the only
     one that finds an ABSENCE: --}}
// .github/workflows/lint.yml
- run: php artisan views:verify     {{-- renders every template:
                                           finds an unregistered
                                           directive, an
                                           unresolvable
                                           component, an
                                           unbalanced @section --}}
- run: php artisan views:lint       {{-- the raw forms with no
                                           justification, the
                                           nesting, the arbitrary
                                           values --}}
{{-- ^ both are cheap, both fail the build, and together they are
     the mechanical half of this checklist. What remains — is the
     eager load right, is the key complete — is what a human is
     for, and it is the last two items on the list rather than the
     first two. --}}
